* TODO Multiple Testing

I will first review the traditional approach to statistical testing,
including p-values, multiple testing, and sequential testing,
before presenting the notion of e-values.

** Statistical tests

Statistical tests answer the question "is there something noticeable in my data?".
More precisely, we start with a descroption of what we mean by "there is nothing noticeable":
this is the null hypotheses, H₀ -- a set of probability distributions,
for instance the set of all probability distributions on ℝ with mean zero.
We then compute a quantity the test statistic,
which has a known distribution(*)
if the data comes from one of the distributions in H₀.
If the test statistic is above its 1-α quantile, for a predefined value of α
(often 5%), we reject H₀ (we claim that there is something noticeable in the data), otherwise, we do not reject H₀
(we answer that we do not have enough data to see if there is something noticeable in the data).

We can make two types or error:
type I error, incorrectly rejecting H₀;
type II error, not rejecting H₀ when we should have.

** p-values

Instead of test statistics (each test has its own distribution,
so test statistics can be difficult to interpret), we can consider p-values,
i.e., transformations of the test statistics that are uniformly distributed(*) in [0,1]
if the data comes from H₀, and closer to 0 if not.

Note that the p-value is the probability of seeing such an extreme test statistic if the data comes from H₀ --
it is not the probability that H₀ is true.

Another way of interpreting the p-value is as a random number,
which either comes from the uniform distribution(*) if H₀ is true,
or from another, unspecified distribution if not.
We have a single observation (n=1), we do not know what that unspecified distribution is,
and we have to decide whether it comes from H₀ or not.

(*) We made a simplification in that distribution: it is not always possible for the p-value to be uniformly distributed.
For instance, if  the null hypothesis is composite, i.e., if it contains several distributions,
the distribution of the test statistic and therefore of the p-value may depend on which distribution in H₀ the data comes from.
In this case, we just require that the distribution of the p-values, for each distribution in H₀,
be stochastically greater than Unif(0,1).
A similar problem happens with discrete distributions: the p-values then follow a discrete distribution as well, so it cannot be uniform.

** FWER and FDR

There are several ways of generalizing the notion of type I error to multiple tests:
we can look at the (expected) proportion of type I erorrs in N tests
(FDR, false discovery rate)
or at the probability of making one or more type I error in N tests (FWER, family-wise error rate).
If the number of tests is large, it will be difficult to control the FWER.

Adjusting the p-values for the FWER is easy:
if the tests are independent, use Šidák's adjustment, p ← 1 - (1-p)ᴺ,
or its approximation, Bonferroni's adjustment, p ← Np.

Those adjustments can be improved if you have the list of p-values (Holm):
sort the p-values (from smallest to largest),
and adjust them as
pₖ ← (N-k+1)pₖ
(more previsely, pₖ ← Max{ (N-ℓ+1)p_ℓ, ℓ≤k };
the Holm-Šidák adjustment is similar).

For FDR, this is trickier:
sort the p-values, from the smallest to the largest, and use the Benjamini-Hochberg (BH) adjustment,
pₖ ← (N/k) pₖ
or, more precisely,
pₖ ← Min{ N p_ℓ / ℓ, ℓ≥k }
(for dependent testsm the Benjamini-Yekutieli (BHY) adjustment multiplies this by
∑{1/k, k≤N}).
(Strictly speaking, the BH procedure does not control the FDR, but P(H₀)×FDR,
where P(H₀) is unknown, but often close to 1.)

TODO: Resampling (White, Romano-Wolf)

If there is a very large number of tests (tens of thousands),
we can do have a better FDR control
by estimating P(H₀) (q-values)
or the distribution of the p-values under H₀ and H₁ (local FDR).

  \emph{Statistical significance for genomewide studies}\\
  J.D. Storey and R. Tibshirani

** Sequential testing

So far, we assumed we had access to all the data but,
in many situations, data is progressively collected over time:
we may want to continuously test as the data arrives.
More precisely, we want to peek at the data,
a times 1,2,...,T, perform a test each time, and stop collecting data as soon as we reject H_0.
Of course, we cannot use the single-test critical values to decide when to reject the null:
the type I error would be too large.

We can proceed as follows (group sequential design).
Design the tests so that the test statistics
form a Brownian motion S₁,...,S_T,
Cov(Sₛ,Sₜ)=min(s,t);
the corresponding standardized statistics are
Zₜ = Sₜ/√t, Cov(Zₛ,Zₜ) = √( s/t ), s≤t.
We can compute the type I errpr rate α
from the critical values c₁,...,c_T:

    α = P[ ∃ t : Zₜ ≥ cₜ ].

If we fix α, there are many solutions for the cₜ's:
for instance, we can choose them to be constant (Pocock boundary)
or (better) to be proportional to 1/√t (O'Brien-Fleming – early stopping requires stronger evidence).
It is also possible to adjust the p-values.

More generally, alpha-spending considers a spending function
α:[0,1]→[0,α],
α(0)=0, α(1)=α, α↗
and uses critical values cₜ such that

  P[ Zₜ<cₜ, Z₁<c₁,...,Zₜ₋₁<cₜ₋₁ ] = α(t/T) - α((t-1)/T).


  \emph{Sequential analysis testing}\\
  J.S. Koopmeiners (2012)

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{White's reality check\\ and Romano-Wolf stepdown}
\end{book}
\emph{White's reality check} controls for the FWER as follows:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Put the data (e.g., residuals of $n$ models)
  in a matrix, one model per column;
\item Compute the corresponding $n$ test statistics and take their maximum:
  we want to know if it is significant;
\item Generate $R$ bootstrap samples (e.g., block bootstrap, separately for each row), compute the test statistics (one for each column),
  and take their maximums;
\item The $p$-value is the quantile of the observed maximum statistic among the $R$ bootstrap maximum statistics.
\end{compactitem}
This only tests of the best test statistic is significant.
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=.1,yscale=.15,inner sep=1pt,outer sep=1pt,every node/.style={anchor=base}]
\begin{scope}
  \foreach \i in {0,...,7} {
    \draw (0,\i) -- (10,\i) -- (10,\i+1) -- (0,\i+1) -- cycle;
  }
  \foreach[count=\i] \a in {2,6,7,0,4,5,3,1} {
    \draw[very thick,red!50!black] (\a,\i-1) -- (\a+3,\i-1) -- (\a+3,\i) -- (\a,\i) -- cycle;
  }
\end{scope}
\begin{scope}[shift={(20,0)}]
  \foreach \i in {0,...,7} {
    \draw[very thick,red!50!black] (0,\i) -- (3,\i) -- (3,\i+1) -- (0,\i+1) -- cycle;
  }
\end{scope}
\begin{scope}[shift={(33,.5)}]
  \draw (0,3) -- (3,3) -- (3,4) -- (0,4) -- cycle;
  \foreach \i in {0,2,4} {
    \draw (\i*.5,3) -- (\i*.5+1,3) -- (\i*.5+1,4) -- (\i*.5,4) -- cycle;
  }
\end{scope}
\draw[->] (12,4) -- (18,4);
\draw[->] (25,4) -- (31,4);
\end{tikzpicture}
\]

The \emph{Romano-Wolf stepdown} approach
applies White's reality check iteratively:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Sort the hypotheses, from the smallest $p$-value (largest statistic) to the largest;
\item Apply White's reality check;
\item If significant, reject the first null hypothesis and continue with the remaining ones;
\item If not, accept the remaining nulls.
\end{compactitem}

These approaches to FWER control are more powerful than Bonferroni,
because they account for the dependence between the tests.

** DONE TO DELETE Multiple-testing: q-values

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Statistical significance for genomewide studies}\\
  J.D. Storey and R. Tibshirani
\end{book}
The $p$-value controls the false positive rate
\[ \dfrac{ \text{FP} }{ \text{FP} + \text{TN} }. \]
Instead, we may be interested in the false discovery rate (FDR)\
\[ \dfrac{ \text{FP} }{ \text{FP} + \text{TP} }, \]
but the number of true positives depends on the alternative hypothesis $H_1$, which is unknown.
The proportion of true $H_0$ can be estimated by looking at the proportion of $p$-values beyond $\lambda=0.5$
(no $H_1$ should be there, and the $p$-values for $H_0$ are uniformly in $[0,1]$):
\begin{align*}
  \hat \pi_0 &= \dfrac{ \# \{ p_i > \lambda \} }{ m ( 1 - \lambda ) } \\
  m &= \text{number of tests} \gg 1.
\end{align*}
Instead of fixing $\lambda = 0.5$, we can use all the values $\lambda \in [0,1]$,
fit a cubic spline $f$ (with df=3) to the corresponding $\hat \pi_0(\lambda)$,
and use $\hat\pi_0 = f(1)$.

The FDR, if we reject $H_0$ when $p_i < t$, is approximately
\[ \widehat{ \text{FDR} } (t) = \dfrac{ \hat \pi_0 \cdot m \cdot t }{ \# \{ p_i \leq t \} }. \]
The \d{q-values} are
\[ q_i = \hat q(p_i) = \min_{ t \geq p_i } \widehat{ \text{FDR} } (t). \]
The $q$-value is not the probability of a false positive --
it is the expected proportion of false positives if we use that value as a threshold --
the probability can be much higher if there are smaller $q$-values.

** DONE TO DELETE Sequential Testing
*** Multiple testing: sequential testing

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Sequential analysis testing}\\
  J.S. Koopmeiners (2012)
\end{book}
Given
$X_1,\dots,X_n \overset{\text{iid}}{\sim} N(\mu_x, \sigma^2)$,
$Y_1,\dots,Y_n \overset{\text{iid}}{\sim} N(\mu_y, \sigma^2)$,
$\sigma$ known,
we want to test
\[ H_0: \mu_x = \mu_y
\qquad
\text{vs}
\qquad
H_1: \mu_x \neq \mu_y \]
using the test statistic
\[ Z_k = \dfrac{ \bar x_k - \bar y_k }{ \sqrt{ 2 \sigma^2 / k } }. \]

If we only perform the test at $k=n$,
since $Z_n \sim N(0,1)$ under $H_0$,
we reject $H_0$ if $\va{Z_n} > z_{ 1 - \alpha/2 }$.
But what if we perform $K$ tests, at $k=n/K$, $2n/K$, \ldots, $Kn/K$?
We can use a stopping rule of the form
``if $\va{ z_k} > c_k$, reject $H_0$''.
There are many choices for the $c_k$:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Pocock bounds are constant: $c_k = C_{\text{PK}}(\alpha, K)$;
\item O'Brian-Fleming bounds progressively decrease the critical values
  \[ c_k = C_{\text{OF}}(\alpha,K) \sqrt{ \dfrac K k } \propto k^{-1/2}; \]
\item Wang-Tsiatis bounds are inbetween
  \[ c_k = C_{\text{WT}}(\alpha, K, \Delta) \left( \dfrac k K \right) ^{ -1/2+\Delta }
  \propto k ^{ -1/2+\Delta } ; \]
\item Haybittle-Peto bounds are locally constant
  \[
  c_k = \begin{cases}
    3 & \text{if } k < K \\
    C_{\text{HP}}( \alpha, K ) & \text{ otherwise.}
  \end{cases}
  \]
\end{compactitem}
R implementation in \texttt{gsDesign}.

We could also have stopping rules of the form
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item If $z_k > b_k$, stop and reject $H_0$,
\item If $z_k < a_K$, stop and fail to reject $H_0$,
\end{compactitem}
(with $a_K = b_K$, to ensure we take a decision at the end), for instance, thw power family of tests
\begin{align*}
  b_k &= C_u \cdot \left( \dfrac kK \right) ^{ \Delta - 1/2 } \\
  a_k &= \alpha + C_\ell \cdot \left( \dfrac kK \right) ^{ \Delta - 1/2 }.
\end{align*}

We could even have an inner wedge:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item If $\va{ z_k } > b_k$, stop and reject $H_0$;
\item If $\va{ z_k } < a_k$, stop and fail to reject $H_0$.
\end{compactitem}

The \emph{error spending} approach selects the thresholds so that the type I error after the $k$th step
be $\alpha_1 + \cdots + \alpha_K$
(with $\alpha = \sum \alpha_k$) or, equivalently,
$f(k/K)$ (with $f(0)=0$, $f(1)=\alpha$, $f$ non-decreasing), where $f$ is the \emph{error spending function}, e.g.,
$f(t) = \alpha ( 1 - e^{\gamma t})(1-e^{-\gamma})$ or $f(t) = \alpha t^\rho$.
As before, we can choose two error spending functions to control both error types,
and even allow for an inner wedge.

If your test statistic is not Gaussian, convert the (Gaussian) critical values to $p$-values.
% T-test with unknown and unequal variances, binomial data, survival analysis. etc.

The several stopping points change the distribution of $\hat\beta$:
it is no longer unbiased.
\[ \E[ \hat\beta ] = \beta + b(\beta) \]
(You can adjust for the bias by finding $\beta_w$ such that
$\hat\beta = \beta_w + b(\beta_w)$ -- but this is only bias-adjusted, not unbiased.)

To assess the strength of the evidence
(usually done with $p$-values), we need to compare pairs $(k, z_k)$:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Stagewise ordering (lexicographic order);
\item MLE: compare the $z_k / \sigma_k$;
\item Likelihood: compare the $z_k$;
\item Score: compare the $\sigma_k z_k$.
\end{compactitem}
The $p$-value is then
\[ \operatorname*{P}_{ (T, Z_T) \sim H_0 } \bigl[
  (T, Z_T) \succcurlyeq (k, z_k)
  \bigr] \]
(only the stagewise ordering is consistent with the test result).

Confidence intervals are trickier:
first, find $k_u$, $z_u$, $k_\ell$, $z_\ell$ such that
\begin{align*}
  \mathrm{P} \bigl[ (T,z_T) \succcurlyeq (k_u,z_u) \bigr] &= \alpha/2 \\
  \mathrm{P} \bigl[ (T,z_T) \preccurlyeq (k_u,z_u) \bigr] &= \alpha/2,
\end{align*}
then, compute the acceptance region
\[ A(\theta_0) = \bigl\{\,
(k,z) \ {:}\ (k_\ell,z_\ell) \preccurlyeq (k,z) \preccurlyeq (k_u,z_u)
\,\bigr\}
\]
and the confidence set
\[ \bigl\{ \, \theta \ {:} \ (k,z) \in A(\theta) \,\} . \]
Under reasonable assumptions (and no inner wedge),
it is an interval with the stagewise ordering
(otherwise, use the MLE ordering).

We can also compute repeated confidence intervals from the thresholds $c_k$ --
they will be larger than the naive ones.

Sequential designs only change the sample size.
\emph{Adaptive designs} also change treatment dose
(e.g., for phase I trials -- toxicity tests and dose finding),
randomization ratio, etc.

A Bayesian approach can be used for phase I trials,
with a prior on the maximum tolerable dose;
we can jointly model toxicity and efficacy.

Stage II trials test efficacy, and may allow early termination for futility:
stop when the \emph{predictive probability}
(the probability of rejecting $H_0$ if we were to continue the trial until the end)
or the posterior probability
$P\bigl[ p > p_0 + \delta \,|\, \mathscr F_k \bigr]$
becomes too small or too large.
We can also jointly monitor $P[\text{efficacy}]$
and $P[\text{toxicity}]$.
Hierarchical models allow for several populations (or diseases).

Phase III trials can use Bayesian sequential designs, to incorporate historical data
(from phases I and II,
for for the standard of care), e.g., with a power prior
\[ \theta \sim \text{Lik} ( \theta \,|\, \text{history} )^ {\alpha_0}  \pi_0(\theta); \]
phases II and III can be combined (hierarchical design,
or seamless phase II/III design).
Computing the sample size requires the estimation of nuisance parameters.

*** Multiple testing: group sequential methods

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{An introduction to group sequential methods: planning and multi-aspect optimization}\\
  F.I. Lewis (2023)
\end{book}
For a group sequential design with $K$ analyses,
assume the test statistics
$(Z_1,\dots,Z_K)$ are multivariate Gaussian with
\[ \E[Z_k] = \theta \sqrt{ I_k }, \quad \cov[ Z_k,Z_\ell] = \sqrt{ I_k / I_\ell } \quad (k\leq \ell). \]
For $K=2$, the thresholds $c$
(and either the power $\beta$ or the sample size $N$) can be chosen sUch that
\begin{align*}
  & \mathrm P_{\!H_0} \bigl[ Z_1 > c_{12} \bigr] = \alpha_1  \\
  & \mathrm P_{\!H_0} \bigl[ Z_1 > c_{12} \wedge Z_2 > c_2 \bigr] = \alpha_2  \\
  & \mathrm P_{\!H_1} \bigl[ Z_1 < c_{11} \bigr] = \beta_1  \\
  & \mathrm P_{\!H_1} \bigl[ Z_1 \in (c_{11},c_{12}) \wedge Z_2 < c_2 \bigr] = \beta_2
\end{align*}
where
\begin{align*}
  \alpha_1 &= f(I_1/I_2)        & \beta_1 &= g(I_1/I_2) \\
  \alpha_2 &= \alpha - \alpha_1 & \beta_2 &= \beta - \beta_1 \\
  f(t) &= \alpha t & g(t) &= \beta t^3.
\end{align*}
(other alpha spending functions can be used, and we can add more interim analyses on the way.)

*** Multiple testing: sequential testing, alpha investing

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Online rules for control of false discovery rate and false discovery exceedance}\\
  A. Javanmard and A. Monanari (2017)
\end{book}
To adjust $p$-values to control the FDR,
Benjamini-Hochberg (BH) assumes all the $p$-values are known.
In an online context,
\emph{alpha investing} uses a potential function $W$ to select the test thresholds $\alpha_j$ such that
\begin{align*}
  \phi_j &= \dfrac{ \alpha_j }{ 1 - \alpha_j } \leq W_{j-1} \\
  \psi_j &= b + \phi_j \\
  R_j &= \mathbf 1_{ p_j \leq \alpha_j } \\
  W_j &= \begin{cases}
    W_{j-1} + \psi_j - \phi_j & \text{if } R_j = 1 \\
    W_{j-1} - \phi_j & \text{if } R_j = 0 .
  \end{cases}
\end{align*}
The FDR and mFDR (marginal FDR) are
\begin{align*}
  \text{FDR} &= \sum_\theta \E \left[ \dfrac{ \text{FP} }{ \text{FP} + \text{TP} } \right] \\
  \text{pFDR} &= \E \left[ \dfrac{ \text{FP} }{ \text{FP} + \text{TP} } \mid| \text{FP} + \text{TP} > 0 \right] \\
  \text{mFDR} &= \sum_\theta \left[
    \dfrac{ \E[ \text{FP} ] }{ \E[ \text{FP} + \text{TP} ] \color{gray} + \eta }
    \right]
  \overset{\text{convexity}}{\leq} \text{FDR} \\
\end{align*}
The false discovery exceedance is
\[ \text{FDX}_\gamma = \sum_\theta \mathrm{P} \left[ \
  \dfrac{ \text{FP} }{ \text{FP} + \text{TP} } \geq \gamma \right].
% q-values control pFDR rather than FDR
\]

*** Multiple testing: sequential testing

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Sequential testing}\\
  R. Nowak (2011)
\end{book}
Consider the null and alternative hypotheses
\begin{align*}
  H_0 &: X_i \overset{\text{iid}}{\sim} p_0 \\
  H_1 &: X_i \sim p_1
\end{align*}
(they have to be simple);
choose $\alpha$ and $\beta$,
the desired type I and type II error rates;
compute the thresholds
\begin{align*}
  a &= \log \dfrac{ \beta }{ 1 - \alpha } < 0\\
  b &= \log \dfrac{ 1 - \beta }{ \alpha } > 0\
\end{align*}
and the cummulated sums
\[ \Lambda_k = \sum_{ i = 1 } ^k \log \dfrac{ p_1(x_i) }{ p_0(x_i) }. \]
The \emph{sequential probability ratio test} (SRPT)
accepts $H_p$ (resp $H_0$)
as soon as $\Lambda_k > b$
(resp $\Lambda_k < a$).

** TODO E-values
*** Multiple testing: SAVI

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Game theoretic statistics and\\ sequential anytime valid inference (SAVI):\\ a martingale theory of evidence}\\
  (ICML 2025)
\end{book}
\textbf{1a.}
\emph{P-value peeking} is the continuous monitoring of a p-value until it becomes ``significant'':
even if we have
$\forall n \ P[ p_n \leq \alpha ] \leq \alpha$,
for the stopping time
$\tau = \min \{ n : p_n \leq \alpha \}$,
we have
$P[ p_\tau \leq \alpha ] \not\leq \alpha$ (it is usually 1).
SAVI (safe, valid anytime inference) will provide that for arbitrary stopping times.

\textbf{1b.}
Fisher's tea lady claimed she could tell if milk had been poured before or after the tea.
To test her claim, we can design a \emph{betting game} so that,
under $H_0$ (she cannot tell) she cannot make money.
\begin{align*}
  & L_t \text{: wealth} \\
  & \lambda_t \text{: proportion of wealth gambled} \\
  & R_t = \pm 1 \text{: whether she is right} \\
  & L_t = \prod_{ i \leq t } ( 1 + \lambda_i R_i )
\end{align*}
Under $H_0$, $L_t$ is a non-negative martingale (a fair game):
\begin{compactitem}[(ii)]
\item[(i)] $\E_{ H_0 }[ L_\tau ] \leq 1$ for any stopping time $\tau$
  (optional stopping theorem);
\item[(ii)] $P[\, \exists t \,:\, L_t \leq 1/\alpha \,] \leq \alpha $ (Ville's theorem).
\end{compactitem}
We say that:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item $L_t$ is an \emph{e-process};
\item $\inf\limits_{ s \leq t } 1 / L_s$ is a \emph{p-process} (an any-time valid p-value);
\item $\mathbf1_{ L_t \geq 1/\alpha }$ is a level-$\alpha$ sequential test for $H_0$.
\end{compactitem}
If $H_0$ is true, she cannot make money, if it is false, she can.
A strategy to make money if $H_0$ is false defines a test and an estimator
(there can be good and bad strategies, tests, estimators).

\textbf{1c.}
To find a good betting stratgy, let us look at \emph{Kelly's game}:
we bet on a biased coin ($p>1/2$, $p$ unknown) a proportion $\lambda$ of our wealth each time.
\begin{align*}
  W_t &= \prod_{ i \leq t } ( 1 + \lambda R ) \\
  &= \exp \bigl[ t \E [ \log( 1 + \lambda R ) ] + o(t) \bigr]  % ???
\end{align*}
We can maximize the rate of growth
\[ \lim_{ t \to \infty } \dfrac{ \E \bigl[ \log W_t(\lambda) \bigr] }{ t } = \E \bigl[ \log(1+\lambda R) \bigr] ; \]
this gives
\begin{align*}
  & \lambda^*** = 2( p - \tfrac12 ) \\
  & W_t(\lambda^*) = \exp \bigl[ t \text{KL}( p \| \tfrac12 ) + o(t) \bigr] \\
  & \lim\limits_{ t\to\infty } \dfrac{ \E[ \log W_t ] }{ t } = \text{KL} ( p \| \tfrac12 ).
\end{align*}
The Kelly principle also maximizes:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item The expected time to reach a threshold wealth;
\item The expected wealth at a threshold time.
\end{compactitem}

\textbf{1d.}
A \emph{null hypothesis} $H_0$ is a set $\mathscr P$ of distributions
on a filtered probability space $(\Omega, \mathscr F)$.
An \emph{alternative hypothesis} $H_1$ is another set $\mathscr Q$
of distributions, disjoint from $\mathscr P$.
An \emph{e-variable} (or \emph{e-value})
is a $[0,\infty]$-valued random variable $e$ such that
\[ \forall P \in \mathscr P \quad \E\nolimits_P[e] \leq 1 . \]
An \emph{e-process} is a sequence of e-variables
$(e_t)_t$ such that, for any stopping time $\tau$
\[ \forall \tau \quad \forall P \in \mathscr P \quad \E\nolimits_P[ e_\tau ] \leq 1. \]
A \emph{test supermartingale} is a sequence $(M_t)_t$ of random variables such that
$M \geq 0$, $M_0 = 1$ and
\[ \forall P \in \mathscr P \quad \forall t \geq 1 \quad \E\nolimits_P[ M_t \mid M_0 , \dots, M_{t-1} ] \leq M_{t-1}, \]
\ie, in expectation, the wealth decreases
(for a \emph{test martingale}, replace $\leq$ with $=$).

If $L$ is an e-process, then (Ville's inequality)
\[ \forall P \in \mathscr P \quad P[ \, \exists t \geq 1 \, :\, L_t \geq /1\alpha \, ] \leq \alpha \]
and $\tau = \inf \{ t \geq 1 \ :\ L_t \geq 1/\alpha \}$
is a level-$\alpha$ test for $\mathscr P$.
All level-$\alpha$ sequential tests are of this form.

The corresponding e-process need not be a martingale, or even a supermartingale.

\textbf{1e.}
Consider the case when both $H_0$ and $H_1$ are simple
\[ H_0: X_i \sim P \qquad H_1: X_i \sim Q \]
and a game, starting with initial capital $W_0 = 1$
in which the bets are
$S_t : \mathscr X \to [0,\infty]$
such that
$\E\nolimits_P \bigl[ S_t(X_t) \mid X_1,\dots,X_{t-1} \bigr] \leq 1 $
and the wealth is updated, once $X_t$ is revealed, as
$W_t = W_{t-1} \cdot S( X_t )$.
The log-optimal bet is the likelihood ratio (aka Radon Nikodym derivative)
\[ S_t(x) = \dfrac{ q(x) }{ p(x) } \]
and the wealth process
\[ W_T^*** = \prod_{ i = 1 } ^T \dfrac{ q(X_i) }{ p(X_i) } \]
is a test martingale.

We have $\E_p[ \log W_\tau ] \leq 0$ and
$\E\nolimits_Q[ \log W_t ]$ is maximized -- it is $T \cdot \text{KL}( Q \| P )$.

\textbf{1f.}
Consider now a composite null and a simple alternative.
The optimal bet is still a likelihood ratio
\[ X^*** = \dfrac{ p(X) }{ p^*(X) }, \]
where $p^*$ is the \emph{reverse information projection} and $X^*$ is the \emph{numeraire}:
\begin{compactitem}[(ii)]
\item [(i)] $X^*** \geq 0$, $\forall P \ \E\nolimits_P[ X^*] \leq 1$ ($X^*$ is an e-variable);
\item For all e-variables $X$, $\E\nolimits_Q[X/X^*] \leq 1$.
\end{compactitem}
From Jensen's inequality,
\begin{align*}
  & \E\nolimits_Q[ X^*/X] \geq 1 \\
  & \E\nolimits_Q\bigl[ \log(X/X^*)\bigr] \leq 1.
\end{align*}
We can define $P^*$ from its likelihood ratio
\[ \dfrac{ dP^*}{ dQ } = \dfrac1{X^*}. \]
The \emph{polar} and the \emph{bipolar} of the null $\mathscr P$ are
\begin{align*}
  \mathscr P^\circ &= \{ X \geq 0 \ :\ \forall P \in \mathscr P \ \, \E\nolimits_P[X] \leq 1 \} \\
  &= \{ \text{e-variables} \} \\
  \mathscr P^{\circ\circ} &= \{ P \geq 0 \ :\ \forall X \in \mathscr P^\circ \ \, \E\nolimits_P[X] \leq 1 \} \\
  &= \text{``effective $H_0$''}.
\end{align*}
There is a strong duality
\begin{align*}
  \E\nolimits_Q[ \log X^*]
  &= \sup_{ X \in \mathscr P^\circ} \E\nolimits_Q[ \log X ] \\
  &= \inf_{ P \in \mathscr P^{\circ\circ} }\text{KL}(Q\|P) \\
  &= \text{KL}( Q \| P^*** ).
\end{align*}

Examples include
\begin{align*}
  \mathscr P &= \{ \text{symmetric distributions} \} \\
  \intertext{or}
  \mathscr P &= \{ \text{sub-gaussian distributions} \}.
\end{align*}

\textbf{1g.}
The case of a simple null and composite alternative
\[ H_0: X \sim \mathscr P \qquad H_1 : X \sim Q_\theta, \text{ for some $\theta\in\Theta$} \]
is easier:
just hedge your bets with a prior $\pi$
\begin{align*}
  W_T &= \int_\Theta \prod_{ i = 1 }^T \dfrac{ q_\theta(X_i) }{ p(X_i) } d \pi(\theta) \\
  \intertext{or forecasts}
  W_t &= \prod_{ i = 1 }^T \dfrac{ q_{ \hat\theta_i } ( X_i ) }{ p(X_i) }
\end{align*}
for some $\hat\theta_i = \theta_i( X_1,\dots,X_{i-1})$.

\textbf{2a.} A \emph{confidence sequence} is a sequence of intervals providing \emph{uniform} coverage
\[ P\bigl[ \forall t \ \theta \in [L_t, U_t] \bigr] \geq \alpha , \]
\ie, new confidence intervals do not contradict earlier ones
\[ P \left[ \bigcap_{ t \geq 1 } [L_t, U_t ] \ni \theta \right] \geq 1-\alpha \]
(with pointwise confidence intervals, the intersection is often $\nothing$, a.s.).

For a random time $T$ (not only a stopping time -- hindsight is allowed),
\[ P \bigl[ \theta \not\in [L_T, U_T] \bigr] \leq \alpha. \]

For instance, for the mean of a distribution bounded in $[-1,+1]$,
\[ \dfrac1n \sum_{i=1}^n X_i \pm 1.71 \sqrt{
    \dfrac{
      \log \log 2n + 0.72 \log( 10.4/\alpha )
    }{
      n
    }
  }
\]
(or the intersection of those intervals).
There are similar formulas for quantiles or sequential covariance matrix estimation.

\textbf{2b.}
Given $X_1,X_2,\dots,$ random variables in $[0,1]$,
we want to estimate their mean.
The Hoefding inequality gives confidence intervals
\[ \left[
    \bar X_n \pm \dfrac{ \log(2/\alpha) }{ 2n }
  \right] \inter [0,1]
\]
but assumes the worst possible variance.
The empirical Bernstein inequality
improves on it by using both $\hat\mu$ and $\hat\sigma$.
Betting can give an even tighter interval.

We convert the problem into a game.
Since it is an estimation problem,
and not a hypothesis testing problem.
we consider several games, one for each $m\in[0,1]$.
\begin{align*}
  & K_0^{(m)} = 1 \text{ initial capital}\\
  & \lambda_t^{(m)} \in \left[ - \dfrac1{1-m} , \dfrac1m \right]
    \text{ bets, to ensure $K_t^{(m)}\geq 0$} \\
  \intertext{after revealing $X_t$, update the wealth}
  & K_t^{(m)}  = K_{t-1}^{(m)}  \bigl[ 1 + \lambda_t^{(m)} ( X_t-m) \bigr]
\end{align*}

The confidence regions are (for any betting strategy)
\begin{align*}
  C_t &= \{ m \in [0,1] \ :\ K_t^{(m)} < \alpha \} \\
  &= \{ \text{games in which we did not make enough money} \}.
\end{align*}
The GRAPA (growth rate adapted to a particular alternative) strategy is
\[ \lambda_t^{(m)} (P) = \argmax_\lambda \E\nolimits_P \bigl[
  \log \bigl( 1 + \lambda( X_t - m ) \bigr) \bigm| \mathscr F_{t-1}
  \bigr]
\]
(we do not known $P$: approximate it with $\hat\mu_t$ and $\hat{\sigma}_t$).

One can also use a mixture of betteing strategies,
\[ C_t = \left\{
    m \ :\ \int \prod_{i=1}^t \bigl( 1 + \lambda (X_i - m ) \bigr) d\nu(\lambda) < 1/\alpha
  \right\}.
\]

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{E-detectors: a nonparametric framework\\ for sequential change detection}\\
  J. Shin et al. (2024)
\end{book}
Given a sequence of random variables $(X_n)_{n\geq 1}$
and disjoint sets of probability distributions $\mathscr P$ (null hypothesis)
and $\mathscr Q$ (alternative),
we want to test wether all the $X_n$'s follow a distribution from $\mathscr P$,
of if they follow a distribution from $\mathscr Q$ after a while.

An \emph{e-detector} is a process $M$ such that
\[ \forall P \in \mathscr P \quad \forall \tau \ \, \E\nolimits_{P,\infty}[ M_\tau ] \leq \E\nolimits_{P,\infty}[ \tau ] \]
where $\E\nolimits_{P,\infty}$ is the expectation if all $X_i$'s follow $P$.

An \emph{e-process} is a non-negative process $E$ such that
\[ \forall P \in \mathscr P \quad \forall \tau \quad
  \E\nolimits_{P,\infty}[ E_t \mid \mathscr F_{t-1} ] \leq 1 . \]

An e$_j$-process is a sequence of e-processes $\Lambda^{(j)}$,  $j\geq1$,
with $\Lambda^{(j)}$ starting at time $j$
\[ \forall t < j \quad \Lambda_t^{(j)} = 1 . \]
The following are then e-detectors:
\begin{align*}
  M_n^{\text{SR}} &= \sum_{j=1}^n \Lambda_n^{(j)} \\
  M_n^{\text{CU}} &= \max_{ j \leq n } \Lambda_n^{(j)}.
\end{align*}
Reject the null at the stopping time
\[ N = \inf \{ n\geq 1 \ : \ M_n \geq 1/\alpha \}. \]

A \emph{baseline increment} is a nonnegative process $L$
such that
\[ \sup_{ P \in \mathscr P } \E\nolimits_{P,\infty} [ L_n \mid \mathscr F_{n-1} ] \leq 1. \]
It defines a baseline e$_j$-process
\[ \Lambda_n^{(j)} = \begin{cases}
  1 & \text{if } n < j \\
  \displaystyle\prod_{i=j}^n L_i & \text{if } n \geq j
\end{cases}
\]
for which the SR and \textsc{Cusum} e-detectors can be computed as
\begin{align*}
  M_n^{\text{SR}} &= L_n \cdot ( M_{n-1}^{\text{SR}} + 1 ) \\
  M_n^{\text{CU}} &= L_n \cdot \max \{ M_{n-1}^{\text{CU}} , 1 ) \}
\end{align*}

If $\mathscr P$ and $\mathscr Q$ are singletons (simple $H_0$, simple $H_1$),
\[ \mathscr P = \{ p_0 \} \qquad \mathscr Q = \{ p_1 \} , \]
use the likelihood ratio
\[ L_n = \dfrac{ p_1(X_n) }{ p_0(X_n) }. \]

If $\mathscr Q$ is composite, use a mixture likelihood ratio
\[ L_n = \dfrac{
    \int q_\theta(X_n) \nu(d\theta)
  }{
    p_0( X_n )
  }
\]
($\nu$  fixed or predictable)
or a plug-in likelihood ratio
\[ L_n = \dfrac{ \hat p_{n-1} (X_n) }{ p_0( X_n ) }. \]

If both $\mathscr P$ and $\mathscr Q$ are composite, use universal inference
\begin{align*}
  \Lambda_n^{(j)} &= \prod_{ i=j}^n \dfrac{ \hat q_{i-1} ( X_i ) }{ \hat p_n (X_i ) } \\
  \intertext{or}
  \Lambda_n^{(j)} &= \prod_{ i=j}^n \dfrac{ \int q ( X_i ) \nu(dq) }{ \hat p_n (X_i ) }.
\end{align*}


\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Hypothesis testing with e-values}\\
  A. Ramdas and R. Wang (2025)
\end{book}

\textbf{1.}
E-variables have a \emph{betting} interpretation, with two players:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item A skeptic, who chooses $E$ such that $\E\nolimits_Q[E] > 1$;
\item A forecaster, who accepts the bet if $\E\nolimits_P[E]\leq 1$.
\end{compactitem}
The skeptic pays \$1 and receives $E[\omega]$ back.

For instance, when both $\mathscr P$ and $\mathscr Q$ are \emph{simple},
we can use the likelihood ratio, $E = dQ/dP$.

For instance, for
$P=N(0,1)$, $Q=N(\mu,1)$, $\mu$ known,
the E-variable is
\begin{align*}
  E_n &= \exp( \delta Z - \delta^2/2 ) \\
  Z &= S_n / \sqrt n \\
  S_n &= \sum X_i \\
  \delta &= \mu \sqrt n
\end{align*}
(but any $\delta\in\RR$ will do).

E-values generalize likelihood ratios.

We can convert between e-values and p-values,
but the p-to-e transformation loses information.
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=2,yscale=.5,inner sep=1pt,outer sep=1pt,every node/.style={anchor=base}]
\node[anchor=east] (A) at (0,0) {E-values};
\node[anchor=west] (B) at (1,0) {P-values};
\draw[->] ([yshift=+3pt]A.east) to node[anchor=south] {\footnotesize $p=1/e$} ([yshift=+3pt]B.west);
\draw[->] ([yshift=-3pt]B.west) to node[anchor=north] {\footnotesize $e=\mathbf1_{p\leq\alpha} / \alpha$} ([yshift=-3pt]A.east);
\end{tikzpicture}
\]

P-values can be combined:
$\sup\limits_{ P \in \mathscr P } F_{ X|P} (X)$.

E-values can be combined:
arithmetic average and, if they are independent, product.

For a simple null and composite alternative described by a parameter $\theta$,
\[ \mathscr P = \{ P = Q_0 \}
  \qquad \mathscr Q = \{ Q_\theta , \  \theta \in \Theta\}, \]
we can consider the likelihood ratios
\[ \ell( x, \theta ) = \dfrac{ d Q_\theta }{ dP } (x) \]
and the e-variables
$E_i = \ell( X_i, \theta_i)$,
with $\theta_i$ fixed beforehand,
or estimated adaptively
\[ \theta_k = \text{MLE}( \theta \mid X_1, \dots, X_{k-1} ) \]
to define a test martingale
\[ M_n = \prod_{ i = 1 }^n E_i .\]
The e-process with adaptive values,
and that with a mixture, are better (have a larger growth rate)
than random or mis-specified $\theta_i$'s.

To test
$H_0\!: X \indep Y$ vs $H_1\!: X \not\indep Y$ on iid pairs $(X_i,Y_i)$,
compute the sample correlation $L_0$ between $(X_1,\dots,X_n)$
and $(Y_1,\dots,Y_n)$,
and $L_1,\dots,L_B$
between $( X_{\sigma(1)}, \dots, X_{\sigma(n)})$
and $( Y_{\sigma(1)}, \dots, Y_{\sigma(n)})$
for $B$ random permutations $\sigma \in \mathfrak S_n$.
Under $H_0$, $L_0,L_1,\dots,L_B$ is \emph{exchangeable}.
Monotonically transform $L_0,\dots,L_B$ into
nonnegative scores $R_0,\dots,R_B$,
e.g.,
\[ R_b = \dfrac{  e^{ r L_b} - e^{ r L_*} }{ r } , \qquad L_*** = \min_b L_b , \]
and set
\[ E = \dfrac{ (B+1) R_0 }{ \sum R_b } = \dfrac{ R_0 }{ \operatorname{Mean} R_b }. \]
Since
$p = \operatorname{Mean} \mathbf1_{ L_b \geq L_0 } \leq 1/E$,
we call $E$ the \emph{soft-rank} e-value
(and $1/E$ the soft-rank p-value).


A \emph{hypothesis} is a set of probability measures.
We will denote $\mathscr P = H_0$
and $\mathscr Q = H_1$ the null and alternative hypotheses.
They are \emph{simple} if $\mathscr P$ (resp. $\mathscr Q$) are singletons,
composite otherwise.

An \emph{e-variable} is a random variable $E$ such that $E\geq 0$
and
\[ \forall P \in \mathscr P \quad \E\nolimits_P[E] \leq 1. \]
A \emph{p-variable} is a random variable $p$ such that $p \geq 0$ and
\[ \forall P \in \mathscr P \quad \forall \alpha\in[0,1] \quad P[ p \leq \alpha ] \leq \alpha, \]
\ie, $p$ is stochastically greater than $\text{Unif}(0,1)$ for all $P$.

A \emph{test} is a $\{0,1\}$-valued random variable $\phi$;
it has level $\alpha$ if the type-I error $\E[\phi]$ satisfies
\[ \forall P \in \mathscr P \quad \E\nolimits_P [ \phi] \leq\alpha \]
(we can also consider $[0,1]$-valued tests: $\phi$ is then the probability of rejecting $H_0$).

\textbf{2.}
From Markov's inequality, if $E$ is an e-variable,
\[ \forall P \in \mathscr P \quad P[ E \leq 1/\alpha] \leq \alpha, \]
\ie,
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item $1/E$ is a p-variable;
\item $\min(\alpha E, 1 )$ is a level-$\alpha$ test;
\item $\mathbf 1_{ E \geq 1/\alpha }$ is a level-$\alpha$ binary test.
\end{compactitem}
Note that if $p$ is a p-variable, $1/p$ is generally not an $E$-variable.

Note that $t=1/\alpha$ is a valid threshold for the test, but you can have a better one if you know
$P[E\geq x]$ for $P \in \mathscr P$:
\[ t^*** = \inf \{ t > 0 \ :\
  \forall P \in \mathscr P \quad P[ E \geq x ] \leq \alpha \}.
\]

A p-to-e (resp. e-to-p) \emph{calibrator} is a function transforming e-values into p-values (resp. the opposite).
The function $t \mapsto \min( 1 , 1/t)$ is the only non-dominated e-to-p calibrator.

There are many non-dominated p-to-e calibrators, e.g.,
\begin{align*}
  f(p) &= \kappa p^{ \kappa - 1 } \qquad \kappa\in(0,1) \\
  f(p) &= \int_0^1 \kappa p^{\kappa-1} d\kappa \\
  f(p) &= 2(1-p) \\
  f(p) &= p^{-1/2} - 1 \\
  f(p) &= - \log p .
\end{align*}
If $E$ is an e-vriable and $U \sim \text{Unif}(0,1)$, $U \indep E$,
then $U/E$ is a p-variable (randomized e-to-p calibrator).

For a simple null, non-dominated e-variables are the exact e-variables
($\E\nolimits_P[E]=1$); they are all likelihood ratios
$dS/dP$ for some $S$.
For composite nulls, exact e-variables are non-dominated (sufficient condition).

Consider a parametric family of distributions
with densities
$(p_\theta)_{\theta\in\Theta}$, $\Theta\subset \RR$, satisfying the
\emph{monotone likelihood ratio assumption}
\[ \forall \theta < \theta' \quad p_{\theta'} (x) / p_\theta(z) \text{ is monotonic in $z$,} \]
then $E = p_{\theta_1}(Z) / p_{\theta_0}(Z)$
is an e-variable for
\[ \mathscr P = \{ p_\theta \ :\ \theta \leq \theta_0 \}
  \quad \text{vs} \quad
  \mathscr Q = \{ p_\theta \ :\ \theta \geq \theta_1 \}
\]
(you can replace $\theta_1$ in the ratio with any $\theta'>\theta_0$).

Consider a non-negative random variable $Z$.
To test if $\E[Z] \leq \mu$, \ie
\[ \mathscr P = \{ P \ :\ \E\nolimits_P[Z] \leq \mu \}, \]
notice that $Z/\mu$ and $1$ are e-variables, so
\[ (1-\lambda) + \lambda \dfrac Z\mu, \qquad \lambda\in[0,1] \]
is one as well.
Similarly, to test if
$E[Z] \leq \mu$ and $\var[Z] \leq \sigma^2$,
notice that $Z^2 / (\mu^2+\sigma^2)$ is an e-variable:
any convex combination of $1$, $Z/\mu$, $Z^2 / (\mu^2+\sigma^2)$ is an e-variable.

To test if $Z$ and $-Z$ have the same distribution, \ie,
\[ \mathscr P = \{ P \ :\ Z \overset{d}{=}_P  - Z \} , \]
pick a non-symmetric density $q$:
the likelihood ratio
\[ E = \dfrac{ q(Z) }{ q^{\text{sym}}(Z) } =
  \dfrac{ 2 q(Z) }{ q(Z) + q(-Z) } \]
is an e-variable.
For instance,
\[ E = \dfrac{ 2 \exp( \lambda Z )}{ \exp( \lambda Z ) + \exp( - \lambda Z ) }. \]
One can also use Wilcoxon's signal rank test.

To test if the data comes from a \emph{1-sub Gaussian} distribution with non-positive mean
\[ \mathscr P = \{ P \ : \ \forall \lambda \geq 0  \quad \E\nolimits_P[ e^{ \lambda Z - \lambda^2/2} ] \leq 1 \}
\]
(the condition is $\E[ e^{ \lambda Z}] \leq e^{ \lambda^2/2}$,
where $\E[e^{\lambda Z} ]$ is the moment generating function of $Z$).
Any mixture (convex combination) of
$e^{ \lambda Z - \lambda^2/2}$, $\lambda\geq 0$
is an e-variable.
There are similar results for 1-sub-Gaussian distributions with zero mean
\[ \mathscr P_0 = \bigl\{ \, P \ :\
  \forall \lambda \in \RR \quad
  \E\nolimits_P[ e^{ \lambda Z - \lambda^2/2 } ] \leq 1 \,\bigr\} .\]
Since the sub-Gaussian condition is applied to both tails,
the variance is at most 1,
and $Z^2$ is also an e-variable.

If the null hypothesis is defined by integral constraints
\[ \mathscr P = \{ P \ : \ \forall f \in \Psi \ \int f dP \leq 0 \} \]
for some finite set of functions $\Psi$
(e.g., to test the mean, or mean and variance),
then the admissible e-variables are
\[ 1 + \sum_k \pi_k g_k ,
  \qquad \Psi = \{ g_1,\dots,g_K \} ,
  \quad \pi \geq 0
\]
(provided those functions are non-negative).
This generalizes to infinite $\Psi$.

To test if the data is close to a benchmark distribution $P_0$,
\[ \mathscr P = \left\{ P \ :\ \dfrac{ dP }{ dP_0} \leq \gamma \right\} \]
we could use the e-variable
$(dQ/dP_0)/\gamma$, but it can be improved:
\begin{align*}
  Z^*** &= \dfrac{ dQ }{ dP_0} \\
  q_t(Z) &= \text{right quantile od $Z$ under $P_0$} \\
  z_0 &= \max \left\{ z_0 \ :\ \int_0^{1/\gamma} \max \{ q_t(z^*), z_0 \} dt = 1 \right\} \\
  E^*** &= \max \{ Z^*** , z_0 \} / \gamma
\end{align*}

To test exchangeability
\[ \mathscr P = \{ P \ :\ \forall \sigma\in\mathfrak S_n \quad Z \overset{d}{=} Z_\sigma \}, \]
pick a non-exchangeable distribution $Q$ and use
\[ E = \dfrac{ q(z) }{ \operatorname{Mean}\limits_{\sigma \in \mathfrak S_n } q( Z_\sigma ) } \]
(it is exact, therefore admissible).

An e-value of $1/a$ carries more information than a $p$-value of $a$.
Use Jeffrey's rule of thumb for likelihood ratios.
\begin{center}
  \begin{tabular}{lcc}
    Evidence & p-value & e-value \\
    \hline
    substancial & $p < 5\%$     & $E > 3$ \\
    strong      & $p < 1\%$     & $E > 10$ \\
    very strong & $p < 10^{-3}$ & $E > 30$ \\
    decisive    & $p < 10^{-4}$ & $E > 100$
  \end{tabular}
\end{center}

\textbf{3.}
A level-$\alpha$ test is a $[0,1]$-valued random variable $\phi$ such that
\[ \forall P \in \mathscr P \quad \E\nolimits_P[\phi] \leq \alpha . \]
It is \emph{powered} if
\[ \forall Q \in \mathscr Q \quad \E\nolimits_Q[\phi] > \alpha \]
and uniformly powered if $\sup_Q \E\nolimits_Q [ \phi] > \alpha$.

An e-variable is powered against $\mathscr Q$ if
\[ \forall Q \in \mathscr Q \quad \E\nolimits_Q[E] > 1 . \]
here is a correspondance between level-$\alpha$ tests and $[0,1/\alpha]$-valued e-variables:
$E = \phi/\alpha$.
A p-variable $p$ is powered if it is stochastically smaller than $\text{Unif}(0,1)$
under each $Q \in \mathscr Q$.
There exists a powered p-variable iff there exists a bounded powered e-variable (under some assumptions,
which exclude discrete sample spaces).

The \emph{e-power} against $Q$ is $\E\nolimits_Q[\log E]$.
For a finite alternative $\mathscr Q$, there is an e-variable with positive e-power iff there is a powered e-variable.

There is a uniformly powered bounded e-variable $E$ for $\mathscr P$ against $\mathscr Q$ iff
$d_{\text{TV}} ( \operatorname{Conv} \mathscr P , \operatorname{Conv} \mathscr Q ) > 0 $
where the total variation distance is
\begin{align*}
  d_{\text{TV}} (P,Q) &= \sup_A \va{ P(A) - Q(A) } \\
  d_{\text{TV}} (\mathscr P, \mathscr Q) &= \inf_{\substack{ P \in \mathscr P \\ Q \in \mathscr Q }} d_{\text{TV}} (P,Q)
\end{align*}

For simple $H_0$ and $H_1$, the \emph{likelihood ratio} $E = dQ / dP$ is log-optimal
and its e-power is $\text{KL}( Q \| P )$.
The log-optimal e-variable is unique.

For a simple null against a composite alternative $\mathscr Q$,
the \emph{mixture} e-variables
\[ E_n = \int \prod_{ i=1 }^n \dfrac{ q(X_i) }{ p(X_i) } \nu(dq) \]
and the \emph{plugin} e-variables
\[ E_n = \prod_{i=1}^n \dfrac{ \hat q_{i-1} ( X_i ) }{ p(X_i ) } \]
are asymptotically log-optimal
\[ \lim_{n\to\infty} n^{-1} \E\nolimits_{ Q^n } [ \log E_n ] \longrightarrow \text{KL} ( Q \| P ). \]
% What is $Q^n$? This is $Q\times\cdot\times Q$, n times -- we look at the first n observations.

For the T-test
\begin{align*}
  \mathscr P &= \bigl\{ N( 0, \sigma^2 ) \ : \ \sigma > 0 \bigr\} \\
  \mathscr P &= \bigl\{ N( \mu, \sigma^2 ) \ : \ \sigma > 0, \ \mu\neq 0 \bigr\}
\end{align*}
use
\begin{align*}
  E_n &= \sqrt{ \dfrac{ c^2 }{ n + c^2 } }\left(
        \dfrac{
        (n+c^2) V_n
        }{
        (n+c^2) V_n - S_n^2
        }
        \right) ^{ n/2 } \\
  S_n &= \sum X_i \\
  V_n &= \sum X_i^2
\end{align*}
with $c>0$.

For change point detection ($P$, $Q$ known),
\begin{align*}
  \mathscr P &= \{ P^n \} \\
  \mathscr Q &= \bigl\{ P^{n-k} Q^k , \ k \in \llbracket 0, n \rrbracket \bigr\}
\end{align*}
use the mixture
\[ \dfrac1n \sum_{ k = 1 }^n \prod_{ i = n-k+1 } ^n \dfrac{ q(X_i) }{ p(X_i) }. \]

\textbf{4.}
A p-variable satisfies
\[ \forall P \in \mathscr P \quad \sup_{ \alpha \in (0,1) } \E\nolimits_P [ \mathbf 1_{ p \leq \alpha } / \alpha ] \leq 1. \]
A \emph{post hoc p-variable} is one satisfying
\[ \forall P \in \mathscr P \quad
  \E\nolimits_P \left[ \sup_{ \alpha \in (0,1) } \mathbf 1_{ p \leq \alpha } / \alpha \right] \leq 1. \]

Post hoc p-variables are the random variables $p$ satisfying
$p \geq \min \{ 1/E, 1 \} $ for some e-variable $E$.

Consider the decision problem
\begin{align*}
  L(0,d) &= \text{loss of decision $d$ if $H_0$} \\
  L(1,d) &= \text{loss of decision $d$ if $H_1$},
\end{align*}
with $L(0,\cdot)$ increasing and $L(1,\cdot)$ increasing,
\ie, we prefer small (resp. large) $d$ if $H_0$ (resp. $H_1$).
We want to solve the optimization problem
\[ \begin{array}{ll}
  \text{Find} & \delta \text{: decision rule} \\
  \text{To minimize} & \E\nolimits_Q\bigl[ L\bigl(1,\delta(x)\bigr) \bigr]\\
  \text{Such that} & \sup\limits_{ P \in \mathscr P } \E\nolimits_P \bigl[ L\bigl(0,\delta(x)\bigr) \bigr]\ \leq \gamma.
\end{array} \]
Given a p-variable and an e-variable, consider
\begin{align*}
  \delta_p(X) &= \max \bigl\{ d \ : \ p(X) L(0,d) \leq \gamma \bigr\}  \\
  \delta_E(X) &= \max \bigl\{ d \ : \ L(0,d) \leq \gamma E(X) \bigr\} .
\end{align*}
For binary decisions, both p-value and e-value based decision rules satisfy the risk constraint.
If there are more than two decisions, or if the set of decisions is data-dependent,
only the e-value-based decision rules satisfy the constraint in general.

You can multiply (independent) e-variables:
you can decide to continue an experiment: $E_1$ (experiment from scientist 1)
and $E_1 E_2$ (scientist 2 adds her data to scientist 1's data)
are e-variables. You may not discard one of the $E_i$'s.
To avoid destroying previous evidence
(if any of the $E_i$'s is zero, everything is over),
you may prefer to accumulate information as
\[ \prod_i ( 1+E_i)/2
  \quad \text{ or } \quad
  \prod_i \bigl( \lambda_i + (1-\lambda_i) E_i\bigr).
\]

\textbf{5.}
It is difficult to design tests for ``irregular problems'' such as:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Testing whether the data comes from a Gaussian
  \[ \mathscr P = \bigl\{\, N(\mu,1) \,\bigr\} \]
  or a mixture of two Gaussians
  \[ \mathscr Q = \bigl\{\, (1-\lambda) N(\mu_1,1) + \lambda N(\mu_2,1) \,\bigr\} \setminus \mathscr P; \]
\item Testing if the data comes from a log-concave distribution.
\end{compactitem}

\emph{Universal inference} reduces hypothesis testing to maximum likelihood estimation under $H_0$:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Split the data in two, $D = D_0 \sqcup D_q$;
\item Use $D_1$ to pick an alternative $\hat q_1 \in \mathscr Q$;
  % Use a Bayes estimator, with a prior that puts mass everywhere in $\mathscr Q$
\item Use $D_0$ to pick the MLE $\hat p_0 \in \mathscr P$;
\item Use $D_0$ to compute the log-ratio
  \[ E = \prod_{ i \in D_0 } \dfrac{ \hat q_1( X_i ) }{ \hat p_0(X_i) }. \]
\end{compactitem}
Instead of a plugin $\hat q_1$, you can use a mixture
\[ E = \int_{\mathscr Q} \prod_{ i \in D_0 } \dfrac{ q(X_i) }{ \hat p_0 (X_i) } \nu( dq ). \]
This can be improved by averaging over several splits
$D = D_0^{(b)} \sqcup D_1^{(b)}$, $b \in \llbracket 1, B \rrbracket$
(subsampled likelihood ratio e-variable -- $B$ can even be data-dependent).

In a parametric setup
\begin{align*}
  \mathscr P &= \{ p_\theta \ :\ \theta \in \Theta_0 \} \\
  \mathscr Q &= \{ p_\theta \ :\ \theta \in \Theta_1 \},
\end{align*}
let
\[ C( X^n) = \left\{
    \, \theta \ :\ \prod_{ i \in D_0 } \dfrac{ p_{ \hat\theta} (X_i) }{ p_\theta(X_i) } < \dfrac1\alpha \,
  \right\} \]
where $\theta$ is any estimator on $D_1$;
it is a $(1-\alpha)$ confidence set for $\theta^*$.

In presence of nuisance variables
$\theta = (\theta_0,\theta_1)$, use the profile likelihood
\begin{align*}
  \mathscr L ^\dagger ( \theta_0) &= \sup_{ \theta_1 } \mathscr L( \theta_0, \theta_1 ) \\
  C(X^n) &= \left\{ \, \theta_0 \ :\ \dfrac{ \mathscr L^\dagger(\theta_0) }{ \mathscr L( \hat\theta_0, \hat\theta_1 ) } \geq \alpha \, \right\}.
\end{align*}
You can smooth the likelihood, or more generally relax it (use an upper bound).
If model misspecification is a concern,
consider the power likelihood $\mathscr L^\eta$, $\eta\in(0,1)$,
instead of $\mathscr L$.

\textbf{6.}
The univsesal inference e-variable is only \emph{asymptotically} log-optimal.
Without loss of generality,
consider a point alternative $\mathscr Q = \{ Q \}$
(for a composite alternative, use a plugin or a mixture e-variable).

A \emph{numeraire} is an e-variable (for $\mathscr P$) $E^*$ ,
such that, for each e-variable, $E_Q[E/E^*] \leq 1$.
If it exists,
it is (essentially) unique;
it is (the only) log-optimal e-variable.

If $\mathscr P$ is finite, there is a duality
\begin{align*}
  \E\nolimits_Q[ \log E^*** ] &= \max_{ E \in \mathscr E} \E\nolimits_Q[\log E] \\
  &=
    \min_{ P \in \text{Conv} \mathscr P } \text{KL}( Q \| P ) \\
  &= \text{KL}(Q,P^*).
\end{align*}
This remains true in general,
with the convex hull $\text{Conv} \mathscr P$ replaced by the
\emph{bipolar} $\mathscr P^{\circ\circ}$
(the \emph{effective} null hypothesis).
\begin{align*}
  \mathscr P^\circ &= \{ E \ :\ E \geq 0 , \ \forall P \in \mathscr P \quad \E\nolimits_P[E] \leq 1 \} \\
  \mathscr P^{\circ\circ} &= \{ P \ :\ \forall E \in \mathscr P^\circ \quad \E\nolimits_P[E] \leq 1 \}
\end{align*}
The minimum $P^*$ is the \emph{reverse information projection} (RIPr) of $Q$ onto $\mathscr P$, defined by
\[ \dfrac{ d P^*** }{ d Q } = \dfrac1{E^*}. \]

Bayesian hypothesis testing looks at the Bayes factor,
a ratio of two mixtures,
from priors $\mu$, $\nu$ over $\mathscr Q$ and $\mathscr P$.
Frequentist hypothesis testing looks at a ratio of maximum likelihoods.
Universal inference looks at the ratio of a mixture over a maximum likelihood.
\begin{align*}
  B_n &= \dfrac{
        \int \prod_i q(X_i) \mu(dq)
        }{
        \int \prod_i p(X_i) \nu(dp)
        } \\
  G_n &= \dfrac{
        \sup\limits_{ q \in \mathscr Q} \prod_i q(X_i)
        }{
        \sup\limits_{ p \in \mathscr P } \prod_i p(X_i)
        } \\
  U_n &= \dfrac{
        \int \prod_i q(X_i) \mu(dq)
        }{
        \sup\limits_{ p \in \mathscr P } \prod_i p(X_i)
        }
\end{align*}
When the null is simple, $B_n = U_n$ is the numeraire.

For the monotone likelihood ratio test, the numeraire is
$p_{ \theta_1} / p_{\theta_0}$.

To test symmetry (with a simple alternative),
\begin{align*}
  P^*(Z) &= \dfrac12 \bigl[ q(Z) + q(-Z) \bigr] \mathbf 1_{ q(Z) > 0 } \\
  E^*** &= \dfrac{ dQ}{ dP^*} = \dfrac{
        2 q(Z) }{ q(Z) + q(-Z)
        } \mathbf 1_{ q(Z) > 0  }.
\end{align*}

To test for 1-sub-Gaussian distributions with nonpositive means,
$e^{ \lambda Z - \lambda^2/2}$, $\lambda\in[0,\infty)$
are e-variables,
and $\lambda=\mu$ maximizes the e-power,
\begin{align*}
  E^*** &= e^{ \mu Z - \mu^2/2} \\
  P^*** &= N(0,1)
\end{align*}

To test for likelihood ratio bounds
\[ \mathscr P = \{ P \ :\ dP/dP_0 \leq \gamma \} , \]
the numeraire is
\[ E^*** = \gamma^{-1} \max \{ dQ/dP_0, Z_0 \}. \]

To test for exchangeability,
the numeraire is
\[ E^*** = \dfrac{
    q(Z)
  }{
    \operatorname*{Mean}\limits_{ \sigma \in \mathfrak S_n } q(Z_\sigma )
  }.
\]

\textbf{7.}
P-values satisfy
\[ \forall n \quad  P[p_n \leq \alpha ] \leq \alpha \]
(\ie, peeking is not allowed) but, for sequential testing,
we want
\[ \forall \tau \quad P[ p_\tau \leq \alpha ] \leq \alpha. \]

The \emph{Neyman-Pearson lemma} states that the likelihood ratio test
\[ L_n = \prod_{ i \leq n } \dfrac{ P_1(x_i) }{ P_0(x_i) } \]
(for simple $H_0$ and $H_1$)
achieves the best trade-off between type-I and type-II error:
choose $n$ and $\gamma$, and reject $H_0$ if $L_n > \gamma$
(for some choices of $\alpha$ and $\beta$, you can have $\gamma<1$).

The \emph{Wald sequential probability ratio test} (SRPT) uses the same statisic:
choose $\gamma_0$ and $\gamma_1$ (e.g., $\gamma_0=\beta$, $\gamma_1=1/\alpha$ --
this is sufficient to keep the type I and type II errors below $\alpha$ and $\beta$),
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Accept $H_0$ if $L_t \leq \gamma_0$;
\item Reject $H_1$ if $L_t \geq \gamma_1$;
\item Continue to collect data otherwise.
\end{compactitem}
It achieves the best trade-off between type I error, type II error, and stopping time,
in the following sense:
if $\alpha$, $\beta$ are the type I and type II errors of the LRT with $n$ samples,
if $\alpha'$, $\beta'$ are the type I and type II errors of the SPRT (for some $\gamma_0$, $\gamma_1$)
and $\tau$ its stopping time,
then \[ \alpha \leq \alpha' , \ \beta \leq \beta' \implies
  \max \{ \E\nolimits_{H_0}[\tau] , \E\nolimits_{H_1}[\tau] \} \leq n. \]

E-values generalize the SPRT to arbitrary stopping times (instead of a single stopping rule),
but optimize for the e-power, instead of the power $1-\beta$.

A \emph{level-$\alpha$ sequential test} for $\mathscr P$ is a binary proces $\phi$ such that
\[ \forall P \in \mathscr P \quad P[\, \exists t \ :\ \phi_t = 1 \,] \leq \alpha \]
or, equivalently,
\[ \forall P \in \mathscr P \quad \forall \tau \quad \E\nolimits_P[ \phi_\tau ] \leq \alpha . \]

An e-process is a sequence of e-values
$(E_t)_{t \geq 0}$ such that
\[ \forall \tau \text{ stopping time} \quad \E\nolimits_P[E_\tau] \leq 1 . \]
A nonnegative martingale
($\E[M_t | \mathscr F_{t-1}] = M_t$)
or supermartingale
($\E[M_t | \mathscr F_{t-1}] \leq M_t$)
such that $\E\nolimits_P[M_0] \leq 1$ is an e-process --
a \emph{test (super)martingale}.

\emph{Ville's inequality} states that, if $M$ is an e-process, then
\[ \forall P \in \mathscr P \quad \forall \alpha\in[0,1] \quad
  P[\, \exists t \,:\, M_t \geq 1/\alpha \,] \leq \alpha. \]

With e-processes, p-value peeking is allowed.

A non-negative process $(p_t)$ is a p-process if
$p_\tau$ is a p-variable for any stopping time $\tau$.

For simple $H_0$ and $H_1$, the likelihood ratio process
\[ M_t = \prod_{ i = 1 } ^ t \dfrac{ dQ }{ dP } (X_i) \]
is an e-process.
It is log-optimal: for any stopping time $\tau$ and any other e-process $M'$,
\[ \E\nolimits[\log M_t ] \geq \E\nolimits_Q [ \log M'_t ]. \]
Its asymptotic growth rate is $\text{KL}(Q\|P)$.

For a composite $H_1$,
\begin{align*}
  M_t &= \prod_{ i = 1 }^t \ell( X_i , \hat\theta_{ <i } ) \\
  \ell(X,\theta) &= \dfrac{ dQ_\theta }{ dP } (X)
\end{align*}
is an \emph{asymptotically log-optimal} e-process.

Test (super)martingales have a betting interpretation.
For a simple $H_0$:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Start with wealth $W_0=1$;
\item At time $t$, choose $S_t : \mathscr X \longrightarrow [0,\infty]$
  such that $\E\nolimits_P\bigl[ S_t(X) | X_1,\dots,X_{t-1} \bigr] \leq 1$;
\item Observe $X_t$;
\item Update the wealth $W_t = W_{t-1} \cdot S_t( X_t )$.
\end{compactitem}
The wealth $(W_t)_t$ is a test supermartingale.
The optimal bet $S_t$ maximizes
\[ \E\nolimits_Q \bigl[ \log S_t(X) | X_1, \dots, X_{t-1} \bigr]. \]
This generalizes to a composite null, by considering a different bet $S^P$
and wealth process $W^P$ for each $P \in \mathscr P$,
and setting the overall wealth to
$W_t = \inf_{ P \in \mathscr P} W_t^P$.

The \d{universal inference} e-porcess is
\begin{align*}
  M_t &= \prod_{ i = 1 }^ t \dfrac{ \hat q_{i-1} ( X_i) }{ \hat p_t( X_i ) } \\
  &= \dfrac{ \text{out-of-sample $H_1$-likelihood} }{ \text{in-sample $H_0$-likelihood} }
\end{align*}
(note that the denominator uses the same $\hat p_t$ for all $i$).
We can replace the plugin method with a mixture
\[ M_t = \int_{ \mathscr Q} \prod_{ i = 1 } ^ t \dfrac{ q(X_i ) }{ \hat p_t( X_i ) } \nu(dq) . \]

A sequence $(E_t)_t$ of e-variables is \emph{sequential} if
\[ \E\nolimits_P[ E_t | E_1, \dots, E_{t-1} ] \leq 1. \]
Then, $M_t = \prod_{ s = 1 }^t E_s$ is an e-process.
We can replace $E_t$ with a convex combination
of 1 and $E_t$ with predictable weights
\[ M_t = \prod_{ s = 1 }^t \bigl[ ( 1 - \lambda_s ) + \lambda_s E_s \bigr],
\]
e.g.,
\begin{align*}
  \lambda_t &= \argmax_{ \lambda \in [0,1] } \E\nolimits_Q\bigl[
              \log \bigl( (1-\lambda) + \lambda E_t \bigr) \bigm| \mathscr F_{t-1}
              \bigr] \\
  \intertext{or}
  \lambda_t &= \argmax_{ \lambda \in [ 0, \gamma ] } \dfrac1{t-1} \sum_{ s = 1 } ^ {t-1} \log \bigl( (1-\lambda) + \lambda E_t \bigr)
\end{align*}
(empirically adaptive e-process; it is asymptotically log-optimal).

Let $E^{(n)}$ be an e-variable based on $(X_1,\dots,X_n)$, e.g.,
the numeraire, and $(w_n)_n$ weights such that
$\sum_{ n \geq 0 } w_n = 1$,
e.g.,
\[ w_n = \dfrac{ c }{ n (\log n)^2 }; \]
then,
\[ M_n = \sum_{ j \leq n } w_j E^{(j)} \]
is an (increasing) e-process.

\textbf{8.}
E-values (from different tests, or different dataplits) can be combined:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item For dependent e-values, use the arithmetic mean (if you want symmetry)
  or a convex combination
  \[ \sum_{ k = 1 }^K \lambda_k e_k + \lambda_{k+1}, \qquad \lambda\in\Delta_{K+1}; \]
\item For independent e-values, use the product
  (optimal in a weak sense, but it has the largest possible variance) or,
  more generally, a \emph{U-statistic}
  \[ U_n ( e_1, \dots, e_K ) = \operatorname*{Mean}_{\substack{
        A \subset \llbracket 1, K \rrbracket \\
        \va A = n
      }} \prod_{ k \in A } e_k ; \]
\item For sequential e-values, use
  \[ F(e_1,\dots,e_K) = \prod_{ k = 1 } ^ K \bigl[
    (1-\lambda_k) + \lambda_k e_k \bigr] \]
  where $\lambda_k$ depends on $e_{<k}$;
  this includes all U-statistics (in particular, the product and the arithmetic mean),
  and the empirically adaptive e-process,
  \[ \lambda_k = \argmax_{ \lambda \in [ 0, \gamma ] }
    \operatorname*{Mean}_{ s < k } \log \bigl[
    (1-\lambda) + \lambda e_s \bigr] . \]
\end{compactitem}

To use e-values for FWER control, adjust them as
\[ E^*_k = \min_{\substack{
      A \subset \llbracket 1, K \rrbracket \\
      k \in A } } E_A \]
where $(E_A)_{A \subset \llbracket 1, K \rrbracket}$ is the \emph{mean e-collection}
\[ E_A = \operatorname*{Mean}_{ k \in A } E_k \]
or the product one
\[ E_A = \prod_{ k \in A } E_k . \]

\textbf{9.}
Consider $K$ null hypotheses,
$\mathscr P_1 , \dots, \mathscr P_K$.
Let $E_k$ be an e-variable for $\mathscr P_k$
(the $E_k$'s can be dependent). We can reweight those e-values as
\[ \tilde E_k = w_k E_k \]
where $\forall k \ w_k \geq 0$ and $\sum w_k \leq K$;
they satisfy
\[ \sum_k \sup_{ P \in \mathscr P_k } \E\nolimits_P[ \tilde E_k ] \leq K. \]
More generally, we can consider \emph{compound e-variables}
\[ \sup_{ P \in \mathscr P_k } \sum_{ k : P \in \mathscr P _k }
  \E\nolimits_P[ \tilde E_k ] \leq K. \]

The \emph{e-BH} multiple testing procedure
rejects hypotheses with the largest $k^*$ e-values,
\[ k^*** = \max \left\{
    k \in \llbracket 1 , K \rrbracket \ :\ \dfrac{ k e_{[k]} }{ K } \geq \dfrac1\alpha \right\} \]
where the $e_{[k]}$ are the e-values, in decreasing order.
The false discovery rate (FDR) is at most $\alpha$;
$\alpha$ can be data-dependent.

With some information on the distribution of $E_k$ under $P$,
we can increase the power of e-BH by using \emph{boosted e-values},
$E'_k = b_k E_k$,
where
\begin{align*}
  & \forall P \in \mathscr P_k \quad \E\nolimits_P\bigl[ T( \alpha b_k E_k ) \bigr] \leq \alpha \\
  & T(x) = \dfrac{ K }{ \lceil K/x \rceil } \mathbf 1_{ x \geq 1 }.
\end{align*}
BH (independent p-values) is a special case of boosted e-BH;
BHY is a special case of e-BH.
All FDR controlling procedures can be written as the e-BH procedure
for some compound e-values.

Consider sequence data,
$ X = (X_k)_{ 1 \leq k \leq K }$.
Simple separable e-variables are e-variables for the form
$E_k = f(X_k)$ for some function $f$.
The log-optimal simple separable e-variables, \ie,
maximizing
\[ \operatorname*{Mean}_k \E\nolimits_{Q_k} [ \log E_k ] \]
are $E_k = s(X_k)$, where $s$ is the likelihood ratio of mixtures
\[ s(x) = \dfrac{
    \sum_{ j = 1 }^K q_j(x)
  }{
    \sum_{ j = 1 } ^ K p_j(x)
  }.
\]

For small $K$ ($K\leq10$), we can slightly improve e-BH:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item First test the global null $\bigcap \mathscr P_k$,
  rejecting it if $F(e_1,\dots,e_K) \geq 1/\alpha$
  (with $F=\text{average}$ for instance);
\item Then, if it is rejected, apply e-BH with
  $\alpha' = K \alpha / (K-1)$.
\end{compactitem}
(This can be adapted to boosted e-values.)

Stochastic rounding $S$
(choose a grid, e.g., $\{0, \alpha^{-1}\}$, and randomly round up or down,
with probabilities proportional to the distances;
keep the input if it is outside the grid)
preserves e-values, decreases e-power, but increases power.

\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Re-BH applies e-BH to
  $\bigl( S_\alpha(E_k) \bigr)_{ 1 \leq k \leq K}$;
\item Eu-BH applied e-BH to
  $E_1/U, \dots, E_k/U$,
  where $U \sim \text{Unif}(0,1)$;
\item Se-BH
  \begin{compactitem}[\ensuremath{\bigcdot}]
  \item Applies e-BH to
    $\bigr(S_{\llbracket 1, K \rrbracket} (E_k) \bigr)_{ 1 \leq k \leq K } $,
    where $S_{\llbracket 1, K \rrbracket}$ is the stochastic rounding for the grid
    $\{0,1/k,2/k,\dots,K/K\}$;
  \item Defines $\hat \alpha^*** = \alpha (k^*** + 1 ) / K$,
    where $k^*$ is the number of rejections;
  \item Rejects $\mathscr P_i$ if
    \[ S_{ \hat\alpha^*} \bigl( S_{\llbracket 1, K \rrbracket} (E_i) \bigr) \geq \alpha_{k^*+1}^{-1}. \]
  \end{compactitem}
\end{compactitem}

An \emph{e-collection}
$(E_A)_{ A \subset \llbracket 1, K \rrbracket}$
is the datum of an e-variable $E_A$ for each $\bigcap_{ k \in A } \mathscr P_k$,
e.g., the \emph{mean e-collection}
\[E_A = \operatorname*{Mean}_{ k \in A } E_k. \]
Let
\[ \text{FDP}_A(R) = \dfrac{ \va{ A \inter R } }{ \va R } , \]
for $R \subset \llbracket 1, K \rrbracket$,
be the FDP if $R$ were the set of true nulls.
The \emph{candidate discovery sets} are
\[ \mathscr C_\alpha = \left\{
    R \subset \llbracket 1, K \rrbracket \ :\
    \forall A \subset \llbracket 1, K \rrbracket \quad
    E_A \geq \dfrac{ \text{FDP}_A(R)}{ \alpha }
  \right\}. \]
The \emph{closed e-BH procedure},
$\overline{\text{e-BH}}$, rejects the largest set in $\mathscr C_\alpha$
(any of them, if there are several).
In contrast, e-BH rejects the largest set in
\[ \mathscr C_\alpha = \left\{
    R \subset \llbracket 1, K \rrbracket \ :\
    \min_{ k \in R } E_k \geq \dfrac{ K }{ \alpha \va R }
  \right\}. \]
Every FDR controlling procedure can be written as the closed e-BH procedure applied to some e-collection.

In the definition of $\mathscr C_\alpha$, you can change the error metric, $\text{FDP}_A(R)$, e.g.,
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item FWER: $L_A(R) = \mathbf 1_{ \va{ A \inter R} \geq k } $;
\item PFER: $L_A(R) = \va{ A \inter R } / K $;
\item FDX: $\mathbf 1_{ \text{FDP}_A(R) \geq \gamma }$.
\end{compactitem}

*** Multiple testing: SAVI

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Always valid inference:\\ continuous monitoring of A/B tests}\\
  R. Johari et al.
\end{book}
Given random variables
$X \overset{\text{iid}}{\sim} F_\theta$,
from an exponential family, we want to test $H_0: \theta = \theta_0$
vs $H_1: \theta \neq \theta_0$.
A \emph{sequential test} is a family of decision rules
$\bigl( T(\alpha), \delta(\alpha) \bigr)$,
$\alpha \in (0,1)$, where $T$ is a stopping time
for $( \mathscr F _n )_{ n \geq 1 }$,
and $\delta$ a binary $\mathscr F_T$-measurable random variable, nested
($T$ non-increasing in $\alpha$, $\delta$ non-decreasing), such that
\[ \mathrm{P}_{H_0} \bigl[ \delta(\alpha) = 1 \bigr] \leq \alpha. \]
Sequential tests of power one (which do not accept $H_0$ in finite time)
define \emph{always valid $p$-values} $p_n$
\[ \forall\, T \text{ stopping time} \ \ \
\forall s \in [0,1] \ \ \
\mathrm{P}_{\!H_0} [ p_T \leq s ] \leq s \]
as
\[ p_n = \inf \bigl\{ \alpha \ {:}\ T(\alpha) \leq n , \ \delta(\alpha) = 1 \bigr\} \]
and always valid confidence regions
\[ \text{CI}_n = \{ \theta \ {:} \ p_n^\theta > \alpha \} . \]

For instance, given a prior $H$ on $\theta$, the
\emph{mixture sequential probability ratio test} (mSPRT):
\begin{align*}
  \Lambda_n(s) &= \int_\Theta \left( \dfrac{ f_\theta(s) }{ f_{\theta_0}(s) } \right)^{\!\!n} dH(\theta) \\
  T(\alpha) &= \inf \bigl\{ n \ {:}\ \Lambda_n(S_n) \geq \alpha^{-1} \bigr\} \\
  \delta(\alpha) &= \mathbf1_{ T(\alpha) < \infty } \\
  S_n &= \dfrac1n \sum_{ i=1 }^n X_i.
\end{align*}

*** Introduction

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Why do tree-based models still outperform deep learning on tabular data?}\\
  L. Grinsztajn et al.
\end{book}
Neural networks:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Are biased towards overly smooth functions;
\item Are more affected (than tree-based methods) by uninformative features;
\item Are rotation-invariant (rotating the input does not affect training).
\end{compactitem}

** Deep Learning

Deep learning models are still often seen as inferior to tree-based models
(gradient descent, random forests)
for tabular data,
in part because of their rotation invariance --
trees are axis-aligned, which often helps them identify structure in tabular data.

  \emph{Why do tree-based models still outperform deep learning on tabular data?}\\
  L. Grinsztajn et al.

This may be changing thanks to better pre-processing

  \emph{On embeddings for numerical features\\ in tabular deep learning}\\
  Y. Gorishniy et al. (2022)

transformers

  \emph{Revisiting deep learning models\\ for tabular data}\\
  Y. Gorishniy et al.

better tuning

  \emph{Well-tuned simple nets\\ excel on tabular datasets}\\
  A. Kadra et al.

and more agressive ensembling

  \emph{TabM: advancing tabular deep learning\\ with parameter-efficient ensembling}\\
  Y. Grishniy et al.

  \emph{BatchEnsemble: an alternative approach\\ to efficient ensemble and lifelong learning}\\
  Y. Wen et al.

  \emph{Packed-Ensembles\\ for efficient uncertainty estimation}\\
  O. Laurent et al. (2023)

** Kernel methods

Kernel methods with axis-aware kernels (ℓᵖ) also have that axis-aligned prior.
For instance, recursive feature machines (RFM)
fir a ridge regression in a warped space,
x↦Mx,
where M is the average gradient outer product (AGOP)

    Mean ∇f(xᵢ) ∇f(xᵢ)'

whose eigenvectors identify the directions along which the predictor varies the most.
One can stack such models,
or use them to recursively partition the data
(note that training such models does not require backpropagation).

  \emph{Mechanism of feature learning in convolution}\\
  D. Beaglehole et al.

  \emph{Average gradient outer product\\ as a mechanism for deep neural collapse}\\
  D. Beaglehole et al.

  \emph{xRFM: accurate, scalable and interpretable feature learning models for tabular data}\\
  D. Beaglehole et al.

Kernel methods do not scale to large datasets, but one can use the Nyström approximation.

    A ≈ A[:,I] A[I,I]⁺ A[I,:]

where I is a random subset of columns (rows).

  \emph{Towards large kernel models}\\
  A. Abedsoltan et al.

  \emph{Randomly pivoted Cholesky:\\ practical approximation of a kernel matrix\\ with few entry evaluations}\\
  Y. Chen et al.

** DONE TO DELETE Ensembling, fine-tuning
*** DONE TO DELETE Tabular data: efficient ensembles of neural nets

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{TabM: advancing tabular deep learning\\ with parameter-efficient ensembling}\\
  Y. Grishniy et al.
\end{book}
\emph{BatchEnsemble} is a shared-parameter ensemble,
replacing linear layers
\[ \ell(x) = Wx + b \]
with
\[ \ell_i(x) = s_i \odot \bigl( W ( r_i \odot x ) \bigr) + b_i , \]
\ie, multiplying inputs and outputs elementwise by $r_i$ and $s_i$;
equivalently, it replaces $W$ with
$W \odot ( s_i r_i^\top)$;
the scaling factors are initialized to $\pm1$.

TabM is a BatchEnsemble of MLPs, defined as $n$ blocks
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=1,yscale=.5,inner sep=1pt,outer sep=2pt,every node/.style={anchor=base}]
\node (O) at (0,0) {};
\node[fill=blue!15, right=5mm of O] (A) {linear\strut};
\node[fill=blue!15, right=5mm of A] (B) {ReLU\strut};
\node[fill=blue!15, right=5mm of B] (C) {Dropout\strut};
\node[right=5mm of C] (D) {};
\draw[->] (O) -- (A);
\draw[->] (A) -- (B);
\draw[->] (B) -- (C);
\draw[->] (C) -- (D);
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
it keeps the random initialization of the first adapter $R_1$
(ablation sutdies suggest it is the most important)
but initializes all the other ones to 1,
so that they initially have no effect.

Nonlinear feature embedding also helps.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{BatchEnsemble: an alternative approach\\ to efficient ensemble and lifelong learning}\\
  Y. Wen et al.
\end{book}
BatchEnsemble is an ensemble of MLPs with partially shared weights,
$W_i = W \odot ( r_i s_i^\top)$,
which can be used for lifelong learning
by training new rank-1 matrices $r_i s_i^\top$ for each new task $i$.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Packed-Ensembles\\ for efficient uncertainty estimation}\\
  O. Laurent et al. (2023)
\end{book}
Deep ensembles can be trained more efficiently,
all at once,
by stacking activations and weight matrices into higher-order tensors.

*** Fine-tuning
\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Well-tuned simple nets\\ excel on tabular datasets}\\
  A. Kadra et al.
\end{book}
Search for the optimal combination of regularization techniques
(each with its own hyperparameters):
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item BatchNorm;
\item Stochastic weight averaging;
\item Look-ahead optimization;
\item Weight decay;
\item Dropout;
\item Snapshot Ensemble;
\item Skip connections;
\item ShakeShake;
\item ShakeDrop;
\item Mixup;
\item CutMix;
\item CutOut;
\item Adversarial learning.
\end{compactitem}

** DONE TO DELETE Embeddings, Transformers
*** Embeddings of numerical features
\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{On embeddings for numerical features\\ in tabular deep learning}\\
  Y. Gorishniy et al. (2022)
\end{book}
The advantage of transformers on MLP-like models for tabular data
may come from feature embeddings: try to
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Bin the feature values, using empirical quantiles,
  or splits of a (greedily-built, single-feature) decision tree,
  and use piecewise linear transformations.
  % TODO: PLOT? No
\item Or use periodic transformations,
  \[ x \longmapsto ( \cos 2\pi \mathbf c x, \sin 2\pi \mathbf c x ), \]
  with trained frequencies $\mathbf c$.
\end{compactitem}

*** Transformers, embeddings

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{TabICL: a tabular foundation model\\ for in-context learning on large data}\\
  J. Qu et al.
\end{book}
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item
  Compute column embeddings (TFcol)
\begin{align*}
  &\begin{aligned}
    & V = V_0 \text{ (learned)} \\
    & U = \text{Lin}(c) \text{ (train + test)} \\
  \end{aligned} \\
  &\llap{
    \ensuremath{
      3{\times}
      \left\{
      \vphantom{\ensuremath{
          \begin{aligned}
            &M = \text{MHAtt}( V, U_{\text{train}}, U_{\text{train}} ) \\
            &V = \text{MHAtt}( U, M, M ) \\
        \end{aligned}}
      }
      \right.
    }%
  }
  \begin{aligned}
    &M = \text{MHAtt}( V, U_{\text{train}}, U_{\text{train}} ) \\
    &V = \text{MHAtt}( U, M, M ) \\
  \end{aligned}
  \\
  &\begin{aligned}
    &W, B = \text{Lin}(V) \\
    &e = W \odot c + B
  \end{aligned}
\end{align*}
\item Add a positional column embedding (RoPE) -- this breaks the permutation invariance,
  but prevents representation collapse -- the column embeddings only depends on the distribution of the values;
\item Add four \textsc{cls} tokens to each row, and apply a transformer (TFrow);
  only keep the \textsc{cls} embeddings, $H$;
\item Add $y$ (1-hot encoding, limited to 10 classes -- use a hierarchical encoding if there are more)
  and apply a 12-layer transformer (TFicl),
  only allowing attention between $H_{\text{train}}$ and $H_{\text{train}}$,
  and between $H_{\text{test}}$ and $H_{\text{train}}$.
\end{compactitem}
The accuracy is comparable to TabPFNv2,
but it is more scalable.

The training data is synthetic: dense hierarchical structural model (SCM),
\ie, MLP, with random weights and activations sampled from a list of 20
(some discontinuous or non-monotinic)
or from a GP,
or from a tree-based model
(xgboost trained on random data).

*** Transformer
\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Revisiting deep learning models\\ for tabular data}\\
  Y. Gorishniy et al.
\end{book}
Try a ResNet
\begin{align*}
  & \text{ResNet} = \text{Pred}  \circ \text{ResNetBlock}^n  \circ \text{Linear} \\
  & \text{ResNetBlock} = \text{Id} + \text{Dropout} \circ \text{Linear} \circ \text{Dropout} \circ {}\\
  & \hspace*{3.5cm} \text{ReLU} \circ \text{Linear} \circ \text{BatchNorm} \\
  & \text{Pred} = \text{Linear} \circ \text{ReLU} \circ \text{BatchNorm}
\end{align*}
or an FT-Transformer (feature tokenizer transformer):
tokenize all features, numeric ($k$ affine transformations for each feature) or categorical,
add a \textsc{cls} token, apply a transformer

and use the \textsc{cls} token for the forecast.

** TODO TabPFN, k-NN
*** TabPFN, transformer

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Zero-shot meta-learning\\ for tabular prediction tasks\\ with adversarially pre-trained transformer}\\
  Y. Wu and D.L. Bergman
\end{book}
TabPFN is trained on synthetic data, from \emph{noisy sparsified random MLPs}:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Pick hyperparameters at random: number of layers, layer sizes, activation functions, dropout rate
  (this makes the network sparse), noise scale (inject noise in each layer);
\item Pick the weights at random;
\item Pick a few neurons at random as predictors $X$, and another as response $y$; clip at $4\sigma$;
  use \emph{ranking discretization} to produce categorical variables;
\end{compactitem}
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,scale=.25,inner sep=1pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline=0]
  \begin{scope}
    \draw[->] (0,0) -- (9,0);
    \draw[->] (0,0) -- (0,6);
    \foreach \i in {1,2,3,4,5}
      \draw[densely dotted] (0,\i) -- (8,\i);
    \draw[ultra thick,blue!70!black!80] (0,5) -- (2,5);
    \draw[ultra thick,blue!70!black!80] (3,4) -- (6,4);
    \draw[ultra thick,blue!70!black!80] (6,3) -- (7,3);
    \draw[ultra thick,blue!70!black!80] (2,2) -- (3,2);
    \draw[ultra thick,blue!70!black!80] (7,1) -- (8,1);

    \node[circle,fill] at (3,4) {};

    \node[circle,fill] at (2,2) {};
    \node[circle,fill] at (7,1) {};
    \node at (4.5,-1) {\footnotesize ranking discretization};
  \end{scope}
  \begin{scope}[shift={(16,0)}]
    \draw[->] (0,0) -- (9,0);
    \draw[->] (0,0) -- (0,6);
    \foreach \i in {1,2,3,4,5}
      \draw[densely dotted] (0,\i) -- (8,\i);
    \draw[ultra thick,blue!70!black!80] (0,5) -- (2,5.4);
    \draw[ultra thick,blue!70!black!80] (3,4) -- (6,4.6);
    \draw[ultra thick,blue!70!black!80] (6,3) -- (7,3.2);
    \draw[ultra thick,blue!70!black!80] (2,2) -- (3,2.2);
    \draw[ultra thick,blue!70!black!80] (7,1) -- (8,1.2);
    \node[circle,fill] at (0,5) {};
