  \end{scope}
\end{tikzpicture}
\]

Using \emph{adversarial} synthetic data agents improves performance (reset every $N$ epochs to avoid convergence).

To produce the output,
(the post-transformer embedding of) each test data point is compared with (that of) the training data,
producing two sets of logits, one for softmax probabilities, one for a sparsification gate (concrete distribution).

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{TabPFN unleashed: a scalable and effective solution to tabular classification problems}\\
  S.Y. Liu and H.J. Ye
\end{book}
To improve TabPFN:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Once the model is trained, add and fine-tune an \emph{adapter},
  an encoder to align the new dataset with the training datasets and reduce the bias;
  this dimension reduction also allows high-dimensional features;
\item Use a bag of such adapters to reduce variance;
\item Use bootstrap (aggregate the outputs for bootstrap samples of the test dataset);
\item Use ECOC (error correcting output codes) if there are more than 10 classes.
\end{compactitem}

*** k-NN
\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{TabR: Tabular deep learning\\ meets nearest neighbours in 2023}\\
  Y. Gorishniy et al. (2024)
\end{book}
Put a $k$-NN component inside an MLP:
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=1.2,yscale=.7,inner sep=1pt,outer sep=2pt,every node/.style={anchor=base}]
\node[anchor=base east] (x) at (0,2) {$x$};
\node[anchor=base east] (xi) at (0,1) {$\{x_i\}_i$};
\node[anchor=base east] (yi) at (0,0) {$\{y_i\}_i$};
\node[draw] (e1) at (1,2) {enc\strut};
\node[draw] (e2) at (1,1) {enc\strut};
\node[draw] (R) at (2,1) {R\strut};
\node[draw] (p) at (3,1) {pred\strut};
\node (y) at (4,1) {$\hat y$};
\draw[->] (x) -- (e1);
\draw[->] (xi) -- (e2);
\draw[->] (yi) to[out=0,in=-135] (R);
\draw[->] (e1) -- (R);
\draw[->] (e2) -- (R);
\draw[->] (R) -- (p);
\draw[->] (p) -- (y);
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
The retrieval component $R$
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Computes similarities between the latent representation of the input $\tilde x$ and of the candidates
  $\tilde x_i$, e.g.,
  \[ S( \tilde x , \tilde x_i ) = W_Q ( \tilde x ) ^\top W_k (\tilde x_i ) d^{-1/2} \]
  as in transformers or, better
  \[ S( \tilde x , \tilde x _i ) = - \n{ W_k(\tilde x _i ) - W_k(\tilde x ) } ^2 d^{-1/2}, \]
  \item Takes their top-$k$ softmax and multiplies it with
  \item The value
    \[ V( \tilde x , \tilde x_i , y_i ) = W_y( y_i) + T\bigl( W_k(\tilde x ) - W_k( \tilde x_i ) \bigr) \]
    where $T$ is a 1-hidden layer MLP.
\end{compactitem}

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Modern neighbourhood component analysis:\\ a deep tabular baseline two decades later}\\
  H.J. Ye et al. (2024)
\end{book}
Metric learning is $k$-NN with a learned metric,
\[ d_M ( x_i , x_j ) = \n{ L x_i - L x_j }_2. \]
Neighbour component analysis (NCA)
\[ \hat y = \sum_i \dfrac{
    \exp - d( L x , L x_i )
  }{
    \sum_j \exp - d( L x , L x_j )
  } y_i  \]
can be generalized to (Modern NCA)
\[ \hat y = \sum_i \dfrac{
    \exp - d\bigl( \phi(x) , \phi(x_i) \bigr)
  }{
    \sum_j \exp - d\bigl( \phi(x) , \phi(x_j) \bigr)
  } y_i  \]
where
\[ \phi = \text{Linear} \circ \text{Dropout} \circ \text{ReLU} \circ \text{Linear} \circ \text{BatchNorm} \]
and training uses random subsets of neighbours
instead of the whole dataset.

** DONE TO DELETE RFM, kernels
*** Tabular data, RFM

% RFM (Recursive feature machines); AGOP (Average Gradient Outer Product)

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{xRFM: accurate, scalable and interpretable feature learning models for tabular data}\\
  D. Beaglehole et al.
\end{book}
A \emph{kernel machine} is a linear model (ridge regression) on transformed data
\begin{align*}
  f(x) &= K( x, X ) \alpha \\
  \alpha &= \bigl( K(XmX) + \lambda I \bigr)^{-1} y .
\end{align*}

The \emph{average gradient outer product} (AGOP)
\[ \text{AGOP} ( \hat f , X ) =
  \operatorname*{Mean}_i \nabla \hat f(x_i ) \nabla \hat f( x_i ) ^\top \in \RR^{d \times d}
\]
(uncentered covariance)
captures the subspace of the feature space $\RR^d$
where the predictor varies the most;
it makes the model interpretable.

The \emph{kernel RFM} algorithm uses it to adapt the kernel to the data:
it iterates $T \leq 10$ times
\begin{align*}
  \hat f_t(x) &= K ( M_t x , X M_t ) \alpha_t \\
  \alpha+t &= \bigl( K( X M_t , X M_t ) + \lambda Y \bigr)^{-1} y \\
  M_{t+1} &= \text{AGOP} \bigl( \hat f_t( M_t x ) , X ) ^c, \qquad c=\tfrac12 \text{ or } \tfrac14 \\
  M_i &= I.
\end{align*}

Instead of kernels invariant to orthogonal transformations such as
$ \kappa(x,y) = \exp - L \n{ x - y }_2^2 $ or
$ \kappa(x,y) = \exp - L \n{ x - y }_2$ ,
prefer \emph{axis-aware kernels},
\[ \kappa_{p,q}(x,y) = \exp \bigl( - \n{ x - y }_q^p / L^p \bigr) . \]
To introduce an axis-aligned bias, consider using only the \emph{diagonal} of the AGOP.

\emph{xRFM} recursively builds a tree, splitting the data in half each time, on the median along the top eigenvector of the AGOP of
an axis-aware kernel RFM as long as the leaves contain at least 60,000 samples;
it then trains an axis-aware kernel RFM on each leaf.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Mechanism of feature learning in deep fully connected networks and kernel machines\\ that recursively learn features}\\
  A. Radhakrishnan et al.
\end{book}
Neural networks trained with gradient descent ``learn features'',
in the sense that they upweigh features with an impact on the output;
more formally,
\[ W_i ^\top W_i \propto \operatorname*{Mean}
  \left( \dfrac{ \partial \hat y }{ \partial h_i } \right)
  \left( \dfrac{ \partial \hat y }{ \partial h_i } \right)^\top \]
(gradient of the output $\hat y$ wrt the activations $h_i$ of the $i$th layer).
This leads to a backpropagarion-free learning method,
the \emph{recursive feature machine} (RFM)
\begin{align*}
  & M = I \\
  & \llap{\ensuremath{\times T\left[
    \vphantom{\ensuremath{
            \begin{aligned}
              & f(x) = y K_M( X, X )^{-1} K_M(X, x)  \\
              & M = \operatorname*{Mean}_x \nabla f(x) \nabla f(x)^\top
            \end{aligned}
    }}
    \right.}}
    \begin{aligned}
      & f(x) = y K_M( X, X )^{-1} K_M(X, x)  \\
      & M = \operatorname*{Mean}_x \nabla f(x) \nabla f(x)^\top
    \end{aligned}
\end{align*}
where
\[ K_M(x,y) = \exp \bigl[ - \gamma ( x-y )^\top M (x-y) \bigr]. \]

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Emergence in non-neural models:\\ grokking modular arithmetic\\ via average gradient outer product}\\
  N. Mallinar et al.
\end{book}
Double descent (``grokking'') when learning modular arithmetic with RFM (recursive feature machines);
it discovers Fourier multiplication.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Average gradient outer product\\ as a mechanism for deep neural collapse}\\
  D. Beaglehole et al.
\end{book}
\emph{Neural collapse}, in a trained neural network for classification, refers to the activations of the last layer being concentrated
around points corresponding to the different classes --
all other information is discarded,
and the network output can be computed by finding the nearest class center.

That phenomenon also happens with DeepRFM models.

The \emph{DeepRFM} model is
\begin{align*}
  & X_1 \text{ input} \\
  & X_\ell \leftarrow \text{normalize}( X_\ell ) \\
  & f_\ell(\bigcdot) = Y \bigl( \kappa(X_\ell, X_\ell) + \gamma I \bigr)^{-1}  \kappa( X_\ell, \bigcdot) \\
  & M_\ell = \operatorname*{Mean}_{i,j}
    \left( \dfrac{ \partial f_\ell( x_{ij}^\ell ) }{ \partial x^\ell } \right)
    \left( \dfrac{ \partial f_\ell( x_{ij}^\ell ) }{ \partial x^\ell } \right) ^ \top  \\
  & X_{ \ell+1 } = \Phi_\ell ( M_\ell ^{1/2} X_\ell )
\end{align*}
where $\kappa$ is a kernel, $\gamma$ the ridge parameter,
and $\Phi_\ell$ a feature map, e.g.,
\[ \Phi_\ell(x) = \sigma( W_\ell x + b_\ell ) \]
with $W_\ell$ and $b_\ell$ random, $\sigma=\text{ReLU}$ or cosine.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Mechanism of feature learning in convolution}\\
  D. Beaglehole et al.
\end{book}
The \emph{DeepRFM} model stacks several RFM models
\begin{align*}
  & X_1 \text{ input} \\
  & \text{For } \ell=1 \text{ to } L: \\
  & \qquad M_\ell = I \\
  & \qquad \text{For } t=1 \text{ to } T: \\
  & \qquad\qquad f_\ell(\bigcdot) = y K_{ M_\ell} ( X_\ell, X_\ell )^{-2} K_{ M_\ell } ( X_\ell, \bigcdot ) \\
  & \qquad\qquad M_\ell \leftarrow \operatorname{Mean}
    \bigl( \nabla f_\ell(x_\ell) \bigr)
    \bigl( \nabla f_\ell(x_\ell) \bigr) ^\top \\
  & \qquad W_{\ell,k} \sim  N( 0, M_\ell ) \\
  & \qquad X_{ \ell+1} = \sigma( W_\ell X_\ell )
\end{align*}

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Towards universal steering and monitoring\\ of AI models}\\
  D. Beaglehole et al.
\end{book}
To extract \emph{concepts} from an LLM, such as
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Liberal
\item Conservative
\item Conspiracy
\item Social media influencer
\item Deception
\item Antirefusal
\item Persona of X (e.g., Shakespeare)
\item Expert in X (e.g., algebra)
\item Places (e.g., Paris)
\item Moods (e.g., snarky)
\item Fear of X (e.g., bugs)
\item Languages (e.g., French, C++)
\item etc.
\end{compactitem}
learn the directions, in the activation space,
that have the most influence on the output,
using RFM (recursive feature machines --
a kind of nonlinear, supervised PCA).

Those concepts can be usd to steer and/or monitor LLMs and VLMs.

** DONE TO DELETE Kernel methods and large data
*** Tabular data, Kernel machines on large data

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Towards large kernel models}\\
  A. Abedsoltan et al.
\end{book}
A \emph{kernel machine} is a model of the form
\[ f( \mathbf x ) = \sum_i \alpha_i k( \mathbf x, \mathbf x_i ) \]
where the $\alpha_i$'s minimize the loss
\[ \sum_i \bigl( f( \mathbf x_i ) - y_i \bigr) ^2 +  \lambda \n{ f }_{ \mathscr H }, \]
\ie, $\alpha$ is the solution of
\[ \bigl( k(X,X) + \lambda I \bigr) \alpha = y . \]
The \emph{Nystr\"om approximate} model replaces the points used in the definition of $f$
with a random subset $Z \subset X$,
\[ f(\mathbf x ) = \sum_j  \alpha_j k( \mathbf x , \mathbf z_j ) \]
(\emph{general kernel model}).

\emph{EigenPro1.0} uses a preconditioned stochastic gradient descent to compute $\alpha$,
\[ \alpha \leftarrow \alpha - \eta ( I - Q ) \bigl( k(X,X() \alpha - y \bigr) \]
where $Q$ is a rank-k approcimation of $k(X,X)$.

\emph{EigenPro2.0} uses the Nystr\"om approximation.

\emph{EigenPro3.0} also uses minibatches.  % Not only.

Pytorch code available.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Diving into the shallows: a computational perspective on large-scale shallow learning}\\
  S. Ma and M. Belkin (2017)
\end{book}
Original paper on EigenPro1.0

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Randomly pivoted Cholesky:\\ practical approximation of a kernel matrix\\ with few entry evaluations}\\
  Y. Chen et al.
\end{book}
The \d{Nystr\"om approximation} of a positive semidefinite (psd) matrix $A$ is
\[ \hat A = A(:,S) A(S,S)^\dagger A(S,:) \]
where $S \subset \llbracket 1, N \rrbracket$
is a subset of the columns (and rows).

The \emph{pivoted partial Cholesky} algorithm computes $\hat A$ iteratively,
in factorized form, $\hat A = F F^*$,
one column of $F$ at a time.
\begin{align*}
  & F \leftarrow 0_{ N \times k} \\
  &\text{For } i = 1 \text{ to } k: \\
  &\qquad g \leftarrow A[ :, s_i ] \\
  &\qquad g \leftarrow g - F[ : , 1 : (i-1) ] F[ s_i , 1:(i-1) ]^*** \\
  & \qquad F[:,i] \leftarrow g / \sqrt{ g[ s_i ] }
\end{align*}

The \emph{randomly pivoted Cholesky} algorithm picks the pivots $s_i$ at random,
with probabilities proportional to the diagonal of the residual
\begin{align*}
  A^{(i)} &= A - \hat A^{(i)} \\
  &= A - F[:,i] F[:,:i]^*.
\end{align*}
This does not require evaluating all the elements of $A$.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Falkon: an optimal large scale kernel method}\\
  A. Rudi et al.
\end{book}
Preconditioning, with a Nystr\"om approximation, to fit a kernel machine.

*** Synthetic data
\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{TabSDS: a lightweight, fully\\ non-parametric, and model free approach\\ for generating synthetic tabular data}\\
  E.C. Neto (2025)
\end{book}
To generate synthetic tabular data:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Discretize column 1 into $n$ classes;
\item Shuffle the rows while preserving column 1;
\item Restore column 1;
\item Do the same for the other columns;
\item Optionally replace the values in each column with data from a piecewise uniform distribution approximating the data, preseving the ranks.
\end{compactitem}
This generalizes to mixed (continuous+categorical) data.

Baselines (in \texttt{synthcity}) include
TabDDPM (SOTA),
ARF (adversarial random forests -- fast),
TabularVAE,
conditional tabular GAN,
Bayesian neural network (DAG from \texttt{pgmpy}).

  \emph{Synthcity: facilitating innovative use cases\\ of synthetic data in different data modalities}\\
  Z. Qian et al.
\end{book}
Unified interface to synthetic data generators (tabular, censored, (ir)regular time series).

Applictions include privacy, fairness, data augmentation.

Similar libraries: YData, Gretel, SDV, DataSynthesizer, SmartNoise, nbsynthetic.

*** Forecast evaluation

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Forecast evaluation for data scientists: common pitfalls and best practices}\\
  H. Hewamalage et al.
\end{book}
Long list of error measures for time series forecasting, with their suggested use and pitfalls.
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Use a simple baseline (naive, seasonal naive);
\item Forecast plots $t,\hat y \sim \text{time}$ are misleading;
\item Beware of non-stationarity (seasonality, trend, unit root (stochastic trend), heteroskedasticity, structural breaks)
  and non-normality (symmetry, tails, outliers, intermittency (zero-inflation));
\item Beware of ddata leakage.
\end{compactitem}

*** Multimodal (time series + text)

\nextbook

  A.R. Williams et al.
\end{book}
Cik is a benchmark for \emph{multimodal}
probabilistic time series forecasting models
(models also provided with a textual context for each time series),
such as UniTime,
Time-LLM, LagLlama, Chronos, Moira, TimeGEN.

The CRPS (continuous ranked probability score))
\begin{align*}
  \text{CRPS}( F, x ) &= \int_{-\infty}^{+\infty} \bigl( F(y) = \mathbf1_{y\geq x} \bigr)^2 dy
  \qquad (\text{dim}=1) \\
  &= \E_{ X \sim F } \va{ X - x } - \dfrac12 \E_{ X, Y \sim F } \va{ X-Y}
\end{align*}
\[
<<python-CRPS,engine='python',results='hide',echo=FALSE,out.width='.8\\hsize',fig.show='hold'>>=
import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import scipy
xs = np.linspace( -5, 5 )
ys = scipy.stats.norm.cdf(xs)
i = ( 3*len(xs) ) // 5
fig, ax = plt.subplots( figsize = (4,1.5) )
ax.fill_between( xs[:i+1], 0*ys[:i+1], ys[:i+1], color = 'tab:orange', alpha = .3 )
ax.fill_between( xs[i:], ys[i:], 1+0*ys[i:], color = 'tab:orange', alpha = .3 )
ax.plot(xs, ys, linewidth = 3)
ax.axhline( 0, color = 'black', linewidth = 1 )
ax.axhline( 1, color = 'black', linewidth = 1, linestyle = ':' )
ax.set_xlim(-5, 5)
ax.axis('off')
plt.show()
@
\]
(the energy score uses $\n{\cdot}_2^p$ insead of $\va\cdot$)
is a proper scoring rule generalizing the MAE
to assess \emph{probabilistic} forecasts --
cross-entropy
\begin{align*}
  - \operatorname*{Mean}_x \log f(x) &= H( \text{data}, F ) \\
  H(p,q) &= - \E_{ X \sim p } \log q(x)
\end{align*}
is often a better choice if the forecasted density is available.

The RCRPS (region-of-interest CRPS) is a weighted and penalized CRPS:
to give more weight to some forecasting horizons and penalize (problem-specific) constraint violations.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Predict, refine, synthesize:\\ self-guiding diffusion models\\ for probabilistic time series forecasting}\\
  M. Kollovieh et al.
\end{book}
TSDiff is an unconditional diffusion model for time series with S4 layers.
To make it conditional, \emph{diffusion guidance} uses Bayes rule
\[ \nabla _{\! x_t } \log p( x_t | c ) = \nabla_ {\! x_t } \log p(x_t ) +
\nabla_{\! x_t} \log p( c | x_t )  \]
which only requires an auxiliary classifier (for forecasting, $c = x^{\text{obs}}$).

The \emph{continuous ranked probability score} (CRPS)
is the quantile (pinball) loss
integrated from 0 to 1;
it can be used to evaluate probabilistic forecasts.

** TODO Non-stationarity, domain adaptation
*** Non-stationarity

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{TimeBridge: non-stationarity matters\\ for long-term time series forecasting}\\
  P. Liu et al. (2025)
\end{book}
When forecasting time series, we often have to choose between
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Eliminating non-stationarity (e.g., to preserve correlations);
\item Preserving non-stationarity (to reveal long-term dependencies, e.g., cointegration).
\end{compactitem}
Split the data into patches,
and process both the normalized (stationary) and raw (non-stationary) embeddings
with attention.
\begin{align*}
  & X_{it} && \text{patches} \\
  & P_{it} = \text{MLP}_i( X_{it} ) && \text{patch embeddings} \\
  & P_{it}^{\text{stationary}} = P_{it} - \text{MA}( P_{i\bigcdot})_u && \text{normalized patches} \\
  & P_{it}^{\text{down}} = \text{Downsample}( P_{i\bigcdot} )_t \\
  & \text{Att}( P_{i\bigcdot}^{\text{stat}}, P_{i\bigcdot}^{\text{stat}}, P_{i\bigcdot} ) && \text{integrated attention} \\
  & \text{Att}( P_{i\bigcdot}^{\text{down}}, P_{i\bigcdot}, P_{i\bigcdot} ) && \text{integrated attention} \\
  & \text{Att}( P_{i\bigcdot}, P_{i\bigcdot}, P_{i\bigcdot} ) && \text{cointegrated attention}
\end{align*}
Each attention is followed by two LayerNorms:
\begin{align*}
  x &\leftarrow \text{LayerNorm}( P + x ) \\
  x &\leftarrow \text{LayerNorm}\bigl( x + \text{MLP}(x) \bigr).
\end{align*}

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Reversible instance normalization\\ for accurate time-series forecasting\\ against distribution shift}\\
  T. Kim et al.
\end{book}
Before forecasting a time series (with N-BEATS, SciNet, Informer, etc.),
RevIN normalizes it (makes it stationary),
and denormalizes the forecasts,
with a learned affine transformation
\[ \hat x_{it} = \gamma \cdot \dfrac{
  x_{it} - \E_i[ x_{it} ] }{
  \sqrt{
    \var_i[ x_{it} ] + \epsilon
  }
} + \beta. \]

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Dish-TS: a general paradigm for alleviating distribution shift in time series forecasting}\\
  W. Fan et al.
\end{book}
Forecast a non-stationaty time series by normalizing the input and denormalizing the forecast,
where the normalization and denormalization are (different) transformations of the form
\[ \mathbf x \longmapsto \dfrac{ \mathbf x - \mu(\mathbf x) }{ \sigma( \mathbf x ) } \]
where $\mu$ and $\sigma$ are neural nets
(for non-stationary time series,
$x_{\leq t}$ and $x_{>t}$ have different distributions).
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=2,yscale=.8,inner sep=1pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline=0]
  \node (A) at (0,1) {$x_{\leq t}$};
  \node (B) at (1,1) {$x_{>t}$};
  \node (C) at (0,0) {$\tilde x_{\leq t}$};
  \node (D) at (1,0) {$\tilde x_{>t}$};
  \draw[->] (A) to node[anchor=east,pos=.4] {\footnotesize normalize} (C);
  \draw[->] (C) to node[anchor=north] {\footnotesize forecast} (D);
  \draw[->] (D) to node[anchor=west,pos=.5] {\footnotesize denormalize} (B);
\end{tikzpicture}
\]

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Adaptive normalization\\ for non-stationary time series forecasting:\\ a temporal slice perspective}\\
  Z. Liu et al.
\end{book}
Idem, but the time series is first cut into slices, which are then normalized.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Non-stationary diffusion\\ for probabilistic time series forecasting}\\
  W. Ye et al.
\end{book}
% Not read
To account for non-stationaryty in \emph{probabilistic} time series forecasting,
do not use the additive noise model (ANM)
\[ Y = f_\phi(X) + \epsilon \qquad \epsilon \sim N(0,I), \]
but the location scale noise model (LSNM)
\[ Y = f_\phi(X) + \sqrt{ g_\psi(X) } \epsilon \qquad \epsilon \sim N(0,I) \]
(previous diffusion-based models assumed homoskedasticity).

*** Synthetic data

% FTS-Diffusion

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Generative learning for financial time series with irregular and scale-invariant patterns}\\
  H. Huang et al. (ICLR 2024)
\end{book}

Scale-invariant subsequence clustering (SISC) is similar to $k$-means on length-$\ell$ subsequences, but it lets $\ell$ vary.
\[ \begin{array}{lll}
  \text{Find} & p_k \in \RR^L & \hspace*{-5mm} \text{length-$\ell$ patterns} \\
              && \hspace*{-5mm}\text{(cluster centers)} \\
              & k_t & \hspace*{-5mm} \text{cluster of } x_{ [t, t + \ell_k] } \\
              & \ell_k \in [ \ell_{\text{min}}, \ell_{\text{max}} ] & \hspace*{-5mm} \text{subsequence length} \\
  \text{To minimize} & d_{ \text{DTW} }\bigl( x_{ [t , t + \ell_k ] } , p_k \bigr)
\end{array} \]
Initialize the algorithm with $k$-means++ on maximum-lengh subsequences.

Train a VAE to learn a fixed-length representation of those variable-length sequences,
and train a diffusion model to generate more such subsequences.
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=2,yscale=.5,inner sep=1pt,outer sep=2pt,every node/.style={anchor=base}]
\node (A) at (0,0) {subsequences\strut};
\node[right=15mm of A] (B) {latent\strut};
\node[right=15mm of B] (C) {noise\strut};
\draw[->] ([yshift=+3pt]A.east) to node[anchor=south] {\footnotesize enc} ([yshift=+3pt]B.west);
\draw[->] ([yshift=-3pt]B.west) to node[anchor=north] (b) {\footnotesize dec} ([yshift=-3pt]A.east);
\draw[->] ([yshift=+3pt]B.east) to node[anchor=south] {\footnotesize +noise} ([yshift=+3pt]C.west);
\draw[->] ([yshift=-3pt]C.west) to node[anchor=north] (c) {\footnotesize denoise} ([yshift=-3pt]B.east);
\node[below=1mm of b] {VAE};
\node[below=1mm of c] {diffusion};
\end{tikzpicture}
\]

Learn a transition function between patterns
\[ \phi: ( p, \alpha, \beta)_t \longmapsto (p, \alpha, \beta)_{t+1}, \]
where $p$ is a latent representation,
$\alpha$ its duration, $\beta$ its scale.

\emph{FTS Diffusion} combines those elements to generate financial time series.

  H. Juang et al.

Use FTS-Diffusion for data augmentation,
when forecasting future S\&P\,500 daily returns
from the past 60 days, with deep learning models
(iTransformer, pretrained on a domain-adjacent dataset, 8 FX time series).

** TODO Probabilistic forecasts
*** Probabilistic forecasts, scoring rules,

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Regions of reliability in the evaluation\\ of multivariate probabilistic forecasts}\\
  \'E. Marcotte et al. (2023)
\end{book}
A \emph{scoring rule} is a function $S$ measuring how unlikely an event $y \sim \text{Data}$ is,
wrt a forecasted distribution $F$; it is \emph{proper} if
\[ \E_{ y \sim \text{Data} } S(y,F) \]
is minimal for $F = \text{Data}$.

In practice, however, we do not have a ground truth distribution, but only a finite sample from it:
for some combinations of
\begin{align*}
  d &: \text{ dimension} \\
  n &: \text{ground truth sample size} \\
  m &: \text{ forecast sample size}
\end{align*}
the statistical power of most scoring rules to detect
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Differences in mean;
\item Positive correlations;
\item Skewness;
\item Difference in marginal variance;
\item etc.
\end{compactitem}
is low -- you need to increase $n$ and $m$.

*** Koopman, ODE

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{KoNODE: Koopman-driven neural ordinary differential equations with evolving parameters for time series analysis}\\
  H. Bai and W. Ding
\end{book}
A neural ODE (NODE) looks for an ODE
\[ \dot x = f(x,t,\theta) \]
fitting the data.
KoNODE lets the ODE parameters $\theta$ vary with time,
with its dynamics described by
\begin{align*}
  \theta_t &= h( w_t ) \\
  \dot w_t &= A w_t
\end{align*}
(the dynamics of $\theta$ are non-linear, but they become linear when lifted to a higher-dimension space).

* TODO Interpretability
** TODO Introduction
*** Mechanistic interpretability, ICML tutorial

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Mechanistic interpretability\\ for language models}\\
  (ICML 2025)
\end{book}
\emph{Mechanistic interpretability} (MI) studies
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item The \emph{features} encoded in the model (in layers, components (e.g., attention heads), or neurons);
\item \emph{Circuits}, \ie, computational subgraphs whose nodes are features;
\item \emph{Universality}: whether those features or circuits generalize to other architectures or tasks.
\end{compactitem}
Feature study can be targeted (``is feature X present? where?''):
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Probing: use a simple model (linear) to distinguish presence/absence of a feature at a given layer
  (but the probe should not be too powerful, and it can only detect the presence of a feature, not whether it is used);
\item Vocabulary projection: decode an activation (from the residual stream) into a token
  (you may need another matrix than the embedding matrix: it was trained from the final layer only)
\item Transplant the activation in another context, e.g., to complete the sentence ``tell me more about''
\end{compactitem}
or open-ended
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Neuron activation visualization: highlight tokens which activate a neuron and try to find what they have in common --
  but most neurons are polysemantic (\emph{superposition} phenomenon);
\item Sparse autoencoders (SAE) try to disentangle superposed features.
\end{compactitem}

To study circuits,
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Choose a behaviour, e.g., subject-verb agreement;
\item Define the computational graph (the nodes could be layers, bits of layers (attention heads) or neurons);
\item Find the subgraph explaining that behaviour.
\end{compactitem}
Try:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Intervention:
  \begin{compactitem}[\ensuremath{\bigcdot}]
  \item Noising: remove the component of interest (use resampling rather than zero or random values -- that would be out-of-distribution);
  \item Denoising: keep the component of interest but remove the rest,
    by adding noise, or changing the input (counterfactual input);
  \end{compactitem}
\item Attention visualization.
\end{compactitem}

Results are mixed:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Only 1\% to 5\% of neurons are universal;
\item Most neurons are polysemantic;
\item SAE probes are worse than logistic regression;
\item SAEs are worse than prompting to steer neural nets;
\item MI study is not scalable, lacks benchmarks and ground truth;
\item It is unclear how to generalize MI beyond text.
\end{compactitem}

** TODO SAE, Transcoder
*** Better SAEs
\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Scaling and evaluating sparse autoencoders}\\
  L. Gao et al.
\end{book}
To train SAEs:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Use a TopK activation function (this eliminates the need for a sparsifying $L^2$ penalty)
  or MultiTopK (combine losses for several values of $k$, e.g., $\ell_k + \tfrac18 \ell_{4k}$);
\item Initialize the decoder to the transpose of the encoder;
\item Add an \emph{auxilliary loss}, for the reconstruction error from the top $k_{\text{aux}}$ dead latents --
  this reduces the number of dead latents, which can otherwise reach 90\% of the activations.
\end{compactitem}

*** Better SAEs

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Scaling and evaluating sparse auto-encoders}\\
  L. Gao et al.
\end{book}
The SAE with top-$k$ activations ($k=32$)
does not require an $\ell^0$ or $\ell^1$ penalty,
but may suffer from a large number of dead neurons:
add an auxiliary loss, the reconstruction error from the top $k_{\text{aux}}$ (512).
You can use several values of $k$,
e.g., $\ell(k) + \ell(4k)/8$, and change $k$ at test time.

% TODO: WHAT IS $\ell$?

\begin{align*}
  y &= \sigma \bigl( W_1 (x-b_2) + b_1 \bigr) \\
  \hat x &= W_2 y + b_2
\end{align*}

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Improving dictionary learning\\ with gated sparse autoencoders}\\
  S. Rajamanoharan et al.
\end{book}
The $L^1$ penalty in SAE
\begin{align*}
  x &= x - b_2 \\
  y &= \text{ReLU}( W_1 x + b_1 ) \\
  \hat x &= W_2 y + b_2
\end{align*}
causes shrinkage.
Instead, \emph{gated SAE}s use
\begin{align*}
  x &= x - b_2 \\
  g &= W_0 x + b_0 \\
  y &= \mathbf 1 _{g\geq0} \odot \text{ReLU}( W_1 x + b_1 ) \\
  \hat x &= W_2 y + b_2 \\
  (W_1)_{ij} &= e^{ r_i } \cdot ( W_0 )_{ij}.
\end{align*}

*** SAE: Matryoshka

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Learning multi-level features\\ with Matryoshka sparse autoencoders}\\
  B. Bussmann et al.
\end{book}
Sparse autoencoders (SAE) with a large dictionary size struggle to capture the hierarchical structure of real-world concepts
and suffer from:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item \emph{Feature splitting}: high-level features disappear and are replaced by lower-level ones, e.g.,
  ``punctuation mark'' is split into ``period'', ``comma'', ``question mark'', etc.;
\item \emph{Feature absorption}: high-level features only partially split, leading to high-level features with holes, e.g.,
  ``tokens stating with E except `Elephant'{}'';
\item \emph{Feature composition}: high-level concepts can merge, e.g., ``red'' and ``triangle'' can lead to ``red triangle''.
\end{compactitem}
Train a single SAE with a Matryoshka loss
\[ \ell = \sum _{ m \in \mathcal M} \n{ \mathbf x - f_n( \mathbf x ) }_2^2 + \alpha \ell_{\text{aux}} \]
where $\mathcal M$ is a set of dictionary sizes (either fixed or sampled randomly for each batch --
fixed seems to work better)
and $f_m(\mathbf x)$ is the reconstruction using only the first $m$ features.

Use JumpReLU (zero out activations below some threshold),
Top-$k$ (zero out all bit the top $k$ activations, for each sample),
or BatchTopK (zero our all but the top $B\times k$ activations in a batch of size $B$).

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Matryoshka representation learning}\\
  A. Kusupati et al.
\end{book}
Use $\displaystyle \sum_{k=0}^K \ell( z_{ 1: d / 2^k} )$ as loss ($K=4$).

*** SAE, JumpReLU

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Jumping ahead: improving reconstruction fidelity with JumpReLU sparse autoencoders}\\
  S. Rajamanoharan et al.
\end{book}
To train an SAE,
with discontinuous JumpReLU activations
and $\ell^0$ sparsity penalty,
use straight-through estimation for the gradients
\[
\dfrac{ \partial \text{JumpReLU} }{ \partial \theta } *** k_\epsilon ,
\quad
\dfrac{ \partial \mathbf 1 _{ z \geq \theta } }{ \partial \theta } *** k_\epsilon
\]
where $k_\epsilon$ is a (rectangle) kernel of bandwidth $\epsilon$;
for the $\ell^0$ penalty, use the pre-activations:
\[ \n{y} _0 = \mathbf 1 _{ W_1 x + b_1 \geq 0 }. \]

*** Universal SAE

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Universal sparse autoencoders:\\ interpretable cross-model concept alignment}\\
  H. Thasarathan et al.
\end{book}
Train a single, overcomplete SAE on the activations of several pretrained models.
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=2,yscale=.8,inner sep=2pt,outer sep=0pt,baseline=0]
  \node (i) at (1,6) {image};
  \node[fill=blue!15] (mi) at (2,5) {model $i$};
  \node[fill=blue!15] (mj) at (0,5) {model $j$};
  \node (ai) at (2,4) {activations $i$};
  \node (aj) at (0,4) {activations $j$};

  \node (hj) at (0,0) {$\widehat{\text{activations }j}$};

*** Gemma Scope (is there a website?)

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Gemma Scope: open sparse autoencoders everywhere all at once on Gemma 2}\\
  T. Lieberum et al.
\end{book}
To explain an LLM,
train a \emph{sparse autoencoder} (SAE, sparse and nonnegative)
on its activations
(after the MLP, or the activations on the residual stream -- for all layers)
\begin{align*}
  & x \mapsto y = \sigma( W_1 x + b_1 ) \mapsto \hat x = W_2 y + b_2 \\
  & \sigma(z) = \text{JumpReLU}_\theta(z) = z \odot \mathbf{1}_{ z \geq \theta } =
  \smash{%
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,scale=.5,inner sep=1pt,outer sep=0pt,baseline=-2]
  \draw[->] (-1,0) -- (1.5,0);
  \draw[->] (0,-1) -- (0,1.5);
  \draw[densely dotted] (-1,-1) -- (1.5,1.5);
  \draw[ultra thick,blue!50] (-1,0) -- (.75,0);
  \draw[ultra thick,blue!50] (.75,.75) -- (1.5,1.5);
\end{tikzpicture}%
}
  \\
  & \theta = 10^{-3}
\end{align*}
\emph{Transcoders} (which reconstruct the output of an MLP from its input,
using a sparse hidden layer) do not work that well.

*** SAE: applications (Claude)
\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Scaling monosemanticity: extracting interpretable features from Claude 3 Sonnet}\\
  A. Templeton et al.
\end{book}
% With an interactive feature UMAP
Applications of sparse autoencoders (SAE) to interpret LLMs include
identifying features activated when
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item The model gives instructions to build chemical, biological or nuclear weapons;
\item The user probes the model's goals and values, or tries to jailbreak it;
\item The model is trained to be a sleeper agent;
\item etc.
\end{compactitem}


*** Transcoders; circuits

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Transcoders find\\ interpretable LLM feature circuits}\\
  J. Dunefsky et al.
\end{book}
\emph{Circuit analysis} looks for sparse subgraphs corresponding to specific behaviours.
Transcoders approximate a densely activating MLP layer with a wider, sparsely activating layer.
\begin{align*}
  z &= \text{ReLU}( W_1 x + b_1 ) \\
  \hat y &= W_2 z + b_2.
\end{align*}
Train with a faithfulness loss
$\n{ \hat y - y }^2$
and a sparsity penalty $\n z _1 $.

%TODO: FIGURE 1?

To find interpretable circuits,
pick the top $k$ activations;
among their children, pick the top $k$ activations;
among the resulting $k^2$ graphs, pick the top $k$ ones;
and continue.

%TODO: FIGURE 2?

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Sparse cross-coders\\ for cross-layer features and model diffing}\\
  J. Lindsey et al.
\end{book}
Adjacent layers are almost parallel:
apply dictionary learning to them jointly
(a cross-coder is a multi-layer transcoder).

*** Circuits

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Sparse feature circuits: discovering and editing interpretable causal graphs in language models}\\
  S. Marks et al.
\end{book}
Neurons or attention heads are polysemantic.
Instead, use sparse autoencoders and look for sparse feature circuits.
After interpreting them,
you may decide to remove some of them
(e.g., gender, for fairness).

** TODO ML: RuleFit, adaptive lasso
*** ML, interpretablity: RuleFit

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Predictive learning via rule ensembles}\\
  J.H. Friedman nad B.E. Popescu (2008)
\end{book}
\emph{RuleFit} is an interpretable machine learning model:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Fit a random forest;
\item Extract rules (each path from the root of a tree to a leaf defines a rule);
\item Fit a lasso on the initial features and the rules (to capture interactions),
  after standardizing both features and rules.
\end{compactitem}

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Node Harvest}\\
  N. Meinshausen (2010)
\end{book}
\emph{NodeHarvest} is similar to RuleFit,
but looks for a \emph{convex} combination of leaf forecasts
(instead of a linear combination of leaf indicators).

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Interpretable random forests\\ via rule extraction}\\
  C. B\'enard et al.
\end{book}
\emph{Sirus}
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Trains a random forest, with shallow trees, and splits at the $q$-empirical quantiles of the marginal distribution ($q=10$);
\item Selects the most frequent rules (internal nodes or leaves);
\item Removes the redundant ones (linear combinations of more frequent ones);
\item Fits a ridge regression with the rule values.
\end{compactitem}

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Rule induction partitioning estimator:\\ design of an interpretable prediction algorihm}\\
  V. Margot et al.
\end{book}
RIPE
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Builds a set of ``suitable'' rules (hyper-rectangles, using a small number of coordinates);
\item Computes the forecast of an observation $x$ by using the average of the values in
  \begin{compactitem}[\ensuremath{\cdot}]
  \item The intersection of the cells containing $x$
  \item Minus the union of the cells not containing $x$;
  \end{compactitem}
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,scale=.25,inner sep=1pt,outer sep=2pt,every node/.style={anchor=base}]
  \fill[yellow!50!white] (0,2) -- (0,4) -- (2,4) -- (2,3) -- (1,3) -- (1,2) -- cycle;
  \draw[ultra thick,green!50!black] (0,2) -- (5,2) -- (5,5) -- (0,5) -- cycle;
  \draw[ultra thick,red!70!white] (0,-2) -- (0,4) -- (2,4) -- (2,-2) -- cycle;
  \draw[ultra thick,blue!40!white] (1,-2) -- (1,3) -- (5,3) -- (5,-2) -- cycle;
  \node[circle,fill,inner sep=1pt] (A) at (.5,3.5) {};
  \node[right=-1.5mm of A]{\footnotesize$x$};
  \begin{scope}[shift={(8,-.5)}]
    \draw[ultra thick,red!70!white]   (0,5.7) -- (1,5.7) -- (1,5.15) -- (0,5.15) -- cycle;
    \draw[ultra thick,green!50!white] (0,4.85) -- (1,4.85) -- (1,4.3) -- (0,4.3) -- cycle;
    \draw[ultra thick,blue!40!white]  (0,2.7) -- (1,2.7) -- (1,3.3) -- (0,3.3) -- cycle;
    \draw[ultra thick, yellow!50!white] (0,0.7) -- (1,0.7) -- (1,1.3) -- (0,1.3) -- cycle;
    \fill[ultra thick, yellow!50!white] (0,0.7) -- (1,0.7) -- (1,1.3) -- (0,1.3) -- cycle;
    \node[anchor=west] at (1.5,5) {cells containing $x$};
    \node[anchor=west] at (1.5,3) {cells not containing $x$};
    \node[anchor=west] at (1.5,1) {\strut intersection of cells containing $x$};
    \node[anchor=west] at (1.5,-.2) {\strut minus};
    \node[anchor=west] at (1.5,-1.4) {\strut union of cells not containing $x$};
  \end{scope}
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
\item Prunes the set of suitable rules to minimize the empirical risk.
\end{compactitem}
Those rules do not form a tree -- they can be converted to a partition of a tree,
but it could be exponentially larger than the rule set.

*** ML, interpretability: Adaptive lasso

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{The adaptive lasso and its oracle properties}\\
  H. Zou (2006)
\end{book}
The adaptive lasso (AdaLasso)
first fits a lasso (or an OLS)
\[ \hat\beta_{\text{lasso}} = \argmin_\beta \n{ y - X \beta }_2^2 + \lambda \n \beta _1 \]
and then a weighted lasso
\[ \hat\beta_{\text{AdaLasso}} = \argmin_\beta \n{ y - X \beta }_2^2 + \lambda \sum_i \va{ \hat\beta_{\text{lasso}, i} }^{-1} \cdot \va{ \beta_i } \]
\ie, it penalizes even further small coefficients.
It has better asymptotic properties.

** TODO Relevance
*** Statistics: Relevance

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Dual interpretation\\ of machine learning forecasts}\\
  P. Goulet Colombe et al.
\end{book}
The output of many models ((kernel) ridge regression, neural nets, random forests, boosting)
can be expressed as linear combinations of training observations:
\[ \hat y_j = \sum_{ i \in \text{Train} } w_{ij} y_i . \]
Those weights can be interpreted as the weights of a ``data portfolio''.
We can compute and plot:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item The contributions $c_{ji} = w_{ji} y_i$, and their cummulated sums;
\item The concentration of $w_{\bigcdot j }$;
\item The sum of the negative weights (short positions);
\item The sum of the weights (not necessarily 1 -- but tree-based models are long-only, fully-invested);
\item The turnover $\n{ w_{ \bigcdot, j} - w_{ \bigcdot, j-1} }_1$;
\item The overall historical importance
  $\n{ w_{ i \bigcdot } }_1$.
\end{compactitem}

** TODO LIME, LRP, Taylor, etc.
*** Layerwise relevance propagation, fine-tuning, attacks

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{The hidden dimensions of LLM alignment:\\
  a multi-dimensional analysis\\ of orthogonal safety directions}\\
  W. Pan et al.
\end{book}
Approximate the activation shift before and after fine-tuning
$T : \mathcal X \to \mathcal X$ with an affine function
\[ \operatorname*{Argmin}_{ w, b } \E_x \bigl\| T(x) - (Wx+b) \bigr\| ^2 \]
and interpret the first $k$ components
(right singular vectors)
of $W-I$ back to prompt tokens using layerwise relevance propagation (\texttt{lxt});
they are:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Sensitive topics;
\item Story-telling;
\item Porn and hate speech;
\item Real-world problems;
\item ``Start your response with `Sure, I am happy to help'{}''
\end{compactitem}
This suggests a few attacks:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Removing the non-dominant directions;
\item Removing trigger words (identified by LRP) by rephrasing the prompt.
\end{compactitem}

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{AttnLRP: Attention-aware layer-wise relevance propagation for transformers}\\
  R. Achtibat et al.
\end{book}
LRP can be generalized to transformers, in spite of the nonlinear operations
(softmax, matrix multiplication, layernorm, MoE: linearize them).

Implementation in \texttt{lxt}.

*** LIME variants

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Local rule-based explanations\\ of black box decision systems}\\
  R. Guidotti et al.
\end{book}
LoRE is a tree- and counterfactual-based variant of LIME:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Fit a decision tree on the output of the model on a synthetic neighbourhood of the point of interest;
  use a genetic algorithm to better explore the decision boundary;
\item Also give counterfactuals: features to change to change the output.
\end{compactitem}

*** Taylor

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Explaining nonlinear classification decisions with deep Taylor decomposition}\\
  G. Montavon et al.
\end{book}
To decompose a function $f: \RR^d \to \RR_+$ into
$f(x) = \sum_i R_i(x)$,
ideally such that $\forall i, x \ R_i(x) \geq 0$
(which implies $f(x)=0 \Rightarrow R(x) = 0$),
we can think of sensitivity analysis
\[ R(x) \propto
\left( \dfrac{ \partial f }{ \partial x } \right)^2
\] or the Taylor decomposition
\begin{align*}
  R(x) &= \left. \dfrac{ \partial f }{ \partial x } \right|_{ x = \tilde x } \odot (x - \tilde x ) \\
  \tilde x &= \operatorname*{Argmin}_{ f(\tilde x) = 0 } \n{ \tilde x - x }
\end{align*}
but finding the anchor point $\tilde x$
(e.g., an image with only the background and without the subject that $f$ tries to detect)
is expensive.

For a neural network, we can consider the mapping from the activations in one layer $\ell$
to the relevance of a neuron $j$ in the next layer $\ell+1$
\[ f_j^{ \ell \to \ell + 1 } : ( x_i^\ell)_i \longmapsto R_j^{\ell+1} \]
and apply the Taylor decomposition --
the root points $(x_{ij}^\ell)_i$ are easier to find.

The resulting relevance propagation rules are similar to the LRP ones,
but without hyperparameters to choose.

** TODO TO SORT
*** Sparsity, Discrete variables

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{SparseMAP:\\ differentialble sparse structured inference}\\
  V. Niculae et al. (2018)
\end{book}
The \emph{softmax} of $x \in \RR^n$ is the intersection of the half-line
$[0, \exp(x))$ with the probability simplex.

The \emph{sparsemax} is
the projection of $x$ onto the probability simplex.

The \emph{sparseMAP} generalizes this to other polytopes
(e.g., the permutahedron, the convex hull of permutation matrices).
\begin{align*}
  \text{softmax}\,\theta &= \argmax_{ y \in \Delta }
  \theta' y - \sum y_i \log y_i \\
  \text{sparsemax} \, \theta &=
  \argmax_{ y \in \Delta } \theta' y - \dfrac12 \n{ y }_2^2 \\
  &= \argmin_{ y \in \Delta } \n{ y - \theta }_2^2 \\
  \text{sparseMAP}_{M,N}(\eta) &= \argmax_{\substack{
      u \\
      u = My \\
      y \in \Delta \\
      \theta = [M \ N ] \eta
  }}
  \theta' y - \dfrac12 \n{ M y }_2^2
\end{align*}

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Efficient marginalization of discrete and structured latent variables via sparsity}\\
  G.M. Correia et al.
\end{book}
% Top-k sparsemax
\begin{align*}
  \text{sparsemax}(s) &= \argmin_{ \xi \in \Delta } \n{ \xi - s }_2^2 \\
  \text{sparsemax}_k(s) &= \argmin_{\substack{
      \xi \in \Delta \\
      \n \xi _0 \leq k
  }} \n{ \xi - s }_2^2 \\
  \text{sparseMAP}(s) &= \argmin_{ \xi \in \Delta } \n{ A \xi - s } _2^2
\end{align*}

*** Foundation models

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Sample, estimate, aggregate: a recipe\\ for causal discovery foundation models}\\
  M. Wu et al.
\end{book}
SEA is a supervised causal discovery model, combining
global statistics (e.g., $\Sigma^{-1}$)
with the output of causal discovery algorithms,
observational (PC, FCI, GES, LiNGAM, CAM)
or interventional (JCI, GIES, IGSP)
on subsets of the data (random subsets of observations, random subsets of \emph{connected} variables),
with a neural net: node embeddings,
edge type embeddings (the PC algorithm does not return a DAG but a CPDAG; other algorithms have even more edge types),
axial attention (attention along the rows, then along the columns, instead of 2-dimensional attention).

** TODO Causal Discovery: topological ordering
*** Causal discovery: topological order

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Score matching enables causal discovery\\ of nonlinear additive noise models}\\
  P. Rolland et al.
\end{book}
The Hessian of the data log-likelihood can be used to identify the leaves of the DAG, and find a topological order.

The nonlinear additive (Gaussian) noise model
\[ X_i = f_i\bigl( \text{Pa}_i(X) \bigr) + \epsilon_i \]
is identifiable: the DAG can be recovered from the joint distribution of $X$ or, equivalently, from the score function
\[ s(x) = \nabla_{\!x} \log p(x) . \]

From Stein's identity
\[ \E_{ \mathbf x \sim p } \bigl[
  \mathbf h(x) \nabla \log p(\mathbf x ) ^\top + \nabla \mathbf h( \mathbf x )
  \bigr] = 0 \]
we can estimate the score at the sample points
\begin{align*}
  G &= \bigl( s( x^1) , \dots, s(x^n) \bigr) \\
  \hat G &= - ( K + \eta I )^{-1} \langle \nabla , K \rangle \\
  K_{ij} &= \mathbf h ( \mathbf x_i )^\top \mathbf h ( \mathbf x_j ) = \kappa( \mathbf x_i, \mathbf x_j ) \quad \text{RBF kernel} \\
  \langle \nabla K \rangle _{ij} &= \sum_{ k=1 }^n \nabla_{\!x_j^k} \kappa( \mathbf x_i , \mathbf x_k ).
\end{align*}

The leaves of the DAG are exactly the variables $j$ satisfying
\[ \var \left[ \dfrac{ \partial s_j(\mathbf x ) }{ \partial x_j } \right] = 0 : \]
to find a topological order,
iteratively remove the nodes $j$ with the smallest $\var[ \partial x_j / \partial x_j ]$
(you need to recompute the score after each removal).

To compute the Jacobian of the score, use the second order Stein identity
\[ \E[ h \nabla s ] = \E[ \nabla^2 h - h s s' ]. \]

*** Causal discovery: topological order, diffusion

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Diffusion models for causal discovery\\ via topological ordering}\\
  P. Sanchez et al.
\end{book}
The Hessian of the data log-likelihood can be used to identify the leaves of the DAG, and find a topological order.

Instead of computing the Hessian with a second order Stein estimator
over an RBF kernel
(kernel methods scale poorly to a large number of observations),
DiffAn trains a neural net (diffusion -- denoising
uses score matching) to compute the score.
The model can be updated after each iteration (leaf removal) without retraining:
\[ \nabla \log p( \mathbf x_\ell) = \nabla \log p(x) - H_\ell \dfrac{ s_\ell }{ H_{\ell\ell} }. \]

*** Causal discovery: topological ordering

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{DAG learning on the permutahedron}\\
  V. Zantedeschi et al.
\end{book}
% Not clear
One can learn a DAG in two steps:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item First, find a topological ordering,
\item Then, find a DAG consistent with that ordering -- an upper triangular matrix.
\end{compactitem}
The \emph{permutahedron} is
\[ P_n = \text{Conv} \{ \rho^\sigma \ : \ \sigma \in \mathfrak S_n \} \]
where Conv is the convex hull,
\begin{align*}
  \rho &= [1,2,\dots,n] \\
  \rho^\sigma &= [ \sigma(1), \sigma(2), \dots, \sigma(n) ].
\end{align*}
The sorting operator
\[ \sigma(\theta) \in \argmax_{ \sigma \in \mathfrak S_n } \theta' \rho ^\sigma \]
can be relaxed to
\[ \mu(\theta) = \argmax_{ \mu \in P_n } \theta' \mu - \lambda \n{ \mu }_2^2 . \]

The combinatorial optimization problem
\[ \argmax_{ \alpha \in \Delta } s' \alpha \]
can be relaxed to the \emph{top-k sparsemax}
\[ \argmax_{\substack{
    \alpha \in \Delta \\ \n \alpha _0 \leq k
}} s' \alpha - \n{ \alpha }_2^2 \]
or the \emph{sparseMAP}, if $s$ has a low-dimensional parametrization $s = B' r$
\[ \argmax_{ \alpha \in \Delta } r' B \alpha - \n{ B \alpha }_2^2. \]

*** Topological ordering

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Constraint-free structure learning\\ with smooth acyclic orientations}\\
  R. Massidda et al.
\end{book}
\textsc{Cosmo} learns a DAG without an acyclicity constraint or penalty (like \textsc{Notears})
by parametrizing the adjacency matrix as
\[ A_{ij} = H_{ij} \sigma \left( \dfrac{ p_i - p_j }{ T } \right)  \]
where $H$ is an adjacency matrix, $p$ a ``priority'' vector
and the temperature $T$ decreases to 0.

*** Topological order

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Differentiable DAG sampling}\\
  B. Charpentier et al.
\end{book}
The \emph{Gumbel-Softmax} distribution is an approximation of the (one-hot-encoded) categorical distribution
\begin{align*}
  U &\sim \text{Cat}( p_1,\dots, p_k ) \\
  G_1 &\sim \text{Gumbel}(0) \\
  U_1 &\propto \exp \dfrac{ \log(p_i) + G_i }{ T } .
\end{align*}
The \emph{Gumbel-Sinkhorn} distribution, on the space of permutation matrices,
can be sampled as
\begin{align*}
  G_{ij} &\overset{\text{iid}}{\sim} \text{Gumbel}(0) \\
  \Pi &= \text{Sinkhorn}(\psi+G) \\
  \widehat\Pi &= \text{Hungarian}( \Pi )
\end{align*}
where the Sinkhorn operator iteratively normalize rows and columns.

The \emph{Gumbel-top-$n$} distribution can be sampled as
\begin{align*}
  G_i &\overset{\text{iid}}{\sim} \text{Gumbel}(0) \\
  \pi &= \text{sort}( \psi + G ) \in \mathfrak S_k .
\end{align*}


The \emph{softsort} operator is
\[
\text{softsort}_T(s) = \text{softmax}
- \dfrac{ \va{ \text{sort}(s) \mathbf 1' - \mathbf 1 s' } }{ T }
\in [0,1] ^{k \times k } \]
\[
\text{softsort}_T(s) \underset{T \to 0}{\longrightarrow}
\pi_{ \text{argsort}(s) } \in \mathfrak S _k
\]

To sample DAGs, first sample a topological order on the nodes
(Gumbel-Sinkhorn, or Gumbel-top-$n$ + sortsort),
then sample the edges (Gumbel softmax).

You can learn a DAG, end-to-end,
with variational inference (VI-SP-DAG).

** TODO Causal Discovery: neural nets
*** Causal discovery: notears and spectral radius

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Scaling structural learning with NoBears to\\ infer causal transcriptome networks}\\
  H.C. Lee et al. (2019)
\end{book}
A square matrix with nonnegative entries is acyclic iff
\[ \forall k \geq 1 \quad \tr A^k = 0 \]
(this leads to the \textsc{Notears} condition),
iff its only eigenvalue is zero, \ie, its spectral radius $\rho(A)$ is zero.
The spectral radius can be approximated as
\begin{align*}
  \llap{\ensuremath{
      5\times \left[
      \vphantom{
        \begin{aligned}
          u &\leftarrow \dfrac{ A' u }{ \n{ A' u }_2 } \\
          v &\leftarrow \dfrac{ Av }{ \n{ Av }_2 }
        \end{aligned}
      }
      \right.
  }}
  &\begin{aligned}
    u &\leftarrow \dfrac{ A' u }{ \n{ A' u }_2 } \\
    v &\leftarrow \dfrac{ Av }{ \n{ Av }_2 }
   \end{aligned}\\
  &\rho(A) \approx \dfrac{ u' A v }{ u' v }
\end{align*}
If the elements of the weight matrix $W$ can have arbitrary signs, use $A = W \odot W$.
To ensure differentiability, add $\epsilon = 10^{-6}$,
\[ \dfrac{ \partial \rho( W \odot W + \epsilon ) }{ \partial W_{ij} } = 2 \dfrac{ W_{ij} v_i u_j }{ u' v }. \]
To allow for nonlinear relations, use a polynomial loss
\[ L(W) = \sum_t \sum_i \left(
X_{it} - \sum_ { j \in \text{Pa}(i) } W_{ij} \sum_{ k = 0 }^3 \alpha_{jk} X_{jt}^k
\right)^2 . \]
To solve
\[ \operatorname*{Minimize}_W L(w) \ \text{ st } \ \rho(W) = 0, \]
the \emph{augmented Lagrangian} method iterates
\begin{align*}
  W &\leftarrow \argmin_W L(W) + \lambda \rho(W) + \dfrac\eta2 \rho(W)^2 \\
  \lambda &\leftarrow \lambda + \eta \rho(W) \\
  \eta &\leftarrow 1.1 \times \eta \text{ (optional)}.
\end{align*}
Implementation in \texttt{BNGPU}.

** TODO Causal Discovery: LLMs
*** Causal discovery, LLM to simulate the PC algorithm

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Can large language models\\ infer causation from correlation?}\\
  Z. Jin et al.
\end{book}
Can an LLM learn the PC algorithm, \ie, infer causation from correlations
(independence and conditional independence relations, expressed as text)?
No. Fine-tuning does not help much.

*** Causal discovery: LLM, topological ordering

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Causal inference using LLM-guided discovery}\\
  A. Vashishtha et al.
\end{book}
Do not ask the LLM to find the whole causal graph
(it would truggle to distinguish direct from indirect relations)
but only the partial order it induces (the \emph{transitive reduction},
aka Hasse diagram it induces).

You can compare DAGs with the $L^1$ distance betwen their transitive reductions.

Use a triplet prompt to infer this order:
provide 3 variables and ask for their causal graph.

Applications include
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Post-processing the output of the PC algorithm, to orient the remaining unoriented edges;
\item Constraints (transitive closure) for the PC or GES algorithms.
\end{compactitem}

*** Causal discovery: LLM

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Causal order: the key to leveraging\\ imperfect experts in causal inference}\\
  A. Vashishtha et al. (2024)
\end{book}
Building a causal graph by asking pairwise questions to an LLM creates spurious edges --
with pairwise queries alone, the LLM cannot distinguish between direct and indirect effects.

The not build a DAG, but just a \emph{partial order}: it can be used
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item To obtain a valid adjustment set (the ancestors of a node in a topological order);
\item As constraints or prior for classical causal discovery algorithms.
\end{compactitem}

Do not use pairwise queries, but triplet queries (either all triplets,
or $k=10$ triplets for each pair if the graph is large),
aggregated with a majority vote:
this creates fewer cycles.

The \emph{topological divergence} between a partial order $\preccurlyeq$
and a DAG defined by its adjacency matrix
is the number of edges incompatible with the order,
\[ D( {\preccurlyeq} , A ) = \sum_{ i \not\preccurlyeq j } A_{ij}. \]

*** Do LLMs know do-calculus?

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{CLadder: assessing causal reasoning\\ in language models}\\
  Z. Jin et al.
\end{book}
Dataset to check if LLMs already know do-calculus
and can answer queries on the three rungs of teh causality ladder
(association, intervention, counterfactuals),
without resorting to commonsense.

*** No, LLM
\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Causal reasoning and large language models: opening a new frontier for causality}\\
  E. K\i c \i man et al.
\end{book}
LLMs are good, but have unpredictable failures.

*** Causal discovery with interventions

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Differentiable causal discovery\\ from interventional data}\\
  P. Brouillard et al.
\end{book}
DCDI is a nonlinear NOTEARS for interventional data;
the log-likelihood is
\[ \prod_j p \bigl( x_j ; f_\theta( A_j \odot x ) \bigr) ^{1 - I_j}
p \bigl( x_j ; g_\theta( A_j \odot x ) ) ^{ I_j }
\]
where $A \in \{0,1\}^{d\times d}$ is the adjacency matrix,
$I \in \{0,1\}^d$ are the (unknown) interventions
and $p$ is Gaussian or a normalizing flow.

*** Causal discovery: Interventions

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Efficient neural causal discovery\\ without cyclicity constraints}\\
  P. Lippe et al.
\end{book}
Conditional distributions $X | \text{Pa}\,X$
from a causal graph are invariant under interventions
(except for the nodes intervened on).
The causal graph is the graph that generalizes best from observational to interventional data.

\textsc{Enco} separately models the conditional distributions
$ p_\phi( X_i | X_{-i} )$
and the edge presence and orientation
\begin{align*}
  & p_{ij} = \sigma( \gamma_{ij} ) \sigma( \theta_{ij} ) && \text{edge probability} \\
  & \gamma_{ij} && \text{edge presence} \\
  & \theta_{ij} = - \theta_{ji} && \text{edge orientation}
\end{align*}
by alternating distribution fitting
\[ \operatorname*{Maximize}_\phi \E_{ X \sim \text{data} }
\E_{\substack{
    M_{ij} \sim \text{Ber}(p_{ij}) \\
    \text{(dropout)}
}}
\log p_\phi( X_i | M_{-i} \odot X_{-i} )
\]
and graph fitting
\[ \operatorname*{Maximize}_{ \gamma, \theta}
\E_{\substack{
    I \sim \text{intervention} \\
    X \sim \text{data}_I \\
    M_{ij} \sim \text{Ber}\, p_{ij}
}}
\log p_\phi( X_i | M_{-i} \odot X_{-i} )
+ \lambda \sum_{ij} p_{ij} .
\]
Use \textsc{Reinforce} for the gradients (the adjacency matrix $M$ is discrete).

*** Causal Interventions, No

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Shaking the foundations: delusions in sequence models for interaction and control}\\
  P.A. Ortega et al.
\end{book}
In imitatation learning, the model takes its own actions as evidence about the wolrd:
this leads to self-delusin.
Actions are causal interventions:
do not model $P[O|A]$, but $P\bigl[O |\text{do}(A) \bigr]$.
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=1,yscale=1,inner sep=2pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline=0]
  \node[circle,draw,densely dotted,inner sep=1pt] (T) at (1,1) {$\theta$};
  \node[circle,draw,inner sep=1pt] (A) at (0,0) {$A$};
  \node[circle,draw,inner sep=1pt] (O) at (2,0) {$O$};
  \draw[->] (T) -- (A);
  \draw[->] (T) -- (O);
  \draw[->] (A) -- (O);
  \node[below=-1mm of A,font=\footnotesize,align=center] {expert\\actions};
  \node[below=-1mm of O,font=\footnotesize,align=center] {observations};
  \node[right=0mm of T,font=\footnotesize,align=left] {expert\\knowledge};
\end{tikzpicture}
\]

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{CUTS: neural causal discovery\\ from irregular time series data}\\
  Y. CHeng et al.
\end{book}
Neural Granger causal discovery with missing values:
jointly (alternatively) impute missing values and fit the DAG (no instantaneous effect)
\begin{align*}
  & s_{\tau i j } \sim \text{Ber} ( 1 - m_{\tau i j } ) &&\text{DAG} \\
  & \hat x_t = f_\phi( X \odot S ) \\
  &\tilde x_t \leftarrow (1-\alpha) \tilde x_t + \alpha \hat x_t && \text{imputed values} \\
  & m_{\tau i j } = \sigma ( \theta_{\tau i j } ) \\
  & \sigma: \text{ sigmoid} \\
  & \theta, \phi \text{: parameters to estimate}
\end{align*}
