*** Hawkes

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Causal structure learning in Hawkes processes with complex latent confounder networks}\\
  S. Jin and B. Huang
\end{book}
Rank conditions to detect unobserved confounders in multivariate Hawkes processes.

*** Causal discovery: extreme values

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Causal discovery in heavy-tailed models}\\
  N. Gnecco et al. (2020)
\end{book}
Extreme observations can be viewed as natural experiments --
they facilitate causal analysis.
The (positive) \emph{causal tail coefficient} between random variables $X_1$ and $X_2$ is
\[ \Gamma_{12} = \lim_{ u \to 1^- } \E\bigl[ F_2(X_2) \mid F_1(X_2) < u \bigr]. \]
It is close to 1 id $X_1$ causes $X_2$ (positively).

It can be estimated as
\begin{align*}
  \hat F_2(x) &= \dfrac1n \sum_{i=1}^n \mathbf 1_{ X_{ i2 \leq x } } \\
  \hat\Gamma_{12} &= \dfrac 1k \sum_{ i=1 } ^n \hat F_2( X_{i2} ) \mathbf 1 _{ X_{i1} > X_{n-k,1} }
\end{align*}

Extremal ancestor search (EASE, R implementation in \texttt{causalXtreme})
recovers a causal order on the variables by identifying the root as the node minimizing the score
\[ M_i = \max_{ j \neq i } \Gamma_{ji} . \]

To account for both positive and negative relations, use
\begin{align*}
  \psi_{12} &= \lim_{ u \to 1^- } \E\bigl[
              \sigma\bigl( F_2(X_2) \bigr) \mid \sigma\bigl( F_1(X_1)\bigr) > u
              \bigr] \\
  \sigma(x) &= \va{ 2x - 1 } \\
  \psi_{12} &= \dfrac12 ( \Gamma_{1,2} + \Gamma_{-1,2} )
\end{align*}

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Decomposition of dependence\\ for high-dimensional extremes}\\
  D. Cooley and E. Thibaud (2018)
\end{book}
A random vector $X$, with values in the positive orthant,
is \emph{multivariate regularly varying}
if its joint tail decays like a power function.
\begin{align*}
  & n \mathrm{P}[ b_n^{-1} X \in \bigcdot ] \overset{v}{\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}} \nu_X(\bigcdot) \\
  & b_n = L(n) n^{1/\alpha}, \ L\ \text{slowly varying} \\
  & C(r,B) = \left\{ x \ :\ \n x \geq r , \ \dfrac{ x }{ \n x } \in B \right\}
    , \ B \subset \mathbf S_{p-1}^+ \\
  & \nu_X \text{: angular measure} \\
  & \nu_X\bigl( C(r,b) \bigr) = r^{-\alpha} H_X(B), \ H_X \text{ measure on } \mathbf S_{p-1}^+ \\
  & \nu_X( dr \times dw ) = \alpha r^{ -\alpha-1} dr \, dH_X(w)
\end{align*}

The \emph{tail pairwise dependence matrix} of the random variable $X$ is
\begin{align*}
  \Sigma_{ij} &= \int w_i w_j d H_X(w) \\
  \hat\Sigma_{ij} &= \dfrac{ r_0^2 }{ N } \sum_{t=1}^N w_{ti} w_{tj} \mathbf 1_{ r_t > r_0 }.
\end{align*}

$(\RR_+^\times)^p$ can be made into a vector space, with \emph{transformed linear} operations.
\begin{align*}
  x_1 \oplus x_2 &= t( t^{-1} x_1 + ^{-1} x_2 ) \\
  a \circ x_1 &= t \bigl( a t^{-1}(x_1) \bigr) \\
  A \circ \mathbf x &= t\bigl( A t^{-1}(x) \bigr) \quad A \in \RR^{p \times q } \\
  \langle x_1, x_2 \rangle _{ (\RR_+^\times)^p } &= \bigl\langle t^{-1}(x_1), t^{-1}(x_2) \bigr\rangle_{ \RR^p } \\
  t(u) &= \log( 1 + e^u )
\end{align*}
Regular variation is preserved by transformed linear operations.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Separation-based causal discovery for extremes}\\
  J. Jiang et al.
\end{book}
To focus on extreme events, replace the linear operations in a structural causal model (SCM)
with transformed linear ones;
run the PC algorithm using tests on the (partial) tail pairwise dependence matrix.

*** Causal discovery: boosting

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Boosting differentiable causal discovery\\ via adaptive sample reweighting}\\
  A. Zhang et al.
\end{book}
Differentiable score-based causal discovery (e.g., \textsc{Notears}, Golem, GraN-DAG, GES)
relies too much on easy-to-fit samples:
\emph{ReScore} uses boosting
(fit the DAG, reweigh the samples, iterate).

*** Causal Discovery: DAG-GNN

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{DAG-GNN: DAG structure learning\\ with graph neural networks}\\
  Y. Yu et al. (2019)
\end{book}
One can sample from the linear structural causal model
$ X = AX + Z$, $Z \sim N(0,I)$ as
\[ X = (I-A)^{-1} Z , \quad Z \sim N(0,I). \]
This can be made nonlinear
\[ X = f_2 \bigl[ (I-A)^{-1} f_1(Z) \bigr] , \quad Z \sim N(o,I)  \]
and $f_1$, $f_2$ can be learned as an autoencoder
\begin{align*}
  X &= f_2 \bigl[ (I-A)^{-1} f_1(Z) \bigr] && \text{decoder} \\
  Z &= f_4 \bigl[ (I-A) f_2(Z) \bigr] && \text{encoder}.
\end{align*}
The paper uses a VAE.

*** Causal Discovery: GranDAG

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Gradient-based neural DAG learning }\\
  S. Lachapelle et al.
\end{book}
NOTEARS solves the optimization problem
\[ \operatorname*{Minimize}_U \operatorname*{Mean}_U \n{ X - XU }_{F}^2 + \lambda \n U _1 \text{ st } \operatorname{Tr} e^{ U \odot U } = d . \]
GraNDAG replaces the linear transformation $X \mapsto XU$ with a neural network,
and uses the neural network connectivity matrix
\[ C = \va{ W^{(L)} } \va{ W^{(L-1)} } \cdots \va{ W^{(1)} } \]
(where $\va W$ is the element-wise absolute value) for the constraint.

*** Causal Discovery: CAM

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{CAM: Causal additive models,\\ high-dimensional order search\\ and penalized regression}\\
  P. P\"uhlmann et al. (2014)
\end{book}
CAM is a nonlinear score-based causal discovery algorithm
which first looks for a topological order,
and only then, the causal graph:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item If there are too many variables (>30), select 10 candidate parents for each node with GAM boosting
  (preliminary neighbourhood selection);
\item Build a complete DAG with unpenalized GES,
  adding the edge improving the sum of squared residuals (of a GAM) the most;
\item Prune the DAG by looking at the $p$-values of the GAM terms
  (keep those with $p<10^{-3}$).
\end{compactitem}

*** Causal discovery: A*

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{A\!*** Lasso for learning a sparse Bayesian network structure for continuous variables}\\
  J. Xiang and S. Kim
\end{book}
GES is a greedy algorithm to find the DAG with the best score.
Exhaustive search is unreasonable: there are too many DAGs.
But the score is decomposable: the search can be formulated as a shortest path problem
in the subset lattice
(the value of a subset is the best score attainable with the variables in that subset)
which can be solved with dynamic programming.
The lasso loss gives a consistent A\!*** heuristic.

*** Causal Discovery: cGAN

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{A graph autoencoder approach\\ to causal structure learning}\\
  I. Ng et al. (2019)
\end{book}
NOTEARS is linear:
\[ \operatorname*{Minimize}_A \operatorname*{Mean}_i \n{ X_i - A X_i }^2 + \lambda \n A _1 \text{ st } \operatorname{Tr} e^{ A \odot A } = d ; \]
cGAN generalizes it to nonlinear relations
%\[ \operatorname*{Minimize}_A \operatorname*{Mean}_i \n{ X_i - g_2 \bigl( A g_1 (X_i) \bigr)  }^2 + \lambda \n A _1 \text{ st } \operatorname{Tr} e^{ A \odot A } = d \]
\[ \begin{array}{ll}
  \text{Find} & A \\
  \text{To minimize} & \operatorname*{Mean}_i \n{ X_i - g_2 \bigl( A g_1 (X_i) \bigr)  }^2 + \lambda \n A _1 \\[3pt]
  \text{Such that} & \operatorname{Tr} e^{ A \odot A} = d
\end{array} \]
where $g_1$ and $g_2$ are pointwise.

*** Causal Discovery: MCSL

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Masked gradient-based\\ causal structure learning}\\
  I. Ng et al. (2022)
\end{book}
MCSL combines NOTEARS and the Gumbel softmax approach to learn the binary adjacency matrix of a nonlinear structural model
\[ X_i = g_i ( A_{\bigcdot i } \odot X ) + \epsilon_1 \]

*** Causal Discovery: RL

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Ordering-based causal discovery\\ with reinforcement learning}\\
  X. Wang et al.
\end{book}
Use reinforcement learning to find, not directly the DAG,
but just a topological order compatible with it
(there are much fewer orders than DAGs):
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item State: embedding of the latest variable selected;
\item Action: variable to add;
\item Reward: BIC improvement;
\item Encoder: $\RR^{n\times d} \to \RR^{n \times d}$ (self-attention);
\item Decoder: LSTM;
\item Optimization: policy gradient, actor critic.
\end{compactitem}
Pretrain on simulated data; fine-tune on the data of interest.
\[ \text{BIC} = \sum_{jk} \log p\bigl( x_{jk} | \text{Pa}(x_{jk}); \theta_j \bigr) - \dfrac{ \va{ \theta_j } }2 \log m \]

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Causal discovery with reinforcement learning}\\
  S. Zhu et al.
\end{book}
Score-based causal discovery algorithms, such as GES, are greedy -- finding the best DAG is NP-hard.
Reinforcement learning is an alternative to greedy, exhaustive or random walk search:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item State: solution;
\item Action: new (nearby) solution;
\item Reward: score improvement.
\end{compactitem}
(You may need to tweak the reward to help the system learn, and you may want to look into variants of reinforcement learning optimizing
$\max_t R_t$ instead of $\sum_t \delta^t R_t$ -- \emph{risk-seeking reinforcement learning}.)
Here, the approach is slightly different:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item State: bootstrap sample;
\item Action: DAG;
\item Reward: $\text{score} + \lambda_1 \mathbf 1_{ \text{DAG} } + \lambda_2 \text{NOTEARS}$.
\end{compactitem}

*** Causal Discovery: VAR-LiNGAM

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Estimation of a structural vector autoregression model using non-Gaussianity}\\
  A. Hyv\"arinen et al.
\end{book}
The VAR model is
\[ X_t = \sum_{ k = 1 }^m B_k X_{t-k} + \epsilon_t . \]
The VAR-LiNGAM model also allows for contemporaneous effects, $B_0$, provided they are acyclic and the noise $\epsilon_t$ is non-Gaussian.
\[ X_t = \sum_{ k = 0 }^m B_k X_{t-k} + \epsilon_t  \]

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{The evolving causal structure\\ of equity risk factors}\\
  G. D'Acunto et al. (2021)
\end{book}
VAR-LiNGAM, on a moving window, on 11 equity risk factors, with the \texttt{lingam} Python package.

*** Causal discovery: low-rank factorization

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Large-scale differentiable causal discovery\\ of factor graphs}\\
  R. Lopez et al.
\end{book}
A factor DAG is a bipartite DAG,
where the two classes of nodes correspond to variables and factors, e.g.,
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=.2,yscale=.4,inner sep=1pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline=0]
  \begin{scope}[every node/.style={thick,circle,draw,inner sep=1.5pt}]
    \node (v1) at (0,4) {};
    \node (v2) at (2,4) {};
    \node (v3) at (4,4) {};
    \node (v4) at (6,4) {};
    \node (v5) at (3,2) {};
    \node (v6) at (2,0) {};
    \node (v7) at (4,0) {};
  \end{scope}
  \begin{scope}[every node/.style={thick,draw,inner sep=2pt}]
    \node (f1) at (1,3) {};
    \node (f2) at (5,3) {};
    \node (f3) at (3,1) {};
  \end{scope}
  \draw[->] (v1) -- (f1);
  \draw[->] (v2) -- (f1);
  \draw[->] (v3) -- (f2);
  \draw[->] (v4) -- (f2);
  \draw[->] (v5) -- (f3);
  \draw[->] (f1) -- (v5);
  \draw[->] (f2) -- (v5);
  \draw[->] (f3) -- (v6);
  \draw[->] (f3) -- (v7);
  \node at (3,-1) {$A$};
\end{tikzpicture}
\qquad\qquad
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=.2,yscale=.4,inner sep=1pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline=0]
  \begin{scope}[every node/.style={thick,circle,draw,inner sep=1.5pt}]
    \node (v1) at (0,4) {};
    \node (v2) at (2,4) {};
    \node (v3) at (4,4) {};
    \node (v4) at (6,4) {};
    \node (v5) at (3,2) {};
    \node (v6) at (2,0) {};
    \node (v7) at (4,0) {};
  \end{scope}
  \begin{scope}[every node/.style={thick,draw,inner sep=2pt}]
    \node (f1) at (1,3) {};
    \node (f2) at (5,3) {};
    \node (f3) at (3,1) {};
  \end{scope}
  \draw[->] (v1) -- (f1);
  \draw[->] (v2) -- (f1);
  \draw[->] (v3) -- (f2);
  \draw[->] (v4) -- (f2);
  \draw[->] (v5) -- (f3);
  \node at (3,-1) {$U$};
\end{tikzpicture}
\qquad\qquad
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=.2,yscale=.4,inner sep=1pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline=0]
  \begin{scope}[every node/.style={thick,circle,draw,inner sep=1.5pt}]
    \node (v1) at (0,4) {};
    \node (v2) at (2,4) {};
    \node (v3) at (4,4) {};
    \node (v4) at (6,4) {};
    \node (v5) at (3,2) {};
    \node (v6) at (2,0) {};
    \node (v7) at (4,0) {};
  \end{scope}
  \begin{scope}[every node/.style={thick,draw,inner sep=2pt}]
    \node (f1) at (1,3) {};
    \node (f2) at (5,3) {};
    \node (f3) at (3,1) {};
  \end{scope}
  \draw[->] (f1) -- (v5);
  \draw[->] (f2) -- (v5);
  \draw[->] (f3) -- (v6);
  \draw[->] (f3) -- (v7);
  \node at (3,-1) {$V$};
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
Its adjacency matrix has a low-rank factorization,
$A = U \diamond V$, where $\diamond$ is the boolean matrix product,
and $U$ and $V$ are the adjacency matrices of edges towards factor (resp. variable) nodes.
DCD-FG is a low-rank DCID.

*** Causal discovery: A*

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Learning optimal Bayesian networks:\\ a shortest path perspective}\\
  C. Yuan and B. Malone (2013)
\end{book}
One can find the causal graph minimizing
\[ \text{Score}(G) = \sum_i \text{Score} ( x_i, \text{Pa}_i ) \]
with dynamic programming, adding successor nodes (leaves)
one by one
(finding a path from $\nothing$ to $V$
in the ''order graph'' -- the Hasse diagram of the nodes)
\begin{align*}
  \text{Score} (V) &= \min_{ X \in V } \left[
    \text{Score} \bigl( X \setminus \{X\} \bigr) +
    \text{BestScore} \bigl( X , V \setminus \{X\} \bigr)
    \right] \\
  \text{BestScore}(X,V) &= \min_{ P \subset V } \text{Score} (X, P )
\end{align*}
or with A*, with a heuristic relaxing the acyclicity constrain
\[ h(U) = \sum_{ X \in V \setminus U } \text{BestScore}\bigl( X , V \setminus \{ X \} \bigr)  \]
(better heuristics exist).
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=1,yscale=1.5,inner sep=2pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline=0]
  \node (V) at (0,1) {$V$};
  \node (X) at (0,0) {$V \cup \{X\}$};
  \draw (V) to node[anchor=west]{$\text{BestScore}(X,V)$} (X);
\end{tikzpicture}
\]

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Learning optimal Bayesian networks\\ using A*** search}\\
  C. Yuan et al. (2011)
\end{book}

*** Causal Discovery: deterministic relations

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{On causal discovery\\ in presence of deterministic relations}\\
  L. Li et al.
\end{book}
Deterministic relations create independence
and wreak havoc in constraint-based causal discovery algorithms:
for instance,
if $X \to Y$ is deterministic,
then $y$ is constant when conditioned on $X$, and therefore independent from everything:
$ *** \indep Y \,|\, X$.
Similarly,
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=1,yscale=1,inner sep=2pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline=0]
  \node (H) at (0,1) {height};
  \node (W) at (2,1) {weight};
  \node (B) at (1,0) {BMI};
  \draw[->] (H) -- (B);
  \draw[->] (W) -- (B);
  \node[anchor=west] at (3,1)  {$*\indep \text{height} \ |\  \text{MBI}, \text{weight}$};
  \node[anchor=west] at (3,.5) {$*\indep \text{weight} \ |\  \text{MBI}, \text{height}$};
  \node[anchor=west] at (3,0)  {$*\indep \text{BMI} \ |\  \text{height}, \text{weight}$.};
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
GES can be modified to account for deterministic relations:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Identify deterministic variables (by looking at the variance of the residuals of regressions)
  and ignore independence relations coming from determinism;
\item Use a (RKHS) ridge-adjusted BIC as score
  (because the variance matrix is singular in presence of determinism).
\end{compactitem}

*** Causal discovery, GES and misspecification

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Generalized score functions\\ for causal discovery}\\
  B. Huang et al. (2018)
\end{book}
Using linear models in GES can introduce spurious relations, e.g., in
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=1.1,yscale=.8,inner sep=2pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline=0]
  \node (x1) at (0,2) {$X_1$};
  \node (x2) at (0,1) {$X_2$};
  \node (x3) at (0,0) {$X_3$};
  \draw[->] (x1) -- (x2);
  \draw[->] (x2) -- (x3);
  \draw[->,densely dotted] (x1) to[out=-60,in=60] (x3);
  \begin{scope}[shift={(-4,0)}]
    \node[anchor=west] at (0,2) {$X_1 = \epsilon_1$};
    \node[anchor=west] at (0,1) {$X_2 = \smash{X_1 + X_1^2 + \epsilon_2}$};
    \node[anchor=west] at (0,0) {$X_3 = \smash{X_2 + X_2^2 + \epsilon_3}$};
  \end{scope}
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
the influence of $X_1$ on $X_3$ cannot be blocked by a linear function of $X_2$,
and can miss relations, e.g.,
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=1.1,yscale=.8,inner sep=2pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline=0]
  \node (x1) at (0,1) {$X_1$};
  \node (x2) at (0,0) {$X_2$};
  \draw[->] (x1) -- (x2);
  \begin{scope}[shift={(-4,0)}]
    \node[anchor=west] at (0,1) {$X_1 = \epsilon_1$};
    \node[anchor=west] at (0,0) {$X_2 = \smash{(\sin X_1 + \epsilon_2)^2}$};
  \end{scope}
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
Use a kernel conditional independence test
to rule out potential parents of a node.

For the score function, use the
(ridge-regularized) CV-log-likelihood or marginal likelihood of an RKHS regression
(regression on
\[ \begin{bmatrix} \kappa(x_1,x) \\ \vdots \\ \kappa( x_n, x ) \end{bmatrix} \in \RR^n \]
instead of $\phi(x) \in \mathscr H$).

*** Causal discovery: DynoTEARS (SVAR + NOTEARS)

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{DynoTears:\\ structure learning from time series data}\\
  R. Pamfil et al. (2020)
\end{book}
VAR model, with acyclicity constraint and sparsity penalty.

*** PC algorithm: other conditional independence tests

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Score matching through the roof:\\ linear, nonlinear, and latent variables\\ causal discovery}\\
  F. Montagna et al.
\end{book}
The score function $\nabla \log p$
can be used to test for conditional independence:
\[ X \indep Y \,|\, Z \ \Longleftrightarrow\
\dfrac{ \nabla^2 }{ \nabla X \nabla Y } \log p(X, Y, Z ) = 0. \]
This leads to a generalization of the PC and PCI algorithms: AdaScore.

*** Notears

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Truncated matrix power iteration\\ for differentiable DAG learning}\\
  Z. Zhang et al.
\end{book}
% TMPI
The \textsc{Notears} consraint
\[ h(A) = \tr( e^A) = \tr \sum_{ k \geq 1 } \dfrac1{k!} A^k \]
can be generalized to
\[ h(A) = \sum_{k=1}^d c_k A^k, \ c_k > 0 \]
(where $d$ is the number of nodes).
To avoid numerical explosion, we are tempted to choose
$c_k \to 0$,
but a geometric series
\[ h(A) = \sum_{k=1}^d A^k \]
still works, and has more informative (\ie, non-vanishing) gradients.

*** Causal Discovery, SEM

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{semopy: a Python package\\ for structural equation modeling}\\
  G. Meschcheryakov and A.A. Igolkina (2019)
\end{book}
A \d{structural equation model} (SEM) is a model of the form
\begin{align*}
  \eta &= B \eta + \epsilon && \text{latent} \\
  y &= \Lambda \eta + \delta && \text{observed}
\end{align*}
where the sparsity structure of $B$ and $\Lambda$
(and of $\var \epsilon$, $\var \delta$) is known, e.g.,
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=1,yscale=.5,inner sep=0pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline={(0,.3)}]
  \node (y1) at (0,8) {$y_1$};
  \node (y2) at (0,6) {$y_2$};
  \node (y3) at (0,4) {$y_3$};
  \node (y4) at (3,2) {$y_4$};
  \node (y5) at (3,0) {$y_5$};
  \node (y6) at (4,2) {$y_6$};
  \node[circle,thick,draw=blue!50!black,fill=blue!10,inner sep=1pt] (e1) at (1,6) {$\eta_1$};
  \node[circle,thick,draw=blue!50!black,fill=blue!10,inner sep=1pt] (e2) at (1,4) {$\eta_2$};
  \node[circle,thick,draw=blue!50!black,fill=blue!10,inner sep=1pt] (e3) at (2,1) {$\eta_3$};
  \node[circle,thick,draw=blue!50!black,fill=blue!10,inner sep=1pt] (e4) at (3.5,4) {$\eta_4$};
  \node[thick,draw=blue!50!black,fill=blue!10,inner sep=2pt] (x1) at (1,2) {$x_1$\vrule height .65em depth 0pt width 0pt};
  \node[thick,draw=blue!50!black,fill=blue!10,inner sep=2pt] (x2) at (1,0) {$x_2$\vrule height .65em depth 0pt width 0pt};
  \node[thick,draw=blue!50!black,fill=blue!10,inner sep=2pt] (x3) at (2,4) {$x_3$\vrule height .65em depth 0pt width 0pt};
  \node[thick,draw=blue!50!black,fill=blue!10,inner sep=2pt] (x4) at (5,4) {$x_4$\vrule height .65em depth 0pt width 0pt};
  \node[thick,draw=blue!50!black,fill=blue!10,inner sep=2pt] (x5) at (6,4) {$x_5$\vrule height .65em depth 0pt width 0pt};
  \draw[->] (e1) -- (y1);
  \draw[->] (e1) -- (y2);
  \draw[->] (e1) -- (y3);
  \draw[->,thick,blue!50!black] (e1) -- (x3);
  \draw[->] (e2) -- (y3);
  \draw[->,thick,blue!50!black] (e2) -- (x3);
  \draw[->] (e3) -- (y4);
  \draw[->] (e3) -- (y5);
  \draw[->] (e4) -- (y4);
  \draw[->] (e4) -- (y6);
  \draw[->,thick,blue!50!black] (e4) -- (x4);
  \draw[->,thick,blue!50!black] (x1) -- (e3);
  \draw[->,thick,blue!50!black] (x1) -- (x3);
  \draw[->,thick,blue!50!black] (x2) -- (e3);
  \draw[->,thick,blue!50!black] (x3) -- (e4);
  \draw[->,thick,blue!50!black] (x4) to[out=140,in=40] (x3);
  \draw[->,thick,blue!50!black] (x4) -- (x5);
  \draw[<->,thick,purple!80!blue!60] (e2) to [out=-120,in=120] (x2);
  \draw[<->,thick,purple!80!blue!60] (y5) to[out=0,in=-90] (y6);
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
It can be estimated by comparing the sample variance matrix of the observed variables, $S = \var y$,
with that of the model, $\Sigma_\theta$, e.g.,
\begin{align*}
  \ell(\theta) &= \n{ \Sigma_\theta - S }_F^2 \\
  \ell(\theta) &= \n{ I - \Sigma_\theta S^{-1} }_F^2 \\
  \ell(\theta) &= \tr( S \Sigma_\theta^{-1} ) + \log \det \Sigma _\theta \qquad \text{(Wishart)}.
\end{align*}

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{semopy 2: a structural equation modeling package with random effects in Python}\\
  G. Mosccheryakov et al. (2021)
\end{book}
A \emph{structural equation model} (SEM) is a linear model
\[ x = A x + \epsilon \]
where some of the variables are not observed (latent)
and the sparsity structure of $A$ and $\var \epsilon$ is known.
There are special cases:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item A \emph{path analysis} (PA) model only has observed variables
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=1,yscale=.5,inner sep=0pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline={(0,.3)}]
  \node[thick,draw=blue!50!black,fill=blue!10,inner sep=2pt] (x1) at (0,1) {$x_1$\vrule height .65em depth 0pt width 0pt};
  \node[thick,draw=blue!50!black,fill=blue!10,inner sep=2pt] (x2) at (1,1) {$x_2$\vrule height .65em depth 0pt width 0pt};
  \node[thick,draw=blue!50!black,fill=blue!10,inner sep=2pt] (x3) at (2,1) {$x_3$\vrule height .65em depth 0pt width 0pt};
  \node[thick,draw=blue!50!black,fill=blue!10,inner sep=2pt] (x4) at (3,2) {$x_4$\vrule height .65em depth 0pt width 0pt};
  \node[thick,draw=blue!50!black,fill=blue!10,inner sep=2pt] (x5) at (3,0) {$x_5$\vrule height .65em depth 0pt width 0pt};
  \node[thick,draw=blue!50!black,fill=blue!10,inner sep=2pt] (x6) at (4,2) {$x_6$\vrule height .65em depth 0pt width 0pt};
  \node[thick,draw=blue!50!black,fill=blue!10,inner sep=2pt] (x7) at (4,0) {$x_7$\vrule height .65em depth 0pt width 0pt};
  \draw[->,thick,blue!50!black] (x1) -- (x2);
  \draw[->,thick,blue!50!black] (x2) -- (x3);
  \draw[->,thick,blue!50!black] (x3) -- (x4);
  \draw[->,thick,blue!50!black] (x3) -- (x5);
  \draw[->,thick,blue!50!black] (x4) -- (x6);
  \draw[->,thick,blue!50!black] (x5) -- (x7);
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
\item A \emph{confirmatory factor analysis} (CFA) model
  has no relations between the latent variables
  and between observed variables,
  just relations from latent to observed variables,
  and covariance between latent variables
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=1,yscale=.5,inner sep=0pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline={(0,.3)}]
  \node (y1) at (0,0) {$y_1$};
  \node (y2) at (1,0) {$y_2$};
  \node (y3) at (2,0) {$y_3$};
  \node (y4) at (3,0) {$y_4$};
  \node (y5) at (4,0) {$y_5$};
  \node (y6) at (5,0) {$y_6$};
  \node (y7) at (6,0) {$y_7$};
  \node[circle,thick,draw=blue!50!black,fill=blue!10,inner sep=1pt] (e1) at (1.5,2) {$\eta_1$};
  \node[circle,thick,draw=blue!50!black,fill=blue!10,inner sep=1pt] (e2) at (4.5,2) {$\eta_2$};
  \draw[->] (e1) -- (y1);
  \draw[->] (e1) -- (y2);
  \draw[->] (e1) -- (y3);
  \draw[->] (e1) -- (y4);
  \draw[->] (e2) -- (y4);
  \draw[->] (e2) -- (y5);
  \draw[->] (e2) -- (y6);
  \draw[->] (e2) -- (y7);
  \draw[<->,thick,purple!80!blue!60] (e1) to [out=30,in=150] (e2);
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
\end{compactitem}
Historically, SEMs distinguished between:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Observed variables (non-leaves: $x_i$);
\item Indicators (leaves: $y_i$).
\end{compactitem}

\emph{Exploratory factor analysis} (EFA) looks for a latent structure explaining the observed variables.
A heuristic approach could be:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Compute the correlations $(\rho_{ij})_{1 \leq i,j\leq m}$
  and the corresponding distances $D_{ij} = 1 - \va{\rho_{ij}}$;
\item Run the OPTICS clustering algorithm,
  with \texttt{min\_cluster\_size=2}, to estimate the number $k$ of latent factors;
\item Compute a sparse PCA (SPCA) and keep the first $k$ components;
\item Refine the CFA by fitting the SEM and dropping the coefficients with a large $p$-value.
\end{compactitem}

*** Causal discovery: DAG learning

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{DAGs with no curl:\\ an efficient DAG structure learning approach}\\
  Y. Yu et al.
\end{book}
Given a set of vertices $V$, consider alternating functions on vertices, edges and triangles
\begin{align*}
  L^2(V) &= \{ f : V \to \RR \} \\
  L^2_\wedge(E) &= \{ Y: Y \times Y \to \RR \ :\ \forall i,j \ Y(i,j) = - Y(j,i) \} \\
  L^2_\wedge(T) &= \{ \Theta: V \times V \times V \to \RR \ :\
  \forall i,j,k \ \Theta(i,j,k) = - \Theta(j,i,k) = - \Theta( i,k,j)
  \}
\end{align*}
and define gradient, divergence and Laplacian
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,scale=2.6,inner sep=1pt,outer sep=2pt,every node/.style={anchor=base}]
  \node (A) at (0,0) {$L^2(V)$};
  \node (B) at (1,0) {$L^2_\wedge(E)$};
  \node (C) at (2,0) {$L^2_\wedge(T)$};
  \draw[->] ([yshift=+.6pt]A.east) to node[anchor=south,outer sep=0pt]{\footnotesize grad} ([yshift=+.6pt]B.west);
  \draw[->] ([yshift=-.6pt]B.west) to node[anchor=north,outer sep=0pt]{\footnotesize $\text{grad}^*=\text{div}$} ([yshift=-.6pt]A.east);
  \draw[->] ([yshift=+.6pt]B.east) to node[anchor=south,outer sep=0pt]{\footnotesize curl} ([yshift=+.6pt]C.west);
  \draw[->] ([yshift=-.6pt]C.west) to node[anchor=north,outer sep=0pt]{\footnotesize $\text{curl}^*$} ([yshift=-.6pt]B.east);
  \draw[->] (A) to[out=-120,in=-60,looseness=4] node [anchor=north east, pos=.4, outer sep=-2pt] {\footnotesize $\Delta_0$} (A);
  \draw[->] (B) to[out=-120,in=-60,looseness=4] node [anchor=north east, pos=.4, outer sep=-2pt] {\footnotesize $\Delta_1$} (B);
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
\begin{align*}
  & ( \grad p )(ij) = p(j) - p(i) \\
  & ( \grad^*** Y)(i) = - \sum_j Y(i,j) \\
  & \div Y = - \grad^*** Y \\
  & (\curl Y)(i,j,k) = Y(i,j) + Y(j,k) + Y(k,i) \\
  & (\curl^*** \Theta) (i,j) = \sum_k \Theta(i,j,k) \\
  & \Delta_0 = \grad^*** \grad \\
  & \Delta_1 = \grad \grad^*** + \curl^*** \curl
\end{align*}
Note that
\begin{align*}
  \curl \grad &= 0 \\
  \grad^*** \curl^*** &= 0.
\end{align*}

Any alternating edge function $Y$ can be decomposed as
\[ Y = \grad \phi + \curl^*** \Psi + H \]
where $\Delta_1 H = 0$, $\curl H = 0$, $\div H = 0$;
$\phi$ can be recovered as
\begin{align*}
  \phi &= - \Delta_0^\dagger \div Y \\
  \phi(d) &= 0 \\
  \Delta_0 &=
\left[\!\!\!
\begin{tikzpicture}[baseline={([yshift=-.8ex]current bounding box.center)},outer sep=0pt]
\matrix (m) [
  matrix of math nodes,
  nodes in empty cells,
  row sep={2.5ex,between origins},column sep={4ex,between origins},
  ampersand replacement=\&% <= to change col separator for align env
]{
  d-1 \&\&\&\& 0 \\
  \&\& -1 \\
  \& -1 \\
  \&\&\& d-1 \\
  0 \&\&\&\& 0 \\  % Do not forget the \\ on the last line!
};
\draw (m-1-1) -- (m-4-4);
\draw (m-1-5) -- (m-5-5);
\draw (m-5-1) -- (m-5-5);
\end{tikzpicture}
\!\!\!\right]
\end{align*}

Weighted adjacency matrices of DAGs are exactly matrices of the form
$A = W \odot \text{ReLU}( \grad p )$,
where $W$ is skew symmetric;
the vector $p$ defines a topological order on the graph
(it is a complicated way of saying that $A$ is defined by a topological order and an upper triangular matrix).

We could use that parametrization to find a DAG from data,
but the problem is non-convex and restarts do not help much.

Instead, use
\begin{align*}
  A &= \argmin_A F(A,X) + \lambda h(A) \\
  \intertext{(with $\lambda=10^3$ instead of NOTEARS's $\lambda=10^{16}$)}
  C(A)_{ij} &= 1 \text{ iff there is a directed path from $i$ to $j$} \\
  p &= - \Delta_0^\dagger \div \dfrac12 \bigl( C(A) - C(A)^\top \bigr) \\
  W_{ij} &= \begin{cases}
    0 & \text{if } p_i = p_j \text{ or } A_{ij} = A_{ji} = 0 \\
    \dfrac{ A_{ij} }{ p_j - p_i } & \text{if } A_{ij} \neq 0 \text{ and } A_{ji} = 0 \\
    \dfrac{ A_{ji} }{ p_j - p_i } & \text{if } A_{ij} = 0 \text{ and } A_{ji} \neq 0
  \end{cases} \\
  A &= W \odot \text{ReLU}( \grad p ) .
\end{align*}
You can refine the solution
\[ W = \argmin_W F\bigl( W \odot \text{ReLU}( \grad p ) , X \bigr). \]

[Note that this removes \emph{all} edges involved in a cycle --
that may be too much: I would remove one, or remove the weakest.]

*** Causal discovery

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Causal discovery for observational sciences using supervised machine learning}\\
  A.H. Petersen et al.
\end{book}
Traditional causal discovery algorithms are sequential
(errors accumulate) and focus on sparse graphs
(PC tests for small separating sets;
GES starts with an empty graph) --
but, in some domains, e.g., epidemiology, causal graphs are dense.

Train, on simulated linear Gaussian data,
a CNN (sic) to predict the CPDAG adjacency matrix (not a DAG) from the correlation matrix
(truncate the predicted probabilities, and remove weak edges until you get a CPDAG).

*** Causal discovery: latent factors

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{From causal to concept-based\\ representation learning}\\
  G. Rajendran et al.
\end{book}
With no additional assumptions or data, we cannot recover
latent factors $Z$ from observed data $X = f(Z)$.
If $Z$ is a causal representaion , and if we have interventions an all latent variables $Z_j$,
it is identifiable -- but that is a lot of interventions.
A \emph{concept} is a projection $AZ$ of the latent factors;
concepts are identifiable through conditioning.

*** Causal discovery, unobserved confounders, proxies

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Deep proxy causal learning and its application to confounded bandit policy evaluation}\\
  L. Xu et al. (2024)
\end{book}
PCL estimates causal effects in presence of unobserved confounders using proxies.
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=1,yscale=1,inner sep=2pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline=0]
  \node (Z) at (-1,1) {$Z$};
  \node (W) at (3,1) {$W$};
  \node (A)  at (0,0) {$X$};
  \node (Y)  at (2,0) {$Y$};
  \node[circle,draw,densely dotted,inner sep=0pt] (U) at (1,1) {$U$};
  \draw[<->] (Z) -- (A);
  \draw[->] (U) -- (Z);
  \draw[->] (U) -- (A);
  \draw[->] (U) -- (Y);
  \draw[<->] (U) -- (W);
  \draw[->] (A) -- (Y);
  \draw[->] (W) -- (Y);
  \node[below=-1mm of A,font=\footnotesize] {treatment};
  \node[below=-1mm of Y,font=\footnotesize] {outcome};
  \node[above=-.5mm of U,font=\footnotesize,align=center] {unobserved\\confounder};
  \node[above=-.5mm of Z,font=\footnotesize,align=center] {treatment-\\inducing\\\smash{proxy}};
  \node[above=-.5mm of W,font=\footnotesize,align=center] {outcome-\\inducing\\\smash{proxy}};
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
\begin{align*}
  & \text{ATE}(a) = \E_U \bigl[ \E_Y [ Y \,|\, A = a , U ] \bigr] \\
  & h \text{ s.t. } \E\bigl[ Y \,|\, A = a , Z = z \bigr] = \int h(a,w) \rho_W( w | A = a , Z = z ) dw  \\
  & \text{ATE}(a) = \E_W \bigl[ h(a,W) \bigr]
\end{align*}
The ``bridge function'' $h$
can be found with 2-stage regression.

Sensitivity analysis answers the question

*** Book (statistics)

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Causal inference:\\ a statistical learning approach}\\
  S. Wager (2024)
\end{book}

The book only considers \emph{binary} treatments.
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=1,yscale=1,inner sep=1pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline=0]
  \node (X) at (1,1) {$X$};
  \node (W) at (0,0) {$W$};
  \node (Y) at (2,0) {$Y$};
  \draw[->] (X) -- (W);
  \draw[->] (X) -- (Y);
  \draw[->] (W) -- (Y);
  \node[below=-1mm of W,font=\footnotesize] {treatment};
  \node[below=-1mm of Y,font=\footnotesize] {outcome};
  \node[right=-1mm of X,font=\footnotesize] {confounders};
\end{tikzpicture}
\]

% 1. RCT
\textbf{1.}
We want to estimate the ATE
$\tau = \E[ Y^{(1)} - Y^{(0)} ].$
The \emph{difference-in-means} estimator, for RCTs,
\[ \hat \tau _{\text{DM}} = \operatorname*{Mean}_{ W_i = 1 } Y_i - \operatorname*{Mean}_{ W_i = 0 } Y_i  \]
is unbiased and asymptotically Gaussian.
Adding causes of $Y$
reduces the variance of $\hat\tau$, even if the model is misspecified.
Use a linear regression
\[ Y \sim \alpha + W \tau + X \beta \]
or, better, an \emph{interacted linear regression}.
\begin{align*}
  & Y \sim \alpha + W \tau + X \beta + W X \gamma \\
  &\hat \tau_{\text{IREG}} = \hat\tau_{\text{DM}} + \bar X \hat\gamma
\end{align*}

% 2. Unconfoundedness and the propensity score
\textbf{2.}
We now assume \emph{unconfoundedness}, \ie, there are no unobserved confounders.
In presence of a discrete confounder (for instance, if you have two RCTs)
use a \emph{stratified estimator}
$\hat\tau_{\text{STRAT}}$: the average of the difference-in-means estimator in each stratum.

Since the \emph{propensity score}
\[ e(x) = \P[ W = 1 | X = x ] \]
is a ``sufficient confounder''
\[ \{ Y^{(0)}, Y^{(1)} \} \indep W \,|\, e(X) \]
you could stratify using a discretization (of an estimator) of the propensity score.

The propensity score can be used to compute the \emph{inverse propensity weighted} estimator
\[ \hat\tau_{\text{IPW}} = \operatorname*{Mean}_i \left[
  \dfrac{ W_i Y_1 }{ \hat e(X_i) } -
  \dfrac{ (1-W_i) Y_1 }{ 1-\hat e(X_i) } -
  \right] \]
and its \emph{oracle} variant, $\hat\tau^*_{\text{IPW}}$ (which uses the true propensity score $e$ instead of an estimator $\hat e$).

With continuous covariates $\hat\tau^*_{\text{IPW}}$ is Gaussian, but not efficient.
With discrete propensity scores, $\hat\tau_{\text{IPW}} = \hat\tau_{\text{STRAT}}$ is better than $\hat\tau^*_{\text{IPW}}$.

The \emph{self-normalized} IPW estimator is
\[ \hat\tau_{\text{IPW}} =
\dfrac{ \sum W_i Y_1 / \hat e_i }{ \sum W_i / \hat e_i } -
\dfrac{ \sum (1-W_i) Y_1 / (1-\hat e_i) }{ \sum (1-W_i) / (1-\hat e_i) }
\]

% 3. Doubly robust methods
\textbf{3.} We have two simple estimators of the ATE $\tau$:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item IPW;
\item Controlling for confounders
  \[ \hat\tau_{\text{REG}} = \operatorname*{Mean}_i \hat\mu_{(1)}(X_i) - \hat\mu_{(0)}(X_i) . \]
\end{compactitem}
They may be combined: first control for confounders, then use IPW on the residuals:
\begin{align*}
  \hat\tau_{\text{AIPW}} = \dfrac1m \sum_{ i=1 }^n \left[ \vphantom{ \dfrac{ Y_i - \hat\mu_{(1)}(X_i) }{ \hat e(X_i) } } \right.
    &
  \hat\mu_{(1)}(X_i) - \hat\mu_{(0)}(X_i) \\
  & {} + W_i \dfrac{ Y_i - \hat\mu_{(1)}(X_i) }{ \hat e(X_i) } \\
  & {} + \left. (1-W_i) \dfrac{ Y_i - \hat\mu_{(0)}(X_i) }{ 1-\hat e(X_i) }
  \right].
\end{align*}
%\[ \hat\tau_{\text{AIPW}} = \dfrac1m \sum_{ i=1 }^n \left[
%  \hat\mu_{(1)}(X_i) - \hat\mu_{(0)}(X_i)
%  + W_i \dfrac{ Y_i - \hat\mu_{(1)}(X_i) }{ \hat e(X_i) }
%  + (1-W_i) \dfrac{ Y_i - \hat\mu_{(0)}(X_i) }{ 1-\hat e(X_i) }
%  \right].
%\]
This \emph{augmented IPW} estimator satisfies:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Weak double robustness: if either $\hat e$ or $\hat\mu$ is consistent, then $\hat\tau$ is consistent;
\item Strong double robustness: if they are both consistent and converge at rates $n^{-\alpha_1}$ et $n^{-\alpha_2}$,
  $\alpha_1+\alpha_2>\tfrac12$, then $\hat\tau_{\text{AIPW}}$ is consistent and converges at rate
  $n^{-\alpha_1-\alpha_2}$, \ie, faster than $1/\sqrt n$.
\end{compactitem}

\emph{Double machine learning} (DML) combines doubly robust (DR) estimators and \emph{cross-fitting}
($\hat\mu$ and $\hat\epsilon$ are estimated and evaluated on different subsets of the data):
in particular, it uses \emph{honest residuals}.

AIPW and stratification (if $X$ is discrete) have the same variance; it is the best possible -- they are efficient.

% 4. Estimating heterogeneous treatment effects
\textbf{4.} The \emph{T-learner}, which separately estimates $\hat\mu_{(1)}$ and $\hat\mu_{(0)}$,
and then computes $\hat\tau = \hat\mu_{(1)} - \hat\mu_{(0)}$, is biased,
in particular in small unbalanced samples,
because $\hat\mu_{(1)}$ and $\hat\mu_{(0)}$ end up with different regularizations (e.g., one could end up with a straight line,
because there is not enough data for a more complex model,
while the other would be allowed more variation).

The \emph{semi-parametric model}
\begin{align*}
  \tau(x) &= \psi(x) \cdot \beta \qquad \psi \text{: known features} \\
  Y_i(w) &= \mu_o(X_i) + w \psi(X_i) \beta + \epsilon_i(w)
\end{align*}
can be estimated by running a \emph{residual-on-residual} regression:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Fit non-parametric models
  \begin{align*}
    m(X) &= \E[ Y | X ] \\
    e(X) &= \E[ W | X ];
  \end{align*}
\item Compute cross-fit residuals
  $\tilde Y_i = Y_i - \hat m (X_i) $
  and transformed features
  $\tilde Z_i = \psi(X_i) \bigl[ W_i - \hat e(X_i) \bigr]$;
\item Finish with a linear regression $\tilde Y \sim \tilde Z$.
\end{compactitem}

The \emph{R-learner} uses a nonlinear model to learn $\psi$.

\textbf{5.}
Estimating the ATE is not the end goal:
we want to know which subjects to treat, \ie, we want a \emph{policy}
\[ \pi: \mathscr X \longrightarrow \{0,1\}. \]
The \emph{value} of a policy $\pi$ is
\[ V(\pi) = \E \bigl[ Y \bigl( \pi(X) \bigr) \bigr]. \]
To estimate this value, you could use IPW or regression
\begin{align*}
  \hat V_{\text{IPW}}(\pi) &= \dfrac1n \sum_i \dfrac{
    \mathbf 1_{ W_i = \pi(X_i) } - Y_i
  }{
    \P\bigl[ W = \pi(X) \,|\, X = X_i \bigr]
  } \\
  \hat V_{\text{REG}}(\pi) &= \dfrac1n \sum_i \hat\mu _{ \pi(X_i) } ( X_i )
\end{align*}
or, better, AIPW (with cross-fitting)
\begin{align*}
\hat V_{\text{AIPW}}(\pi) &= \dfrac1n  \sum_i
\hat\mu _{ \pi(X_i) } ( X_i ) + \\
&\hspace*{-5mm}\dfrac{
    \mathbf 1_{ W_i = \pi(X_i) } - Y_1
  }{
    \P\bigl[ W = \pi(X) \,|\, X = X_i \bigr]
}
\bigl( Y_i - \hat\mu_{\pi(X_i)}( X_i ) \bigr).
\end{align*}
%\[
%\hat V_{\text{AIPW}}(\pi) = \dfrac1n  \sum_i
%\hat\mu _{ \pi(X_i) } ( X_i ) +
%\dfrac{
%    \mathbf 1_{ W_i = \pi(X_i) } - Y_1
%  }{
%    \P\bigl[ W = \pi(X) \,|\, X = X_i \bigr]
%}
%\bigl( Y_i - \hat\mu_{\pi(X_i)}( X_i ) \bigr).
%\]

The policy can be defined by a \emph{priority function}
$S : \mathscr X \longrightarrow \RR$,
assigning treatment to the top $q$th fraction of units.

The \emph{QINI curve} plots the benefit of treating the top $q$th fraction of units
\[ \Delta ( \pi_S^q) = \Delta( \pi_S^q, 0) = V(\pi_{S^q}) - V(0) \]
versus $q$.

The \emph{TOC curve} considers the benefit of treating the top $q$th fraction minus that of treating people at random,
\[ q^{-1} \Delta( \pi_S^q) - \Delta(1) \sim q. \]

Empirical welfare maximization (maximizing $\hat V$ -- we do not know $V$) can be formulated as a weighted classification problem,
forecasting the sign of
\begin{align*}
  \hat\Gamma_i &=
  \hat\mu_{(1)}(X_i) - \hat\mu_{(0)}(X_i) \\
  &\hphantom{{}=} + \dfrac{ W_i }{ \hat e(X_i) } \bigl( Y_1 - \hat\mu_{(1)}(X_i) \bigr) \\
  &\hphantom{{}=} + \dfrac{ 1-W_i }{ 1-\hat e(X_i) } \bigl( Y_1 - \hat\mu_{(0)}(X_i) \bigr)
\end{align*}
\[ \hat\Gamma_i =
\hat\mu_{(1)}(X_i) - \hat\mu_{(0)}(X_i)
+ \dfrac{ W_i }{ \hat e(X_i) } \bigl( Y_1 - \hat\mu_{(1)}(X_i) \bigr)
+ \dfrac{ 1-W_i }{ 1-\hat e(X_i) } \bigl( Y_1 - \hat\mu_{(0)}(X_i) \bigr)
\]
(the policy may be good, even if the classifier is bad, in particular in low signal-to-noise situations).
Some of the features of $X$ may be problematic:
difficult to measure in production, gamable, or protected.


% 6. Adaptive experiments (MAB)
\textbf{6.}
For \emph{adaptive experiments} (multiarm bandits, MAB),
prefer Thompson sampling to UCB.
Since adaptive data collection yields non iid data,
the naive means is biased. We can consider weighted means,
with $w=1$, $w=1/e$, or $w=1/\sqrt e$.
\begin{align*}
  \hat\mu_{(k)}^{\text{AVG}} &= \dfrac1{ n_{k,T} } \sum_{t=1}^T \mathbf1_{ W_t = k } Y_t \\
  \hat\mu_{(k)}^{\text{IPW}} &= \dfrac1{ T } \sum_{t=1}^T \dfrac{ \mathbf1_{ W_t = k } }{ e_{t,k} } Y_t \\
  \hat\mu_{(k)}^{\text{AW}} &= \left.
  \sum_{t=1}^T \dfrac{ \mathbf1_{ W_t = k } }{ \sqrt{ e_{t,k} } } Y_t
  \middle/
  \sum_{t=1}^T \dfrac{ \mathbf1_{ W_t = k } }{ \sqrt{ e_{t,k} } }
  \right.
\end{align*}
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item $\hat\mu^{\text{AVG}}$ is biased downwards and non-Gaussian;
\item $\hat\mu^{\text{IPW}}$ is unbiased but still non-Gaussian;
\item $\hat\mu^{\text{AW}}$ is unbiased and asymptotically Gaussian.
\end{compactitem}

You cannot have good in-sample regret,
and good confidence intervals (\ie, good out-of-sample regret).

% 7. Balancing estimators
\textbf{7.}
The propensity score tries to balance out pre-treatment confounders
\[ \E \left[ \dfrac{ W }{ e(X) } \psi(X) \right] =
\E \left[ \dfrac{ 1-W }{ 1-e(X) } \psi(X) \right] =
\E \left[ \psi(X) \right].
\]
To achieve \emph{sample balance}, we can choose a few basis functions $\psi_j$,
and look for $\hat e_{(1)}$ and $\hat e_{(0)}$ such that
\begin{align*}
  \dfrac1n \sum_i \dfrac{ W_i }{ \hat e_{(1)}( X_i ) } \psi_j(X_i) &\approx \dfrac1n \sum_i \psi_j(X_i) \\
  \dfrac1n \sum_i \dfrac{ 1-W_i }{ 1 - \hat e_{(0)}( X_i ) } \psi_j(X_i)&\approx \dfrac1n \sum_i \psi_j(X_i) \\
\end{align*}
(we end up with two propensity scores, for $W=0$ and for $W=1$).
For instance, for linear models
$\mu_{(w)}(X)=X\cdot\beta_{(w)}$,
$e_{(w)}(X) = ( 1 + e^{-X \cdot \theta_{(w)} })^{-1}$.
These become convex optimization problems and the
\emph{covariate-balancing propensity score}
\[
\hat\tau =
\dfrac1n \sum_i \dfrac{ W_i }{ \hat e_{(1)}(X_i) } Y_i -
\dfrac1n \sum_i \dfrac{ 1 - W_i }{ 1 - \hat e_{(1)}(X_i) } Y_i
\]
is $\sqrt n$-consistent and asymptotically normal.

To generalize this to a high-dimensional non-parametric context:
\[ \begin{array}{ll}
  \text{Find} & t \geq 0, \ \gamma_i^{(1)} \geq 0 \\[1mm]
  \text{To minimize} & \displaystyle t^2 + \dfrac1n \sum_i \gamma_i^{(1)} {}^2  \\
  \text{Such that} & \displaystyle \forall j \ \va{ \dfrac1n \sum_i ( \gamma_i^{(1)} W_i - 1 ) X_i } \geq t
\end{array} \]
(and similarly for $\gamma_i^{(0)}$).
This leads to the AIPW-like \emph{augmented balancing} estimator
\[ \hat\tau_{\text{AB}} = \dfrac1n X_i ( \hat\beta_{(1)} - \hat\beta_{(0)} )
+ W_i \hat \gamma_i ^{(1)} ( Y_i - X_i \hat\beta_{(1)} )
- (1-W_i) \hat \gamma_i ^{(0)} ( Y_i - X_i \hat\beta_{(0)} )
\]
(where $\hat\beta_{(1)}$ and $\hat\beta_{(0)}$ come from the lasso).

% 8. Regression discontinuity designs
\textbf{8.}
If the treatment assignment is deterministic,
$W = \mathbf 1_{ Z \geq c}$
(for instance, the passing score in education,
the disease severity threshold determining the treatment in medicine,
voting (c=50\%), etc.)
we can expect the treatment is as good as random if $Z$ is in the vicinity of $c$.

We cannot use IPW because $W$ is deterministic: $e=0$ or $1$,
so we cannot divide by $e$ or $1-e$. Instead, use
\[ \tau = \lim_{ z \to c^+ } \E[ Y | Z = z ] -\lim_{ z \to c^- } \E[ Y | Z = z ], \]
which can be estimated with a local broken line regression
\[ Y \sim W + (Z-c)_+ + (Z-c)_- \]
(with weights decreasing as $Z$ moves away from $c$).
But this does not easily generalize to discrete running variables $Z$ and more complex cut-off functions.
Local regression is actually not optimal, but it is ``linear'', in the sense that
$\hat\tau = \sum \gamma Y_i$ (where the $\gamma_i$'s depend on $Z_i$),
and the optimal linear estimator is the solution of a convex problem;
% unclear
this also applies to discrete $Z$, or multivariate $Z$ with
$W = \mathbf 1_{ Z \in \mathscr A}$, $\mathscr A \subset \RR^k$.
(All this requires, however, a known bound on
$d^2 \mu_{(w)}(z) / dz^2$.)

% 9. Causal inference with endogenous treatments
\textbf{9.}
If there are unobserved confounders, use do-calculus.
For instance, an \emph{instrumental variable} (IV).
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=1,yscale=1,inner sep=1pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline=0]
  \node[thick,circle,draw,dotted] (U) at (1,1) {$X$};
  \node (W) at (0,0) {$W$};
  \node (Y) at (2,0) {$Y$};
  \node (Z) at (-2,0) {$Z$};
  \draw[->] (U) -- (W);
  \draw[->] (U) -- (Y);
  \draw[->] (W) -- (Y);
  \draw[->] (Z) -- (W);
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
In particular, for linear models,
\[ \tau = \dfrac{ \cov(Y,Z) }{ \cov(W,Z) } = \dfrac{ \cov(Y,Z) / \var Z }{ \cov(W,Z) / \var Z } = \dfrac{ \beta_{Y,X} }{ \beta_{W,Z} } \]
(provided the denominator is non-zero).

More generally, if $Z$ is high-dimensional, for any function $w: \mathscr Z \to \RR$,
\[ \tau = \dfrac{ \cov\bigl[ Y, w(Z) \bigr] }{ \cov\bigl[ W, w(Z) \bigr] } \]
(provided the denominator is non-zero, \ie, $w(Z)$ nudges the treatment).
The \emph{optimal instrument} is
\[ w^*(z) = \E[ W | Z = z ]; \]
estimate it with \emph{cross-fitting}.

The linearity assumption
$Y = \alpha + W \tau + \epsilon$
can be relaxed to an additivity assumption
$Y = g(W) + \epsilon$ (but not easily).

% 10. Local averate treatment effect (LATE)
\textbf{10.}
Without the linearity assumption, we can still compute
\[ \tau_{\text{LATE}} = \dfrac{ \cov[Y,Z] }{ \cov[W,Z] } \]
(latent average treatment effect):
is is a weighted treatment effect.
For instance, if $Z$ and $W$ are both binary (e.g., to model treatment compliance in a RCT):
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=2,yscale=1,inner sep=1pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline=0]
  \node (Z) at (0,0) {$Z$};
  \node (W) at (1,0) {$W$};
  \node (Y) at (2,0) {$Y$};
  \draw[->] (Z) to node[anchor=south,pos=.5,font=\footnotesize]{nudge} (W);
  \draw[->] (W) -- (Y);
  \node[below=-1mm of Z,align=center,font=\footnotesize] {treatment\\[-.5mm]assignment};
  \node[below=-1mm of W,align=center,font=\footnotesize] {actual\\[-.5mm]treatment};
  \node[below=-1mm of Y,font=\footnotesize] {outcome};
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
and if there are no defiers (monotonicity:
the treatment assignment $Z$ nudges the actual treatment $W$),
then
\[ \tau_{\text{LATE}} = \E[ Y(1) - Y(0) \mid \text{complier} ]. \]
Since different instruments may induce different compliance patterns, each has its own LATE.

We can also compute the LATE in \emph{equilibrium models}
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=2,yscale=.8,inner sep=1pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline=0]
  \node (Z) at (0,0) {$Z$};
  \node (P) at (1,0) {$P$};
  \node (S) at (2,1) {$S$};
  \node (Q) at (2,-1) {$Q$};
  \draw[->] (Z) to[out=45,in=180] (S);
  \draw[->] (Z) -- (P);
  \draw[->] (P) -- (S);
  \draw[->] (P) -- (Q);
  \draw[double equal sign distance] (S) -- (Q);
  \node[right=-1mm of S,font=\footnotesize] {supply};
  \node[right=-1mm of Q,font=\footnotesize] {demand};
  \node[below=-1mm of Z,font=\footnotesize] {weather};
  \node[below=-1mm of P,font=\footnotesize,align=center] {price\\of fish};
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
or in \emph{threshold crossing models}.
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=1.3,yscale=1,inner sep=1pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline=0]
  \node (Z) at (0,0) {$Z$};
  \node (c) at (1,1) {$c(Z)$};
  \node (W) at (2,0) {$Z=\mathbf 1_{ U \geq c(Z)}$};
  \node (U) at (3,1) {$U$};
  \node (Y) at (4,0) {$Y$};
  \draw[->] (Z) -- (c);
  \draw[->] (c) -- (W);
  \draw[->] (U) -- (W);
  \draw[->] (W) -- (Y);
  \node[below=-1mm of Z,font=\footnotesize] {tuition};
  \node[below=-1mm of W,align=center,font=\footnotesize] {attend\\[-.8mm]college};
  \node[below=-1mm of Y,align=center,font=\footnotesize] {lifelong\\[-.8mm]income};
  \node[above=-1mm of U,align=center,font=\footnotesize] {utility of\\[-.8mm]attending\\[-.8mm]college};
  \node[above=-1mm of c,align=center,font=\footnotesize] {cost of\\[-0.8mm]attending\\[-0.8mm]college};
\end{tikzpicture}
\]

\begin{align*}
  \tau(u) &= \E\bigl[ Y(1) - Y(0) | U = u ] \quad \text{marginal treatment effect} \\
  \tau\bigl( c(z) \bigr) &= \dfrac{
    \dfrac{ d }{ dz \strut} \E [ Y | Z = z ]
  }{
    \dfrac{ d \strut}{ dz } \P[ W=1 | Z = z ]
  }
\end{align*}

% 11. Spillovers and interference
\textbf{11.}
In presence of potential \emph{spillovers}
(the SUTVA assumption is no longer satisfied),
we no longer have two potential outcomes,
$Y(1)$ and $Y(0)$,
but $2^n$ of them, $Y(\mathbf w)$, $\mathbf w \in \{0,1\}^n$.

Assume that $Y_i$ only depends on $\mathbf w$
through an \emph{exposure} function
$H_i: \{0,1\}^n \to \mathscr H$, e.g.,
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item $H_i(\mathbf w) = (w_j)_{ j \in C_i}$, where $C_i$ is the cluster containing $i$;
\item $H_i(\mathbf w) = (w_j)_{j \in \{i\} \union N_i}$,
  where $N_i$ are the neighbours of $i$ in a social graph;
\item $H_i(\mathbf w) = (w_i,z_i)$, where
  $z_i = \operatorname*{Mean}_{j\in N_i} w_j$ is the fraction of friends treated.
\end{compactitem}

To test for the presence of causal effects
\begin{align*}
  H_0 &: \text{ no causal effect} \\
  H_1 &: \text{ causal effect with no spillover}
\end{align*}
use a permutation test (scramble the treatment variable) with the statistic
\[ T = \va{
  \operatorname*{Mean}_{ W_i = 1 } Y_i -
  \operatorname*{Mean}_{ W_i = 0 } Y_i
} \]
(there are better test statistics).

To test for interference,
\begin{align*}
  H_0&\text{: SUTVA} \\
  H_1&\text{: Anonymous network interference},
\end{align*}
choose a set of \emph{focal units}
$\mathscr F \subset \llbracket 1, n \rrbracket$
and use the statistic
\[ T = \beta( Y \sim Z \,|\, i \in \mathscr F , \ W_i = 0 ) \]
where $Z_i = \operatorname*{Mean}_{ j \in N_i } W_j$
is the proportion of neighbours treated,
with permutations preserving the actual treatment on the focal set.

% 12. Estimating treatment effects under interference
\textbf{12.} After assessing the presence of interference, we want to quantify it
\[ \tau( h, h') = \operatorname*{Mean}_i Y_i(h') - Y_i(h). \]
This can be done with IPW
\[ \hat\tau_{\text{IPW}} = \operatorname*{Mean}_i \bigl( \Gamma_i(h') Y_i - \Gamma_i(h) Y_i \bigr) \]
where the weights are
\[ \Gamma_i(h) = \dfrac{ \mathbf 1_{ H_1(\mathbf w) = h } }{ e_i(h) } \]
or, better, self-normalized IPW;
$\hat\tau_{\text{SIPW}}$ is asymptotically Gaussian and we can compute an upper bound on its variance.

% 13. Event study designs
\textbf{13.}
In an \emph{event study}, all units start in the control state
and, after some time, some start the treatment, either all at the same time (\emph{block adoption}),
or not (\emph{staggered adoption}).

For block adoption, the \emph{difference-in-means}
\[ \hat\tau_{\text{DM}} = \operatorname*{Mean}_{\substack{ D = 1 \\ t > H }} Y_{it} -
\operatorname*{Mean}_{\substack{ D = 0 \\ t > H }} Y_{it} \]
completely ignores the pre-treatment period;
the \emph{difference-in-differences} (DiD) estimator
\begin{align*}
  \hat\tau_{\text{DiD}} &=
  \operatorname*{Mean}_{ D_i = 1 } \left( \operatorname*{Mean}_{t>H} Y_{it} - \operatorname*{Mean}_{t\leq H} Y_{it} \right) \\
  &\hphantom{={}}
  \operatorname*{Mean}_{ D_i = 0 } \left( \operatorname*{Mean}_{t>H} Y_{it} - \operatorname*{Mean}_{t\leq H} Y_{it} \right)
\end{align*}
compares the jump (after minus before) in the treated to that in the non-treated.
In a non-randomized trial, under the \emph{parallel trends} assumption,
\[ \forall \, i, t \qquad \E\bigl[ Y_{it}(0) - Y_{i1}(0) \bigr] = \beta_t \]
it is an unbiased estimator of the ATT.

In the staggered case, the ATT is
\[ \text{ATT} = \operatorname*{Mean}_{\substack{ D_i = 1 \\ t > H_1 }} Y_{it}(H_i) - Y_{it}(\infty) \]
where $H_i$ is the treatment start time.
It can be estimated with an ``\emph{average saturated regression}''.  % Not clear

From the parallel trends assumption
\[ \forall \, t \leq H \qquad \operatorname*{Mean}_{ D_i = 0 } Y_{it} \approx \alpha + \operatorname*{Mean}_{ D_i = 1 } Y_{it}, \]
we can use a weighted mean (regression)
to better match the control and treated groups
\[ \forall \, t \leq H \qquad \sum_{ D_i = 0 } \gamma_i Y_{it} \approx \alpha + \operatorname*{Mean}_{ D_i = 1 } Y_{it}  \]
and estimate the ATT with a weighted DiD (\emph{synthetic DiD}).
\begin{align*}
  \hat\tau_{\text{SDiD}} &=
  \operatorname*{Mean}_{ D_i = 1 } \left( \operatorname*{Mean}_{t>H} Y_{it} - \operatorname*{Mean}_{t\leq H} Y_{it} \right) - {} \\
  &\hphantom{={}}
  \sum_{ D_i = 0 } \gamma_i \left( \operatorname*{Mean}_{t>H} Y_{it} - \operatorname*{Mean}_{t\leq H} Y_{it} \right)
\end{align*}

% Evaluating dynamic policies
\textbf{14.}
We do not only want to find if the treatment is valuable, or for whom it is,
but also when to start and stop it the treatment (medical, financial (quantitative easing), etc.).
With a dynamic policy, the treatment $W_{it}$ depends on the past covariates $(X_{is})_{s \leq t}$.
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=1.3,yscale=1,inner sep=1pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline=0]
  \begin{scope}[every node/.style={blue!70!black}]
    \node (X1) at (0,1) {$X_{i1}$};
    \node (X2) at (1,1) {$X_{i2}$};
    \node (X3) at (2,1) {$\cdots$};
    \node (XT) at (3,1) {$X_{iT}$};
    \node[above=1mm of X3,font=\footnotesize] {state};
  \end{scope}
  \begin{scope}[every node/.style={red!60!black}]
    \node (Y) at (4,1) {$Y$};
    \node[above=1mm of Y,font=\footnotesize] {outcome};
  \end{scope}
  \begin{scope}[every node/.style={green!50!black}]
    \node (W1) at (0,0) {$W_{i1}$};
    \node (W2) at (1,0) {$W_{i1}$};
    \node (W3) at (2,0) {\vphantom{$W_{i1}$}};
    \node (WT) at (3,0) {$W_{i1}$};
    \node[below=-1mm of W3,font=\footnotesize] {treatment};
  \end{scope}
  \draw[->] (X1) -- (X2);
  \draw[->] (X2) -- (X3);
  \draw[->] (X3) -- (XT);
  \draw[->] (XT) -- (Y);
  \draw[->] (X1) -- (W1);
  \draw[->] (X2) -- (W2);
  \draw[->] (XT) -- (WT);
  \draw[->] (W1) -- (X2);
  \draw[->] (W2) -- (X3);
  \draw[->] (WT) -- (Y);
\end{tikzpicture}
\]

The value of a policy $\pi$ is
$ V(\pi) = \E \bigl[ Y(\pi) \bigr]$.
We will assume sequential unconfoundedness
\[ X_{i, t+1} \indep W_{it} \,|\, X_{it}. \]
In particular, there can be treatment-confounder feedback.
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=2,yscale=.5,inner sep=1pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline=0]
  \node (H0) at (0,0) {healthy};
  \node (H1) at (1,1) {healthy};
  \node (S1) at (1,-1) {sick};
  \node (H2) at (2,1) {healthy};
  \node (S2) at (2,-1) {sick};
  \draw[->] (H0) -- (H1);
  \draw[->] (H0) -- (S1);
  \draw[->] (H1) -- (H2);
  \draw[->] (H1.south east) -- (S2);
  \draw[->] (S1) -- (H2);
  \draw[->] (S1) -- (S2);
  \node[below=-1mm of H0,font=\scriptsize] {$P(W)\!=\!.3$};
  \node[below=-1mm of H1,font=\scriptsize] {$P(W)\!=\!.4$};
  \node[below=-1mm of S1,font=\scriptsize] {$P(W)\!=\!.8$};
  \draw[->] (-.3,-2.2) -- (2.5,-2.2);
  \node at (0, -2.5) {\footnotesize$t=0$};
  \node at (1, -2.5) {\footnotesize$t=1$};
  \node at (2, -2.5) {\footnotesize final outcome};
  \node[anchor=west] at (2.5,-2.2) {\footnotesize time};
\end{tikzpicture}
\]

Stratification does not work: the strata we would like to have
(how each patient would react to each sequence of treatments)
are not observable,
but we can use IPW
\begin{align*}
  \hat V_{\text{IPW}} (\pi) &= \dfrac1n \sum_i \gamma_{iT} (\pi) Y_i \\
  \gamma_{it} (\pi) &= \gamma_{i, t-1} (\pi) \cdot \dfrac{
    \mathbf 1_{ W_t = \pi_t( X_{it} ) }
  }{
    \P \bigl[ W_t = \pi_t(X_{it}) | X_{it} \bigr]
  }
\end{align*}
or SIPW, or AIPW (aka DR).

% 15. Markov decision processes
\textbf{15.}
In particular, the DR estimator can be applied to MDPs.

In a \emph{switchback} experiment, we repeatedly switch the treatment on and off, each time waiting for the system to re-equilibrate
(either a fixed amount of time, or a random, memory-less one).

% 16. Exercises (not read)

*** Book/Workshop: Causal Inference

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Causal inference in R workshop}\\
\end{book}
To estimate causation from observational data
with a known causal graph
(no unmeasured confounders, and $\forall x \ P[ T=1 | X=x ] \neq 0, 1$),
one can:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Adjust for confounders;
\item Fit a propensity model $T \sim X$,
  and use inverse propensity weights (IPW), e.g.,
  \[ w_{\text{ATE}} = \dfrac{ T_i }{ p_i } - \dfrac{ 1 - T_i }{ 1 - p_i } \]
  (there are other similar formulas for ATT, ATC, ATM, ATO, etc.);
\item Use the propensity score to match treated and control samples (\texttt{MatchIt}).
\end{compactitem}
These are estimators of the same quantity.
Use the bootstrap for the confidence intervals
(after reweighting or matching, the observations are no longer independent).

Correlation is sometimes causation (RCT, A/B testing),
but even in these cases,
you may want to adjust for the confounders or use IPW:
the resulting estimators are more efficient.
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=.6,yscale=.7,inner sep=0pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline={(0,.3)}]
  \node (T) at (0,0) {$T$};
  \node (X) at (1,1) {$X$};
  \node (Y) at (2,0) {$Y$};
  \draw[->] (X) -- (T);
  \draw[->] (T) -- (Y);
\end{tikzpicture}
\]

To graphically assess the propensity scores:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Histogram of the propensity scores for $T=0$ and $T=1$,
  before and after reweighting;
\item ``Love plot'', $i \sim \text{smd}_i$,
  before and after reweighting, where the standardized mean difference (smd) for variable $i$ is
  \[ \text{smd} = \dfrac{ \bar x_{ T=1 } - \bar x_{ T = 0 } }{ \sqrt{
      \dfrac{ s^2_{T=1} + s^2_{T=0} }2
    } }
  \]
\item ecdf, and weighted acdf.
\end{compactitem}

\emph{G-computation} (G-formula),
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Fits a model $y \sim T + X$;
\item Computes $\widehat{ \text{ATE} } = \left. \hat y \right|_{T=1} - \left. \hat y \right|_{T=0}$
  (using the model on copies of the data with $T:=0$, resp, $T:=1$, for all the observations)
\end{compactitem}

For continuous exposures, one can:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Fit a linear model $T \sim X$ and convert the residuals into probabilities
  \[ p = \phi \biggl( \dfrac{ T - \hat T }{ \hat \sigma } \biggr) \]
\item or bin $T$ into quantiles and use IPW.
\end{compactitem}

\emph{Tipping point sensitivity analysis} (\texttt{tipr}) quantifies
unmeasured confoundedness.

To address \emph{selection bias},
fit a probability of censoring model,
and use inverse probabilities as weights (multiply the probabilities, if you have several such models).
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=2,yscale=1,inner sep=0pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline={(0,.3)}]
  \node[align=center] (A) at (.2,2) {New HIV drug};
  \node[align=center] (B) at (1,1) {Symptoms};
  \node[align=center] (C) at (1.8,2) {Underlying\\ HIV severity};
  \node[align=center] (D) at (3,2) {CD4 count\mathstrut};
  \node[align=center,draw=blue!70!black, inner sep=2pt] (E) at (1,0) {loss to followup};
  \draw[->] (A) -- (B);
  \draw[->] (C) -- (B);
  \draw[->] (C) -- (D);
  \draw[->] (B) -- (E);
  \draw[->,color=blue!50!black] (A) to[out=40,in=140] node[anchor=south,inner sep=1pt,font=\footnotesize\color{blue!50!black}] {relation of interest} (D);
  \node[right=1mm of E,align=left,font=\footnotesize\color{blue!50!black}] {we condition\\on a descendant of\\a collider};
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=1.5,yscale=1,inner sep=0pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline={(0,.3)}]
  \node[align=center] (A) at (0,0) {Smoking};
  \node[align=center] (B) at (1,1) {Reckless\\behaviour};
  \node[align=center] (C) at (2,0) {Broken\\bones};
  \node[align=center,draw=blue!70!black, inner sep=2pt] (D) at (3,-1) {Hospitalization};
  \node[align=center] (E) at (4,0) {Glioma};
  \draw[->] (B) -- (A);
  \draw[->] (B) -- (C);
  \draw[->] (C) -- (D);
  \draw[->] (E) -- (D);
  \node[right=1mm of D,align=left,font=\footnotesize\color{blue!50!black}] {we condition\\on a collider};
  \draw[->,color=blue!50!black] (A) to[out=90,in=90,looseness=1.5] node[anchor=south,inner sep=1pt,font=\footnotesize\color{blue!50!black}] {relation of interest} (E);
\end{tikzpicture}
\]

*** Book: Causal inference and discovery (Molak)

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Causal inference and discovery in Python}\\
  A. Molak (2023)
\end{book}
\textbf{6.}
\texttt{dowhy} provides three ways of computing causal effects:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Backdoor criterion (aka controlling for confounders):
  the control set should not contain any descendant of $X$ and should block all paths with an arrow into $X$;
\item Front-door criterion (known, unobserved confounder, and observed mediator)
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=1,yscale=.7,inner sep=4pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline=0]
  \node[thick,circle,draw,dotted] (Z) at (1,1) {};
  \node[thick,circle,draw] (T) at (0,0) {};
  \node[thick,circle,draw] (M) at (1,0) {};
  \node[thick,circle,draw] (Y) at (2,0) {};
  \draw[->] (Z) -- (T);
  \draw[->] (Z) -- (Y);
  \draw[->] (M) -- (Y);
  \draw[->] (T) -- (M);
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
\item Intrumental variable (IV)
\[ % Instrumental variable
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=1,yscale=.7,inner sep=4pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline=0]
  \node[thick,circle,draw,dotted] (Z) at (1.5,1) {};
  \node[thick,circle,draw] (T) at (0,0) {};
  \node[thick,circle,draw] (M) at (1,0) {};
  \node[thick,circle,draw] (Y) at (2,0) {};
  \draw[->] (Z) -- (M);
  \draw[->] (Z) -- (Y);
  \draw[->] (M) -- (Y);
  \draw[->] (T) -- (M);
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
\end{compactitem}
For more general do-calculus, check \texttt{grapl-causal}.

\textbf{7.} \texttt{dowhy} encourages a 4-step causal inference process:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Define the graph;
\item Find the estimane (e.g., with the backdoor criterion);
\item Compute the estimator (\texttt{dowhy} can use the models in \texttt{econml});
\item Perform refutation tests.
\end{compactitem}
With an SCM (instead of just a graph), \texttt{dowhy} can compute the \emph{causal strength} of an edge:
the outcome variable variance change if we remove it.

\textbf{8.} To test a causal model, try:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item \texttt{dowhy}'s refutation tests;
\item RCT;
\item Simulated data (\texttt{RealCause});
\item Sensitivity analysis (\texttt{Pysensemakr}).
\end{compactitem}

We often make the following assumptions:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Positivity: $P[T|Z] > 0$;
\item Exchangebility: $Y^{(0)}, Y^{(1)} \indep T | Z$;
\item SUTVA: the units need not be independent, but the treatment of one should not affect the others.
\end{compactitem}

\textbf{9.}
To compute the ATE (or ATT, or ATC), you can use matching
(exact for categorical variables, Euclidean distance otherwisse -- Mahalanobis may have good theoretical properties,
but it may amplify insignificant differences:
stick to Euclidean distance on standardized data).
You could also match on the propensity score
(but it is suboptimal, and gets worse if the propensity model is good).

Inverse propensity weighting (IPW) is a better use of the propensity score, but extreme values are problematic
(some suggest to discard observations with $e \not\in [0.05, 0.95]$).

For heterogeneous treatment effects (CATE),
we need interactions between treatment and covariates: linear models are not enough.

The \emph{S-learner} fits a model
$Y = f(T,X)$ but, if the treatment effect is small, it could choose not to use the treatment at all.

The \emph{T-learner} learns separate models for $T=0$ and $T=1$
(if the models are trees, this is equivalent to an S-learner with the first split on $T$).

S- and T-learners do not estimate the CATE directly:
they estimate $Y^{(1)}$ and $Y^{(0)}$ and take the difference.
The simplified \emph{X-learner} uses 3 models:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Fit models $\mu_0$, $\mu_1$ to predict $Y$ when $T=0$ or $T=1$ (as for the T-learner);
\item Compute
  \[ \hat d_i = \begin{cases}
    Y_i^{(1)} - \hat Y_i^{(0)} & \text{if } T_i = 1 \\
    \hat Y_i^{(1)} - Y_i^{(0)} & \text{if } T_i = 0;
  \end{cases} \]
\item Fit a model to predict $\hat d$ from $X$.
\end{compactitem}
The full X-learner is more complicated:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Fit models $\mu_0$, $\mu_1$ to predict $Y$ when $T=0$ or $T=1$ (as for the T-learner);
\item Compute
  \begin{align*}
    \hat d_i^{(1)} &= Y_i^{(1)} - \hat Y_i^{(0)} && \text{if } T_i = 1 \\
    \hat d_i^{(0)} &= \hat Y_i^{(1)} - Y_i^{(0)} && \text{if } T_i = 0
  \end{align*}
\item Learn separate models to predict $\hat d^{(0)}$ and $\hat d^{(1)}$;
\item Learn a propensity model;
\item Combine those forecasts
  \[ \hat \tau = \hat e \hat{\hat d}^{(0)} + (1 - \hat e) \hat{\hat d}^{(1)} \]
  (this gives more weight to the model which had more data to train on).
\end{compactitem}
These models are called ``meta'' learners because they contain several models.

\textbf{10.}
\emph{Doubly robust} (DR) models combine treatment models (IPW) and outcome models (controlling for confounders).
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=1,yscale=1.3,inner sep=0pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline=0]
  \begin{scope}
    \node (T) at (0,0) {$T$};
    \node (Y) at (2,0) {$Y$};
    \node (X) at (1,1) {$X$};
    \draw[->] (X) -- (T);
    \draw[->] (X) -- (Y);
    \draw[->] (T) -- (Y);
  \end{scope}
  \node at (2.75,.5) {$\rightsquigarrow$};
  \begin{scope}[shift={(3.5,0)}]
    \node (T) at (0,0) {$T$};
    \node (e) at (.5,.5) {$e(X)$};
    \node (Y) at (2,0) {$Y$};
    \node (X) at (1,1) {$X$};
    \draw[->] (X) -- (e);
    \draw[->] (e) -- (T);
    \draw[->] (X) -- (Y);
    \draw[->] (T) -- (Y);
  \end{scope}
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
In practice, they have lower variance than IPW.

\emph{TMLE} is a DR model with statistical guarantees
(available in \texttt{causallib} or \texttt{zEpid}).

\emph{Double machine learning} (DML) uses:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item \emph{Cross-fitting} to reduce overfitting;
\item \emph{Orthogonalization} to reduce the bias introduced by regularization:
  to compute $\beta_1$ in
  \[ Y = \beta_1 T + \beta_2 X + \epsilon, \]
  we can regress $Y \sim X$ and $T \sim X$,
  and regress the residuals;
  this still works for partially linear models
  \begin{align*}
    Y &= \theta T + f_Y(X) + \epsilon_Y \\
    T &= f_T(X) + \epsilon_T
  \end{align*}
  (only use regularization in $f_Y$ and $f_T$, not in the final (linear) model).
\end{compactitem}
(\texttt{econml}'s DR-learner also uses cross-fitting and orthogonalization.)

While DR and T/X learners assume categorical treatments, DML (and the S-learner) also works for continuous treatments.

\emph{Causal forests} choose the splits and compute the leaf values with different subsets of the data (\emph{honest trees}).

With experimental data:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Check this is a RCT: we should not be able to predict the treatment from the covariates;
\item Look at the \emph{Qini curve} (cumulative number of positive outcomes versus the number of treatment units)
  and the Qini coefficient (comparison with a random model);
\item Compute the AUUC (area under the uplift curve -- see \texttt{uplift-analysis});
\item Plot the uplift by decile,
  $y^{(1)} - y^{(0)} \sim \text{decile}(\hat y)$.
\end{compactitem}

\textbf{11.} TARNet and SNet are deep learning models
between S- and T-learners: they first compute a shared latent representation of the covariates $X$, before computing $\hat \mu_1$, $\hat\mu_0$ and $\hat e$.
\[  % TARNet, SNet
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=1,yscale=.6,inner sep=1pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline=0]

(implementation in \texttt{CATENets})

\emph{Natural experiments} produce time series, from which you can build synthetic controls (\texttt{causalpy}).

\textbf{13.} The third part of the book is devoted to causal discovery,
including deep learning approaches such as DECI (\texttt{causica}).

*** Book: Causal Inference in R

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Fundamentals of causal inference with R}\\
  B.A. Brumback (2022)
\end{book}
% Confusing notations: the treatment is sometimes $T$, sometimes $A$,
% and this can change in the middle of an equation!
% ($T$ looks better, until you introduce time, e.g., for DiD)

Effect measures include:
\begin{align*}
  & p_0 = \E[ Y(0) ] \\
  & p_1 = \E[Y(1)] \\
  & \dfrac{ p_1 }{ 1-p_1 }, \dfrac{ p_0 }{ 1-p_0 } &&\text{odds} \\
  &\text{RD} = p_1 - p_0 && \text{risk difference} \\
  &\text{RR} = p_1 / p_0 && \text{relative risk} \\
  &\text{RR}^*** = \dfrac{ 1 - p_0 }{ 1 - p_1 } && \text{relative risk} \\
  &\text{OR} = \left. \dfrac{p_1}{1-p_1} \middle/ \dfrac{p_0}{1-p_0} \right. && \text{odds ratio} \\
  &\text{NNT} = 1 / \text{RD} && \text{number needed to treat} \\
  &\text{AF} = 1 - 1/\text{RR} && \text{attributable fraction} \\
  &\text{CP} = \dfrac{p_1-p_0}{1-p_0} = 1 - 1/\text{RR}^*** && \text{causal power}
\end{align*}

Check the R packages:
\texttt{AER},
\texttt{boot},
\texttt{car},
\texttt{faraway},
\texttt{gee},
\texttt{geepack},
\texttt{Matching},
\texttt{resample}.

\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,scale=.7,inner sep=0pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline={(0,.3)}]
  \node (H) at (1,1) {$H$};
  \node (T) at (0,0) {$T$};
  \node (Y) at (2,0) {$Y$};
  \draw[->] (H) -- (T);
  \draw[->] (H) -- (Y);
  \draw[->] (T) -- (Y);
\end{tikzpicture}
\]

\textbf{6.}
There are several ways of adjusting for a sufficient confounder $H$.
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Outcome modeling (aka backdoor adjustment),
  using a model $Y \sim T + H$,
  \[ \E \bigl[ Y(t) \bigr] = \sum_h \E\bigl[ Y | T=t, H=h ] P[H=h]; \]
\item Exposure modeling
  (aka inverse propensity score weighting), using a model
  $e: E[T|H] \sim H$,
  \begin{align*}
    \E\bigl[ Y(1) \bigr] &= \E \biggl[ \dfrac{ TY }{ e(H) } \biggr] \\
    \E\bigl[ Y(0) \bigr] &= \E \biggl[ \dfrac{ (1-T) }{ 1-e(H) } Y \biggr] \\
  \end{align*}
\item Doubly robust standardization
  \begin{align*}
    \E\bigl[ Y(1) \bigr] &= \E \biggl[ \dfrac{ T }{ e(H) } Y
      - \dfrac{ T - e(H) }{ e(H) } \hat Y(H,1) \biggr] \\
    \E\bigl[ Y(0) \bigr] &= \E \biggl[ \dfrac{ (1-T) }{ 1-e(H) } Y
      + \dfrac{ T-e(H) }{ 1 - e(H) } \hat Y(H,0) \biggr] \\
  \end{align*}
\item Matching; % Not mentioned
\item Difference-in-differences (DiD),
  under the ``additive equi-confounding'' assumption
  \begin{align*}
  &\E\bigl[ Y_1(0) | A=1 \bigr] - \E\bigl[ Y_1(0) | A=0 \bigr]
  = \\
  &\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad
  \E\bigl[ Y_0 | A=1 \bigr] - \E\bigl[ Y_0 | A=0 \bigr]
  \end{align*}
  (the difference between the treated and the non-treated, if we had not treated them,
  is the same as the difference between those two groups before treatment),
  \begin{align*}
    \text{ATT}
    &= \E \bigl[ Y_1(1) - Y_1(0) | A\!=\!1 \bigr] \\
    &= \E\bigl[ Y_1(1) | A\!=\!1\bigr] - \E\bigl[ Y_1(0) | A\!=\!1 \bigr] \\
    &= \E[ Y_1 | A\!=\!1 ] -
    \\ &\hphantom{={}}
    \bigl(
    E[Y_0 | A\!=\!1] - E[Y_0|A\!=\!0] + E[Y_1(0)|A\!=\!0]
    \bigr) \\
    &= \E[ Y_1-Y_0 | A\!=\!1] - \E[ Y_1-Y_0|A\!=\!0]
  \end{align*}
  ($T$ is time, $A$ is the treatment).
  \item Front-door adjustment, given a surrogate marker of the effect of $T$ on $Y$ (a mediator)
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=1,yscale=.7,inner sep=0pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline={(0,.3)}]
  \node (T) at (0,0) {$T$};
  %\node (X) at (-1,1) {$X$};
  \node (Y) at (2,0) {$Y$};
  \node (M) at (1,0) {$M$};
  \node[circle,densely dotted,draw] (U) at (1,1) {$U$};
  \draw[->] (U) -- (Y);
  \draw[->] (U) -- (T);
  \draw[->] (T) -- (M);
  \draw[->] (M) -- (Y);
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
\begin{align*}
  E\bigl[ Y(t) \bigr] = \sum_m P[ M\!=\!m | T\!=\!t] \sum_{t'} E[ Y |M\!=\!m,T\!=\!t'] P[T\!=\!t']
\end{align*}
  \item Instrumental variables
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=.6,yscale=.7,inner sep=0pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline={(0,.3)}]
  \node (T) at (0,0) {$T$};
  \node (I) at (-2,0) {$I$};
  \node (Y) at (2,0) {$Y$};
  \node[circle,densely dotted,draw] (U) at (1,1) {$U$};
  \draw[->] (U) -- (Y);
  \draw[->] (U) -- (T);
  \draw[->] (T) -- (Y);
  \draw[->] (I) -- (T);
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
    with \emph{principal stratification}
    \begin{align*}
      &I\rlap{\text{: treatment assignment}} \\
      &T\text{: treatment} \\
      &C\text{: compliance} \\
      &T(0)=T(1)=1 &&\text{ always taker} \\
      &T(0)=T(1)=0 &&\text{ never taker} \\
      &\forall t \ A(t)=t &&\text{ complier} \\
      &\forall t \ A(t)=1-t &&\text{ defier}
    \end{align*}
    assuming there are no defiers (monotonicity assumption),
    we can compute the complier average causal effect
    \begin{align*}
      \text{CACE} &= \E\bigl[ Y(1) - Y(0) | C=1 ] \\
      &= \dfrac{
        E[ Y|T=1] - E[Y|T=0]
      }{
        P[A=1|T=1] - P[A=1|T=0]
      }
    \end{align*}
  \item Instrumental variables with \emph{structural nested mean models}:
    assuming $Y - Y(0) \indep I | T$,
    we can compute the ATT (with the same formula).
\end{compactitem}

The \emph{propensity score} has many uses:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Inverse propensity weighting;
\item Backdoor adjustment: the propensity score is a sufficient confounder,
  \[ \forall t \ Y(t) \indep T \,|\, H \implies \forall t \ Y(t) \indep T \,|\, e(H); \]
\item Stratification: computing the ATE in each quartile;
\item Matching: easier to do on the propensity score $e(H)$
  than on the original confounders $H$.
\end{compactitem}

Adding \emph{precision variables} reduces variance.
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=.6,yscale=.7,inner sep=0pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline={(0,.3)}]
  \node (T) at (0,0) {$T$};
  \node (Y) at (2,0) {$Y$};
  \node (V) at (1,1) {$V$};
  \draw[->] (V) -- (Y);
  \draw[->] (T) -- (Y);
\end{tikzpicture}
\qquad
\text{or}
\qquad
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=.6,yscale=.7,inner sep=0pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline={(0,.3)}]
  \node (T) at (0,0) {$T$};
  \node (V) at (2,2) {$V$};
  \node (Y) at (2,0) {$Y$};
  \node[circle,densely dotted,draw] (U) at (1,1) {$U$};
  \draw[->] (U) -- (Y);
  \draw[->] (U) -- (V);
  \draw[->] (T) -- (Y);
\end{tikzpicture}
\]

\emph{Mediation analysis} measures the importance of a mediator.
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=.6,yscale=.7,inner sep=0pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline={(0,.3)}]
  \node (T) at (0,0) {$T$};
  \node (Y) at (4,0) {$Y$};
  \node (M) at (2,0) {$M$};
  \node (H3) at (1,1) {$H_3$};
  \node (H2) at (3,1) {$H_2$};
  \node (H1) at (2,2) {$H_1$};
  \draw[->] (T) -- (M);
  \draw[->] (M) -- (Y);
  \draw [->] (H3) -- (T);
  \draw [->] (H3) -- (M);
  \draw [->] (H2) -- (M);
  \draw [->] (H2) -- (Y);
  \draw [->] (H1) to[out=-165,in=90] (T);
  \draw [->] (H1) to[out=-15,in=90] (Y);
\end{tikzpicture}
\]

Denote the potential outcome $T(t,m)$.
\begin{align*}
  &\text{TE} = T( 1, M(1)) - Y(0,M(0)) && \text{total effect} \\
  &\text{CDE}(m) = Y(1,m) - Y(0,m) &&\text{controlled direct effect} \\
  &\text{CIE}(m) = \text{TE} - \text{CDE}(m) &&\text{controlled indirect effect} \\
  &\text{NDE} = Y(1,M(0)) - Y(0,M(0)) && \text{natural direct effect} \\
  &\text{NIE} = Y(1,M(1)) - Y(1,M(0)) &&\text{natural indirect effect} \\
  &\text{TE} = \text{CDE} + \text{CIE} = \text{NDE} + \text{NIE} \\
  &\text{PE} = \dfrac{ \text{CIE}(m) }{ \text{TE} } \\
  &\text{PM} =  \dfrac{\text{NIE}}{\text{TE}}
\end{align*}

\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=1,yscale=.7,inner sep=0pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline={(0,.3)}]

  \draw[->] (T1) -- (H);
  \draw[->] (H)  to[out=-30,in=-150] (Y);
  \draw[->] (T1) to[out=-30,in=-135] (Y);
