we want to know the optimal value of $T_1$ and,
given $T_1$ and $H$, the optimal value of $T_2$.

*** Book: Causal Inference and ML/AI

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Applied causal inference\\ powered by ML and AI}\\
  V. Chernozhukov et al. (2024)
\end{book}

\textbf{1.} To estimate $\beta_1$ in $Y = \beta_1 D + \beta_2 W + \epsilon$,
we can use ``partialing out'', \ie,
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Compute the residuals $\tilde Y$ and $\tilde D$ of $Y \sim W$ and $S \sim W$;
\item Fit a linear model $\tilde Y = \beta_1 \tilde D + \eta$.
  \begin{align*}
    \beta_1 &= \argmin_{ b_1} \E ( \tilde Y - b_1 \tilde D ) ^ 2 \\
    &= \E[ \tilde D ^2 ]^{-1} \E[ \tilde D \tilde Y ]
  \end{align*}
\end{compactitem}

\textbf{2.}
The average treatment effect and the average predictive effect
are usually distinct, because of selection bias,
\begin{align*}
  \text{ATE} &= \E\bigl[ Y(1) - Y(0) \bigr] \\
  \text{APE} &= \E[ Y | T=1] - \E[ Y | T=0 ]
\end{align*}
but, for randomized control trials, they are equal.

Including covariates can help denoise the ATE estimator.

RCTs are not always possible,
for instance because of externalities
(positive, as for herd immunity after vaccination,
or negative, e.g., the drop in college wage premium as more people go to college).

\textbf{3.}
The lasso assumes \emph{approximate sparsity}
\[ \va{ \beta_j } \leq A j ^ { -a } \qquad \text{for } a > 1/2. \]
Ridge regression assumes the coefficients are dense
(small, approximately of the same magnitude).
ElasticNet assumes the true model is sparse+dense.

The $L^1$ penalty is
$\lambda \sum \va{ \beta_i}$
only if the predictors are normalized, $\E[ X_j ^2 ] = 1$
(if not, multiply $\lambda$ by $\hat \sigma_j$).
Choose the penalty scale as
\begin{align*}
  \lambda &= 2 \cdot c \cdot \hat\sigma \sqrt n z_{ 1 - a / (2p) }
  \qquad x = 1.1, \ a = 0.05 \\
  z_{ 1 - a / (2p) } &\approx \sqrt{ 2 \log( 2p/a) }.
\end{align*}
The lasso sets $\hat{ \beta_j }$ to zero if the marginal benefit of moving it away from zero is smaller than the marginal increase in penalty.
\[ \hat\beta_j = 0 \ \text{ if }\  \va{
  \dfrac{ \partial }{ \partial \hat\beta_j } \sum_i \bigl( Y_i - \hat\beta_i ' X_i \bigr) ^2
} < \lambda \]

The \d{post-lasso} uses the lasso to select the predictors,
and them fits and OLS regression (unbiased) on those predictors.
Cross-validate the whole post-lasso process, not just the variable selection.

The \d{lava} method splits the predictors into sparse and dense ones,
with $L^1$ and $L^2$ penalties respectively.
\[ \operatorname*{Minimize}_{ b = \delta + \xi }
\sum ( Y_i - b' X_i )^2 + \lambda_1 \sum \delta_i^2 + \lambda_2 \sum \va{ \xi_i } \]

\textbf{4.}
The \d{double lasso}
uses partialing out  to infer the effect of $T$ on $Y$,
with high-dimensional confounders $W$.

\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=.6,yscale=.7,inner sep=0pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline=0]
  \node (T) at (0,0) {$T$};
  \node (W) at (1,1) {$W$};
  \node (Y) at (2,0) {$Y$};
  \draw[->] (W) -- (T);
  \draw[->] (W) -- (Y);
  \draw[->] (T) -- (Y);
\end{tikzpicture}
\]

\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Compute the residuals $\tilde T$, $\tilde Y$
  of lasso (or post lasso, or any lasso variant) models
  for $T \sim W$, $Y \sim W$;
\item Use a least squares regression $\tilde Y \sim \tilde T$.
\end{compactitem}

% Neyman orthogonality (p 107)

Using the lasso just once (on $Y \sim T + W$,
to select the variables) is invalid -- the double lasso
may select different variables for $Y \sim W$ and $T \sim W$.

\textbf{5.}
If we have all the confounders
(unconfoundedness, aka conditional ignorability)
$\text{CAPE} = \text{CATE}$
and $\E[\text{CAPE}] = \E[\text{CATE}]$.

\emph{Conditioning} requires you to model $Y | T, X$;
\begin{align*}
  \delta(x) &= \E \bigl[ Y(1) - Y(0) | X = x \bigr] \\
  &= \E \bigl[ Y | T = 1 , X = x \bigr] - \bigl[ Y | T = 0 , X = x \bigr]  \\
  \delta &= \E \bigl[ \delta(X) \bigr]
\end{align*}
while \emph{propensity score weighting} only requires you to model $T|X$.
\begin{align*}
  H &= \dfrac{ \mathbf1( T = 1 ) }{ \P[ Y = 1 | X ] } - \dfrac{ \mathbf1( T = 0 ) }{ \P[ Y = 0 | X ] } \\
  \delta(X) &= \E \bigl[ Y H | X \bigr]
\end{align*}

In \emph{stratified RCT}, the propensity score is known.

Propensity score weighting gives unbiased estimators,
but they may have high variance:
also accounting for the link between the onfounders $X$ amd the outcome $Y$ can ``denoise'' the estimator.

The ATE can be computed by regressing $Y \sim H$ (clever covariate).
\begin{align*}
  \phi(t,x) &= \dfrac{ (-1)^t }{ \P  \bigl[ T = t | X = x \bigr] } \\
  H &= \phi(T,X) \\
  Y &= \beta H + \epsilon \\
  \text{ATE} &= \E \bigl[ \beta \bigl( \phi(1,X) - \phi(0,X) \bigr) \bigr]
\end{align*}

\textbf{6.} Do not condition on colliders.

\textbf{7.}
The \d{do} operation can be represented graphically in two ways,
either by removing incoming edges
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=.6,yscale=.7,inner sep=0pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline={(0,.3)}]
  \node (T) at (0,0) {$T$};
  \node (X) at (1,1) {$X$};
  \node (Y) at (2,0) {$Y$};
  \draw[->] (X) -- (T);
  \draw[->] (X) -- (Y);
  \draw[->] (T) -- (Y);
\end{tikzpicture}
\ \ \ \overset{\text{do}(T=t)}{\leadsto}\ \ \
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=.6,yscale=.7,inner sep=0pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline={(0,.3)}]
  \node (T) at (0,0) {$t$};
  \node (X) at (1,1) {$X$};
  \node (Y) at (2,0) {$Y$};
  %\draw[->] (X) -- (T);
  \draw[->] (X) -- (Y);
  \draw[->] (T) -- (Y);
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
or by splitting the node in two
(SWIG, single world intervention graph,
of \d{fix} intervention).
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=.6,yscale=.7,inner sep=0pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline={(0,.3)}]
  \node (T) at (0,0) {$T$};
  \node (X) at (1,1) {$X$};
  \node (Y) at (2,0) {$Y$};
  \draw[->] (X) -- (T);
  \draw[->] (X) -- (Y);
  \draw[->] (T) -- (Y);
\end{tikzpicture}
\ \ \ \overset{\text{do}(T=t)}{\leadsto}\ \ \
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=.8,yscale=.7,inner sep=0pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline={(0,.3)}]
  \node (T) at (0,0) {$T$};
  \node (t) at (.67,0) {$t$};
  \node (X) at (1,1) {$X$};
  \node (Y) at (2,0) {$Y$};
  \draw[->] (X) -- (T);
  \draw[->] (X) -- (Y);
  \draw[->] (t) -- (Y);
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
D-separation implies conditional independence,
but the converse requires faithfulness.
While the model is almost surely faithful,
the neighbourhood of unfaithful models can be large,
all the more so in high dimensions.

\textbf{8.} There are many \emph{valid adjustment strategies}:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Parents of the treatment $T$;
\item Propensity score;
\item Parents of the outcome $Y$ (excluding the descendants of $T$);
\item $\text{Parents}(T) \union \text{Parents}(Y) \setminus \text{Descendants}(T)$
    (robust against perturbations o the DAG);
\item $\text{Causes}(T) \inter \text{Causes}(Y)$
\item Backdoor creterion: a set $S$ blocking all backdoor (\ie, non-causal) paths, and containing no descendant of $T$;
\item More generally, any set $S$ such that $Y \indep T | S $ in the SWIG.
\end{compactitem}
\begingroup
  \allowdisplaybreaks
  \begin{align*}
  \begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=.6,yscale=.7,inner sep=0pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline={(0,.3)}]
    \node (T) at (0,0) {$T$};
    \node[draw,inner sep=1pt] (X) at (1,1) {$X$};
    \node (Y) at (2,0) {$Y$};
    %\draw[->] (X) -- (T);
    \draw[->] (X) -- (Y);
    \draw[->] (T) -- (Y);
  \end{tikzpicture}
  &\qquad\text{decreases variance} \\
  \begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=.6,yscale=.7,inner sep=0pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline={(0,.3)}]
    \node (T) at (0,0) {$T$};
    \node[draw,inner sep=1pt] (X) at (1,1) {$X$};
    \node (Y) at (2,0) {$Y$};
    \draw[->] (X) -- (T);
    %\draw[->] (X) -- (Y);
    \draw[->] (T) -- (Y);
  \end{tikzpicture}
  &\qquad\text{increases variance} \\
  \begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=.6,yscale=.7,inner sep=0pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline={(0,.3)}]
    \node (T) at (0,0) {$T$};
    \node[draw,inner sep=1pt] (X) at (-1,1) {$X$};
    \node (Y) at (2,0) {$Y$};
    \node[circle,densely dotted,draw] (U) at (1,1) {$U$};
    \draw[->] (X) -- (T);
    \draw[->] (U) -- (Y);
    \draw[->] (U) -- (T);
    \draw[->] (T) -- (Y);
  \end{tikzpicture}
  &\qquad\text{increases bias} \\
  \begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=.8,yscale=.5,inner sep=0pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline={(0,.3)}]
    \node (T) at (0,0) {$T$};
    \node (Y) at (2,0) {$Y$};
    \node[draw,inner sep=1pt] (X) at (1,1) {$X$};
    \node[circle,densely dotted,draw] (U1) at (0,2) {$U_1$};
    \node[circle,densely dotted,draw] (U2) at (2,2) {$U_2$};
    \draw[->] (U1) -- (T);
    \draw[->] (U1) -- (X);
    \draw[->] (U2) -- (Y);
    \draw[->] (U2) -- (X);
    \draw[->] (T) -- (Y);
  \end{tikzpicture}
  &\qquad\text{M bias} \\
  \begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=.8,yscale=.5,inner sep=0pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline={(0,.3)}]
    \node (T) at (0,0) {$T$};
    \node (Y) at (2,0) {$Y$};
    \node[draw,inner sep=1pt] (X) at (1,1) {$X$};
    \node[circle,densely dotted,draw] (U1) at (0,2) {$U_1$};
    \node[circle,densely dotted,draw] (U2) at (2,2) {$U_2$};
    \draw[->] (U1) -- (T);
    \draw[->] (U1) -- (X);
    \draw[->] (U2) -- (Y);
    \draw[->] (U2) -- (X);
    \draw[->] (T) -- (Y);
    \draw[-] (U1) -- (U2);
  \end{tikzpicture}
  &\qquad\text{M bias, correlated factors} \\
  \begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=.8,yscale=.5,inner sep=0pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline={(0,.3)}]
    \node (T) at (0,0) {$T$};
    \node (Y) at (2,0) {$Y$};
    \node[draw,inner sep=1pt] (X) at (1,1) {$X$};
    \node[circle,densely dotted,draw] (U1) at (0,2) {$U_1$};
    \node[circle,densely dotted,draw] (U2) at (2,2) {$U_2$};
    \draw[->] (U1) -- (T);
    \draw[->] (U1) -- (X);
    \draw[->] (U2) -- (Y);
    \draw[->] (U2) -- (X);
    \draw[->] (T) -- (Y);
    \draw[->] (X) -- (Y);
  \end{tikzpicture}
  &\qquad\text{M bias, confounding} \\
  \begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=.8,yscale=.5,inner sep=0pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline={(0,.3)}]
    \node (T) at (0,0) {$T$};
    \node (Y) at (2,0) {$Y$};
    \node[draw,inner sep=1pt] (X) at (1,1) {$X$};
    \node[circle,densely dotted,draw] (U1) at (0,2) {$U_1$};
    \node[circle,densely dotted,draw] (U2) at (2,2) {$U_2$};
    \draw[->] (U1) -- (T);
    \draw[->] (U1) -- (X);
    \draw[->] (U2) -- (Y);
    \draw[->] (U2) -- (X);
    \draw[->] (T) -- (Y);
    \draw[->] (X) -- (T);
    \draw[->] (X) -- (Y);
  \end{tikzpicture}
  &\qquad\text{butterfly bias} \\
  \begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=1,yscale=.7,inner sep=0pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline={(0,.3)}]
    \node (T) at (0,0) {$T$};
    \node[draw,inner sep=1pt] (X) at (1,1) {$X$};
    \node (Y) at (2,0) {$Y$};
    \node (M) at (1,0) {$M$};
    \draw[->] (X) -- (M);
    \draw[->] (M) -- (Y);
    \draw[->] (T) -- (M);
  \end{tikzpicture}
  &\qquad\text{may decrease variance} \\
  \begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=.6,yscale=.7,inner sep=0pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline={(0,.3)}]
    \node (T) at (0,1) {$T$};
    \node[draw,inner sep=1pt] (Z) at (1,0) {$Z$};
    \node (Y) at (2,1) {$Y$};
    \draw[->] (T) -- (Y);
    \draw[->] (T) -- (Z);
    \draw[->] (Y) -- (Z);
  \end{tikzpicture}
  &\qquad\text{collider bias} \\
  \begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=.6,yscale=.7,inner sep=0pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline={(0,.3)}]
    \node (T) at (0,0) {$T$};
    \node[draw,inner sep=1pt] (X) at (1,1) {$X$};
    \node (Y) at (2,0) {$Y$};
    \node[circle,draw,densely dotted] (U) at (2,2) {$U$};
    \draw[->] (T) -- (Y);
    \draw[->] (X) -- (Y);
    \draw[->] (T) -- (X);
    \draw[->] (U) -- (X);
    \draw[->] (U) -- (Y);
  \end{tikzpicture}
  &\qquad\text{collider stratification bias} \\
  \begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=1,yscale=.7,inner sep=0pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline={(0,-.05)}]
    \node (T) at (0,0) {$T$};
    \node[draw,inner sep=1pt] (Z) at (2,0) {$Z$};
    \node (Y) at (1,0) {$Y$};
    \draw[->] (T) -- (Y);
    \draw[->] (Y) -- (Z);
  \end{tikzpicture}
  &\qquad\text{recall bias}
  \end{align*}
\endgroup

% 9. Modern nonlinear regression: trees, forests, boosting, neural nets, ensembling,
% with theoretical results under the "structured sparsity" assumption

\textbf{10.}
We want to compute
\[ \dfrac{ d }{ dt } \E \bigl[ Y | T = t, X = x \bigr]  \]
or
\[ \dfrac{ d }{ dt } \E \bigl[ Y | \text{do}(T = t), X = x \bigr]  \]
(which reduces to the first expression
if $X$ is a sufficient conditioning set).

The partially linear regression model (PLM) is
\[ Y = \beta T + g(X) + \epsilon \qquad \E[ \epsilon | T , X ] = 0; \]
the confounders $X$ have an additive, but nonlinear effect.

If $\tilde Y$ and $\tilde T$ are the residualized $Y$ and $T$ wrt $X$,
then $\beta$ is the coefficient of the linear model
$\tilde Y \sim \tilde T$;
it is given by the first order condition
$\E \bigl[ ( \tilde Y - \beta \tilde T ) \tilde T \bigr] = 0$.
\[ \beta = \E[ \tilde T^2 ]^{-1} \E[ \tilde T \tilde Y ] \]

To precent overfitting, \d{Double ML} (DML)
uses \emph{cross-fitted residuals}:
for observations in fold $k$,
$\tilde Y$ and $\tilde T$ are estimated from models fitted without fold $k$

To reduce bias, if you have several models to predict $T$ and $Y$,
use the best ones by looking at the CVMSE.
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=3,yscale=1,inner sep=0pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline={(0,.3)}]
  \node[rounded corners=2mm,inner sep = 2pt, draw,align=center,densely dotted] (T) at (0,0) {gun\\ownership};
  \node[rounded corners=2mm,inner sep = 2pt, draw,align=center] (X) at (1.1,1) {country\\characteristics};
  \node[rounded corners=2mm,inner sep = 2pt, draw] (Y) at (2,0) {\# murders};
  \node[rounded corners=2mm,inner sep = 2pt, draw] (P) at (0,-1) {\# suicides};
  \draw[->] (X) -- (T);
  \draw[->] (X) -- (P);
  \draw[->] (T) -- (P);
  \draw[->] (T) -- (Y);
  \node[above=0mm of X] {\footnotesize\color{gray}$X$};
  \node[above=0mm of T] {\footnotesize\color{gray}$T$};
  \node[above=0mm of Y] {\footnotesize\color{gray}$Y$};
  \node[below=0mm of P] {\footnotesize\color{gray}proxy for $T$};
\end{tikzpicture}
\]

In the \emph{interactive regression model} (IRM)
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=.6,yscale=.7,inner sep=0pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline={(0,.3)}]
  \node (T) at (0,0) {$T$};
  \node (X) at (1,1) {$X$};
  \node (Y) at (2,0) {$Y$};
  \draw[->] (X) -- (T);
  \draw[->] (X) -- (Y);
  \draw[->] (T) -- (Y);
\end{tikzpicture}
\qquad
\begin{aligned}
  T &= m(X) + \tilde T  && \E[ \tilde T | X ] = 0 \\
  Y &= g(T,X) + \epsilon && \E[ \epsilon | T,X] = 0
\end{aligned}
\]
the treatment is not additively separable,
but it is binary.
To estimate the averate treatment effect
\[ \theta = \E \bigl[ g(1,X) - g(0,X) \bigr] . \]
combine regression adjustment
\[ \hat\theta = \E \bigl[ g(1,X) - g(0,X) \bigr] \]
and propensity score reweighting
\begin{align*}
  \hat\theta &= \E[ Y H ] \\
  H &= \dfrac{ \mathbf1(T=1) }{ m(X) } - \dfrac{ \mathbf1(T=0) }{ 1 - m(X) }
\end{align*}
(Horvitz-Thompson transformation):
this is the \emph{doubly robust parametrization}
\[ \hat \theta = \E \bigl[
  g(1,X) - g(0,X) + \bigl( Y - g(T,X) \bigr) H \bigr]. \]

Propensity scores close to 0 or 1 are problematic: they lead to very large values of $H$.
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=5,yscale=1.5,inner sep=0pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline={(0,.3)}]
  \node[align=center,rounded corners=2mm, inner sep = 2pt, draw, densely dotted] (A) at (0,1) {Firm\\characteristics};
  \node[align=center] (B) at (1,1) {Worker\\characteristics};
  \node (C) at (0,0) {401k eligibility};
  \node[align=center] (D) at (1,0) {Financial\\assets};
  \draw[->] (A) -- (C);
  \draw[->] (B) -- (C);
  \draw[->] (B) -- (D);
  \draw[->,very thick,blue!70!black] (C) -- (D);
  \draw[->] ([yshift=1pt]A.east) to node[anchor=south]{\footnotesize hiring} ([yshift=1pt]B.west);
  \draw[->] ([yshift=-1pt]B.west) to node[anchor=north]{\footnotesize applying} ([yshift=-1pt]A.east);
\end{tikzpicture}
\]

More generally, \d{double machine learning} (DML)
uses a \emph{score} function
\[
M(\theta, \eta) = \E \bigl[
  \tikzmarknode{psi}{\psi}
  (
  \tikzmarknode{W}{W}
  ;
  \tikzmarknode{theta}{\theta}
  ,
  \tikzmarknode{eta}{\eta}
  ) \bigr]
\vrule height 1.6cm depth 2.1cm width 0pt
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth, overlay, remember picture]
  \node (psi-b)   [align=center, font=\footnotesize, below= 10mm of psi  ]{score\\function};
  \node (W-b)     [align=center, font=\footnotesize, above=  3mm of W    ]{data};
  \node (theta-b) [align=center, font=\footnotesize, below= 3mm of theta]{parameters\\of interest};
  \node (eta-b)   [align=center, font=\footnotesize, above= 8mm of eta  ]{nuisance\\parameters};
  \draw[->,shorten >=2pt] (psi-b) -- (psi);
  \draw[->,shorten >=2pt] (W-b) -- (W);
  \draw[->,shorten >=2pt] (theta-b) -- (theta);
  \draw[->,shorten >=2pt] (eta-b) -- (eta);
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
\[ M(\theta, \eta_0 ) = 0 \iff \theta = \theta_0 \]
satisfying the \emph{Neyman orthogonality condition}
\[ \left. \dfrac{ \partial M(\theta_0 , \eta ) }{ \partial \eta } \right|_{ \eta = \eta_0 } = 0 \]
\ie, $\hat\theta$ is insensitive to small perturbations of $\eta$ around $\eta_0$:
we can use $\hat \eta$ instead
(from a good machine learning model:
lasso, post-lasso,
$\ell^1$-penalized neural net, random forest,
or ensemble of these).
For instance, for the partially linear model (PLM)
\begin{align*}
  \psi &= ( \tilde Y - \theta \tilde T ) \tilde T \\
  &= \bigl[ Y - \ell(X) - \theta \bigl( T - m(X) \bigr) \bigr] \bigl( T - m(x) \bigr) \\
  \eta &= (\ell, m) \ \text{ nuisance parameters;}
\end{align*}
to estimate the ATE in the IRM
\begin{align*}
  \psi &= \bigl[ g(1,X) - g(0,X) \bigr] + H \bigl[ Y - g(T,X) \bigr] - \theta \\
  H &= \dfrac{ T }{ m(X) } - \dfrac{ 1 - T }{ 1 - m(X) } \\
  \eta &= (g, m)
\end{align*}
(and similar score functions for the GATE or the ATET).

Use \emph{sample splitting}: estimate $\hat \eta_k$ on data without the $k$th fold,
then estimate $\hat\theta$ using $\hat\eta_k$ for  for observations in the $k$th fold;
you can also get an estimator of $\var \hat\theta$
and confidence intervals.

% 11. Dimension reduction: PCA , auto-encoders (AE) and embeddings

\textbf{12a.}
We can measure the impact of a non-observed confounder:
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,scale=1,inner sep=0pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline={(0,.3)}]
  \node (X) at (0,1) {$X$};
  \node[circle,draw,densely dotted,inner sep=1pt] (A) at (1,1) {$A$};
  \node (T) at (0,0) {$T$};
  \node (Y) at (1,0) {$Y$\rlap{;}};
  \draw[->] (X) -- (A);
  \draw[->] (X) -- (Y);
  \draw[->] (X) -- (T);
  \draw[->] (A) to node[pos=.35,anchor=south,font=\footnotesize,inner sep=1pt]{$\gamma$} (T);
  \draw[->] (A) to node[anchor=west, font=\footnotesize]{$\delta$} (Y);
  \draw[->] (T) to node[anchor=north,font=\footnotesize]{$\alpha$} (Y);
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
first, remove the effect of the observed confounders $X$
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=.6,yscale=.7,inner sep=0pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline={(0,.3)}]
  \node[circle,draw,densely dotted,inner sep=1pt] (A) at (1,1) {$\tilde A$};
  \node (T) at (0,0) {$\tilde T$};
  \node (Y) at (2,0) {$\tilde Y$};
  \draw[->] (A) to node[pos=.75,anchor=south,font=\footnotesize,inner sep=2pt]{$\gamma$} (T);
  \draw[->] (A) to node[pos=.75,anchor=south,font=\footnotesize,inner sep=2pt]{$\delta$} (Y);
  \draw[->] (T) to node[anchor=north,font=\footnotesize]{$\alpha$} (Y);
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
then, project $\tilde Y$ on $\tilde T$ to get
\begin{align*}
  \beta &= \E[ \tilde Y \tilde D ] / \E[ \tilde D ^2 ] = \alpha + \phi \\
  \phi &= \dfrac{ \delta \gamma }{ \gamma^2 + \var [ \epsilon_T ] } =
  \text{omitted confounder bias.}
\end{align*}

Assumptions on the size of $\delta$ and $\gamma$ give bounds
on $\phi$
(alternatively, you can make assumptions on the $R^2$ of those models).

\textbf{12b.}
In presence of an \d{instrumental variable} (IV)
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=1.5,yscale=.8,inner sep=0pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline={(0,.3)}]
  \node[circle,draw,densely dotted,inner sep=1pt] (A) at (1,1) {$A$};
  \node (T) at (1,0) {$T$};
  \node (Y) at (2,0) {$Y$};
  \node (Z) at (0,0) {$Z$};
  \node (X) at (0,1) {$X$};
  \draw[->] (Z) to node[anchor=north,font=\footnotesize]{$\beta$} (T);
  \draw[->] (T) to node[anchor=north,font=\footnotesize]{$\alpha$} (Y);
  \draw[->] (X) -- (Z);
  \draw[->] (X) -- (Y);
  \draw[->] (X) -- (Y);
  \draw[->] (A) -- (T);
  \draw[->] (A) -- (Y);
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
the effect $\alpha$ of the treatment $T$
on the outcome $Y$ is identifiable
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=1.5,yscale=.7,inner sep=0pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline={(0,.3)}]
  \node[circle,draw,densely dotted,inner sep=1pt] (A) at (1,1) {$\tilde A$};
  \node (T) at (1,0) {$\tilde T$};
  \node (Y) at (2,0) {$\tilde Y$};
  \node (Z) at (0,0) {$\tilde Z$};
  \draw[->] (Z) to node[anchor=north,font=\footnotesize]{$\beta$} (T);
  \draw[->] (T) to node[anchor=north,font=\footnotesize]{$\alpha$} (Y);
  \draw[->] (A) -- (T);
  \draw[->] (A) -- (Y);
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
e.g., with the score
$\psi = ( \tilde Y - \alpha \tilde T ) \tilde Z $,
leading to
\[ \alpha = \dfrac{ \E[ \tilde Y \tilde Z ] }{ \E[ \tilde T \tilde Z ] } \]
(double machine learning is applicable)
or directly, by estimating $\beta \alpha$ and $\beta$:
\begin{align*}
  \beta\alpha &= \dfrac{ \E[ \tilde Y \tilde Z ] }{ \E[ \tilde Z ^2 ] } \\
  \beta       &= \dfrac{ \E[ \tilde T \tilde Z ] }{ \E[ \tilde Z ^2 ] } \\
  \beta\alpha &= \dfrac{ \beta\alpha}{ \beta } = \dfrac{ \E[ \tilde Y \tilde Z ] }{ \E[ \tilde T \tilde Z ] }.
\end{align*}
(Some of this can be generalized to non-linear IV models.)

\textbf{12c.} \emph{Proxies} of unobserved confounders can also help.
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=1.2,yscale=.7,inner sep=0pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline={(0,.3)}]
  \node[circle,draw,densely dotted,inner sep=1pt] (A) at (1,1) {$A$};
  \node (Q) at (0,1)  {$Q$};
  \node (S) at (2,1)  {$S$};
  \node (T) at (0,0)  {$T$};
  \node (Y) at (2,0)  {$Y$};
  \node (X) at (1,-1) {$X$};
  \draw[->] (A) -- (Q);
  \draw[->] (A) -- (S);
  \draw[->] (A) -- (T);
  \draw[->] (A) -- (Y);
  \draw[->] (Q) -- (T);
  \draw[->] (S) -- (Y);
  \draw[->] (T) -- (Y);
  \draw[->] (X) -- (T);
  \draw[->] (X) -- (Q);
  \draw[->] (X) -- (A);
  \draw[->] (X) -- (S);
  \draw[->] (X) -- (Y);
\end{tikzpicture}
\qquad\qquad
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=1.2,yscale=.7,inner sep=0pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline={(0,.3)}]
  \node[circle,draw,densely dotted,inner sep=1pt] (A) at (1,1) {$\tilde A$};
  \node (Q) at (0,1)  {$\tilde Q$};
  \node (S) at (2,1)  {$\tilde S$};
  \node (T) at (0,0)  {$\tilde T$};
  \node (Y) at (2,0)  {$\tilde Y$};
  \draw[->] (A) -- (Q);
  \draw[->] (A) -- (S);
  \draw[->] (A) -- (T);
  \draw[->] (A) -- (Y);
  \draw[->] (Q) -- (T);
  \draw[->] (S) -- (Y);
  \draw[->] (T) -- (Y);
\end{tikzpicture}
\]

% Not clear (p. 333)

\textbf{13.} With \emph{weak instruments}
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=1.2,yscale=.7,inner sep=0pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline={(0,.3)}]
  \node (Z) at (0,0)  {$\tilde Z$};
  \node (T) at (1,0)  {$\tilde T$};
  \node (Y) at (2,0)  {$\tilde Y$};
  \draw[->] (Z) -- (T);
  \draw[->] (T) to node[anchor=south,inner sep=1pt]{\footnotesize$\alpha$} (Y);
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
\[ \hat\alpha = \dfrac{ \E[ \tilde Z \tilde Y ] }{ \E[ \tilde Z \tilde T ] }, \quad \E[ \tilde Z \tilde T ] \approx 0 , \]
use \emph{Neyman's statistic}
\[ C(\alpha) = \dfrac{
  \E \bigl[ ( \tilde Y - \alpha \tilde T ) \tilde Z \bigr] ^2
}{
  \var \bigl[ ( \tilde Y - \alpha \tilde T ) \tilde Z \bigr]
} \sim N(0,1) ^2 = \chi^2(1)
\]
to test for $H_0 : \alpha = \alpha_0$
and derive confidence intervals.
This generalizes to other score functions $\psi$;
in dimension $m$:
\begin{align*}
  M(\theta) &= \E \bigl[ \psi( W, \theta, \hat\eta ) \bigr] \\
  \Omega(\theta) &= \var \bigl[ \psi( W, \theta, \hat\eta) \bigr] \\
  C(\theta) &= M(\theta) ' \Omega(\theta)^{-1} M(\theta) \sim \chi^2(m)
\end{align*}

\textbf{14.}
``Heterogeneous treatment effects'' refers to the CATE, as opposed to the ATE.
\begin{align*}
  \text{ATE} &= \E[ Y^{(1)} - Y^{(0)} ] \\
  \text{CATE} &= \E[ Y^{(1)} - Y^{(0)} | X = x ] \\
\end{align*}
DML works out of the box:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item For models $\hat\mu$ and $\hat g$ to estimate
  \begin{align*}
    \mu(Z) &= P[ T=1 | Z ] \\
    g(Y,Z) &= \E[ Y | T, Z ]
  \end{align*}
\item Set
  \begin{align*}
    \hat Y_i &= H_1 \bigl( Y_1 - \hat g_k(T_i,Z_i) \bigr) + \hat g_k(1,Z_i) - \hat g_k(0,Z_i) \\
    H_i &= \dfrac{ T_1 }{ \hat \mu_k ( Z_i ) } - \dfrac{ 1 - T_i }{ 1 - \hat \mu_k(Z_i) }
  \end{align*}
  where $\hat g_k$, $\hat \mu_k$ are estimated without the fold containing $i$;
\item Regress (linearly, if you want confidence intervals)
  $\hat Y \sim X$.
\end{compactitem}

To get valid confidence intervals with random forests, use \emph{honest forests}:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Use a separate sample to build the tree and to compute the leaf estimates;
\item Use smaller subsamples, without replacement, instead of boostrap samples.
\end{compactitem}

% Difference with causal forests?

Alternatively, one can look for a \emph{policy}
$\pi: X \mapsto \pi(X) \in [0,1]$
indicating who to treat, maximizing
\[ V(\pi) = \E \bigl[ \pi(X) \bigl( Y^{(1)} - Y^{(0)} \bigr]. \]
This does not give a full picture of treatment heterogeneity:
if the effects are all negative,
$\pi = 0$.
Instead, one can look for a quantile-constrained optimal policy
\[ \operatorname*{Maximize}_\pi \E \bigl[  \pi(X) \bigl( Y^{(1)} - Y^{(0)} \bigr]
\ \text{ st } \ \E \bigl[ \pi(X) \bigr] = q. \]

\textbf{15.} \emph{Meta learning} decomposes the CATE estimation into a sequence of regression problems.
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item S (single) learner
  \begin{align*}
    \hat g(T,Z) &\approx \E[ Y | T, Z ] \\
    \hat \tau (X) &\approx \E\bigl[ \hat g(1,Z) - \hat g(0,Z) | X \bigr]
  \end{align*}
\item T (two) learner
  \begin{align*}
    \hat g_1(Z) &\approx \E[ Y | Z, T=1] \\
    \hat g_0(Z) &\approx \E[ Y | Z, T=0 ] \\
    \hat \tau (X) &\approx \E\bigl[ \hat g_1(Z) - \hat g_0(Z) | X ]
  \end{align*}
\item DR (double robust)
  \begin{align*}
    \hat g_1(Z) &\approx \E[ Y | Z, T=1] \\
    \hat g_0(Z) &\approx \E[ Y | Z, T=0 ] \\
    \hat \mu(Z) &\approx \E[ T | Z ] \\
    \hat g(T,Z) &= \hat g_1(Z) T + \hat g_0(Z) (1-T) \\
    H &= \dfrac{ T }{ \hat \mu(Z) } - \dfrac{ 1 - T }{ 1 - \hat \mu(Z) } \\
    \hat Y &= H \bigl( Y - \hat g(T,Z) \bigr) + \hat g_1(Z) - \hat g_0(Z) \\
    \hat\tau(X) &\approx \E[ \hat Y | X ]
  \end{align*}
\item R-learner
  \begin{align*}
    \hat h(Z) &\approx \E[ Y | Z ] \\
    \hat \mu(Z) &\approx \E[ T | Z ] \\
    \check Y &= Y - \hat h(Z) \\
    \check T &= T - \hat \mu(Z) \\
    \hat\tau(X) &= \dfrac{ \E[ \check T ^2 \cdot \check Y / \check T | X ] }{ \E[ \check T ^2 | X ] }
  \end{align*}
  ($\hat\tau$ is a regression $\check Y / \check T \sim X$, with weights $\check T^2$);
\item X-learner
  \begin{align*}
    \hat g_1(Z) &\approx \E[ Y | Z, T=1] \\
    \hat g_0(Z) &\approx \E[ Y | Z, T=0 ] \\
    \hat \mu(Z) &\approx \E[ T | Z ] \\
    \hat\delta_0(Z) &\approx \E[ g_1(Z) - Y | Z, T=0 ] \\
    \hat\delta_1(Z) &\approx \E[ g_0(Z) - Y | Z, T=1 ] \\
    \hat\delta(Z) &= \delta_1(Z) \bigl( 1 - \hat\mu(Z) \bigr) + \delta_0(Z) \hat\mu(Z) \\
    \hat\tau(X) &\approx \E[ \hat\delta(Z) | X ].
  \end{align*}
\end{compactitem}
Which model is best depends on the data
(imbalance, how complicated $\hat g$ and $\hat \mu$ are, etc.) --
try cross-validation and ensembling.

The optimal (unconstrained) policy only looks at the sign of the CATE
\[ \pi^*** (X) = \mathbf{1}_{ \tau(X) \geq 0 } \]
but the misclassification cost is sample-dependent:
it is a cost-sensitive classification problem.
You may need to penalize the variance of the policy.

\textbf{16.}
With longitudinal data, under the conditional parallel trends assumption
(parallel trends for $T=0$ and $T=1$, once we condition on $Z$),
DML (double/debiased machine learning)
can compute the ATET (DiD, difference in differences).

\textbf{17.}
If the treatment is assigned (deterministically) by some score
$T = \mathbf{1}_{ X \geq c}$
(as in regression discontinuity design, RDD),
observations close to the threshold are comparable.
\begin{align*}
  \tau_{\text{RD}} &= \E\bigl[ Y(1) - Y(0) | X = c ] \\
  &= \lim_{ x \to c^+ } \E[ Y | X = x ] - \lim_{ x \to c^- } \E[ Y | X = x ]
\end{align*}

*** Causal discovery (?), RKHS

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Universality, characteristic kernels\\ and RKHS embeddings of measures}\\
  B.K. Sriperumbudur et al.
\end{book}
A kernel on $X$ is a positive definite function
$\kappa : X \times X \to \RR$.
I defines a subspace
\[ \text{Span} \{ \kappa(\bigcdot , x ) , \ x \in X \} \subset \mathscr F( X , \RR ). \]
It is \emph{universal} if
\[ \mathscr H_0 = \overline{
  \text{Span} \{ \kappa(\bigcdot, x) , \ x \in X_0 \}
} = \mathscr F, \]
for some $X_0 \subset X$
and some Hilbert space $(\mathscr F , \n\cdot)$ of functions on $X$.

It is \emph{characteristic} if
\[ \left\{
\begin{array}{rcl}
  \text{Borel}(X) & \longrightarrow & \mathscr H \\
  P & \longmapsto & \displaystyle \int_X \kappa( \bigcdot , x ) d P(x)
\end{array}
\right. \]
is injective.

Examples include:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item $\mathscr C^0(X)$ and $\n\cdot _\infty$;
\item $\mathscr C^0(X)$ and compact convergence;
\item $\mathscr C_0(X)$ (continuous functions vanishing at infinity) and $\n\cdot _\infty$;
\item $L^0$ and $\n\cdot _p$
\end{compactitem}

with \texttt{dowhy-gcm}, it can answer causal queries:

causal strength, root cause analysis, etc.

* TODO LLM

** LLMs as optimizers

CMA-ES is a simple, population-based optimization algorithm:
Start with random candidates, sampled from a Gaussian distribution;
Keep the best ones;
Fir a Gaussian distribution to them;
Iterate, with that new Gaussian distribution.

The same idea can be applied to LLMs, for instance, for symbolic regression:
Ask the LLM to give a few functions (as Python code) to approximate the data (it does not even need to see the data, but some summary statistics may help);
Compute the error on the data;
Give the error to the LLM and ask it to improve the functions it gave;
Iterate until the solutions are satisfactory.

  \emph{Data-driven discovery of dynamical systems\\ in pharmacology using large language models}\\
  S. Holt et al.

  \emph{Language model cross-over:\\ variation through few-shot prompting}\\
  E. Leyerson et al.

The same idea can be usd to optimize the prompts for an LLM, if you have an objective way of assessing the quality of the answer.

  \emph{Optimizing instructions and demonstrations form multi-stage language model programs}\\
  K. Opsahl-Ong et al.

  \emph{DSPy: compiling declarative language model calls into self-improving pipelines}\\
  O. Khattab et al.

To solve combinatorial problems or find numeric bounds for some mathematical problems,
you can ask, not for a siolution, but for a Python program to search for a solution.

** TODO Scientific Research
*** LLM, AlphaEvolve

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{AlphaEvolve: a coding agent\\ for scientific and algorithmic discovery}\\
  A. Novikov et al.
\end{book}
AlphaEvolve uses an LLM to solve combinatorial problems or find upper and lower bounds, e.g.,
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Maximum number of unit spheres tangent to a unit sphere in dimension $d$ (kissing number);
\item Size of the smallest hexagon in which we can put $n$ unit hexagons;
\item Minimum ratio between the maximum and minimum pairwise distance between $n$ points in dimension $d$;
\item \( \max\limits_{f \geq 0} \dfrac{ \n{ f *** f }_2 }{ \n{ f*f }_1 \n{ f*f }_\infty } \)
  (using step functions);
\item $ \min\limits_{\substack{f\text{ even}\\ f(0), \hat f(o) > 0 }} A(f) A(\hat f)$
  (uncertainty inequality), where
  \[ A(f) = \{ r > - \ :\ \forall x \quad \va x > r \implies f(x) \geq 0 \}; \]
\item Matrix multiplication algorithms (slight improvement on Strassen's algorithm).
\end{compactitem}

AlphaEvolve does not evolve a solution, or even code to build a solution, but
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Code to \emph{search} for a solution;
\item Prompts to ask it to search for a solution.
\end{compactitem}
It is provided with context, sample solution, evaluation function, sample prompts.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{OpenEvolve:\\ an opensource evolutionary coding agent}\\
  A. Sharma (2025)
\end{book}
Open source implementation of Google's AlphaEvolve.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{ShinkaEvolve: towards open-ended\\ and sample-efficient program evolution}\\
  R.T. Lange et al.
\end{book}
Make LLM-based evolutionary algorithms (such as AlphaEvolve) more sample-efficient:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Keep independent island subpopulations;
\item Select the parents with power law sampling,
  from the fitness ranks,
  or weighted sampling,
  $w_i = s_i h_i$, where $s_i$ is a sigmoid-transformed fitness rank,
  and $h_i$ a novelty measure,
  $(1 + \#\text{offsprings})^{-1}$;
\item Use novelty rejection sampling
  (cosine similarity of code embeddings, with lack of novelty confirmed by an LLM call if $\cos > 0.95$;
\item Select which LLM to use with UCB1,
  using the relative improvement as reward;
\item Keep the multiobjective assessment of each solution (metrics + textual description)
  in the population archive, and use it as context;
\item Every $T$ generations,
  have the LLM review recent solutions and issue actionable recommendations.
\end{compactitem}

To optimize prompts and select few-shot samples for an LLM pipeline,
MIPRO (implemented in \texttt{dspy})
generates prompts with another LLM in a CMAES-like fashion,
and selects few-shot samples at random among the best performing inputs;
the optimization hyperparameters (number of few-shot samples, presence of a dataset summary, instruction tips
(e.g., prompt for the prompt-generating LLM, chosen among 6), model, temperature, etc.)
are chosen by \texttt{optuna}.

\texttt{dspy.MIPROv2} needs the pipeline to optimize, inputs, and a metric.

*** DONE LLM, Optimization, Trees

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Decision tree induction through LLMs\\ via semantically-aware evolution}\\
  T. Liu et al.
\end{book}
Decision trees are often built greedily.
Use genetic programming with LLMs to build them, leveraging the semantic prior they provide:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Crossover: ``The problem is (...), the variables are (...);
  here are a few decision trees and their fitness: generate a different, interpretable decision tree with improved fitness'';
\item Mutation: ``(...) generate a diverse decision tree that is more interpretable''.
\end{compactitem}
Those operators are not limited to 1 (mutation) or 2 (crossover) parents.

Baselines include:
CART (sklearn),
\texttt{c45-decision-tree},
\texttt{gosdt-guesses} (sparse),
\texttt{dl8.5} (cynamic programming),
\texttt{gatree} (genetic algorithm).

*** LLM, RAG

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Learning to tokenize for general retrieval}\\
  W. Sun et al.
\end{book}
Sparse retrieval relies on inverted indices (TF-IDF, BM25, etc.).
Dense retrieval compares queries and document embeddings.
Generative retrieval replaces the dense embeddings with discrete ones -- lists of ``tokens''.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Retrieval augmented generation\\ for large language models: a survey}\\
  Y. Gao et al. (2023)
\end{book}
RAG can suffer from low precision, low recall, irrelevance, hallucinations:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Preprocess the chunks: remove irrelevant or duplicate information
  (e.g., boilerplate text, special characters);
  rewrite to eliminate ambiguity; adjust chunk size;
\item Align query and documents:
  given a document,
  ask for corresponding queries and use them to compute the embedding;
  conversely, given a query, ask for (hallicinated) documents answering it,
  and query the vector database with them;
\item Add metadata to the retrieved documents;
\item Use a customized (domain-specific) embedding;
\item Re-rank;
\item Reduce noise in the retrieved documents and compress them (reduce the context length):
  remove irrelevant context, highlight pivotal paragraphs;
  ask the LLM to rewrite the context;
\item Hybrid search (vector + keywords);
\item Recursive retrieval: first retrieve small chunks, to capture key information, then larger blocks for more context;
\item Ask the LLM to ``step back and reason about the general underlying concepts or principles'';
\item Let the LLM populate its own database;
\item Ask the LLM to assess the relevance of the retrieved documents;
\item Rewrite the query; try several formulations;
\item Compute the embeddings on small chunks, but retrieve larger chunks (e.g., abstracts vs full papers);
\item etc.
\end{compactitem}

*** CoT
\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Forest-of-Thought: scaling test-time compute for enhancing LLM reasoning}\\
  Z. Bi et al.
\end{book}
Chain-of-Thought (CoT) splits a problem into a (linear) sequence of steps.

Tree-of-Thought (ToT) explores several alternatives (and needs to prune the tree and eventually select the best path).

Graph-of-Though (GoT) also allows merging of the tree branches (for problems with overlapping subtasks).

Forest-of-Thought (FoT) examines several trees and decides on the final output by voting or sparse gating.

*** LLM, few-shot vs 0-shot

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Prompt programming for large language models: beyond the few-shot paradigm}\\
  L. Reynolds and K. McDonell
\end{book}

*** LLM, PEFT, Soft prompts

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Scaling down to scale up:\\ a guide to parameter-efficient fine-tuning}\\
  V. Lialin et al.
\end{book}
There are many parameter efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) methods:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item \emph{Adapters} are small fully-connected layers (or MoE layers),
  added (for instance) after transformers;
\item \emph{Soft prompts} are prompt prefixes added,
  not as input tokens,
  but in latent space (either only for the input layer,
  or for all layers);
\item Selective methods only fine-tune some of the parameters,
  e.g., only the last layers, or only the biases,
  or a sparse subset of parameters (subset of important rows and columns, learned mask);
\item Reparametrization methods alter the weights $W$,
  e.g., $W \leftarrow W + AB'$ (LoRA),
  $W = W + A \otimes B$ or
  $W = W + \text{fastfood}(A)$.
\end{compactitem}
Those can be combined.

The time vectors are $W_t - W$, where $W$ are the model weights and $W_t$ the weights after finetuning on data from period $t$.
Use them to shift the model's attention to another period, e.g., $W + (W_{t_1} - W_{t_0})$.

** TODO Data
*** Data mixing

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Chameleon: a flexible data-mixing framework for language model pretraining and finetuning}\\
  W. Xie et al. (2025)
\end{book}
In linear regression, the hat matrix is
\[ H = X (X' X)^{-1} X'; \]
it satisfies $\hat y = H y$.
The \emph{leverages} are its diagonal components,
\[ H_{ii} = \dfrac{ \partial \hat y_i }{ \partial y_i }. \]
For ridge regression,
$H = X( X' X + \lambda I ) ^{-1}  X'$,
but since
\[ A ( BA   + \lambda I )^{-1} = (AB + \lambda I )^{-1} A, \]
it can be written
\begin{align*}
  H &= XX' ( XX' + \lambda I )^{-1} \\
  &= \Omega ( \Omega + \lambda I )^{-1} \\
  \Omega &= XX'
\end{align*}
and generalized to kernelized ridge regression
with $\Omega_{ij} = \kappa( x_i, x_j) $.

To find data mixing weights:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Train a proxy model on all the data, with uniform weights, $\alpha_i = 1/k$.
\item Compute the average embedding (from a midlayer's activations) of each dataset
  \[ x_i = \operatorname*{Mean}_{ a \in D_i } h_\theta^{(L)} (a) \]
  (a random sample of documents $a \in D_i$ suffices);
\item Compute the corresponding affinity between datasets
  $\Omega = XX'$, \ie,
  $\Omega_{ij} = \kappa(x_i, x_j) = x_i ' x_j $
  and the leverage score $H_{ii}$ of each dataset
  \[ H = \Omega ( \Omega + \lambda k I)^{-1}; \]
\item Use the dataset weights
  \begin{align*}
    \alpha_i^{\text{pretraining}} &\propto \exp H_{ii}^{-1} \quad \text{(high-density regions)} \\
    \alpha_i^{\text{fine-tuning}} &\propto \exp H_{ii}.
  \end{align*}
\end{compactitem}

Meta-domains are $k$-means clusters of \texttt{bge-small} document embeddings ($k=260$, context size = 512).
The weights come from a classifier (\texttt{Qwen2-1.5b}).

** TODO Models
*** GRPO, DeepSeek

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{DeepSeek-R1: incentivizing reasoning capability in LLMs via reinforcement learning}
\end{book}
DeepSeek-R1-Zero uses RL (GRPO) on DeepSeek-V3-Base to enhance its reasoning capabilities:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Prompts: unspecified (math, leetcode, etc.);
\item Reward: only accuracy (correct final answer) and format, as specified by the prompt:\\
  \texttt{<think>...</think>} and \texttt{<answer>...</answer>}.
\end{compactitem}
During training, the model spends more and more time thinking,
but the \texttt{<think>} section becomes less and less readable
(it mixes Chinese and English).

DeepSeek-R1 uses 4 steps:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item SUpervised fine-tuning (SFT)
  with high-quality data, e.g., readable answers from DeepSeek-R1-Zero;
\item RL, as above;
\item SFT, with 600k reasoning samples (ask the model to generate several answers,
  and have DeepSeek-V3 select the best)
  and 200k non-reasoning samples from (DeepSeek-V3, unspecified prompts);
\item RL, for alignment (unspecified prompts and rewards).
\end{compactitem}

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{DeepSeekMath: pushing the limits\\ of mathematical reasoning\\ in open language models}\\
  Z. Shao et al.
\end{book}
Start with a code model, and fine-tune it on a large mathematical corpus:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Train a FastText classifier on OpenWebMath;
\item Apply it to CommonCrawl;
\item Use the results to identify websites with math contents;
\item Add the corresponding pages to the FastText training data
  (adding arxiv papers does not bring any improvement);
  re-train;
\item To avoid benchmark contamination, exclude pages containing a 10-gram from one of the benchmarks;
\item Apply the new model to CommonCrawl.
\end{compactitem}

Also evaluate on miniF2F (convert an informal proof into a formal one, checked with Isabelle)
and on GSM8K (math with tool use: Python, SymPy).

In PPO,
\[ J = \E_{\substack{
    q \sim \text{queries} \\ o \sim \pi_{\text{old}}( \bigcdot|q) }}
\operatorname*{Mean}_{ 1 \leq t \leq \va o }
\dfrac{
  \pi_\theta ( o_t | q , o_{<t} )
}{
  \pi_{\text{old}} ( o_t | q , o_{<t} )
} - \beta \text{KL}( \pi_\theta \| \pi_{\text{ref}} )
\]
GRPO (group relative polici optimization)
replaces the advantage $A$ with the average reward from $\pi_{\text{old}}$,
\[ A_i = \dfrac{ r_i - \text{mean}( \mathbf r ) }{ \text{std}( \mathbf r ) } \]
and uses
\[ \text{KL} ( \pi_\theta \| \pi_{\text{ref}} ) =
\dfrac{ \pi_{\text{ref}}(\cdots) }{ \pi_\theta( \cdots ) } - \log
\dfrac{ \pi_{\text{ref}}(\cdots) }{ \pi_\theta( \cdots ) } - 1. \]

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{DeepSeek-V3 technical report}\\
\end{book}
DeepSeek-V3 combines:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item MoE (top-k and shared experts);
\item No auxilliary loss to ensure load balancing between experts replaced by a bias term for each expert;
\item Sequence-wise auxilliary loss;
\item Multi-head laent attentio (MLA): joint low-rank compression of $K$ and $V$ to reduce the size of the KV cache;
\item Multi-token predictin;
\item FP8 mixed precision (FP8, BF16, FP32);
\item Context length extension (first 32k, then 128k);
\item Many parallelism and communication improvements).
\end{compactitem}
Training required 2 months on 2048 GPUs (\$5m).

*** Models

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Nomic Embed: training a reproducible\\ long context text embedder}\\
  Z. Nussbaum et al.
\end{book}
Open-source 8192-context-length embedding model,
with \emph{task-specific prefixes},
to break the symmetry of the encoder (we want a different model
for questions and answers, such that the embeddings match).
Tricks include: RoPE, SwiGLU, Flash Attention, AdamW, gradient accumulation, etc.

*** LLM, Bytes instead of tokens

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Byte latent transformer:\\ patches scale better than tokens}\\
  A. Pagnoni et al.
\end{book}
Train a small transformer to forecast the next byte,
from the current and previous bytes,
with \emph{hash} n-gram embeddings, $3 \leq n \leq 8$.
Use it to split the input into variable-length \emph{patches}
(whenever the entropy of the next byte is above some threshold or increases too much).
Feed those patches to a large transformer,
to forecast the next patch.
Decode the patches into bytes, with a small transformer, still using the entropy
to decide when patches end.

The model scales better than tokenizer-based transformers,
and excells at character-level tasks.

*** LLM: BERT variants

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{DeBERTa: decoding-enhanced BERT\\ with disentangled attention}\\
  P. He et al.
\end{book}
BERT model with disentangled attention (content and position embeddings remain separate --
content-content and content-position attention)
and absolute word position embeddings just before the softmax layer.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Electra: pre-training text encoders\\ as discriminators rather than generators}\\
  K. Clark et al.
\end{book}
BERT model trained with a GAN-like task, replaced token detection (RTD) instead of masked language modeling (MLM).
The replaced tokens come from a generator (e.g., a small MLM) trained with maximum likelihood (not adversarially).

\nextbook

*** LLM: NER

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{GLiNER: generalist model for named entity recognition using bidirectional transformer}\\
  U. Zaratiana et al. (2024)
\end{book}
Zero-shot NER with a BERT model:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Compute embeddings $p_i$ of entity types and $h_i$ of sentence tokens;
\item Apply FFNs to have embeddings $\text{FFN}(p_i)$ and $\text{FFN}(h_i,h_j)$,
  ($i<j$, $\va{j-i}<12$) of entity types and spans;
\item Compute the similarity (sigmoid of scalar product)
  between entity types and spans;
\item Return the highest scoring non-overlapping spans
  (you may also allow fully nested spans).
\end{compactitem}

The model is trained on Pile-NER
(50,000 texts from the Pile, annotated by ChatGPT, with open-ended entity types).
Implementation available in \texttt{gliner}.

\begin{book}
  \emph{UniversalNER: targeted distilation\\ from large language models\\ for open named entity recognition}\\
  W. Zhou et al.
\end{book}
\emph{Targeted} distillation of ChatGPT onto Llama for Open NER.

*** LLM training: SFT/RL mix

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Stepwise adaptive integration of supervised\\ fine-tuning and reinforcement learning\\ for task-specific LLMs}\\
  J. Chen et al.
\end{book}
Instead of switching abruptly from SFT to RL (RLHF or GRPO),
blend the two losses by looking at
$\text{KL}( \pi_\theta \| \pi_{ \text{DFT data} } )$
to avoid overfitting (too much SFT)
and mode collapse (too much RL).

*** LLM, KG

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{KaLM: knowledge-aligned autoregressive language modeling via dual view\\ knowledge graph contrastive learning}\\
  P. Yu et al.
\end{book}
Fine-tune (LoRA) an LLM with a knowledge graph
(triples, from Wordnet (WN18RR) or Freebase (FB15k-237),
augmented with inverses
$(h,r,t) \mapsto (t,r^{-1},h)$)
by combining an explicit contrastive loss (InfoNCE)
to push together (pull apart) matching (non-matching) pairs
(head+relation, tail)
and an implicit loss (``given the head entity and relation,
write a tail entity that completes the triple'').

This also mitigates \emph{representation anisotropy},
the tendency of learned tokens to be confined to a narrow cone.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Safety alignment should be made\\ more than just a few tokens deep}\\
  X. Qi et al.
\end{book}
Safety alignment of LLMs with RLHF is shallow: it just trains the
model to identify harmful requests and to \emph{start} the answer with
a refusal (``I cannot'', etc.).
Those models are suceptible to \emph{prefilling attacks}
(provide the begining of the answers, e.g., ``Sure, here is'' and let the model complete it).

*** Unlearning

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{GRU: mitigating the trade-off between unlearning and retention for LLMs}\\
  Y. Wang et al. (2025)
\end{book}
Adjust the unlearning gradient $g_u$ to prevent it from going against the retain gradient $g_r$,
\[ g = \operatorname{Argmin} \n{ g - g_u }^2 \ \text{ st }\  \langle g , g_r \rangle \geq 0 \]
(add EWMA and gradient clipping for better convergence).

*** Jailbreak

\nextbook

  \emph{FlipAttack: jailbreal LLMs via flipping}\\
\end{book}
Jailbreak an LLM by flipping words and/or characters in the prompt,

*** LLM, attacks

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Stealing part of a production language model}\\
  N. Carlini et al.
\end{book}
With access to the logits of an LLM,
one can recover the dimension of the last weight matrix.
The final layer is a projection from the hidden latent dimension to a \emph{higher} dimensional logit vector:
the dimension of the span of the logit vectors is the dimension of the last layer;
estimate it by looking at the singular value of the matrix $Q$ of logits.
The SVD, $Q = U \Sigma V^\top$
also gives the weight matrix, $W = U \Sigma$ (up to symmetries).
The attack still works without direct access to the logits, but is more expensive.

** TODO NLP
*** NLP

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{FastTExt.zip:\\ compressing text classification models}\\
  A. Joulin et al.
\end{book}
Linear models for text classification with $n$-gram features
are easy to train, perform well, but are very large:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Use product quantization (PQ: $k$-means on subsets of the coordinates)
  or orthogonal product quantization
  (OPQ: PQ after applying a learned rotation),
  separating norm and direction of the vectors (the norms span 3 orders of magnitude),
  and retrain the layers after quantization;
\item Prune the vocabulary (largest weightes, ensuring there is at least
  an $n$-gram in each document);
  keep the $n$-grams selected in a \texttt{set} (a Bloom filter degrades performance);
\item Use Vowpal Wabbit's hashing trick to store the weights.
\end{compactitem}

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Enriching word vectors\\ with subword information}\\
  P. Bojanowski et al.
\end{book}
Represent words as bags of character $n$-grams ($3\leq n \leq 6$);
use skipgram to learn the subword representations and add them.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Bag of tricks for efficient text classification}\\
  A. Joulin et al.
\end{book}
\texttt{fastText} is a linear model for text classification,
useing
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Word and $n$-gram embeddings as features;
\item A low-rank constraint
  \[ \operatorname*{Maximize}_{A,B} \operatorname*{Mean} _n y_n \log \text{softmax}( B A x_n ) \]
\item If there are many classes, a hierarchical softmax based on the Huffman coding tree.
\end{compactitem}

*** LoRa variants

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{FRUGAL: Memory-efficient optimization by reducing state overhead for scalable training}\\
  P. Zmushko et al. (2025)
\end{book}
\textsc{Frugal} combines low-dimensional (LoRA) stateful (Adam) updates for some of the parameters,
and full stateless (SGD, SignSGD) for the others;
the stateful and stateless subspaces are changed after a certain number of steps, to better explore the parameter space.

ReLoRA periodically merges the LoRA weights into the base model
and resets them.

DoRA extends LoRA to continual learning,
by encouraging updates orthogonal to those of the previous task.

GaLore reduces the memory requirement of gradient based optimization by replacing the gradients with low-rank approximations.

Other PEFT (parameter-efficient fine-tuning) methods include
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Adapters (small feed-forward layers between layers);
\item Trained soft prompts;
\item BitFit: only re-train the biases;
\item QLoRA.
\end{compactitem}

SignSGD only uses the sign of the gradient.

AdamW decouples weight updates and weight decay.

Low-memory Adam variants (Adammini) include:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Blockwise second moments: MicroAdam, BAdam;
\item Chunking (only one second moment per block): Adamem;
\item Factorization of the second moments:
  Fira (rank-1), LDAdam, AdaFactor (for matrix-shaped weights, combine row- and column-wise second moments: $r_t \otimes c_t$).
\end{compactitem}

*** LMO

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Training deep learning models\\ with norm-constrained LMOs}\\
  T. Pethick et al. (2025)
\end{book}
The \emph{linear minimization oracle} is
\[ \text{lmo}(s) = \operatorname{Argmin}_{ \n x \leq \rho } \langle s , x \rangle \]
where $\n \cdot$ is the $\ell^1$, $\ell^2$, $\ell^\infty$ or spectral norm.
\[  % Linear minimization oracle (LMO)

    \node[anchor=north east] at (-0.94868,-0.31623) {\footnotesize$x$};
    \node at (0,-1.8) {$\ell^2$};
  \end{scope}
  \begin{scope}[shift={(5,0)}]
    \fill[black!10] (-1,0) -- (0,1) -- (1,0) -- (0,-1) -- cycle;

    \node at (0,-1.8) {$\ell^1$};
  \end{scope}
  \begin{scope}[shift={(10,0)}]
    \fill[black!10] (-1,-1) -- (-1,1) -- (1,1) -- (1,-1) -- cycle;
    \draw (-1.3,0) -- (1.3,0);
    \draw (0,-1.3) -- (0,1.3);
    \node[circle,fill] (s) at (1.5,.5) {};
    \node[circle,fill] (x) at (-1,-1) {};
    \draw[densely dotted] (s) -- (-1,-.33333,0);
    \node[anchor=south west] at (1.5,.5) {\footnotesize$s$};
    \node[anchor=north east] at (-1,-1) {\footnotesize$x$};
    \node at (0,-1.8) {$\ell^\infty$};
  \end{scope}
\end{tikzpicture}
\]

Stochastic conditional gradient is
\begin{align*}
  g &\leftarrow \text{gradient}(x) \\
  d &\leftarrow (1-\alpha) d + \alpha g \\
  x &\leftarrow (1-\gamma) x + \gamma \, \text{lmo}(d).
\end{align*}

*** Muon

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Practical efficiency of Muon for pretraining}\\
  Essential AI
\end{book}
Muon updates matrix-shaped weights $W$ as
\begin{align*}
  G &\leftarrow \text{gradient} \in \RR^{m\times n} \\
  O &\leftarrow \operatorname*{Argmin}_{ \n{ O }_2 \leq 1 } \tr ( G' O ) \\
  W &\leftarrow W - \eta O
\end{align*}
(add momentum, learning rate scaling, weight decay).
The direction $O$ can be computed as
$O = U V$
where $G = U \Sigma V'$ is the SVD
or with \emph{Newton-Schluz iterations}
\begin{align*}
  O &\leftarrow G \\
  O &\leftarrow \dfrac12 O (3 I - O^2 ) \text{ until convergence}
\end{align*}
(to compute the matrix sign function).

*** Early stopping

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Rethinking early stopping:\\ refine, then calibrate}\\
  E. Berta et al.
\end{book}
(Multiclass) classifiers minimize two quantities:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Refinement error (whether the classes are in the correct order);
\item Calibration error (whether the probabilities are correct).
\end{compactitem}
They are not simultaneously minimized during training.
Stop training when the refinement loss stops decreasing,
\[ \min_g \operatorname*{Mean}_{ (X,Y) \sim \text{Data} }
\text{loss} \bigl[ g\bigl( f(X) , Y \bigr) \bigr]  \]
where $g$ is the relalibration -- temperature scaling is good enough.

*** Sparsity (jax)

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{JaxPruner:\\ a concise library for sparsity research}\\
  J.H. Lee et al. (2024)
\end{book}
Implementation of pruning and sparse training algorithms.

*** Gradient descent with long steps

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Provably faster gradient descent via long steps}\\
  B. Grimmer
\end{book}
Gradient descent, with periodic long steps
(which may increase the loss function),
speeds up convergence (from $1/2T$ to $1/5T$)
\[
<<python-provably-faster-GD-via-long-steps,engine='python',results='hide',echo=FALSE,out.width='\\hsize',fig.show='hold'>>=

import pandas as pd

ax.axis('off')

*** No (obvious)

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Full parameter fine-tuning for\\ large language models with limited resources}\\
  K. Lv et al.
\end{book}
% LOMO
To reduce memory usage when training large models, replace the traditional
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Forward pass, keeping all the activations;
\item Backward pass, keeping all the gradients;
\item Parameter update
\end{compactitem}
with
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Forward pass
\item For each layer, starting from the last:
  \begin{compactitem}[\ensuremath{\cdot}]
  \item Compute the gradients;
  \item Update the parameters;
  \item Free all the layers after the current one (they are no longer needed).
  \end{compactitem}
\end{compactitem}

*** Loss landscape

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{The training process of many deep networks explores the same low-dimension manifold}\\
  J. Mao et al.
\end{book}
The \emph{intensive PCA} (InPCA) of a distance matrix
$ D = \bigl( d( y_i, y_j ) \bigr)_{ij} $ is the eigendecomposition of the centered distance.
\begin{align*}
  L &= I - \dfrac1n \mathbf 1 \\
  W &= -\tfrac12 L D L \\
  W &= U \Lambda U^\top \\
  X &= U \sqrt{ \va \Lambda }
\end{align*}
Coordinates corresponding to negative (resp. positive) eigenvalues
have a negative (positive)
contribution to distances.

** Portfolio optimization

If you are unfamiliar with portfolio optimization, the following paper covers the basics,
rather exhaustively, from the simple Markowitz optimization problem
to all the constraints and penalty we usually add. It focuses on convex optimization

TODO: paper (Boyd/Schmeltzer)

Here are a few books, if you want to go beyond those basics.

D. Palomar's book focuses on optimization algorithms beyond convex optimization, in particular
MM (Majorization-Minimization), SCA (sequential convex approximation),
ADMM (alternating direction of multipliers), and convex-concave fractional programs.
For instance, for a fully-invested, long-only portfolio,
we cannot add an L¹ penalty to make it sparse:
the fully-invested long-only constraint says that the L¹ norm is always 1;
instead we can use the Lᵖ norm, for 0<p<1 -- the problem is no longer convex, but just quasi-convex.

The book also has clear chapters on learning graphs from data,
and on risk parity.

with code explaining how to  use them in portfolio optimization problems.

M. Noger i Alonso's book is more finance-oriented,
but covers similar topics.

If you only read one of those books, I recommend Palomar's.

** Learning graphs from data

Given many observations from several variables (e.g., returns for several stocks),
we may want to represent the relations between those variables
as a graph.
A naive way of doing so is to compute the correlation matrix between those variables,
and add an edge for all correlations above some threshold.
But this tends to produce a dense graph:
if variables A and B are correlated, and B and C are correlated, then
A and C are correlated, even if they are idependent given C.
To address this problem and only keep "direct" correlations,
we can use the partial correlation matrix instead, which tends to be sparser
(up to sign, scale and diagonal, it is the inverse of the correlation matrix).
This is what the graphical lasso:
find a matrix close to the inverse of the sample correlation matrix,
with a sparsity penalty.

TODO: the lasso book? check which chapter it is.

There are variants of this approach, which look for the Laplacian matrix of the graph instead.
Intuitively, signals (observations, i.e., rows in the data-frame)
should vary slowly along the graph.
We can easily add penalties (for degree, number of components)
and allow for fat tail distributions.

** TODO Books
*** Portfolio construction (review)

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Markowitz portfolio construction at seventy}\\
  S. Boyd et al. (2024)
\end{book}
Long list of constraints and penalties you may want to add to your portfolio optimization
(turnover, risk budget, costs, etc.).

Replace the hard constraints with penalties to ensure the problem is always feasible
(but report constraint violations);
fine-tune the scale of the penalties on historical data.

Use robust optimization to account for the uncertainty in $\mu$ and $\Sigma$.

Some constraints are not convex:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Integral number of shares (lot shares);
\item Maximum number of assets;
\item Minimum position size;
\end{compactitem}

Do not write the target volatility constraint as
$ w' \Sigma w \leq \sigma^2$,
but $\n{ L^\top w }_2 \leq \sigma$,
where $\Sigma = L^\top L$ is the Choleski decomposition.
If $\Sigma$ comes from a factor model, $\Sigma = F v F^\top + \Delta$,
use
\begin{align*}
  w' \Sigma w &= \n{ \ell^\top w }_2^2 + \n{ \Delta^{1/2} w }_2^2 \\
  &= \n{ ( \ell^\top  w , \Delta^{1/2} w ) }_2^2
\end{align*}
where $v = \ell^\top \ell$.

  D.P. Palomar (2025)
