% 1. Introduction
% Derivatives, multi-period optimization, limit order book and copulas are outside the scope of this book.

\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=2,yscale=1,inner sep=2pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline=0]
  \node (d) at (0,0) {data};
  \node[right=5mm of d,fill=blue!10] (m){modeling};
  \node[right=5mm of m] (mu) {$(\mu,\Sigma)$};
  \node[right=5mm of mu,align=center,fill=blue!10] (po) {portfolio\\optimization};
  \node[right=5mm of po] (w) {$w$};
  \draw[->] (d) -- (m);
  \draw[->] (m) -- (mu);
  \draw[->] (mu) -- (po);
  \draw[->] (po) -- (w);
\end{tikzpicture}
\]

% 2. Stylized facts
\textbf{2.}
Stylized facts of financial data include:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Non-stationarity,
\item Volatility clustering;
\item No autocorrelation;
\item Heavy tails (but low-frequency data is more Gaussian);
\item Asymmetry (compare the skewness of log-returns of S\&P 500 and bitcoin at different horizons);
\item Positive correlation between assets (factor model structure).
\end{compactitem}

% 3. iid modeling
\textbf{3.}  Estimators of location include
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Mean, $\argmin_\mu \sum_t \n{ x_t - \mu } _2^2$;
\item Median, $\argmin_\mu \sum_t \n{ x_t - \mu } _1$;
\item \d{Spatial median}, $\argmin_\mu \sum_t \n{ x_t - \mu } _2$.
\end{compactitem}

For the scatter matrix, consider:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Sample covariance matrix, which is also the Gaussian maximum likelihood estimator;
\item The maximum likelihood estimator for the multivariate Student distribution
  \[ p(x) \propto \left[
    1 + \dfrac1\nu (x-\mu) ' \Sigma^{-1} (x-\mu)
    \right]^{ (\nu + N) / 2 }
  \]
  (the fixed point iterations for the first order conditions are just weighted sample means and covariances);
\item M-estimators, which generalize those fixed point iterations
  \begin{align*}
    & \dfrac1T \sum_t u_1(s) (x_t - \mu ) = 0 \\
    & \dfrac1T \sum_t u_2(s) (x_t - \mu ) (x_t - \mu ) ' = \Sigma \\
    & s = (x_t - \mu)' \Sigma^{-1} (x_t - \mu)
  \end{align*}
  for various choices of the weight functions $u_1$, $u_2$;
\item \d{Tyler's estimator}: maximum likelihood estimator for the normalized observations
  $s_t = x_t / \n{ x_t }_2$
  (after centering, e.g., with the geometric median), which follow an
  \emph{angular distribution}
  \[ f(s) \propto \dfrac{ ( s' \Sigma^{-1} s ) ^ {-N/2} }{ \sqrt{ \va \Sigma } } ; \]
  this only gives $\Sigma$ up to a factor: recover it by looking at the variances:
  $\text{diag}\,\Sigma \approx \hat\sigma^2$.
\end{compactitem}

Shrinkage estimators reduce the variance by increasing the bias.
For the mean, the James-Stein estimator is
$ (1-\rho) \hat \mu + \rho \mu_0 $,
with
\[
  \rho = \dfrac{ k + 2 }{
    (k+2) + n (\hat\mu - \mu_0) ' \Sigma^{-1} (\hat\mu - \mu_0)
  }
\]
with $\mu_0 = 0$, or the mean of the means $\hat\mu_i$, or their volatility-weighted mean.
The variance is similar,
$(1-\rho) \hat\Sigma + \rho \Sigma_0$,
with $\Sigma_0$ scalar, diagonal, or equal-correlation,
and
\[ \rho =  \min \left\{
1 , \dfrac{
  \operatorname*{Mean}_t \n{ \hat\Sigma - x_t x_t' }_F^2
}{
  \n{ \hat\Sigma - \Sigma_0 }_F^2
}
\right\}. \]
Better, choose $\rho$ with cross-validation, for a more meaningful measure than the mean square error,
e.g., the Sharpe ratio.

Factor models, and the Black-Litterman framework,
are other ways of estimating variance matrices and incorporating prior information.

% 4. Time series
\textbf{4.}
State space models (SSM, Kalman filter)
can be used to smooth time series,
by modeling them as:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item A random walk plus noise (mean model);
\item An integrated random walk plus noise (mean + velocity);
\item Mean + velocity + acceleration;
\item Idem, with a second order approximation.
\end{compactitem}
The linear transformations for the latent state are respectively
\[
\begin{bmatrix} 1 \end{bmatrix}
\quad
\begin{bmatrix} 1 & \Delta t \\ 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix}
\quad
\begin{bmatrix} 1 & \Delta t & 0 \\ & 0 & 1 & \Delta t \\ 0 & 0 & 0 \end{bmatrix}
\quad
\begin{bmatrix} 1 & \Delta t & \tfrac12 \Delta t^2 \\ 0 & 1 & \Delta t \\ 0 & 0 & 0 \end{bmatrix}.
\]

The VAR($p$) model
\[ x_t = \phi_0 + \sum_{ i = 1 } ^p \Phi_i x_{t-i} + \epsilon_t \]
can be written in VECM form
\[ \Delta x_t = \phi_0 + \Pi x_t + \sum_{ i = 1 }^{ p-1} \tilde \Phi_i \Delta x_{t-1} + \epsilon_t \]
($x_t$ are log-prices, $\Delta x_t$ log-returns);
the matrix $\Pi$ is often low-rank, $\Pi = \alpha \beta'$, and can be used to find cointegration relations
(for pairs trading).

GARCH models are just glorified exponentially weighted averages.

The Kalman filter can approximately estimate stochastic volatility (SV) models.

The GARCH models can be generalized to multivariate time series, modeling
$\Sigma_t$, e.g., as $\Sigma_t = D_t C D_t$,
with $D_t$ diagonal (constant conditional correlation, CCC),
of with a dynamic correlation (DCC), estimated with an exponentially moving average:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Fit univariate GARCH models: estimate the volatilities $\sigma_{it}$,
  the residuals $\epsilon_{it}$, and standardize the residuals
  \[ \bar \epsilon _t = D_t^{-1} \epsilon_t, \qquad D_t = \text{diag}( \sigma_{1t}, \dots , \sigma_{kt} ); \]
\item Compute the conditional correlation
  \begin{align*}
    Q_t &= \alpha \bar \epsilon_{t-1} \bar \epsilon_{t-1}' + (1-\alpha) Q_{t-1} \\
    C_t &= \text{cov2cor}( Q_t ).
  \end{align*}
\end{compactitem}

% 5. Graphs
\textbf{5.}
Given the weight matrix $W$ of a graph,
the Laplacian $L = D - W$, where $D = \text{diag}(W\mathbf 1)$ is the degree matrix,
measures the smoothness of a graph signal.
\[ x' L x = \dfrac12 \sum_{ij} W_{ij} ( x_i - x_j )^2 \]
To learn a graph from data, we can minimize
\[ \sum_t x_t ' L x_t = \tr( X L X' ) = \dfrac12 \tr( W Z ) \]
where
\begin{align*}
  & Z_{ij} = \n{ x_i - x_j }^2 \\
  & L \mathbf 1 = 0 \\
  & L_{ij} = L_{ji} \leq 0 \text{ for } i \neq j \\
  & \text{diag}\, W = 0 \\
  & W = W' \geq 0.
\end{align*}
To avoid the trivial solution $W=0$,
add a constraint on the degrees, $W \mathbf 1 = \mathbf 1$,
or a regularization term $- \mathbf 1 ' \log ( W \mathbf 1 )$.

The precision matrix $\Theta = \Sigma^{-1}$ contains the partial correlations
$ - \Theta_{ij} / \sqrt{ \Theta_{ii} \Theta_{jj} }$.
The graphical lasso estimates $\Theta$ from the sample covariance matrix $S$
with a sparsity penalty
\[ \operatorname*{Maximize}_{\Theta \succcurlyeq 0 }
\log \det \Theta - \tr( \Theta S ) - \rho \n{ \Theta }_{1, \text{off}} \]
or, if we impose a Laplacian structure,
\[ \begin{array}{ll}
  \text{Find} & L \succcurlyeq 0 \\
  \text{To maximize} & \log \operatorname{gdet}(L) - \tr(LS) = \rho \n{ L }_{ 1, \text{off} } \\
  \text{Such that} & L \mathbf 1 = 0 \\
  & \forall i \neq j \ L_{ij} = L_{ji} \leq 0
\end{array} \]
where gdet is the product of the non-zero eigenvalues
($L$ is singular because of the constraint $L\mathbf 1 = 0$).

One can replace $\n{L}_{1,\text{off}}$ with $\n{L}_{0,\text{off}}$,
approximate it as $\sum \phi(L_{ij})$, with $\phi$ concave, e.g.,
$\phi(x) = \log\bigl( \epsilon + \va x \bigr)$,
and solve with majorization-minimization (MM)

The number of connected components is the multiplicity of 0 as eigenvalue of the Laplacian $L$:
we can add a constraint
$\lambda_1 = \cdots = \lambda_k = 0$ if we expect $k$ components;
it can be reformulated as
\[ \sum_{i=1}^k \lambda_i(L) = \min_{\substack{ F \in \RR^{p \times k} \\ F' F = I }}
\tr( F' L F ) = 0 . \]
For instance, if we already have a Lapalcian $L_0$,
\[ \begin{array}{ll}
  \text{Find} & L \succcurlyeq 0 , F \\
  \text{To minimize} &  \n{ L - L_0 }_F^2 + \gamma \tr( F' L F ) \\
  \text{Such that} & L \mathbf 1 = 0 \\
  & \forall i \neq j \ L_{ij} = L_{ji} \leq 0 \\
  & \text{diag}\, L = \mathbf 1 \\
  & F' F = I
\end{array} \]
or the sample variance matrix $S$
\[ \begin{array}{ll}
  \text{Find} & L \succcurlyeq 0 , F \\
  \text{To maximize} & \log \operatorname{gdet}(L) - \tr(LS) + {}\\
  & \qquad - \rho \n{L}_{0, \text{off}} - \gamma \tr( F' L F ) \\
  \text{Such that} & L \mathbf 1 = 0 \\
  & \forall i \neq j \ L_{ij} = L_{ji} \leq 0 \\
  & \text{diag}\, L = \mathbf 1 \\
  & F' F = I
\end{array} \]
which can be solved by alternate minimization.

To avoid isolated nodes, control the degrees:
$\text{diag}\,L=\mathbf 1$
(or $W \mathbf 1 = \mathbf 1$).

To account for heavy-tailed distributions,
replace $\tr(LS)$ with
\[ \dfrac{ p + \nu }{ T } \sum_t \log \left(
1 + \dfrac{ x_t ' L x_t }{ \nu } \right) \]
(use MM with a first order expansion of the log around $L=L_0$:
this gives a sequence of Gaussian formulations,
with weighted covariance matrices).

For time-varying graphs, add a regularization, e.g.,
$\n{ L_{t-1} - L_t }_F^2$ or $\n{ L_{t-1} - L_t}_1$.

% II Portfolio optimization
% 6. Portfolio basics

\textbf{6.}
Performance and risk measures include
expected returns,
volatility,
VaR, CVaR,
maximum drawdown (MDD),
drawdown at risk (DaR),
conditional drawdown at risk (CDaR),
volatility-adjusted returns (multiply $w' r_t$ with $\sigma_\text{target} / \sigma_t$),
Sharpe ratio, information ratio (Sharpe wrt a benchmark),
gain-loss ratio
$\E[ r_t | r_t > 0 ] / \E[ r_t | r_t < 0 ]$,
Calmar ratio (divide by the MDD),
Sterling ratio (divide by $\text{MDD}+0.1$), etc.

Simple portfolios include $1/N$, maximum return,
minimum variance,
maximum decorrelation (replace $\Sigma$ with the correlation),
inverse volatility, quintile,
risk parity,
most diversified (maximize $w' \text{diag}\,\Sigma / \sqrt{ w' \Sigma w }$),
etc.

% 7. Modern portfolio theory
\textbf{7.}
The maximum Sharpe portfolio
\[ \begin{array}{ll}
  \text{Find} & w \\
  \text{To maximize} & \dfrac{ w' \mu - r_f }{ \sqrt{ w' \Sigma w } } \\
  \text{Such that} & \mathbf 1' w = 1 , \ w \geq 0
\end{array} \]
is the solution of a concave-convex \emph{fractional program}.
It can be solved with:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item The \emph{bisection method}: start with an interval
  $[ \ell, u ]$ containing the Sharpe ratio, set $t = (\ell+u)/2$,
  solve the feasibility problem
  \[ \begin{array}{ll}
    \text{Find} & w \\
    \text{Such that} & t \sqrt{ w' \Sigma w } \leq w' \mu - r_f \\
    & w' \mathbf 1 = 1 , \ w \geq 0,
  \end{array} \]
  update $\ell \leftarrow t$ if feasible,
  or $u \leftarrow t$ if not,
  and iterate until $\va{ u - \ell} \leq \epsilon$;
\item \emph{Dinkelbach method}, iteratively solving
  \[ \begin{array}{ll}
    \text{Find} & w \\
    \text{To maximize} &  w' \mu - r_f - y \sqrt{ w' \Sigma w } \\
    \text{Such that} & w' \mathbf 1 = 1, \ w \geq 0
  \end{array} \]
  and updating $y = ( w' \mu - r_f ) / \sqrt{ w' \Sigma w }$;
\item The \emph{Schaible transform}
  \[ \begin{array}{ll}
    \text{Find} & y, t \\
    \text{To maximize} & y' ( \mu - r_f \mathbf 1 ) \\
    \text{Such that} & \sqrt{ y' \Sigma y } \leq 1 \\
    & t \geq 0 , \ y' \mathbf 1 = t , y \geq 0 \\
    & w = y / ( y' \mathbf 1 )
  \end{array} \]
  (which can be further simplified).
\end{compactitem}

The \emph{Kelly criterion portfolio} maximizes growth
\[ \begin{array}{ll}
  \text{Find} & w \\
  \text{To maximize} & \E\bigl[ \log( 1 + w' r ) \bigr] \\
  \text{Such that} & w' \mathbf 1 , \ w \geq 0.
\end{array} \]
The objective can be approximated as
\[ \E\bigl[ \log( 1 + w' r ) \bigr] \approx \operatorname*{Mean}_t \log( 1 + w' r_t ) \]
(exponential cone programming)
or a Taylor expansion around $r = 0$
\[ \E \bigl[ \log(1+w'r) \bigr] \approx w' \mu - \tfrac12 w' \Sigma w \]
(mean-variance optimization) -- expansions around $\mu$,
or on an interval do not add much ($\mu$ is imprecise).

More generally, we can maximize
$\E\bigl[ U(w'r) \bigr]$
for some utility function $U$, e.g.,
$\log(1+x)$, $\sqrt{1+x}$, $-1/x$, $-p/x^p$, $-(1+x)^{-1/2}$,
$1-e^{-\lambda x}$, etc.

All those portfolios are on the efficient frontier, and the optimization problems
can be solved with \emph{sequential quadratic programming} (SQP):
replace $f(w'\mu, w' \Sigma w)$ with $\tilde f(w, w_k) + \tau \n{ w - w_k}_2^2$,
where $\tilde f$ is a linear approximation of $f$ around $w_k$.

You may prefer using other measures of risk and/or avoid using the imprecise $\mu$ altogether
(e.g., risk parity).

% 8. Backtesting
\textbf{8.} In backtesting, beware of:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Survivorship bias;
\item Look-ahead bias;
\item Overfitting: perform sensitivity analysis, stress tests, use model averaging,
  keep track of the number of backtests;
\item Outliers: look into robust optimization, robust portfolios;
\item Transaction costs;
\item Shorting costs.
\end{compactitem}

% 9. Higher-order portfolios
\textbf{9.}
The higher moments of portfolio returns are
\begin{align*}
  \phi_1(w) &= \E[ w' r ] = w' \mu \\
  \phi_2(w) &= \E\bigl[  (w' r)^2 \bigr] = w' \Sigma w \\
  \phi_3(w) &= \E\bigl[  (w' r)^3 \bigr] = w' \Phi (w \tens w ) \\
  \phi_4(w) &= \E\bigl[  (w' r)^4 \bigr] = w' \Psi (w \tens w \tens w ) \\
  \mu &= \E[ r ] \qquad \bar r = r - \mu \\
  \Sigma &= \E[ \bar r \bar r ' ] \\
  \Phi &= \E \bigl[ \bar r ( \bar r \tens \bar r ) ' \bigr] \\
  \Psi &= \E \bigl[ \bar r ( \bar r \tens \bar r \tens \bar r ) ' \bigr].
\end{align*}
With a factor model, $r = \alpha + \beta f + \epsilon$,
\begin{align*}
  \mu &= \alpha + \beta \phi_1^f \\
  \Sigma &= \beta \Phi_2^f \beta' + \Sigma_\epsilon \\
  \Phi &= \beta \Phi_3^f ( \beta ' \tens \beta' ) + \Phi_\epsilon \\
  \Psi &= \beta \Phi_4^f ( \beta ' \tens \beta' \tens \beta' ) + \Psi_\epsilon.
\end{align*}

A multivariate \emph{variance mixture} is
\begin{align*}
  x &= \mu + \sqrt w z & \E[x] &= \mu \\
  z &\sim N(0,\Sigma) & \var[x] &= \E[w] \Sigma \\
  w &\text{: $\RR_+$-valued.}
\end{align*}
For instance, the multivariate $t$ distribution is
\begin{align*}
  \tau &\sim \Gamma\left( \dfrac\nu2, \dfrac\nu2 \right) \\
  X &\sim N( \mu, \Sigma/\tau ).
\end{align*}
A \emph{mean-variance mixture} is
\[ x = m(w) + \sqrt w z, \]
often with $m(w) = \mu + w \gamma$:
\begin{align*}
  \E[x] &= \mu + \E[w] \gamma \\
  \var[x] &= \E[w] \Sigma + \var[w] \gamma \gamma'.
\end{align*}
For instance, the multivariate skewed $t$ distribution is
\begin{align*}
  \tau &\sim \Gamma \left( \dfrac\nu2, \dfrac\nu2 \right) \\
  x &\sim N( \mu + \gamma/\tau, \Sigma/\tau).
\end{align*}
Its higher moments are easy to compute.

The \emph{L-moments} of a random variable $X$ are
\[ \lambda_r = \dfrac1r \sum_{ k=0 }^{r-1}
(-1)^k \binom{r-1}{k} \E[ X_{r-k:r} ] \]
where $X_{1:n} \leq X_{2:n} \leq \cdots \leq X_{n:n}$
are the order statistics of a sample of size $n$.
They can be estimated by sorting the data
%
% TODO: FORMULAS p245?
%
(but for portfolio returns, the corresponding permutation depends on the weights).

The mean-variance-skewness-kurtosis portfolio
maximizes
\[ \lambda_1 \phi_1(w) - \lambda_2 \phi_2(w) + \lambda_3 \phi_3(w) - \lambda_4 \phi_4(w) \]
where the $\lambda_i$ are chosen by the user
(for instance, by expanding the CRRA utility).

The average growth can be approximated as
\begin{align*}
  \E\bigl[ \log(1+w'r) \bigr] &\approx
  \log \bigl[ 1 + \phi_1(w) \bigr]
  - \dfrac{ \phi_2(w) }{ 2 \phi_1^2(w) } + {} \\
  & \hphantom{\approx{}}
  + \dfrac{ \phi_3(w) }{ 3 \phi_1^3(w) }
  - \dfrac{ \phi_4(w) }{ 4 \phi_1^4(w) }
\end{align*}
(Taylor expansion of $\E[ \log(1+X) ]$ around $\E[X]$).

Higher moments can be optimized in many ways:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Scalarization: maximize $\sum (-1)^i \lambda_1 \phi_1(w)$;
\item Constraints: $\phi_i(w) \geq \alpha_i$ or $\phi_i \leq \alpha_i$;
\item Shortage: find $w$, $\delta$ to maximize $\delta$
  such that $\forall i \ \phi_i(w) \lessgtr \phi_1(w_0) - (-1)^i \delta g_i$;
  perhaps with a \emph{tilting} constraint
  $\xi(w) \leq \xi(w_0) + \kappa$,
  where $\xi$ is the Herfindahl index,
  the risk contribution equalization,
  the diversification ratio, a tracking error, etc.
\item Goal programming,
  \[ \begin{array}{ll}
    \text{Find} & w , \ d \geq 0 \\
    \text{To minimize} & \sum_i \va{ \dfrac{ d_i }{ \phi_i^0 } } ^p \\
    \text{Such that} & \forall i \ \phi_i(w) - (-1)^i d_i \lessgtr \phi_i^0
  \end{array} \]
  where the targets $\phi_i^0$ are not jointly attainable.
\end{compactitem}

Optimizing the $L$-moments leads to
\emph{ordered weighted averaging} (OWA) problems
\[ \operatorname*{Maximize}_w \sum v_i w' r_{ \tau(i) } \]
which are tricky to solve.

SCA (successive convex approximation) replaces the problem
\[ \operatorname*{Minimize}_{x \in \mathscr X}  f(x) \]
with a sequence of problems
\begin{align*}
  \hat x_{k+1} &= \argmin_{ x \in \mathscr X } \tilde f( x ; x_k ) \\
  x_{k+1} &= x_k + \gamma ( \hat x_{k+1} - x_k )
\end{align*}
where $\tilde f$ is an approximation of $f$ around $x_k$.

\emph{Majorization-minimization} (MM) requires $\tilde f$
to be a tangent upper bound
\begin{align*}
  & \tilde f ( x ; x_k ) \geq f(x) \\
  & \tilde f( x_k ; x_k ) = f(x_k ) \\
  & \nabla_{\!x} \tilde f ( x_k ; x_k ) = \nabla f( x_k );
\end{align*}
the iterations can then be simplified to
\[ x_{k+1} = \argmin_{ x \in \mathscr X } \tilde f ( x ; x_k ) . \]
\textsc{Squarem} speeds up convergence:
if $\n{ w_{k+1} - w_k } $ increases,
increase the step size (beyond 1).

% 10. Alternative risk measures
\textbf{10.}
Alternative risk measures include:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Semi-variance;
\item Lower -artial moments (LPM) wrt a minimum acceptable return $\tau$;
\item VaR;
\item CVaR;
  \begin{align*}
    \text{VaR}_\alpha(w) &= \argmin_\tau F_\alpha( w, \tau ) \\
    \text{CVaR}_\alpha(w) &= \min_\tau  F_\alpha( w, \tau ) \\
    F_\alpha( w, \tau ) &= \tau + \dfrac1{1-\alpha} \E\bigl[ ( - w' r - \tau )^+ \bigr]
  \end{align*}
\item EVaR (tightest possible upper bound from the Chernoff inequality);
\item Drawdown, maximum drawdown, average drawdown, drawdown at risk,
  conditional drawdown at risk (MDD, ADD, DaR, CDaR).
\end{compactitem}

For the LPM, the optimization problem is convex, but includes all observations --
contrary to the variance, they cannot be condensed into a smaller matrix.
\[ \begin{array}{ll}
  \text{Find} & w \in \mathscr W \\
  \text{To maximize} & w' \mu - \lambda \operatorname*{Mean}_t (\tau - w' r_t )_+^\alpha
\end{array} \]
For the semi-variance, some try to find an approximation
\[ \E \bigl[ ( \tau - w' r_t )_+ ^2 \bigr] \approx w' M w. \]

CVaR leads to a linear problem.

EVaR leads to a convex problem (with a log-sum-exp function
and a perspective transform).

For the drawdown (MDD, ADD, DaR, CDaR), replace the returns with the cummulated returns
$w' r_t^{\text{cum}}$.

% 11. Risk parity
\textbf{11.}
In the \emph{risk parity} portfolio,
all assets have the same contribution to risk
\[ \forall i \quad \dfrac{ w_i ( \Sigma w )_i }{ w' \Sigma w } = \dfrac1N . \]
More generally, for the \emph{risk budgeting} portfolio,
\[ \forall i \quad \dfrac{ w_i ( \Sigma w )_i }{ w' \Sigma w } = b_i . \]
This can be generalized to risk factors
\[ \forall k \quad \dfrac{ (B' w)_k ( B^\dagger \Sigma w ) _k }{ w' \Sigma w } = b_k \]
or other homogeneous risk measures
(VaR, CVaR -- but they are not easily computable).

To find it, let $ x = w / \sqrt{ w' \Sigma w}$:
the equation becomes $\Sigma x = b \oslash x$,
which can be solved by root finding, or by noticing it is the optimality condition
of a convex optimization problem, such as
\begin{align*}
  &\operatorname*{Minimize}_{ x \geq 0 } \dfrac12 x' \Sigma x - b' \log x \\
  &\operatorname*{Minimize}_{ x \geq 0 } \sqrt{ x' \Sigma x } - b' \log x \\
  &\operatorname*{Minimize}_{ x \geq 0 } \sqrt{ x' \Sigma x } \text{ st }  b' \log x \geq c \\
  &\operatorname*{Minimize}_{ x \geq 0 } b' \log x  \text{ st } \sqrt{ x' \Sigma x } \leq \sigma_0
\end{align*}

To add expected returns to the objective, we need to fall back to non-convex formulations, e.g.,
\begin{align*}
  & \sum_i \left( \dfrac{ w_i ( \Sigma w )_i }{ w' \Sigma w } - b_i \right) ^2 \\
  & \sum_i \left( \dfrac{ w_i ( \Sigma w )_i }{ \sqrt{w' \Sigma w} } - b_i  \sqrt{w' \Sigma w} \right) ^2 \\
  & \sum_i \bigl( w_i ( \Sigma w )_i  - b_i w' \Sigma w \bigr)^2 .
\end{align*}
The general problem
\[ \begin{array}{ll}
  \text{Find} & w \in \mathscr W \\
  \text{To minimize} & \sum g_i(w)^2 + \lambda F(w)  \\
  \text{Where, e.g.,}
  & g_i(w) = \dfrac{ w_i ( \Sigma w )_i }{ w' \Sigma w } - b_i \\
  & F(w) = - w' \mu + \dfrac12 w' \Sigma w
\end{array} \]
can be solved with SCA.

% 12. Graph-based portfolios
\textbf{12.}
Do not compute graphs directly from the distance or correlation
(it only contains local, pairwise information),
but from the distance or correlation between the columns of the distance or correlation matrix.

For general graphs, use the \emph{heavy tail MRF} (Markov random field)
with degree control, which maximizes
\[ \log \operatorname{gdet}(L) - \dfrac{ N + \nu }{ T } \sum_t \log( \nu + x_t ' L x_t ) \]
subject to $D=\mathbf1$, or the $k$-component variant,
which adds a penalty $\gamma \tr( F' L F)$ and the constraint $F'F=I$.

Hierarchical portfolios include:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Hierarchical $1/N$: compute a hierarchical clustering
  (Ward -- or single-linkage, if you are seeking risk),
  and give half the weight to each branch, recursively;
\item Hierarchical risk parity (HRP):
  compute a hierarchical clustering, compute its optimal seriation,
  discard the hierarchical clustering (only keep the order on the leaves),
  replace it with a balanced binary tree, and use inverse volatility weighting in each branch;
\item Hierarchical equal risk contribution (HERC): idem, but keep the dendrogram, use risk parity at each node,
  but stop when you have the required number of clusters
  (using the gap statistics), and use $1/N$ in the leaves.
\end{compactitem}

The HRP portfolio can be written
\begin{align*}
 w &\propto \begin{pmatrix}
  \dfrac1{ \nu(\Sigma_A) } w(\Sigma_A) \\[3mm]
  \dfrac1{ \nu(\Sigma_B) } w(\Sigma_B) \\
 \end{pmatrix} \\
 w(\Sigma) &\propto ( \text{diag}\,\Sigma ) ^{-1} \\
 \nu(\Sigma) &= w' \Sigma w.
\end{align*}
The minimum variance portfolio can be written
\begin{align*}
 w &\propto \begin{pmatrix}
  \dfrac1{ \nu(\Sigma_A^c,b_A) } w(\Sigma_A,b_A) \\[4mm]
  \dfrac1{ \nu(\Sigma_B^c,b_B) } w(\Sigma_B,b_B) \\
 \end{pmatrix} \\
 w(\Sigma,b) &=  \dfrac{ \Sigma^{-1} b }{ b' \Sigma^{-1} b } \\
 \nu(\Sigma,b) &= w' \Sigma w = \dfrac1{ b' \Sigma^{-1} b } \\
 b_A &= \mathbf 1 - \Sigma_{AB} \Sigma_B^{-1} \mathbf 1 \\
 \Sigma_A^c &= \Sigma_A - \Sigma_{AB} \Sigma_B^{-1} \Sigma_{BA}.
\end{align*}
Schur portfolios interpolate between the two (convex, or geodesic convex combination).

% 13. Index-tracking portfolios
\textbf{13.} The penalty in sparse least squares
\[ \operatorname*{Minimize}_x \n{ Ax - b }_2^2 + \lambda \n x _0 \]
can be approximated with $\n x _1$ or, better,
with the concave function $\sum_i \log \bigl( 1 + \va{ x_i } / \epsilon \bigr)$;
the resulting problem can be solved with MM, which leads to a sequence of convex problems
\begin{align*}
  & \operatorname*{Minimize}_ x \sum_i \alpha_i \va{ x_i } \ \text{ st }\  Ax = b \\
  & \alpha_i = \dfrac{ 1 }{ \epsilon + \va{ x_i }  }.
\end{align*}
In particular, for sparse portfolios,
the penalty $\n{x}_1$ has no effect
because he long-only fully-invested constraints
$x \geq 0$, $x' \mathbf 1 = 1$ imply $\n x _1 = 1$.

The 1-period portfolio tracking problem seeks to minimize
\[ \text{TE} = \n{ r^p - X w }_2^2 . \]
But for cap-weighted indices, the weights change with time: use weighted returns instead
\begin{align*}
  \tilde r_t &= r_t \odot \alpha_{t-1} \\
  \alpha_t &= \prod_{s = 1 }^t \dfrac{ 1 + r_s }{ 1 + r_s^b} .
\end{align*}
If you want to track prices rather than returns, replace $X$ (or $\tilde X$)
with the cummulated returns $X^{\text{cum}}$ (or $\tilde X^{\text{cum}}$).

In practice, the difference between ratio and log-returns is negligible.

You can replace the tracking error with other measures, e.g.,
downside risk, $\ell^1$ tracking error, Huberized tracking error.

% FDR: not clear

% 14. Robust portfolios
\textbf{14.}
There are several approaches to robust portfolio construction:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item \emph{Stochastic optimization} with expectations:
  for estimated parameters $\hat\theta$, replace
  $f(x;\hat\theta) \leq \alpha$ with
  \[\E_\theta \bigl[ f(x;\theta)\bigr] \leq \alpha\]
  (this preserves convexity), where the expectation
  is estimated by sampling
  $\operatorname*{Mean}_i f(x;\theta_i)$ or, if possible,
  in closed form;
\item Stochastic optimization with \emph{chance constraints}:
  replace $f(x;\hat\theta) \leq \alpha$ with
  \[P \bigl[ f(x;\theta) \leq \alpha \bigr] \geq 1- \epsilon \]
  (much trickier to deal with);
\item Worst-case robust optimization:
  replace $f(x;\hat\theta) \leq \alpha$ with
  \[ \sup_{ \theta \in U } f(x;\theta) \leq \alpha , \]
  where the supremum can be estimated by sampling, in closed form,
  via duality, or saddle point optimization;
\item Resampling (cross-validation, bootstrap).
\end{compactitem}

% 15. Pairs trading
\textbf{15.}
Unit root stationarity is a convenient proxy for mean reversion.

Cointegration and correlation are different: you can have one without the other.
[Using the tracking error for passive investing is questionable.]

You can test for cointegration on either log-prices or log-prices:
the former gives more pairs (the noise if more symmetric), but requires rebalancing.

Pre-filter potential pairs (e.g., with the Euclidean distance) before the cointegration tests.

With a Kalman filter, you can let the cointegration parameters slowly vary with time.

Pairs trading can be generalized to more than two assets (``statistical arbitrage'').

A VAR model
\[ x_t = \Phi_1 x_{t-1} + \cdots + \Phi_p x_{t-p} + \epsilon_t \]
can be written in VECM form
\[ \Delta x_t = \Pi x_{t-1} + \tilde \Phi_1 \Delta x_{t-1} + \cdots + \tilde \Phi_{p-1} \Delta x_{p-1} + \epsilon_t \]
with
\begin{align*}
  \tilde \Phi_j &= - \Phi_{j+1} - \Phi_{j+1} - \cdots - \Phi_p \\
  \Pi &= - I + \Phi_1 + \Phi_2 + \cdots + \Phi_p .
\end{align*}
The matrix $\Pi$ is often low-rank, $\Pi = \alpha \beta'$; $\beta$ contains cointegration relations.

To choose among the many corresponding stationary portfolios,
maximize the zero-crossing rate (which determines the number of trades)
and the variance of the spread (which determines the threshold).
There are various proxies for the zero-crossing rate.

% 16. Deep learning portfolios
\textbf{16.}
Deep learning can be used for time series forecasting,
before feeding the results to an optimizer,
or end-to-end, directly producing portfolio weights to maximize some performance measure, e.g., the Sharpe ratio.

% A. Convex optimization
\textbf{A.}
Elementary \emph{convex sets} include
hyperplanes, half-spaces, polyhedra (e.g., the probability simplex),
ellipsoids, norm balls, norm cones
\[ \bigl\{ (x,t) \in \RR^{n+1} \ : \ \n x \leq t \bigr\} , \]
the positive semidefinite cone.
Convexity is preserved by intersection, affine transformations,
and the \emph{perspective} function
\[ \left\{ \begin{array}{rcl}
  \RR^n \times \RR_+^\times & \longrightarrow & \RR^n \\
  (x,t) & \longmapsto & x/t .
\end{array} \right. \]

Here are examples of \emph{convex functions}:
negentropy ($x \log x$), max, quadratic-over-linear ($x^2/y$), log-sum-exp
($\log \sum e^{x_i}$ approximates $\max x_i$),
maximum eigenvalue (because $x^2/y$ is convex),
and of concave functions: geometric mean, log-determinant.

A function is convex iff its restriction to any line is convex (use this to prove that log-det is concave).
You can also prove convexity with a first order condition
(the function is above its tangents) or a second order condition ($\nabla ^2 f \succcurlyeq 0$).

Convexity is preserved by nonnegative weighted sums,
composition with affine mappings, pointwise maximum,
some compositions (e.g., $f \circ g$ is convex if $g$ is convex and $h$ convex nondecreasing),
partial minimization ($\min_y f(x,y)$),
\emph{perspective}
\[ g(\mathbf x,t) = t f(\mathbf x/t), \]
(e.g., $\mathbf x' \mathbf x/t$ and the relative entropy $t \log(t/x)$ are convex because
$\mathbf x' \mathbf x$ and $-\log x$ are).

A function is \emph{quasi-convex} if its sublevel sets are convex;
it is quasi-linear if it is both quasi-convex and quasi-concave
(in dimension 1, the quasi-linear functions are the monotonic functions;
in higher dimension, they are the functions of the form $f(x) = g(a'x)$
where $g$ is monotonic -- the level sets are parallel hyperplanes).

Inequalities with quasi-convex functions can be replaced by inequalities with convex functions:
for instance, if $f(x) = p(x) / q(x)$, with $p,q>0$, $p$ convex, $q$ concave,
we can replace $f(x) \leq t$ with $p(x) - t q(x) \leq 0$.

A quasi-convex optimization problem
\[ \begin{array}{lll}
  \text{Find} & x \\
  \text{To minimize} & f_0(x) & \text{quasi-convex} \\
  \text{Such that} & \forall i \ \ f_i(x) \leq 0 & \text{convex} \\
  & Ax = b
\end{array} \]
can be solved with the bisection method: solve a sequence
of feasibility problems
\[ \begin{array}{ll}
  \text{Find} & x \\
  \text{Such that} & \phi_t(x) \leq 0 \\
  & \forall i \ \ f_i(x) \leq 0 \\
  & Ax = b
\end{array} \]
where $f(x) \leq t$ iff $\phi_i(x) \leq 0$, with $\phi_t$ convex,
bisecting the interval $[\ell, u]$ into $[\ell,t]$ or $[t,u]$,
$t = (\ell+u)/2$, depending on feasibility. For instance,
\emph{linear fractional programs} (LFP) are quasi-convex.
\[ \begin{array}{ll}
  \text{Find} & x \\
  \text{to minimize} & \dfrac{ c' x + d }{ e' x + f } \\
  \text{Such that} & G x \leq h \\
  & A x = b \\
  & e' x + f > 0
\end{array} \]
Alternatively, with the Charnes-Cooper transform
\[ y = \dfrac{ x }{ e' x + f } , \qquad t = \dfrac1{ e' x + f }, \]
the problem becomes convex.
\[ \begin{array}{ll}
  \text{Find} & y, t \\
  \text{To minimize} & c' y + d t \\
  \text{Such that} & G y \leq h t \\
  & A y = b t \\
  & e' y + f t = 1 \\
  & t > 0
\end{array} \]

Least squares and box-constrained least squares can be formulated as quadratic programs (QP):
quadratic objective, linear constraints.

Robust linear programs with ellipsoid uncertainty sets lead to second order cone programs (SOCP):
linear objective, second order cone constraint.

The maximum eigenvalue minimization problem
\[ \operatorname*{Minimize} _x \lambda_{\max} \bigl( A(x) \bigr) \]
can be formulated as a semi-definite program
\[ \begin{array}{ll}
  \text{Find} & x, t \\
  \text{To minimize} & t \\
  \text{Such that} & A(x) \preccurlyeq t I.
\end{array} \]

\emph{Concave-convex fractional programs}
\[ \operatorname*{Maximize}_{ x \in \mathscr X } \dfrac{ f(x) }{ g(x) }  \]
can be solved with the bisection method
\[ \text{Find } x \in \mathscr X , \ t \in \RR_+^\times \text{ st } t g(x) \leq f(x) \]
or the \emph{Dinkelbach transform}
\[ \operatorname*{Maximize}_{ x \in \mathscr X } f(x) - y_k g(x) , \]
updating $y_k$ as $f(x) / g(x)$ at each step,
or the \emph{Schaible transform}
\[ \begin{array}{ll}
  \text{Find} & y, t \\
  \text{To maximize} & t f(y/t) \\
  \text{Such that} & t g(y/t) \leq 1 \\
  & t > 0 \\
  & y/t \in \mathscr X
\end{array} \]
where $y = x / g(x)$, $t = 1/g(x)$.

A \emph{posynomial} is a (multivariate) polynomial with positive coefficients.
A \emph{geometric program} is a (non-convex) problem of the form
\[ \begin{array}{lll}
  \text{Find} & x > 0 \\
  \text{To minimize} & f_0(x) & \text{posynomial} \\
  \text{Such that} & \forall i \ \ f_i(x) \leq 1 & \text{posynomials} \\
  & \forall j \ \ h_j(x) = 1 & \text{monomials}.
\end{array} \]
By setting $y_i = \log x_i$, the posynomials become log-sum-exp functions, and the problem is convex.

Given an optimization problem of the form
\[ \begin{array}{ll}
  \text{Find} & x \in \mathscr X \\
  \text{To minimize} & f_0(x) \\
  \text{Such that} & \forall i \ f_i(x) \leq 0 \\
  & \forall i \ h_i(x) = 0,
\end{array} \]
the Lagrangian and the dual are
\begin{align*}
  L(x,\lambda,\nu) &= f_0(x) + \lambda' f(x) + \nu' h(x) \qquad \lambda \geq 0 \\
  g(\lambda, \nu) &= \inf_{ x \in \mathscr X } L( x, \lambda, \nu ).
\end{align*}
The dual problem is
\[ \begin{array}{ll}
  \text{Find} & \lambda, \nu \\
  \text{To maximize} & g(\lambda, \nu) \\
  \text{Such that} & \lambda \geq 0.
\end{array} \]
For any feasible $x$, $\lambda$, $\nu$, the optimal value of the primal and dual problems, $p^*$ and $d^*$,
are in $\bigl[ g(\lambda, \nu), f_0(x) \bigr]$.
If the primal is convex and satisfies the \emph{Slater condition}, \ie, there is a strictly feasible point,
\[ \exists x \in \operatorname{relint} \mathscr X
\qquad
\forall i \ f_i(x) < 0
\qquad
\forall j \ h_j(x) = 0,
\]
then strong duality holds, \ie, the spectral gap $p^*-d^*$ is zero.
Strong duality implies the KKT conditions:
\begin{align*}
  & \forall i \ \ f_i(x) \leq 0 && \text{primal feasibility} \\
  & \forall j \ \ h_j(x) = 0  \\
  & \forall i \ \ \lambda_i \geq 0 && \text{dual feasibility} \\
  & \forall i \ \ \lambda_i f_i(x) = 0 && \text{complementarity slackness} \\
  & \nabla_{\! x} L = 0 && \text{zero Lagrangian gradient}
\end{align*}

% B. Optimization algorithms
\textbf{B.}
\emph{Descent methods} iterate
\[ x_{k+1} = x_k + \alpha_k d_k \]
where $d$ is the descent direction
(for instance, $-\nabla f$)
and $\alpha$ the step size, for instance
\[ \argmin_{ \alpha > 0 } f(x + \alpha d) \]
(exact line search), or Armijo's rule, which repeats
$\alpha \leftarrow \beta \alpha$, starting at $\alpha = 1$, until
$f(x+\alpha d ) < f(x) + \sigma \alpha \nabla f ^\top d$,
with $\sigma \in (0,1/2)$, and $\beta \in (0,1)$.

Newton's method uses
\[ d = - \nabla^2 f(x) ^{-1} \nabla f(x) = - H^{-1} g \]
as descent direction. The Newton decrement
$\lambda (x) = \sqrt{ g ^\top H^{-1} g }$
measures the proximity of $x$ to an optimal point.

If there are constraints $x \in \mathscr X$, project $x$ onto $\mathscr X$
after each descent step.

Linear equality constraints can be removed by reparametrization.
\emph{Interior point methods} replace inequality constraints $f_i(x) \leq 0$ with indicator functions
$I\bigl( f_i(x) \bigr)$,
where \[ I(u) = \begin{cases}
  0 & \text{if } u \leq 0 \\
  \infty & \text{otherwise}
\end{cases} \]
and approximate them with a \emph{logarithmic barrier}
\[ I(u) \approx - \dfrac 1t \log( - u ) \qquad t \to 0. \]
This leads to the problem
\[ \begin{array}{ll}
  \text{Find} & x \\
  \text{To minimize} & t f_0(x) + \phi(x) \\
  \text{Such that} & Ax = b \\
  \text{Where} & \phi(x) = - \sum \log - f_i(x)
\end{array} \]
which can be solved with Newton's method;
progressively increase $t$, e.g., $t_0=1$, $t_{k+1} = \mu t_k$,
$\mu = 10$ or $20$, until $m/t<\epsilon$, where $m$ is the number of inequality constraints.
To find a feasible point to start those iterations, solve
\[ \begin{array}{ll}
  \text{Find} & x, s \\
  \text{To minimize} & s \\
  \text{Such that} & \forall i \ \ f_i(x) \leq s \\
  & Ax = b
\end{array} \]

% No details on primal-dual interior point methods

\emph{Block coordinate descent} (BCD) updates one coordinate
(or block of coordinates) at a time: for instance, since the univariate lasso has a closed form solution
with the soft-thresholding operator, the lasso can be solved by applying this soft thresholding operator
one coordinate at a time.

\emph{Majorization-minimization} (MM)
approximates the (non-convex) objective with a convex, tangent, upper bound;
it can be sped up with \textsc{squarem} (if $\n{x_{k+1} - x_k}$ increases, increase the step size).

Successive convex approximation (SCA) uses a convex approximation
of the objective function (not necessarily an upper bound)
but has to use smaller step sizes.

The \emph{alternating direction method of multipliers} (ADMM)
is similar to BCD but can deal with coupled variable blocks in the constraints;
it combines dual decomposition and augmented Lagrangian.

To solve
\[ \begin{array}{ll}
  \text{Find} & x, z \\
  \text{To minimize} & f(x) + g(z) \\
  \text{Such that} & Ax + Bz = c
\end{array} \]
the \emph{dual decomposition} method iterates
\begin{align*}
  x & \leftarrow \argmin_x f(x) + y' A x \\
  z & \leftarrow \argmin_z g(z) + y' B z \\
  y & \leftarrow y + \alpha ( Ax + Bz - c )
\end{align*}
(convergence is only guaranteed under strong conditions).
The \emph{augmented Lagrangian} is
\begin{align*}
  L_\rho( x,z,y) & = f(x) + g(z) + y'(Ax+Bz-c) + {}\\
  & \hphantom{={}} + \dfrac\rho2 \n{ Ax + Bz - c }_2^2;
\end{align*}
the problem can then be solved by iterating
\begin{align*}
  x, z &\leftarrow \argmin_{x,z} L_\rho(x,z,y) \\
  y &\leftarrow y + \rho( Ax + Bz - c )
\end{align*}
but this does not decouple $x$ and $z$.
The ADMM iterations are:
\begin{align*}
  x & \leftarrow \argmin L_\rho(x,z,y) \\
  z &\leftarrow \argmin L_\rho(x,z,y) \\
  y &\leftarrow y + \rho( Ax + Bz - c ).
\end{align*}

Implementations of some of the algorithms presented in the book are available in the R packages
\texttt{fitHeavyTail},
\texttt{fingraph},
\texttt{sparseGraph},
\texttt{spectral\-Graph\-Topology},
\texttt{highOrderPortfolios},
etc.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Optimization algorithms for graph Laplacian estimation via ADMM anbd MM}\\
  L. Zhao et al.
\end{book}
To estimate a Laplacian-structured precision matrix
\[  \begin{array}{ll}
  \text{Find} & \Theta \succcurlyeq 0 \\
  \text{To minimize} & \tr( \Theta S ) - \log \operatorname{gdet} ( \Theta )  + \alpha \n{ \Theta }_1 \\
  \text{Such that} & \Theta \mathbf 1 = 0 \\
  & \forall i \neq j \ \  \Theta_{ij} \leq 0
\end{array} \]
notice that $\n \Theta _1 = \tr( \Theta H ) $,
with $H = 2 I - \mathbf 1 \mathbf 1 ' $,
because the diagonal (resp. off-diagonal) elements of $\Theta$ are nonnegative (resp. nonpositive),
and $\text{gdet}\,\Theta = \det \Xi$, where $\Theta = P \Xi P'$,
with $P$ the orthogonal complement of $\mathbf 1$
(\ie, $P'P = I_{k-1}$ and $P' \mathbf 1_k = \mathbf 0_{k-1}$).
The objective becomes
\[ \tr\bigl( \Xi P' ( S + \alpha H ) P \bigr) - \log \det \Xi . \]
To speed up computations, use ADMM
(or MM if the graph is very sparse).
Note that, in spite of the $L^1$ penalty, the result is not sparse.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Graphical models in heavy-tailed markets}\\
  J.V. Cadoso et al.
\end{book}
To learn a Lapalcian Gaussian Markov random field (LGMRF),
\[ p(x) \propto \sqrt{ \text{det}^*\, L } \exp \bigl[ -\tfrac12 (x-\mu)' L (x-\mu) \bigr], \]
where $\det^*$ is the pseudo-determinant (product of the non-zero eigenvalues:
$\det^*** L = \det(L + \mathbf 1 \mathbf 1' / p )$
if the graph is connected),
solve the Laplacian-structured graphical lasso problem
\[ \begin{array}{ll}
  \text{Find} & L \succcurlyeq 0 \\
  \text{To minimize} & \tr( LS ) - \log \det^*** L + h(L) \\
  \text{Such that} & L \mathbf 1 = 0 \\
  & \forall i \neq j \ \ L_{ij} = L_{ji} \leq 0
\end{array} \]
For a zero-mean Student distribution, replace $\tr(LS)$ with
\[ \dfrac{ p + \nu }{ n } \sum_{ i = 1 }^ n \log \left( 1 + \dfrac{ x_i' L x_i }{ \nu } \right). \]
For a $k$-component graph, add a low-rank penalty
\[ \tr( V' L V ) , \qquad V \in \RR^{p\times k} , \qquad V' V = I_k \]
(indeed, $\min_V \tr(V' L V )$ is the sum of the $k$ smallest eigenvalues of $L$:
if it is zero, $\text{rank}\, L \leq p-k$).

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Learning graphs from data}\\
\end{book}
\emph{Heuristic} methods start from the covariance, correlation or distance matrix
between the data or, better, between the columns of the covariance, correlation or distance matrix, and use:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Truncation, MST, PMFG, TMFG;
\item $k$-nn;
\item Possibly combined with Gaussian weights.
\end{compactitem}

Variants of the concentration matrix,
which contains the \emph{partial correlations} include:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item $L^1$ sparsity penalty (graphical lasso);
\item Laplacian structure on the concentration matrix;
\item $L^0$ sparsity penalty;
\item Low-rank Laplacian penalty (\ie, a $k$-component graph);
\item Heavy tails (Student instead of Gaussian distribution).
\end{compactitem}

To find the graph structure maximizing \emph{signal smoothness}, minimizing the variance of the signal
\[ \sum_{ij} W_{ij} ( x_i - x_j )^2 = \dfrac12 \tr( W Z ) = \tr( X L X' ) \]
where $X$ is the data (signal), $W$ the graph weights, $L$ the corresponding Laplacian, and
$Z_{ij} = (x_i - x_j)^2$, with the usual constraints for $L$ or $W$,
\begin{align*}
  & L\mathbf 1 = 0 && \forall i \neq j \ \ L_{ij} = L_{ji} \leq 0 \\
  & \text{diag} \  W && W = W' \geq 0,
\end{align*}
and
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item A sparsity penalty $\n W _F^2$;
\item A degree constraint $W \mathbf = 1$;
\item A degree penalty $- \mathbf 1 ' \log (W \mathbf 1 )$.
\end{compactitem}

*** Book: Portfolio optimization (Cajas)

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Advanced portfolio optimization}\\
  D. Cajas (2025)
\end{book}
\textbf{1.} Portfolio optimization has a long history:
mean-variance, CAPM, APT, GMD (Gini mean difference),
MAD, Black-Litterman, Fama-French, VaR, CVaR,
MVS (mean-variance-skewness), minimax, MST, robust,
OWA (ordered weighted average), drawdown, Kelly,
risk parity, HRP, EVaR, RLVaR, graphs, etc.

\textbf{3.} Higher moments are sensitive to outliers:
prefer the (order-statistics-based) $L$-moments.
The comoment tensors are very large,
but contain repeated elements: multiply them with the deduplication (or summation,
if you want to keep track of the multiplicities) matrix.

The James-Stein shrinkage estimator of the mean is
\begin{align*}
  \mu &= ( 1 - \alpha ) \hat \mu + \alpha \mu_0 \\
  \alpha &= \dfrac{ n - 1 }{ T } \dfrac{ n \bar\lambda - 2 \lambda_{\text{max}} }{ (\hat\mu-\mu_0)'(\hat\mu-\mu_0) } \\
  &\approx \dfrac{n-1}T \dfrac{ \bar\lambda }{  (\hat\mu-\mu_0)'(\hat\mu-\mu_0) } \\
  \mu_0 &= \dfrac{ \mathbf 1' \hat\mu}n\mathbf1 \quad \text{ or } \quad
  \dfrac{ \mathbf1\Sigma^{-1}\hat\mu}{ \mathbf1\Sigma^{-1}\mathbf1 } \quad \text{ or } \quad
  \dfrac{ \tr\Sigma }{ T } \mathbf 1.
\end{align*}
The Ledoit-Wolf variance estimator comes from the optimization problem
\[ \operatorname*{Minimize}_{ \rho_1, \rho_2 }
\E \n{ \Sigma_{\text{LW}} - \Sigma }_F^2 \text{ st }
\Sigma_{\text{LW}} = \rho_1 I + \rho_2 \hat\Sigma. \]
The OAS estimator uses
\[ \Sigma_{\text{OAS}} = \rho \bar\lambda I + (1-\rho) \hat\Sigma; \]
the shrunk estimator as well, but it lets the user choose~$\rho$.

Random matrix theory (RMT) performs denoising by replacing the small eigenvalues of $\hat\Sigma$
with zero, or with their average,
or by shrinking them.

Detoning removes the largest eigenvalue(s).

The graphical lasso produces a sparse inveerse covariance matrix;
so does the JLogo covariance (based on the TMFG)

% 4. Risk factor models

\textbf{5.} Black-Litterman also allows views on risk factors.

\textbf{6.} Codependence measures include
Pearson and Spearman correlations,
Kendall's $\tau$,
Gerber's statistic (generalization of Kendall's $\tau$ using observations beyond some threshold),
distance correlation,
mutual information (after binning),
(lower) tail dependence coefficient,
etc.

The variation of information (a kind of distance) is
\begin{align*}
  \text{VI}(X,Y) &= 2 H(X,Y) - H(X) - H(Y) \\
  \widetilde{\text{VI}}(X,Y) &= 1 = \dfrac{ I(X;Y) }{ \max \bigl\{ H(X), H(Y) \bigr\} }.
\end{align*}

\textbf{7.}
\emph{Coherent} risk measures satisfy monotonicity, subadditivity, homogeneity and translation invariance.

\emph{Convex} risk measures are more general: they replace subadditivity and homogeneity with convexity.

\emph{Distorsion} risk measures are defined from the distorted cdf of the returns,
$f\bigl( F(x) \bigr)$, e.g., VaR, CVaR.

\emph{Spectral} risk measures are weighted agerages of quantiles,
$\rho(X) = - \int_0^1 \phi(x) F^{-1}(x) dx$, $\phi \!\searrow$;
they are coherent.

\emph{$\phi$-divergence} risk measures are defined as
\[ \rho_{\phi,\beta}(X) = \E_{\substack{
    Z \in L \\ Z \geq 0 \\ \E[Z] = 1 \\ \E[ \phi(Z) ] \leq \beta }} \bigl[  Z' X \bigr]
\]
where $Z$ are scenario weights;
they are coherent and can be estimated as
\[ \hat \rho_{ \phi,\beta }(X) = \max_{\substack{ Z \geq 0 \\ \mathbf 1 ' Z = 1 \\ \sum \phi(Z_i) \leq \beta }} Z' X \]
Examples include
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item The entropic value at risk (EVaR):
  $\phi(X) = X \log X$, $\beta = - \log( \alpha T )$
\item The relativistic value at risk (RLVaR): $\phi(X) = Z \log Z$, $\beta = - \log_\kappa(\alpha T)$, where
  \[ \log_\kappa(x) = \dfrac{ x^\kappa - x^{-\kappa} }{ 2 \kappa }, \qquad \kappa \in (0,1). \]
\end{compactitem}

The \emph{ordered weighted average} (OWA) risk measures are weighted averages of order statistics,
\[ F_w(y) = \sum_i w_i y_{(i)} \]
(if $w$ is monotonic, it is convex). Examples include the Gini mean difference ($w$ is linear)
and the CVaR (step function).
\[
<<OWARiskMeasures,engine='python',results='hide',echo=FALSE,out.width='\\hsize',fig.show='hold'>>=
import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
fig, axs = plt.subplots( 2, 3, figsize = (6,4) )
xs = np.linspace( -1, 1, 100 )
for ax in axs.flatten():
  ax.axis('off')
  ax.axhline(0, linewidth = 1, color = 'black')
  ax.axvline(0, linewidth = 1, color = 'black')
  ax.set_xlim(-1.1,1.1)
axs[0,0].plot( xs, xs, linewidth = 5 )
axs[0,1].plot( xs, np.where( xs <= -.5, -1, 0) , linewidth = 5 )
axs[0,2].plot( xs, np.where( xs <= -.5, 1+2*xs, 0), linewidth = 5 )
axs[1,0].plot( xs, np.where( xs <= -1, -1, np.where( xs >= 1, 1, 0 ) ), linewidth = 5 )
axs[1,1].plot( xs, np.where( xs <= -.5, -1, np.where( xs >= .5, 1, 0 ) ), linewidth = 5 )
axs[1,2].plot( xs, np.where( xs <= -.5, 1+2*xs, np.where( xs >= .5, -1+2*xs, 0 ) ), linewidth = 5 )
axs[0,0].set_title("Gini")
axs[0,1].set_title("CVaR")
axs[0,2].set_title("Tail Gini")
axs[1,0].set_title("Range")
axs[1,1].set_title("CVaR Range")
axs[1,2].set_title("Tail Gini Range")
fig.subplots_adjust( hspace = .4, wspace = .2, left = 0, right = 1, top = .95, bottom = 0 )
plt.show()
@
\]

\emph{Deviation} risk measues are measures of dispersion,
such as
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item The variance (not coherent);
\item The Gini mean difference
  \[ \text{GMD}(X) = \operatorname*{Mean}_{j,k} \va{ X_j - X_k } \]
  which can be optimized with scenario-based optimization
  \[ \operatorname*{Minimize}_w \operatorname*{Mean}_{ s \leq t } \va{ w' r_t - w' r_s } \]
  (but, with the slack variables for the absolute values, we end up with $T^2$ variables) or, since it is the second $L$-moment,
  as an OWA risk measure (a big MIP, but there are approximations);
\item The MAD
  \[ \operatorname*{Minimize}_w \operatorname*{Mean}_t \va{ w' r_t - w' \mu }; \]
\item Brownian distance covariance.
\end{compactitem}

\emph{Downside} risk measures include
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Semi-variance, which can be minimized with scenario-based optimization,
  since
  \[ \sigma^2 = w' \Sigma w = \dfrac{ w' (CR) (CR) ' w }{ T-1 } = \dfrac{ \n{ CR w }_2^2 }{ T-1 } \]
  where $C = \text{Id}_T - \tfrac1T \mathbf 1_{T \times T}$ is the centering matrix,
  \[ \begin{array}{ll}
    \text{Find} & w \in \RR^k , \ d \in \RR^T \\
    \text{To minimize} & \n d _2^2 \\
    \text{Such that} & d \geq 0 , \ d \geq - CR w
  \end{array} \]
  (the semi-variance can also be approximated as $w' \Sigma_L w$);
\item Lower partial moments;
\item VaR (it can be optimized as an MILP, with binary variables indicating which observations are below the VaR);
\item CVaR;
\item Tail Gini
  \begin{align*}
    \text{TG}_\alpha(X) &= \dfrac1{\alpha^2} \int_0^\alpha \text{CVaR}_\gamma (X) d\gamma \\
    \text{CVaR}(X) &= \dfrac1\alpha\int_0^\alpha \text{VaR}_\gamma(X) d\gamma
  \end{align*}
  (it can be approximated as a weighted sum (of a finite number of $\text{CVaR}_\gamma$), which is also an OWA measure);
\item EVaR, an upper bound on VaR and CVaR from the Chernoff inequality
  \begin{align*}
    P[X \leq \alpha ] &\leq \inf_{ t > 0 } M_X(t) e^{-ta} \\
    M_X(t) &= \E[ e^{tX} ] \\
    \text{EVaR}_\alpha(X) &= \inf_{ z > 0 } \dfrac1z \log \dfrac{ M_X(1/z) }{ \alpha };
  \end{align*}
\item RLVaR;
  \begin{align*}
    \text{RLVaR}_\alpha^\kappa & \underset{\kappa \to 0}{\longrightarrow} \text{EVaR}_\alpha \\
    \text{RLVaR}_\alpha^\kappa & \underset{\kappa \to 1}{\longrightarrow} \text{EssSup}
  \end{align*}
  \[ \text{VaR} \leq \text{CVaR} \leq \text{TG} \leq \text{EVaR} \lq \text{RLVaR} \leq \text{EssSup} \]
\item Worst: $\text{VaR}_0$ (convex, not coherent).
\end{compactitem}

\emph{Range} risk measures include:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Range (convex, not coherent);
\item CVaR range (coherent);
\item TG range (coherent);
\item EVaR range;
\item RLVaR range.
\end{compactitem}

\emph{Drawdown} risk measures include
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Average drawdown
  $\text{ADD} = \operatorname*{Mean}_t \text{DD}_t$;
\item Ulcer index $\text{UCI} = \sqrt{ \operatorname*{Mean}_t \text{DD}_t^2 }$;
\item Drawdown at risk DaR (not convex);
\item CDaR, EDaR, RLDaR, MDD.
\end{compactitem}

\emph{Higher moment} risk measures include
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Skewness: it is not convex, but the skewness tensor
  $[S_1|\cdots|S_T]$ can be decomposed into negative and positive contributions
  $S_i^\pm = Q_i \Lambda_i^\pm Q_i$,
  \begin{align*}
    \sigma^3 &= w' ( \sum w_i S_i^+) w - w' ( \sum - w_i S_i^- ) w \\
    &\approx w' V^+ w - w' V^- w \quad \text{(quadratic skewness)}
  \end{align*}
  where $V^\pm = \pm \tfrac1n \sum S_i^\pm$;
  we can then minimize the negative skewness;
\item Kurtosis (convex);
\item $L$-moments (OWA): choose the weights of the first four moments using CRRA utility.
\end{compactitem}

\textbf{8.} The \emph{Kelly criterion} is
\[ \E \bigl[ \log( 1 + w' r ) \bigr] \approx w' \mu - \dfrac12 w' \Sigma w \]
(but you can optimize it directly with the exponential cone).
The optimization objective can combine returns and several risk measures.

For risk-adjusted returns (Sharpe, Sortino, Calman, Sterling, Omega, information ratio, etc.),
the problem is a \emph{convex fractional program},
which can be solved with the \emph{Schaible transform}.

\textbf{9.}
\emph{Constraints} (or penalties)
can include linear constraints (weights, exposure), leverage,
turnover, transaction costs, tracking error, market impact (power cone), effective number of assets ($\n x_2^{-2}$),
or even (with MIP), cardinality, mutual exclusion, minimum weight.

\textbf{10.} \emph{Risk parity} is used to aggregate a small number of strategies,
rather than a large number of assets.
For the volatility, it can be solved with least squares;
for general risk measures, use risk budgeting
(in this case, the risk budgeting constraint is not immediately visible, but it appears when you write the KKT conditions).
For long-short portfolios, you can use a MIP formulation.
You can add a return constraint.
With a risk model, this can be generalized to contributions of risk factors.

\textbf{11.}
The easiest way to make optimization \emph{robust} is to resample, and average the resulting weights.

Stochastic optimization
\[ \begin{array}{ll}
  \text{Find} & w \\
  \text{To maximize} & \E[ w' X ] \\
  \text{Such that} & t \leq \bar t \qquad \text{(maximum VaR)} \\
  & P\bigl[ - w' X \geq t \bigr] \leq \alpha
\end{array} \]
leads to a scenario-based MIP formulation.

Worst case optimization uses an uncertainty region, which can be box-shaped, or elliptical (Mahalanobis).

The \emph{analytic center} of
$\{ x \ {:} \ \forall i \ f_i(x) \leq 0 , \ Ax = b \} $
is the solution of
\[ \begin{array}{ll}
  \text{Find} & x \\
  \text{To minimize} & - \sum \log - f_i(x) \\
  \text{Such that} & Ax = b .
\end{array} \]
The near optimal region around $w^*$ (on the efficient frontier) is
\begin{align*}
  \{ w \ & {:}\
  w' \mu \geq w^{*\prime} \mu - c_1 , \
  \rho(w) \leq \rho(w^*) + c_2 , \\
  & \hphantom{{:}\ } w \geq 0 , \ w' \mathbf 1 = 1
\}.
\end{align*}
The NOC (near-optimal centering) portfolio is the analytic center of the near optimal region;
you can then compute the NOC frontier.

\textbf{12.} There are several variants of \emph{hierarchical risk parity}:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Original HRP, which only uses the leaf order, not the dendrogram;
\item DBHT (direct bubble hierarchical tree), based on the PMFG;
\item HERC (hierarchical equal risk contribution);
\item NCO (nested cluster optimization):
  optimization in each cluster, followed by an optimization between clusters --
  if the inner and outer optimization objective and constraints are the same, this is very close to a global optimization.
\end{compactitem}

\textbf{13.}
You can use a \emph{graph} on the assets (MST, PMFG, TMFG) to define constraints:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Average centrality of the portfolio;
\item Do not invest in neighbours (assets at distance at most $\ell$): MIP or SDP relaxation;
\item Do not invest in the same cluster (MIP, SDP relaxation)
\end{compactitem}

\textbf{14.} To generate \emph{synthetic data},
use the stationary bootstrap
with optimal block length (\texttt{arch.bootstrap}) or copulas
(\texttt{copulae.Gaussian\-Mix\-ture\-Copula}) or a VAR model.

\textbf{15.}
For \emph{back-testing} (\texttt{vectorbt}), use
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item A moving or expanding window;
\item Cross-validation (purge, \ie, with embargo, to limit look-ahead bias);
\item Combinatorial purged cross-validation ($N$ splits, use $k$ of them to train).
\end{compactitem}

\textbf{B.}
The \emph{perspective function} of $f$ is
$p(x,k) = k f(x/k)$; it preserves convexity or concavity.

A \emph{convex fractional program} is of the form
\[ \operatorname*{Minimize}_x \dfrac{ g(x) }{ f(x) } \]
where $g$ is convex and $f$ concave and positive.
This is equivalent to
\[ \begin{array}{ll}
  \text{Find} & y, k, x \\
  \text{To minimize} & k \, g\left( \dfrac y k \right) \\
  \text{Such that} & k \, f\left( \dfrac yk \right) \geq 1 \\
  & k \geq 0 \\
  & k = \dfrac1{f(x)} \\
  & y = x k
\end{array} \]
(if $f$ is affine, you can replace the inequality for $f$ with an equality).

\emph{Conic duality} transforms the primal problem
\[
\begin{array}{ll}
  \text{Find} & x_1, x_2 \\
  \text{To minimize} & c_1' x_1 + c_2' x_2 \\
  \text{Such that} & A_1 x_1 + A_2 x_2 = b \\
  & x_1 \in K
\end{array}
\]
into the dual problem
\[
\begin{array}{ll}
  \text{Find} & s, y \\
  \text{To minimize} & b' y \\
  \text{Such that} &s + A_1' y = c_1 \\
  & A_2' y = c_2 \\
  & s \in K^*.
\end{array}
\]

\textbf{C.} \emph{MIPs}
can encode consraints such as
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item $x \in \{0\} \union [\ell,u]$:
  \( \ell z \leq x \leq uz , \ z \in \{0,1\};  \)
\item $z = \mathbf 1_{ Ax \leq b}$:
  \( Az \leq b + M(1-z), \ M \text{ large}; \)
\item Cardinality constraints.
\end{compactitem}

*** Book: Portfolio optimization

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Quantitative portfolio optimization:\\ advanced techniques and applications}\\
  M. Noguer i Alonso et al. (2025)
\end{book}

% 2. History
\textbf{2.}
Classical portfolio optimization methods include
Markowitz, risk parity, and hierarchical risk parity.

% 3. Modern portfolio theory
%TODO\\
\textbf{3.} The first chapters cover classical topics:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Mean-variance optimization with or without a risk-free asset; tangent portfolio;
\item 2-fund separation theorem;
\item CAPM;
\item Mulifactor model (APT);
\item Shrinkage or random matrix theory (RMT) to estimate the varaince matrix;
\item Returns estimation: historical, Black-Litterman, Jackknife (bias correction), penalized regression, bootstrap (for confidence intervals);
\item Portfolio optimization with CVaR constraint
\end{compactitem}
and Bayesian methods

% 4. Bayesian methods
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Black-Litterman;
\item gfBm (geometric fractional Brownian motion):
  geometric Brownian motion with the Wiener process replaced with a fractional Brownian motion $W^H$,
  with Hurst exponent $H$, defined as a Gaussian process (GP)
  with covariance function
  \[ \E[ W_t^H W_s^H ] = \tfrac12 \bigl(
  \va t ^{2H} + \va s ^{2H} - \va{t-s}^{2H}
  \bigr), \]
  with a beta prior  on the Hurst exponent $H$
\item Metropolis-Hastings
\item Kalman filter, particle filter
\item Hierarchical Bayesian models
\item Bayesian optimization, GP regression, GP classification
\end{compactitem}

% 5. Risk
\textbf{5.}
Besides VaR and CVaR
(technically, tail conditional expectation, espected shortfall and conditional value at risk are different,
but they coincide if the distribution of $X$ is smooth and $\E[X^-]<\infty$),
\begin{align*}
  \text{TCE}_\alpha &= \E\bigl[ Y | Y \geq \text{VaR}_\alpha(Y) \bigr] \\
  \text{ES}_\alpha &= \dfrac1{1-\alpha} \int_\alpha^1 \text{VaR}_u (Y) du \\
  \text{CVaR}_\alpha &= \inf_{ a \in \RR} a + \dfrac{ \E\bigl[ (Y-a)^+ \bigr] }{ 1 - \alpha }
\end{align*}
also consider
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Gini shortfall
  \[ \rho(Y) = \E[ Y_\alpha ] + \beta \E\bigl[ \va{ Y_\alpha' - Y_\alpha'' } \bigr] , \quad \beta < \tfrac12 \]
  where $Y_\alpha \!=\! Y \,|\, Y \!\leq\! \text{VaR}_\alpha$
  and $Y_\alpha'$ and $Y_\alpha''$ are iid copies of $Y_\alpha$;
\item Spectral risk measures,
  \[ - \int_0^1 q_X(u) \phi(u) du, \]
  with $\phi\geq0$,
  $\phi\begin{tikzpicture}[baseline = -0.5ex]
       \node[inner sep=0pt,outer sep=0pt,rotate = -45] (a) at (0,0)  {$\xrightarrow{}$};
    \end{tikzpicture}$,
  $\int\va\phi=1$,
  e.g., the exponential spectral risk measure, with
  \[ \phi(u) = \dfrac{ k e^{ - k u } }{ 1 - e^{-k} }. \]
\end{compactitem}

% 6. Factor models
\textbf{6.} Factor models can be:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Statistical (PCA-like);
\item Macroeconomic (time series);
\item cross-sectional (\`a la Fama-French).
\end{compactitem}
This chapter also contains a proof of a (rigorous version of)
the fundamental law of portfolio management.

% 7. Market impact, transaction costs, liquidity

% 8. Optimal control
\textbf{8.}
The \emph{optimal control} problem
\[ \begin{array}{ll}
  \text{Find} & u \\
  \text{To maximize} &  \displaystyle \int_0^T F( x_t, u_t, t ) dt + g(x_T) \\[3mm]
  \text{Such that} & \dot x = f( x_t , u_t, t ), x(0) = x_0
\end{array} \]
leads to the Hamilton-Jacobi-Bellman (HJB) equation
\[ - \dfrac{ \partial V }{ \partial t } = \max_u F( x, u, t ) + \nabla_x V \cdot f(x,u,t) \]
where $V(x_t,t) = \int_t^T F dt + g$.

A \d{viscosity solution} $V$ of
$F\bigl( x, V(x), \nabla V(x) \bigr) = 0 $,
$ x \in \Omega$
is a function $V$ satisfying
\begin{compactitem}[(ii)]
\item[(i)] For all $\phi \in \mathscr C^1(\Omega)$, if $V-\phi$ has a local maximum at $x_0$,
  then $F \bigl( x_0, V(x_0), \nabla \phi(x_0) \bigr) \leq 0$;
\item[(ii)] For all $\phi \in \mathscr C^1(\Omega)$, if $V-\phi$ has a local minimum at $x_0$,
  then $F \bigl( x_0, V(x_0), \nabla \phi(x_0) \bigr) \geq 0$.
\end{compactitem}

The \emph{Merton problem} looks for the proportion $\pi$ of wealth to invest
in a risky asset (assuming a geometric Brownian motion,
with drift, volatility and risk-free rate known) to maximize expected CRRA utility
\begin{align*}
  U(x) &= \dfrac{ x ^ { 1 - \gamma } }{ 1 - \gamma } \\
  dX &= r X dt + (\mu - r ) \pi X dt + \sigma \pi X dW ;
\end{align*}
it is constant (it does not depend on time and wealth):
\[ \pi^*** = \dfrac{ \mu - r }{ \gamma \sigma^2 }. \]
One can add transaction costs, or change the utility function.

% 9. MDP
\textbf{9.}
Applications of Markov decision processes (MDP) include
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Multi-period portfolio optimization,
  with transaction costs or regime switching;
\item Option hedging.
\end{compactitem}
To make MDPs aware of risk, one could alter the reward;
alternatively, one could direcly alter the Bellman equation,
either replacing the expected reward with the expected utility of reward,
or by adding a variance penalty (risk-sensitive MDPs).

% 10. RL
\textbf{10.}
Optimal control and reinforcement learning (RL) study the same problem
(OC tends to use continuous state and time,
while RL prefers discrete time).
\[ \cdots s_t \xrightarrow{\text{agent}} a_t \xrightarrow{\text{environment}} s_{t+1}, r_{t+1}
\xrightarrow{\text{agent}} a_{t+1} \cdots \]
A RL model keeps estimates of one or more of the following:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Policy $\pi(a|s)$;
\item Value function $V(s)$;
\item Action value function $Q(s,a)$;
\item Model $\hat p(s' | s, a)$, $\hat r( s, a, s')$.
\end{compactitem}
% G-learning: not clear

% 11. Deep learning in portfolio management
\textbf{11.}
\emph{Physics-informed neural nets}
can fit a PDE $D f_\theta=0$
(Black-Scholes or, more generally, the Fokker-Planck PDE of some SDE --
the shape of the PDE is known, but some of its parameters are not) to data:
\[
\operatorname*{Minimize}_\theta \operatorname*{Mean}_i
\n{ f_\theta( x_i, t_i ) \!-\! u_i } ^2 \!+
\operatorname*{Mean}_j \n{ D f_\theta( x_j, t_j ) }^2.
\]
They can also be used for SDEs, $
dX = \mu dt + \sigma dW$,
by minimizing
\[ \left( \dfrac{ dX }{ dt } - \mu \right) ^2
\quad \text{and} \quad
\bigl[
  ( X_{t + \Delta t} - X_t )^2 - \sigma^2 \Delta t
  \bigr]^2;
\]
one can also add jumps.

\textbf{12.}
Build the MST  or TMFG of the correlation matrix of the asset returns,
and invest in low-centrality assets (on average),
while avoiding investing in nearly assets.


% 13. Sensitivity-based portfolio [not clear]
%TODO
\textbf{13.}
Hierarchical risk parity can be applied to other matrices, e.g.:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Compute the sensitivity $B$ of asset returns $\mathbf r$ to risk factors $\mathbf f$:
$\mathbf r = B \mathbf f + \epsilon $
(with a linear model, or a sparse neural net -- use automatic differentiation to compute $B$);
\item Compute the distances between the assets in sensitivity space;
\item Use that distance matrix to build a hierarchical risk parity portfolio.
\end{compactitem}

*** Portfolio optimization: Drawdown

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Constrained max drawdown: a fast and robust portfolio optimization approach}\\
  A. Dorador
\end{book}
The Markowitz portfolio optimization problem
(minimizing risk with a minimum return constraint)
can be linearized:
replace the risk with
\[ \sum_t \va{ \sum_i ( r_{it} - \bar r_i ) w_i } \]
or
\[ \min_t \sum_i r_{it} w_i \]
(add $0 \leq w \leq \tfrac12$ to avoid the 1-asset portfolio).
To limit the number of assets (e.g.,
$\forall i\ w_i \neq 0 \implies w_i \leq 0.05$),
use the big-M transform (with $M=0.5$).

  \emph{Porfolio construction with Gaussian mixture returns and exponential utility\\ via convex optimization}\\
  E. Luxenberg and S. Boyd (2022)
\end{book}
EVaR portfolio optimization has a convex (non-sample-based) formulation.

*** performance measures, Sharpe ratio

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{In-sample and out-of-sample Sharpe ratios\\ for linear predictive models}\\
  A. Jacquier et al. (2025)
\end{book}
Assume returns $\mathbf r$ and signals $\mathbf s$ are Gaussian, and linearly related,
\begin{align*}
  r_{t+1} &= \beta s_t + \epsilon_{t+1} \\
  s_t &\sim N( \mu_s, \Sigma_s ) & s \indep \epsilon \\
  \epsilon_t &\sim N(0, \Sigma_\epsilon)
\end{align*}
The Markowitz weights, P\&L, and Sharpe ratio are
\begin{align*}
  w_t &= \Sigma_\epsilon^{-1} \beta s_t \\
  \text{PnL}_t &= w' r_{t+1}  \\
  \text{SR} &= \dfrac{ \E[ \text{PnL} ] }{ \sqrt{ \var[ \text{PnL} ] } }.
\end{align*}
We can compare SR and
\[ \widehat{\text{SR}} = \dfrac{
    \E \bigl[ \widehat\E[ \text{PnL} ] \bigr]
  }{
    \E\bigl[ \widehat{\var}[\text{PnL}] \bigr]
  }.
\]

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Detection of false investment strategies using unsupervised learning methods}\\
  M. L\'opez de Prado and M.J. Lewis (2018)
\end{book}
Computing the deflated Sharpe ratio
(not a Sharpe ratio, but 1 minus the p-value for the test $\text{SR}=0$, accounting for multiple testing)
requires the number of independent trials and their variance:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Use $k$-means and the silhouette score on the strategy returns
  (distance between the columns of the correlation matrix) to estimate the number of independent trials;
\item Replace each cluster with its minimum variance portfolio to compute the variance.
\end{compactitem}

*** Sharpe ratio

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Detection of false investment strategies\\ using unsupervised learning methods}\\
  M. L\'opez de Prado and M.J. Lewis (2018)
\end{book}
The Sharpe ratio is asymptotically Gaussian
\[ \widehat{\text{SR}} - \text{SR}_0 \longrightarrow
N \left(
0 ,
\dfrac{
  1 - \gamma_2 \text{SR}_0 + \dfrac{ \gamma_4 - 1 }{4} \text{SR}^2
}{
  T
}
\right) \]
where $\gamma_3$ is the skewness and $\gamma_4$ the (non-excess) kurtosis.

The \emph{probabilistic Sharpe ratio} is the Sharpe ratio ``on a probabilistic scale'',
$\text{PSR} = 1 - p$, where $p$ is the $p$-value of the test
\[ H_0: \text{SR} = \text{SR}_0 \qquad \text{vs} \qquad
H_1: \text{SR} > \text{SR}_0. \]

If there are $k$ trials, replace $\text{SR}_0=0$ with
\[ \sqrt{ \var[\text{SR}] } \left[
  (1-\gamma) \Phi^{-1} \left( 1 - 1/K\right) + \gamma \Phi^{-1} \left(1 - \dfrac1{Ke}\right)
  \right].
\]

To estimate the number of clusters, run $k$-means on the columns of the correlation or distance matrix,
and choose $k$ to maximize the \emph{silhouette quality}
\[ q = \dfrac{ E[ S_i ] }{ \sqrt{ \var[ S_i ] } } \]
where $S_i$ is the silhouette of observation $i$.

To compute the returns of a representative strategy in each cluster, use minimum variance
\[ r_t = \sum_i w_i r_{it}, \qquad
w = \dfrac{ \Sigma^{-1} \mathbf 1 }{ \mathbf 1 ' \Sigma^{-1} \mathbf 1 }. \]
Since $\Sigma$ is ill-conditioned, approximate it as a constant correlation variance matrix,
\[ \Sigma \approx \rho \sigma \sigma' + (1-\rho) \text{diag}\,\sigma^2 \]
and use the Sherman-Morrison formula (rank-one update of an inverse).
You can then compute $\var[\text{SR}]$.

\[ \text{Ent}_\gamma[X] = \gamma^{-1} \log \E[ e^{\gamma X} ] \]

** TODO Jump model, other models
*** Jump model

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Regime-aware factor allocation\\ with optimal feature selection}\\
  Bosanic et al. (2025)
\end{book}
To detect regimes on a (multivariate) time series $y$,
use $k$-means with a jump penalty
\[ \operatorname*{Minimize}_{\substack{
    \theta_1, \theta_2 \in \RR^d \\
    s_1 , \dots, s_T \in \{0,1\}
}} \sum_t \n{ y_t - \theta_{s_t} }_2^2 + \lambda \sum_t \mathbf 1_{ s_t \neq s_{t-1} }
\]
or its relaxation (probability simplex and fused lasso),
the \emph{jump model}
\[ \operatorname*{Minimize}_{\substack{
    \theta_1, \theta_2 \in \RR^d \\
    s_1 , \dots , s_T \in \Delta_d  % Shouldn't it be \Delta_2, i.e., [0,1]?
}} \sum_t \sum_k s_{t,k} \n{ y_t - \theta_k }_2^2
+ \lambda \sum_t \n{ s_t - s_{t-1} }_1^2.  % I would use \n{...}_2, the (unsquared) L^2 norm
\]
Add feature selection (to maximize the Sharpe ratio); apply to factor returns
(high P/B, low P/B, high profitability, large, small)
with factor returns, S\&P 500 returns, P/E, D/P as features,
with two regimes (growth and crash),
to decide whether to invest in a factor or in 3-month US treasury bills.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Learning hidden Markov models with persistent states by penalizing jumps}\\
  P. Nystrup et al. (2020)
\end{book}
Hidden Markov models (HMM) can be estimated with \emph{spectral clustering}:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Compute features $\mathbf z_t$:
  value $y_t$;
  left and right absolute change
  $\va{y_t - y_{t-1}}$,
  $\va{y_t - y_{t+1}}$;
  mean and standard deviation over $[t-\ell,t+\ell]$, $[t-\ell,t]$, $[t,t+\ell]$
  ($\ell=2$ and 6)
  and standardize them;
\item Compute the affinity matrix
  \[ A_{ij} = \exp \bigl( - \gamma \n{ z_i - z_j }_2^2 \bigr) \]
  and normalized Laplacian $L_{\text{rw}}= I - D^{-1} A $;
\item Compute the first $k$ eigenvectors of $L$;
\item Group their rows with $k$-means.
\end{compactitem}

The \emph{jump model} is
\[ \operatorname*{Minimize}_{\substack{
    s_1,\dots,s_T \in \llbracket 1, k \rrbracket \\
    \theta_1 , \dots, \theta_k \in \RR^d
}} \sum_t \n{ z_t - \theta_{s_t} }_2^2 + \lambda \sum_t \mathbf 1_{ s_t \neq s_{t-1} } . \]
It can be estimated by iteratively fitting $\theta$ and $s$.
For the state $s$, use dynamic programming:
\begin{align*}
  V(T,s) &= \n{ z_T - \theta_s }_2^2 \\
  V(t,i) &= \n{ z_t - \theta_i }_2^2 + \min_j \bigl[ V(t+1,j) + \lambda \mathbf 1_{i \neq j } \bigr] \\
  s_1 &= \argmin_j V(1,j) \\
  s_t &= \argmin_j V(t,j) + \lambda \mathbf 1_{ s_{t-1} \neq j }.
\end{align*}

Python implementation in \texttt{jumpmodels}.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Fitting jump models}\\
  A. Bemporad et al. (2018)
\end{book}
% Not read. General jump models.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{A framework for feature selection in clustering}\\
  D.M. Witten and R. Tibshirani (2010)
\end{book}
$k$-means clustering minimizes
$\sum_t \n{ y_t - \mu_{s_t}}$ but,
since
\[ \sum_t \n{ y_t - \bar\mu }^2 = \sum_t \n{ y_t - \mu_{s_t} }^2 + \sum_k n_k \n{ \mu_k - \bar\mu }^2 , \]
where $n_k$ is the number of observations in cluster $k$,
we can instead maximize the between-cluster sum of squares.

Sparse $k$-means solves the optimization problem
\[ \begin{array}{ll}
  \text{Find} & \mu, s, w \\
  \text{To maximize} & w' \sum\limits_k n_k ( \mu_k - \bar\mu) ^2 \\[2mm]
  \text{Such that} & \n w ^2 \leq 1 , \ \n w _1 \leq \kappa, \ w \geq 0.
\end{array} \]
Sparse hierarchical clustering is similar.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Feature selection in jump models}\\
  P. Nystrup et al. (2021)
\end{book}
The jump model is an extension of $k$-means.
Using sparse $k$-means instead provides feature selection.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Identifying patterns in financial markets:\\
  extending the statistical jump model\\ for regime identification}\\
  A.O. Ayd\i nhan et al. (2024)
\end{book}
The jump model can be generalized to continuous states $\mathbf s_t \in \Delta _K$.
\[ \operatorname*{Minimize}_{\substack{
    \theta_1, \dots , \theta_K \\
    \mathbf s_1 , \dots , \mathbf s_T
}} \sum_t \sum_k s_{tk} \n{ z_t - \theta_k }_2^2 + \lambda \sum_t \n{ \mathbf s_{t-1} - \mathbf s_t }_1^2 \]
Alternatively, fit $\Theta$ and $S$ (quadratic programming,
or approximate dynamic programming).
Add a mode loss
\[ \log \int_{ \Delta_K } e^{
  - \lambda \n{ \mathbf s_{t-1} - \mathbf s }_2^2
} d\mathbf s
\]
to make sparse vectors sparser.

*** Finance, Jump model

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Regime-aware factor allocation\\ with optimal feature selection}\\
  T. Bosancic et al.
\end{book}
The discrete jump model is
\[ \operatorname*{Minimize}_{\substack{
    \theta_1, \dots, \theta_K \in \RR^D \\
    s \in \llbracket 1 , K \rrbracket ^T
}} \sum_t \ell( y_t , \theta_{ s_t } ) + \lambda \sum \mathbf 1_{ s_{t-1} = s_t }. \]
The continuous jump model replaces states $s \in \llbracket 1, K \rrbracket$
with probabilities $s \in \Delta_K$.
\begin{align*}
& \operatorname*{Minimize}_{ \substack{
    \theta_1, \dots, \theta_K \in \RR^D \\
    s_1 , \dots , s_T \in \Delta_K
}}
\sum_t L ( y_t , \Theta , s_t )
+ \dfrac{ \lambda}{ 4 } \sum_t \n{ s_{t-1} - s_t }_1^2 \\
& \text{where }
L( y_t , \Theta, s_t ) = \sum_k s_{tk} \ell( y_y, \theta_k )
\end{align*}
*** Regimes

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Regimes}\\
  A. Mulliner et al. (2025)
\end{book}
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Take a few macro-economic variables
  (S\&P 500, 10y-3m, 3m, oil, copper, VIX, stock-bond correlation);
\item Compute the (Euclidean) distance between today's state and past states;
\item Buy (sell) assets with positive (negative) average returns in similar past situations.
\end{compactitem}
One can also look at the most dissimilar past states, and take the opposite actions.
[It works better with the Mahalanobis distance;
  a learned Mahalanobis distance gives similar results out-of-sample but overfits in-sample.]

  Q. Luan and J. Hamp (2023)

*** Regimes

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Enhancing portfolio efficiency: a machine learning approach to regime classification}\\
  A.D. Thomson
\end{book}
% No details!
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Take macroeconomic variables (unspecified) and market data (returns, momentum, volatility);
\item Reduce the dimension (PCA, to keep 99\% of the variance);
\item Cluster (hclust) into regimes, ``durable'' and ``fragile'' (less frequent, includes the begining of large drawdowns);
\item Balance the data (\textsc{smote}) and predict the next regime with $k$-NN ($k=9$).
\end{compactitem}
In the durable regime, invest in growth, quality, momentum, size, high-beta ETFs;
in the fragile regime, invest in defensive sectors and low-vol ETFs.

*** Learning to rank

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Enhancing long-short portfolios: a refined approach using learning to rank algorithms}\\
  M. Linger et al.
\end{book}
Portfolio consruction is often a 2-step process:
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,scale=.25,inner sep=1pt,outer sep=2pt,every node/.style={anchor=base}]
  \node[anchor=base] (A) {data\strut};
  \node[right=6mm of A] (B) {score\strut};
  \node[right=6mm of B] (C) {weights\strut};
  \draw[|->] (A) -- (B);
  \draw[|->] (B) -- (C);
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
(the score could be a financial ratio, or a forward return forecast)
where the weights often depend on the ranks of the score.
Instead, learning to rank (LTR) uses a 1-step process.
The loss functions can be
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Poinwise (forecast the rank of an observation);
\item Pairwise (predict which of two observations is ranked higher), e.g., RankNet;
  \begin{align*}
    \text{loss}( X, y, \theta ) &= \sum_{ y_i < y_j } \phi \bigl[ f_\theta(X_j) - f_\theta(X_i) \bigr] \\
    \phi &=
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=.2,yscale=.3,inner sep=1pt,outer sep=2pt,every node/.style={anchor=base}]
\draw[very thick,blue!60!black] (-1.000,1.313)--(-0.737,1.128)--(-0.474,0.958)--(-0.211,0.804)--(0.053,0.667)--(0.316,0.548)--(0.579,0.445)--(0.842,0.358)--(1.105,0.286)--(1.368,0.227)--(1.632,0.179)--(1.895,0.140)--(2.158,0.109)--(2.421,0.085)--(2.684,0.066)--(2.947,0.051)--(3.211,0.040)--(3.474,0.031)--(3.737,0.024)--(4.000,0.018);
\draw (-1,0) -- (4,0);
\draw (2,-.2) -- (2,1.4);
\end{tikzpicture}
  \end{align*}
  (e.g., $\phi(z) = \log(1+e^{-z})$, $(1-z)_+$ or $e^{-z}$);
\item Listwise
  \begin{align*}
    \text{loss}( X, y ; \theta ) &=
    - \sum_i \dfrac{ \phi(y_i) }{ \sum_j \phi(y_j) } \log
    \dfrac{\phi f_\theta(X_i) }{ \sum_j \phi f_\theta( X_j ) }
    \text{ (ListNet, cross-entropy)}
    \\
    \text{loss}( X, y ; \theta ) &=
    - \log \prod_i \dfrac{
      \phi f_\theta( X_{ y^{-1}(i) } )
    }{
      \sum_{ k \geq i } \phi f_\theta( X_{ y^{-1}(k) } )
    }
    \text{ (ListMLE, Plackett-Luce)}
    \\
    \text{loss}( X, y ; \theta ) &=
    - \log \prod_i \dfrac{
      \phi \bigl[
        f_\theta( X_{y^{-1}(i)} ) - f_\theta( X_{y^{-1}(N-i)} )
        \bigr]
    }{
      \sum_{ i \leq u \neq v \leq N-i }
      \phi \bigl[
        f_\theta( X_{y^{-1}(u)} ) - f_\theta( X_{y^{-1}(v)} )
        \bigr]
    }
    \text{ (Listfold)}
  \end{align*}
  (ListNet is based on cross-entropy,
  ListMLE on the Plackett-Luce model,
  ListFold selects long-short pairs one by one starting with the most extreme).
\end{compactitem}
For portfolio construction, we want the ranking to be more accurate at the top and bottom
(for information retrieval, we only care at the top).
Try the following loss functions:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item ListNet-Fold, cross-entropy of $\phi(y_i-y_j)$
  and $\phi\bigl( f_\theta(X_i) - f_\theta(X_j) \bigr)$;
\item Weighted ListMLE, with weights decreasing from extreme pairs down to central ones;
\item Weighted ListFold.
\end{compactitem}

*** Supervised portfolios

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Interpretable supervised portfolios}\\
  G. Chevalier et al.
\end{book}
Instead of forecasting future returns, the \emph{supervised portfolio approach} suggests
to forecast weights -- optimal weights, computed in hindsight.

\emph{RuleFit} can make that model interpretable:
train a random forest or boosted trees,
explode them into a set of rules (each path from a root node to an internal or leaf node defines a rule),
and combine them with the \emph{adaptive lasso}
(which shrinks smaller weights even more).

*** Finance, Conformal prediction for time series

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Conformal predictive portfolio selection}\\
  M. Kato (2024)
\end{book}
Consider a model forecasting future returns $Y_t$ from asset features $X_t$:
\[ \hat Y_{t+1} = \hat f_t( X_{t+1} ) \]
where $\hat f_t$ is trained on
$(X_1,Y_1),(X_2,Y_),\dots,(X_t,Y_t)$.
The returns of a portfolio with weights $w$ are $w' Y_t$;
the forecasted returns are $w' \hat Y_{t+1}$.
Apply conformal prediction (CP)
to those portfolio return predictions:
for each $w$, we have a prediction interval
\[ \hat C_t^w ( X_{t+1} = [ \underline r _{t+1} ^w , \bar r_{t+1}^w ]. \]
Find the portfolio weights minimizing some loss function of those intervals
\[ w^*** = \argmin_w \ell\bigl( \hat C_t^w ( X_{t+1} ) \bigr) .\]

For instance:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Start with a finite set of portfolios $\mathscr E$;
\item Compute their prediction intervals at level $\alpha$:
  $ [ \underline r_w , \bar r_w ] $;
\item Consider a proportion $\beta$ of portfolios,
  $\tilde{\mathscr E} \subset \mathscr E$,
  with the highest
  $\underline r_w$
  (low risk, measured with the VaR);
\item Among those, pick the porfolio with the highest $\bar r_w$.
\end{compactitem}

To account for the time series lack of exchangeability,
use full conformal prediction,
\ie, fit the model on augmented datasets
(add $(X_{t+1},y)$ for all possible values of $y$)
and on all permuted datasets
\[ (X_{\sigma(1)}, y_{\sigma(1)}), \dots, (X_{\sigma(t)}, y_{\sigma(t)}), (X_{\sigma(t+1)}, y) \]
for all $\sigma \in \mathfrak S_n$.
It is actually sufficient to consider only shifts, \ie, permutations of the form
\[ \exists j \in \llbracket 0, t-1 \rrbracket \quad
\forall i \in \llbracket 1, t \rrbracket \quad
\sigma(i) \equiv i+j \ (\text{mod}\ t) \]
