** TODO Technical
*** Small mean-reverting portfolios

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Identifying small mean-reverting portfolios}\\
  A. d'Aspremont (2007)
\end{book}
\textbf{1.}
We assume asset prices are modeled by a stationary structural VAR model.
\begin{align*}
  S_t &= A S_{t-1} + \epsilon_t \\
  \Gamma &= \var[ S ]
\end{align*}
``Stationary'' means that the eigenvalues of $A$ are all less than 1 in absolute value.
``Structural'' means that $A$ (and $\Gamma$) are sparse.

We estimate $A$ with lasso regressions.
We estimate $\Gamma$ with the graphical lasso (a sparsity penalty on $\Gamma^{-1}$, which tends to be sparser than $\Gamma$).

\textbf{2.} We want to find mean-reverting portfolios $w$. We can define ``mean-reverting'' in two ways:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item The portfolio value $p_t = w' S_t$ should follow an Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process,
  \[ p_t - p_{t-1} = \lambda ( \bar p - p_{t-1} ) + \epsilon_t \]
  with a large value of $\lambda$;
\item The portfolio value $p_t = w' S_t$ should not be predictable; the predictability can be defined as
  \[ \nu = \dfrac{ \var[ \hat p ] }{ \var [p] }. \]
\end{compactitem}
Those quantities can be computed either empirically, from $p = w ' S$,
or theoretically, from the VAR model.
\begin{align*}
  \lambda &= 1 - \beta \\
          &= 1 - \dfrac{ \cov[ p_t , p_{t-1} ] }{ \var[ p_{t-1} ] } \\
          &= 1 - \dfrac{ w' A \Gamma w }{ w' \Gamma w }\\[1mm]
  \nu &= \dfrac{ \var[ \hat p ] }{ \var[p] } \\
          &= \dfrac{ \var[ w' \hat S_t ] }{ \var[ w' S_t ] } \\
          &= \dfrac{ \var[ w' A S_{t-1} ] }{ \var[ w' S_t ] } \\
          &= \dfrac{ w' A \Gamma A' w }{ w' \Gamma w }
\end{align*}

\textbf{3.} Optimizing the theoretical quantities is a sparse generalized eigenvalue problem.
It can be solved in three ways:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Greedily: iteratively select the index of the variable that maximizes the ratio;
\item Exactly: exhaustive search for small matrices (up to 15$\times$15);
\item SDP (semidefinite programming) relaxation (only for the predictability: for the mean reversion, $A\Gamma$ is not symmetric).
\end{compactitem}

Instead of looking for mean-reverting portfolios, we can look for momentum portfolios, by minimizing the mean reversion $\lambda$ or
maximizing the predictability $\nu$.

% The paper uses row vectors; I use column vectors: their A is my A'
% They use a continuous time OU process: there is a log in their λ, which is not always defined.

*** small mean-reverting portfolios

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Identifying small mean-reverting portfolios}\\
  A. d'Aspremont (2007)
\end{book}
Assume asset prices come from a stationary structural (\ie, sparse) VAR model
\begin{align*}
  S_t &= A S_{t-1} + \epsilon_t \\
  \Gamma &= \var S_t
\end{align*}
It is stationary if the spectral radius $\rho(A)$ of $A$ is less than 1.

$\Gamma$ satisfies the \emph{discrete-time Lyapunov equation}, $\Gamma = A \Gamma A' + \var \epsilon$ --
to solve it (if $\rho(A)<1$), take the vec of both sides to get a linear equation,
or use iterations $\Gamma \leftarrow A \Gamma A' + V$ (implementation in \texttt{scipy}).

We can estimate $A$ with the lasso and $\Gamma$ with the graphical lasso.

To find a (sparse) mean-reverting portfolio,
look for weights $w$ minimizing the predictability
\[ \nu = \dfrac{ \var[ w' \hat S_t ] }{ \var[ w' S_t ] } = \dfrac{ w' A \Gamma A' w }{ w' \Gamma w }. \]
This is a (sparse) generalized eigenvalue problem,
\ie, a problem of the form
\[ \begin{array}{ll}
  \text{Find} & x \in \RR^n \\
  \text{To maximize} & \dfrac{ x' A x }{ x' B x } \\[2mm]
  \text{Such that} & \n x_2 = 1 , \ \n x _0 \leq k.
\end{array} \]
It can be solved:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Greedily;
\item With an SDP relaxation, setting $X = xx'$, $X \succcurlyeq 0$, $\text{rank}\,X = 1$;
\item By brute force search (by looking at all the subsets of size $k$).
\end{compactitem}

[We could also directly look for sparse portfolios $w$ maximizing the mean reversion $\lambda$
  \[ d p_t = \lambda ( \bar p - p_t ) dt + \sigma dW_t, \]
  or, equivalently, minimizing the half-life $\tau = \log 2 / \lambda$, e.g., with BFGS,
  or directly, since
  \[ \lambda = - \dfrac{ w' A \Gamma w }{ w' \Gamma w } \]
  (it is another generalized eigendecomposition problem, but $A \Gamma$ is not psd).]

*** pairs

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Investment base pairs}\\
  C.L. Goulding and C.R. Harvey (2025)
\end{book}
Given two assets and asset-specific signals,
\begin{align*}
  R &= \begin{pmatrix} r_1 \\ r_2 \end{pmatrix} && \text{asset returns} \\
  X &= \begin{pmatrix} x_1 \\ x_2 \end{pmatrix} && \text{asset signals} \\
  R &= \mu + B X + \epsilon && X \sim N(m,S) \\
  &&& \epsilon \sim N(0,\Delta) \\
  &&& \epsilon \indep X \\
  x &= x_1 - x_2 \\
  r &= r_1 - r_2
\end{align*}
we can compute the expected returns of pairs trading strategies
$ \E\bigl[ \text{sign}(x) r \bigr] $
and
$ \E[ r | x \geq 0 ]$
and decompose them into the contributions of $(\mu,\epsilon)$,
$\text{diag}(B)$ and the off-diagonal of $B$.

and investor views

compute a posterior distribution
\[ \operatorname*{Argmin}_{ p = N(\mu,V) } d( p, p_{\text{prior}} ) \text{\ \ such that\ \ } d( p, p_{\text{view}} ) \leq d_0 \]
or
\[ \operatorname*{Argmin}_{ p = N(\mu,V) } d( p, p_{\text{prior}} ) + \lambda  d( p, p_{\text{view}} ) \leq d_0. \]
This readily generalizes to degenerate views.

The factor zoo can be tamed by separating \emph{price factors} from \emph{attribution factors}.

they are described by the PAPM (popularity asset pricing model) equilibrium model.

*** No

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Attribution-based residual decomposition\\ and overfitting detection in linear regression}\\
  P. Bilokon (2025)
\end{book}
For a linear regression $y = X \hat \beta + e $
and rescale the forecast (separately for each observation)
to match the target variable,
$\alpha \odot X \hat \beta = y$, \ie, $\alpha = y \oslash ( X \hat \beta )$.
The residuals are then
\begin{align*}
  e &= y - X \hat\beta \\
    &= \alpha \odot X \hat\beta - X \hat\beta \\
    &= (\alpha-1) \odot (X \hat\beta).
\end{align*}
Each residual can be decomposed into a sum of contributions from each input feature
\[ e_i = \sum_j ( \alpha_i - 1 ) X_{ij} \beta_j. \]

Compare the decompostion between the training and the test set, e.g.,
\[ R_j = \dfrac{
    \operatorname*{Mean}_{ i \in \text{Test} } e_{ij}^2
  }{
    \operatorname*{Mean}_{ i \in \text{Train} } e_{ij}^2
  }
  \qquad
  \text{or}
  \qquad
  V_j =  \dfrac{
    \operatorname*{Std}_{ i \in \text{Test} } e_{ij}^2
  }{
    \operatorname*{Std}_{ i \in \text{Train} } e_{ij}^2
  }
\]
and adjust the coefficients, e.g.,
\[ \beta_j \leftarrow \dfrac1{ 1 + (V_j-1) \lambda } \beta_j
  \text{ if } V_j > 1 . \]
% Unconvincing: limited to linear regression; not translation-invariant; numerically unstable if $X\hat\beta$ is close to zero; the corrections seem to systematically increase the MSE

\begin{compactitem}[--]

\item Have a ``research associate'' agent extract the information (event overview, time, location, presenter, participants, agenda, strategic initiatives, financial performance, competition, management commentary, analyst Q\&A, key takeaways, commentary, sentiment -- time, location and sentiment matter);

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Systematic thematic investing\\ and risk management}\\
  Wolfe Research (2025)
\end{book}
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Take news and analyst reports;
\item Identify entities, in 12 categories
  (organizations, governments, regulatory bodies, locations, persons, companies, products, events, sectors,
  economic indicators, financial instruments, concepts);
\item Disambiguate them, \ie, merge near-duplicates, using contextual embeddings (BERT, MPNet);
\item Extract time-stamped triples,
  (entity$_1$, relation, entity$_2$, timestamp),
  for 13 relations
  (impact, relate to, has, announce, operate in, introduce, produce, participate in, control, is member of, invest in, raise, decrease);
\item Compute the sentiment of each entity mentioned, at the paragraph level;
\item Use \emph{Katz centrality}
  (on the knowledge graph on a 1-month window) to assess the importance of each entity;
\item Define the \emph{polarity} as the product of the centrality and (average neighbour) sentiment;
\item Merge similar entities, using cosine similarity ($>09\%$) into 20 ``themes'' in each category;
\item Compute the polarity of each theme;
\item Identify companies related to a theme (this is a recommendation system problem:
  users are themes, items companies);
\item Use a GNN to forecast future links. % dubious
\end{compactitem}

To build a risk model from the themes,
controlling the error-in-variables (EIV) bias):
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Define the (raw) theme returns $f$ as the polarity minus the average polarity over the past 3 months;
\item Identify stocks highly relevant for that theme;
\item Compute a supervised PCA on those stock returns;
\item Regress the theme returns on the first principal components;
\item Use the corresponding forecasts as cleaned theme returns $\tilde f$;
\item Compute the exposures with regressions
  \[ R_{it} = \alpha_i + \beta_i \tilde f_t + \beta^{\text{mkt}} f_t^{\text{mkt}} + \epsilon_{it} \]
  (separately for each stock and theme).
\end{compactitem}

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Simple formulas for standard errors\\ that cluster by both firm and time}\\
  S.B. Thompson (2009)
\end{book}
In a panel regression
$ y_{it} = x_{it}' \beta + \epsilon_{it}$,
if there is correlation among $i$, $t$ and $(i,t)$ (up to lag $L$)
\begin{align*}
  & \E[ \epsilon_{it} \epsilon_{is} | x_{it} x_{is} ] \neq 0 \\
  & \E[ \epsilon_{it} \epsilon_{jt} | x_{it} x_{jt} ] \neq 0 \\
  & \E[ \epsilon_{it} \epsilon_{js} | x_{it} x_{js} ] \neq 0 \text{ if } \va{t-s}  < L
\end{align*}
then
\[ \var \hat\beta = \hat V_I + \hat V_{T,0} - \hat V_{W,0} +
\sum_{ 1 \leq \ell \leq L } ( V_{T,\ell} + V_{T,\ell}' ) -
\sum_{1\leq \ell \leq L} ( \hat V_{w,\ell} + \hat V_{w,\ell}' )
\]
where
\begin{align*}
  \hat V_I &= H^{-1} \sum_i \hat c_i \hat c_i' H^{-1} \\
  \hat V_{T,\ell} &= H^{-1} \sum_t \hat s_t \hat s_{t+\ell} ' H^{-1} \\
  \hat V_{w,\ell} &+ H^{-1} \sum_{it} \hat u_{it} \hat u_{i,t+\ell} ' H^{-1} \\
  \hat u_{it} &= x_{it} \hat\epsilon_{it} \\
  \hat c_i &= \sum_t \hat u_{it} \\
  \hat s_t &= \sum_i \hat u_{it} \\
  \hat\epsilon_{it} &= y_{it} - x_{it} \hat\beta \\
  H &= \sum_{it} x_{it} x_{it} '
\end{align*}

Commonly used estimators of the standard error in panel regressions are biased:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Newey-West has a small bias;
\item Fama-MacBeth only accounts for correlation between firms;
\item Clustered standard errors only account for correlation over time.
\end{compactitem}

* TODO Books, Tutorials
*** Book: Conformal prediction

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Introduction\\ to conformal prediction with Python}\\
  C. Molnar (2023)
\end{book}

\textbf{6.}
Conformal prediction (CP) works as follows:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Split the data into training and calibration
  (and test, and validation) sets;
\item Train the model on the training set
\item Compute a \emph{non-conformity score} for all observations in the calibration set
  (any measure of how unusual $y$ is, given $x$), e.g.,
  $ 1 - \hat p(y|x)$, for a classification model;
\item Compute its $1-\alpha$ quantile $\hat q$
  (with a finite-sample adjustment);
\item Given new data, $x$, return all the $y$'s whose non-conformity score is below $\hat q$ as \emph{prediction set}.
\end{compactitem}
(This is inductive CP;
transductive CP uses the whole dataset, but needs to refit the model many times).
For the splits, you can use a single split, cross-validation (CV) or leave-out-one (LOO).

\textbf{7.}
For classification:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item The \emph{score method} uses the probabilities of the true classes to compute the conformity scores:
  $s_i = 1 - \hat p(x_i)_{ y_i }$
  (where $\hat p(x_i)$ is the vector of predicted probabilities);
  it is not \emph{adaptive}:
  it only guarantees \emph{marginal coverage},
  not \emph{class-conditional coverage} -- the coverage is $1-\alpha$ only on average,
  but it can be much higher/lower for easier/harder classes.
\item \emph{Adaptive prediction sets} (APS)
  use cummulated probabilities, from the largest down to the true class;
  at test time, compute the cummulated sorted probabilities, and return the classes until the threshold
  (you can include the label just above the threshold, or not (but this can lead to empty prediction sets),
  or (better) decide at random);
\item Top-$k$ uses the rank of the true class;
  the prediction sets all have the same size;
\item Regularized APS (RAPS)
  penalizes the inclusion of too many classes;
\item Group-balanced conformal prediction applies conformal prediction separately for each group;
\item Class-conditional APS uses group-balanced CP
  for the classes to predict:
  since they are not known at test time, it computes the CP for all classes, and returns the union of the forecasts.
\end{compactitem}

\textbf{8.}
For regression:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item $s(y,x) = \bigl| y - \hat f(x) \bigr|$
  gives prediction intervals all of the same size
  (this is fine if the data is homoskedastic);
\item Locally adaptive CP
  \[s(y,x) = \dfrac{ \bigl| y - \hat f(x) \bigr| }{ \hat \sigma(x) } \]
  accounts for heteroskedasticity
  (but can output excessively large intervals if the data is homoskedastic).
\end{compactitem}
Instead of conformalizing (mean) regression, we can conformalize quantile regression
\[ s(y,x) = \max \bigl\{
\hat f _{\text{low}} (x) - y , y - \hat f_{\text{up}}(x)
\bigr\} \]
(the distance to the nearest interval boundary, with a negative sign if inside).

\textbf{9.} Conformal prediction can be generalized to time series
(EnbPI, in spite of non-exchangeability),
multilabel classification,
outlier detection (CP provides accurate control of the false positive rate).

Implementation: \texttt{mapie}; also check \texttt{crepes}, \texttt{nixtla}.

*** Generative models, ICML tutorial

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Continuous time generative models}\\
  (Q. Liu, ICML 2025)
\end{book}
Generative models sample from a distribution defined by samples
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,scale=1.8,inner sep=1pt,outer sep=1pt,every node/.style={anchor=base}]
  \node (A) at (0,0) {noise\strut};
  \node (B) at (1,0) {data.\strut};
  \draw[->] (A) to node[anchor=south,outer sep=0pt] {$T_\theta$} (B) ;
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
They can be:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item One-step: GAN, VAE, invertible models;
\item Iterative: diffusion (SDE), flows (ODE), GPT (AR).
\end{compactitem}
They can be seen as ``lazy EM'':
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,scale=1.8,inner sep=1pt,outer sep=1pt,every node/.style={anchor=base}]
  \node (A) at (0,0) {latent};
  \node (B) at (1,0) {model};
  \draw[->] (A) to[out=30,in=150] node[anchor=south,outer sep=0pt]{\footnotesize M step} (B);
  \draw[->] (B) to[out=-150,in=-30] node[anchor=north,outer sep=0pt]{\footnotesize E step} (A);
  \node (C) at (2.5,0) {latent};
  \node (D) at (3.5,0) {model.};
  \draw[->] (C) -- (D);
  \node (a) at (1.55,0) {\vphantom{l}};
  \node (b) at (1.95,0) {\vphantom{l}};
  \draw[->,decorate,
    decoration={zigzag,amplitude=0.7pt,segment length=1.2mm,pre=lineto,pre length=2pt,post length=3pt}
  ] (a) -- (b);
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
Given two distributions, $p_0$, $p_1$,
the \emph{flow transport problem} learns a velocity field $v(z,t)$ such that if
\[ Z_0 \sim p_0 \qquad \dfrac{ d Z_t }{ dt } = v( Z_t, t ), \]
then $Z_1 \sim p_1$.
The \emph{transport process} is $(Z_t)_{t \in [0,1]}$.
The \emph{transport plan}, or \emph{coupling},
is the joint distribution of $(Z_0,Z_1)$.
The \emph{transport map} is $T: Z_0 \mapsto Z_1$.
The solution is not unique.
Optimal transport
\[ \min_{\substack{
    (Z_0,Z_1)\\
    Z_0 \sim p_0 \\
    Z_1 \sim p_1
}} \E \bigl[ c(Z_1-Z_0) \bigr]
\quad \text{ or } \quad
\min_{\substack{
    (Z_t)_{ t \in [0,1] } \\
    Z_0 \sim p_0 \\
    Z_1 \sim p_1
}} \E \left[
  \int_0^1 c( \dot Z_t ) dt
  \right]
\]
is difficult and unnecessary.

The Euler discretization of the ODE depends on the curvature of the trajectory:
with straight trajectories, one step suffices. We want straight transport maps.

\d{Rectified flow} provides a solution:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Sample a noise-data pair $(X_0,X_1)$ (independent coupling);
\item Interpolate $X_t = t X_1 + (1-t) X_0$
  (the trajectories intersect: this does not come from an ODE);
\item Convert to an ODE
  $ \dot Z_t = v_t(Z_t), $
  \[ \operatorname*{Minimize}_v \int_0^1 \E_{ X_0,X_1} \n{ \dot X_t - v_t(X_t) }^2 dt, \]
  where $\dot X_t$ are the line directions;
\item Iterate (sample from the new coupling).
\end{compactitem}
\[
<<RectifiedFlow2D,engine='python',results='hide',echo=FALSE,out.width='\\hsize',fig.show='hold'>>=
import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import scipy
M = 6
np.random.seed(1)
def sample_from_p0(size=1):
    p = np.random.uniform(size=size)
    m = np.where( p > .5, 1, -1 ) *** 3
    return np.random.normal( m, 1, size=size )
def sample_from_p1(size = 1):
    return sample_from_p0(size)
N = 10_000
z0 = sample_from_p0(N)
z1 = sample_from_p1(N)
coupling = np.vstack([z0,z1]).T
slope = z1 - z0
def v(x,y, sigma=.1):
    yhat = slope *** x + z0
    weights = np.exp( - (y-yhat)**2 / 2 / sigma**2 )
    weights = weights / np.sum(weights)
    return np.sum( weights*slope )
K = 10
xs = np.linspace(0,1,K)
ys = np.linspace(-M,M,K)
Vs = np.array( [
    [v(x,y) for x in xs]
    for y in ys
] )
Xs = np.tile( xs, (len(ys),1) )
Ys = np.tile( ys, (len(xs),1) ).T
Us = np.cos( np.atan(Vs) )
Vs = np.sin( np.atan(Vs) )
fig, axs = plt.subplots( 1, 3, figsize = (12,3) )
for i in range(50):
    axs[0].plot( [0,1], [z0[i],z1[i]], color = 'tab:orange', linewidth = 1 )
#axs[1].streamplot(Xs,Ys,Us,Vs)
for i in range(50):
    r = scipy.integrate.solve_ivp(v, (0,1), z0[[i]], t_eval=np.linspace(0,1,100), dense_output=True)
    axs[1].plot( r.t, r.y[0,:], color = 'tab:orange', linewidth=1 )
    z = r.y[0,-1]
    axs[2].plot( [0,1], [z0[i],z], color = 'tab:orange', linewidth=1 )
dz0 = scipy.stats.gaussian_kde(z0)
dz0 = scipy.stats.gaussian_kde(z1)
zs = np.linspace(-M,M,200)
for ax in axs:
    ax.axvline( 0, color = 'black', linewidth = 1 )
    ax.axvline( 1, color = 'black', linewidth = 1 )
    s = 2
    ax.plot( -s*dz0(zs), zs, color = 'tab:blue' )
    ax.plot(1+s*dz0(zs), zs, color = 'tab:blue' )
    ax.axis('off')
fig.subplots_adjust( left = 0, right = 1, top = .95, bottom = 0 )
plt.show()
@
\]
For a single data point
\begin{align*}
  x_t &= t x^{\text{data}} + (1-t) x_0 \\
  \dot x_t &= x^{\text{data}} - x_0 = \dfrac{ x^{\text{data}} - x_t }{ 1 - t }
  \intertext{(there is a singularity, but it is numerically stable)}
  v^*(x,t) &= \E[ \dot X_t | X_t = x ].
\end{align*}
More generally, the \emph{rectified flow} of a stochastic process $X_t$ is the ODE
\[ \dot Z_t = v_t( Z_t ) \ \text{ where }\  v_t(x) = \E[ \dot X_t | X_t = x ] . \]
The density $\rho_t$ of $X_t$ satisfies the \emph{continuity equation}
\[ \underset{
  \text{rate of change}
}{\partial _t \rho_t (x)\vphantom{\bigl(}} = -
\underset{
  \text{divergence of the flux}
}{\nabla \cdot \bigl( v_t(x) \rho_t(x) \bigr)}
\]
where the divergence is
\[ \nabla \cdot v(x) = \text{Trace}\, \nabla v(x). \]
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,scale=1,inner sep=1pt,outer sep=1pt,every node/.style={anchor=base}]
  \begin{scope}
    \begin{scope}[yscale=.15]
      \foreach \i in {-2,...,2} {
        \draw[->] (0,\i) -- (.3,\i);
        \draw[->] (.35,\i) -- (1,\i);
      }
    \end{scope}
    \begin{scope}[shift={(1.8,0)},scale=.3]
      \node (O) at (0,0) {};
      \foreach \i in {0,45,...,315}
        \draw[->] (O) -- (\i:1.3);
    \end{scope}
    \node at (1,-.8) {$\text{div}>0$};
    \node at (.5,.5) {\footnotesize accelerate};
    \node at (1.8,.5) {\footnotesize diverge};
  \end{scope}
  \begin{scope}[shift={(3.5,0)}]
    \begin{scope}[yscale=.15]
      \foreach \i in {-2,...,2} {
        \draw[->] (0,\i) -- (.65,\i);
        \draw[->] (.7,\i) -- (1,\i);
      }
    \end{scope}
    \begin{scope}[shift={(1.8,0)},scale=.3]
      \node (O) at (0,0) {};
      \foreach \i in {0,45,...,315}
        \draw[->] (\i:1.3) -- (O);
    \end{scope}
    \node at (1,-.8) {$\text{div}<0$};
    \node at (.5,.5) {\footnotesize decelerate};
    \node at (1.8,.5) {\footnotesize converge};
  \end{scope}
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
The rectified flow reduces the curvature (straightens trajectories --
% TODO: PLOT?
in practice, one reflow is good enough).
The rectified flow reduces the transport cost for all convex costs (Jensen),
but we need not get the optimal transport.
\[
\qquad
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,scale=.5,inner sep=1pt,outer sep=1pt,every node/.style={anchor=base}]
  \begin{scope}
    \node (O) at (0,0) {};
    \foreach[evaluate=\i as \j using int(\i*45)] \i in {0,...,8} {
      \node[circle,draw,thick,blue!70!black] (A-\i) at (\j:1) {};
      \node[circle,draw,thick,red!60!black] (B-\i) at (\j:2) {};
    }
    \foreach[evaluate=\i as \j using int(\i+1)] \i in {0,...,7} {
      \draw (A-\i) -- (B-\i);
    }
    \node at (0,-2.7) {\footnotesize optimal coupling};
  \end{scope}
  \begin{scope}[shift={(8,0)}]
    \node (O) at (0,0) {};
    \foreach[evaluate=\i as \j using int(\i*45)] \i in {0,...,8} {
      \node[circle,draw,thick,blue!70!black] (A-\i) at (\j:1) {};
      \node[circle,draw,thick,red!60!black] (B-\i) at (\j:2) {};
    }
    \foreach[evaluate=\i as \j using int(\i+1)] \i in {0,...,7} {
      \draw (A-\i) -- (B-\j);
    }
    \node at (0,-2.7) {\footnotesize straight, non-optimal coupling};
  \end{scope}
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
(For the quadratic cost)
it is possible to remove the rotation by restricting the velocity to be a gradient,
\begin{align*}
  & \dot Z_t = \nabla f( Z_t , t ) \\
  & f = \argmin_f \int_0^1 \E \n{ (x_1-x_0) - \nabla f(x_t,t) }^2 dt .
\end{align*}
The Helmoltz decomposition of vector fields
\[ v_t = \nabla f_t + r_t \]
can be generalized to other convex costs
\[ v_t = \nabla c^*** ( \nabla f_t ) + r_t . \]
If $X_0$ is Gaussian and ($X_0,X_1)$ is the independent coupling,
then the score is (Tweedie's formula)
\[ \nabla \log \rho_t (x) = \dfrac{ t v_t(x) - x }{ 1 - t } . \]

Different prediction targets
\begin{align*}
  &\text{velocity} & v_t(x) &= \E[ X_1 - X_0 | X_t ] \\
  &\text{noise} & \mu_{0,t}(x) &= \E[ X_0 | X_t ] \\
  &\text{data} & \mu_{1,t}(x) &= \E[ X_1 | X_t ] \\
  &\text{score} & \nabla \log \rho_t(x) &= - \dfrac1{1-t}  \E[ X_0 | X_t ]
\end{align*}

The \emph{Fokker-Plank equation} converts between ODEs and SDEs:
it changes the dynamics but not the marginals.
If
\begin{align*}
  d Z_t &= v_t( Z_t ) dt + \sigma_ dW_t, \\
  \intertext{then}
  \partial_t \rho_t(x) &= - \nabla \cdot \bigl( v_t^{\text{eff}} (x) \rho_t(x) \bigr)  \\
  \intertext{where}
  v_t^{\text{eff}} (x) &= v_t(x) - \dfrac12 \sigma_t^2 \nabla \log \rho_t(x). \\
\end{align*}
In particular,
\begin{align*}
  d Z_t &= v_t(Z_t) dt + \sigma_t dW_t && \text{DDPM} \\
  \intertext{and}
  d\tilde Z_t &= v_t^{\text{eff}}(Z_t) dt && \text{DDIM}
\end{align*}
have the same marginals.

A \emph{diffusion} is an ODE with Langevin noise.
\[ dZ_t = v_t(Z_t) dt + \sigma_f^2 \nabla \log \rho_t(Z_t) dt + \sqrt2 \sigma_t dW_t \]
That noise is an error-correcting mechanism, moving outliers back towards the bulk of the distribution,
and making $\rho_1$ more concentrated (mode-seeking).

Instead of straight lines, you can interpolate with a Brownian bridge (or a jump bridge for discrete data).

Once you have the ODE, use a numeric solver, or train a neural net to approximate the trajectories
(forward loss, backward loss, triconsistency loss, velocity matching loss, KL, score, etc.).

To add a constraint
\[ P_\theta[ X_1 \in \Omega ] = 1 \]
where $\Omega$ is a manifold or a finite set,
use an $\Omega$-bridge, or just add (deterministic) singular forces
\[ d Z_t = v_t^\Omega( Z_t ) dt + \underset{\text{trainable}}{v_t^\theta( Z_t )} dt + \sigma_t dW_t ; \]
for instance, if $\Omega = \{\mu_1,\dots,\mu_k\}$ is a finite set, use
\[ v_t^\Omega(z) = \sum_i w_i(z) \dfrac{ \mu_i - z }{ 1 - t }. \]

*** Diffusion, ICML Tutorial

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Harnessing low dimensionality\\ in diffusion models}\\
  (ICML 2025)
\end{book}
Older generative models (GAN, VAE) have been replaced with diffusion.
From the forward SDE, which adds noise to the data
\[ d X_t = f( X_t , t ) dt + g(t) dW_t \]
we can compute the backward probability flow,
a denoising ODE
\[ dX = \Bigl[ f(X,t) - \dfrac12 g(t)^2 \nabla_{\!x} \log p_t(x) \Bigr] dt . \]
We can use a neural net (U-net or transformer) to approximate the score
\[ \operatorname*{Minimize}_\theta \E_{ t, x_0, x_t} \beta_t^2 \n{
  \mathstrut
  \nabla \log p_t(x_t) - s_\theta(x_t,t)
}^2 .\]
We do not know the marginal distribution $p_t$,
but this is equivalent to predicting the noise
\[ \n{ \epsilon - \beta_t s_\theta( x_t, t) } ^2 . \]

The \emph{reproductibility score}
between two diffusion models $M_1$, $M_2$ is the similarity of their outputs when given the same noise inputs,
\[ \operatorname*{P}_{ \epsilon \sim N(0,I) } \bigl[
  \text{SSCD}\bigl( M_1(\epsilon), M_2(\epsilon) \bigr) > 0.6
  \bigr], \]
where the self-supervised copy detection (SSCD)
is the cosine similarity of some low-dimensional embedding.
For diffusion, it is high ($>0.7$),
while it was close to zero for GANs or VAEs.

The \emph{generalization score} measures whether
a model reproduces some of the training samples
or generates new data.
\[ 1 - \operatorname*{P}_{\epsilon \sim N(0,I) } \left[
  \max_{ i \in \text{Train} \vphantom{(} } \text{SSCD} \bigl( M(\epsilon) , x_i \bigr ) > 0.6
  \right]
\]

When there is little data, diffusion models memorize the training data:
reproducibility is high, but generalization is low.
As the volume of training data increases, reproducibility decreases but,
if there is enough data, the model generalizes:
the generalization score is high,
and the reproducibility score is high again.

We expect generalization to happen when
$ p_{\text{empirical}} \approx p_{\text{data}} $,
\ie, $N \geq (L/\epsilon)^d$,
where $d$ is the \emph{intrinsic dimension} of the data
(it can be as low as $d=45$ for ImageNet).
The training data can be described as a mixture of low-rank Gaussians (MoLRG, subspace clustering).

% Theoretical results
% Application: diffusion inverse solvers (DIS)

*** Statistics: Calibration, ICML Tutorial

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Calibration and bias\\ in algorithms, data and models}\\
  (ICML 2025)
\end{book}
To assess the miscalibration of probabilistic predictions,
\begin{align*}
  S_1,\dots,S_n&:\text{scores, predicted probabilities of success} \\
  R_1,\dots,R_n&:\text{Bernoulli responses}
\end{align*}
assume, wlog, $S_1<\cdots<S_n$ and plot the cummulative differences
\[ C_k = \dfrac1n \sum_{j=1}^k ( R_j - S_j ) , \qquad k=1,\dots,n \]
versus $k/n$
(you can put $k/n$ on one horizontal axis, with equal increments,
and $S_k$ on another horizontal axis, with inequal increments).
The slope (between any two points)
measures the (average) miscalibration (between those two points).
For statistical tests, use
\begin{align*}
  \text{KS} &= \max \va{ C_k } \\
  \text{Kuiper} &= \max C_k - \min C_k;
\end{align*}
under $H_0$ (perfect calibration)
\begin{align*}
  \text{KS} / \sigma &\sim \max( \text{Brownian motion} ) \\
  \text{Kuiper} / \sigma &\sim \text{Range}( \text{Brownian motion} )
\end{align*}
where
\[ \sigma = \sum \dfrac{ S_k ( 1 - S_k ) }{ n^2 }. \]

Do not bin data into $\tilde R_j$, $\tilde S_j$:
the reliability diagram $\tilde R_j \sim \tilde S_j$ and the empirical calibration error
(ECE, sometimes called ICE)
$\operatorname{Mean} \va{ \tilde R_j - \tilde S_j}$ or
$\operatorname{Mean} \bigl( \tilde R_j - \tilde S_j \bigr)^2$ or
is not asymptotically consistent.

Similarly, one can study the deviations of the responses of a subpopulation
from those of the full population
by matching the ``scores'' (age, income, predicted probability, etc. -- fairness)
\[ C_k = \dfrac1n \sum_{ k=1 }^j \bigl( R_{ i_k } - \tilde R_{ i_k } ) \]
where $i_1<\cdots<i_n$ are the subpopulation
and $\tilde R_{ i_k}$  the average response of units matching $i_k$ (inclusing $i_k$).

One can also compare two sub-populations, provided we can find matching units.
We can also match on several covariates.

* TODO ML
** Decision trees

Decision (and regression) trees) are traditionally built greedily:
find the best way (according to some creterion)
to split the data in two,
and continue recursively until some termination criterion (e.g., tree depth, leaf size, leaf homogeneity).
But, as most greedy approaches, this is suboptimal:
for instance, a decision tree cannot learn XOR in this way.

Instead, it is actually possible to look for an optimal decision tree:
optimality can be formulated as a mixed integer program.

TODO: paper

Other methods to build (nearly) optimal decision trees include frequen itemset mining and genetic algorithms.

  \emph{Decision tree induction through LLMs\\ via semantically-aware evolution}\\
  T. Liu et al.

** Rule extraction

Trees are somewhat interpretable but rarely fit the data.
Conversely, random forests provide a better fit,
but are trickier to interpret.

It is actually possible to simplify random forests and make them more interpretable than trees.
After training your random forest, look at all the paths in its trees (from the root to a node or leaf):
each can be seen as a rule, and some of those rules are repeated across many trees.
Keep the mopst frequent rules (this is, again, frequent itemset mining) and combine them with the lasso.

TODO: Papers

TODO: There are variants of this idea

If you want to stick to trees, you can distill your random forest into a tree,
but use data augmentation, (x,f(x)+noise), to better explore the neighbourhood of the decision boundary.

** TODO Trees
*** Decision trees (optimal)

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Optimal classification trees}\\
  D. Bertsimas and J. Dunn (2015)
\end{book}
Decision trees are often built in a (suboptimal) greedy way,
unconstrained (to detect strong splits hidden behind weak ones)
and pruned (to account for the complexity penalty).

The problem of finding the optimal tree of depth at most $D$ can be formulated as
mixed integer program:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Binary variables for each node, indicating if there is a split;
\item Binary variables to select the variable used for the split;
\item Binary variables indicating non-empty leaves;
\item Binary variables indicating, for each observaation and each node,
  whether it goes to the left or to the right;
\item Binary variables indicating the majority class in each leaf
\end{compactitem}
(and many constraints).

For the hyperparameters:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Progressively increase the tree depth, using CART or a shallower tree as a warm start;
\item The complexity penalty can be formulated as a hard constraint on the number of nodes:
  progressively increase the number of nodes;
\item Idem for the minimum number of observations in each leaf.
\end{compactitem}

This can be generalized to multivariate decision trees
(hyperplane splits, instead of axis-aligned ones), with greedy (single-split-optimal-tree based)
warm start (or greedy logistic regressions).

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Learning optimal and fair decision trees\\ for non-discriminative decision-making}\\
  S. Aghaei et al.
\end{book}
Another MIP formulation of the optimal decision (or regression) tree;
also allowing fairness penalties.

Implementation in \texttt{ODTlearn}.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Learning optimal classification trees\\ using a binary linear program formulation}\\
  S. Verwer and Y. Zhang
\end{book}
Reformulation of the optimal decision tree MIP
without constraints for each observation in the training set:
just sum the constrains for the rows having the same feature values.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Optimal constraint-based decision tree induction from itemset lattices}\\
  S. Nijssen and E. Fromont
\end{book}
DL8 uses frequent itemset mining to build optimal decision trees on binary data
(every path, o a decision tree, can be mapped to an itemset):
the best decision tree on a subset of the data is obtained by considering all possible
ways of paritioning it in two,
and recursively determining the best tree for each partition.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Learning optimal decision trees\\ using caching branch-and-bound search}\\
  G. Aglin et al.
\end{book}
Implementation improvements for DL8, in \texttt{PyDL8.5}

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Optimal policy trees}\\
  M. Amram et al.
\end{book}
The optimal decision tree MIP can be generalized to build an optimal \emph{policy} tree,
\[ \operatorname*{Minimize}_{ \tau : \mathscr X \to \{0,1\} }
\sum_i \Gamma_{ i , \tau(x_i) } \]
after estimating the counterfactuals with a doubly robust (DR) estimator
\[ \Gamma_{it} = \dfrac{ y_i - \hat y_{it} }{ \hat p_{it} } \mathbf 1_{ z_i = t } + \hat y_{it}. \]
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=.5,yscale=.8,inner sep=2pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline=0]
  \node (X) at (1,1) {$X$};
  \node (T) at (0,0) {$T$};
  \node (Y) at (2,0) {$Y$};
  \draw[->] (X) -- (T);
  \draw[->] (X) -- (Y);
  \draw[->] (T) -- (Y);
\end{tikzpicture}
\]


\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Optimal prescriptive trees}\\
  D. Bertsimas et al.
\end{book}
Joint (MIP) tree model for counterfactual estimation
(locally constant or piecewise linear)
and optimal treatment assignment.

*** Generalized random forests

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Generalized random forests}\\
  S. Athey et al. (2016)
\end{book}
Generalized random forests fit a quantity $\theta(x)$
identified as the solution of local moment equations
\[ \E \bigl[ \psi_{ \theta(x) } (X) \,|\, X = x \bigr] = 0. \]

The tree is built recursively, splitting a (parent) node $P$ into (children) $C_1$, $C_2$,
maximizing
\[ \Delta( C_1, C_2 ) =
\dfrac{ \va{ C_1 } \cdot \va{ C_2 } }{ \va{ P }^2 }
\n{ \hat\theta_{C_1} - \hat\theta_{C_2} }^2 , \]
which can be approximated with a Taylor expansion
\begin{align*}
  A_p &= \operatorname*{Mean}_{ i \in P } \nabla \psi_{ \hat \theta_P } (x_i) \\
  \rho_i &= - A_P^{-1} \psi_{ \hat\theta_P } (x_i) \\
  \tilde \Delta(C_1,C_2) &= \sum_{ j \in \{1,2\} } \dfrac1{ \va{ C_j } }
  \biggl(\, \sum_{ i \in C_j } \rho_i \biggr)^2.
\end{align*}

The final estimate is not obtained byaveraging estimates from each tree,
but by
using the ensemble to compute similarity weights $\alpha_i(x)$ between the new sample $x$ and training samples $i$
and putting them in the empirical estimating equation
\[ \hat\theta(x) = \argmin_\theta \n{ \sum_i \alpha_i(x) \psi_\theta(x_i) }_2. \]

R implementation in \texttt{grf}.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{evtree: evolutionary learning of globally optimal classification and regression trees in R}\\
  T. Grubinger et al. (2014)
\end{book}
Decision trees are usually built greedily: they are only locally optimal --
genetic algorithms can search for a globally optimal tree.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Parties, models, mobsters:\\ a new implementation\\ of model-based recursive partitioning in R}\\
  A. Zeilleis and T. Hothorn
\end{book}
Model-based recursive partitioning (MOB)
is a ``decision'' tree with models (e.g., linear models) in the leaves.
They can use different variables for the models $y \sim x_1 + \cdots + x_m$
and the splits $z_1,\dots,z_\ell$.
The tree is built recursively:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Fit the model to the current node;
\item Test for parameter instability (SupLM fluctuation test)
  to select the variable to split on;
\item Use exhaustive search to select the split point;
\item Split the node, and iterate until the desired depth;
\item Prune the tree by looking at the AIC or BIC improvement.
\end{compactitem}
R implementation in \texttt{partykit}.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Generalized M-fluctuation tests\\ for parameter instability}\\
  A. Zeileis and K. Hornik (2007)
\end{book}
Given observations
$Y_i \overset{\text{indep}}{\sim} F_{\theta_i}$,
we can test
\begin{align*}
  H_0 &: \ \forall i \ \theta_i = \theta_0 \\
  H_1 &: \ \theta_i \text{ varies over time}
\end{align*}
using a score function $\psi$, \ie, a function such that
\[ \E_{ Y \sim F_\theta } \bigl[ \psi_\theta(Y) \bigr] = 0 , \]
e.g.,
\[ \psi_\theta(y) = \dfrac{ \partial \text{loss} ( y ; \theta ) }{ \partial \theta } \]
where the loss is the log-likelihood or the residual sum of squares.

Since
$\hat B_n^{-1/2} W_n(\cdot, \hat\theta_n ) \overset{\text{d}}{\to} W^0(\cdot)$,
where
\begin{compactitem}[\ensuremath{\cdot}]
\item $n$ is the number of observations;
\item $\hat\theta_n$ is estimated using the whole sample:\\
  \mbox{}\qquad\qquad\qquad$\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^n \psi_{\hat\theta_n} ( Y_i ) = 0 ; $
\item $\displaystyle W_n(t,\theta) = \dfrac{1}{\sqrt n} \sum_{ i=1 }^{ \lfloor nt \rfloor }\psi_\theta(Y_i );$
\item $\displaystyle B(\theta) = \operatorname*{Var}_{ Y \sim F_\theta } \bigl[ \psi_\theta(Y) \bigr] ;$
\item $\displaystyle\hat B_n = \dfrac1n \sum_{i=1}^n \psi_{\hat\theta_n}(Y_i) \psi_{\hat\theta_n}(Y_i) ^\top ;$
\item $W^0(t) = W(t) - t W(1)$ Brownian bridge;
\item $W$ is a standard Brownian motion,
\end{compactitem}
we can use
\begin{align*}
  &\text{SupLM} = \sup_{ t \in \Pi } \dfrac{ \n{ \text{efp}(t) }_2^2 }{ t(1-t) } \\
  &\text{efp}(t) = \hat B_n^{-1/2} W_n(t,\hat\theta_n) \text{ (empirical fluctuation process)}\\
  &\Pi \subset [0,1] \text{ interval with suspected break}
\end{align*}
as test statistic (and approximate $p$-values).
For linear regression,
\[ \psi_B( y_i, x_i ) = x_i ( y_i - x_i^\top \beta ). \]

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Approximate asymptotic p-values\\ for structural change tests}\\
  B.E. Hansen (1997)
\end{book}
$P$-values for the limiting distribution
\[ \sup_{ t \in \Pi } \dfrac{ \n{ W^0(t) }_2^2 }{ t(1-t) }. \]

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{ctree: conditional inference trees}\\
  T. Hothorn et al.
\end{book}
Decision tree using statistical tests (adjusted for multiple testing) to select the splits
(instead of heuristic, overfitting-prone criteria).

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Fast optimal leaf ordering\\ for hierarchical clustering}\\
  Z. Bar-Joseph et al. (2001)
\end{book}

*** Random forests, interpretability

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Approximation trees:\\ statistical stability in model distillation}\\
  Y. Zhou et al.
\end{book}
To explain a blackbox model (e.g., a random forest),
distill it into a decision tree using pseudo observations
(real observations plus Gaussian noise, for a user-chose bandwidth -- \ie,
samples from a KDE of the data).
Choose the number of pseudo observations so tha the trees be stable --
use a statistical test on the Gini  index (which is used to select the splits).
Choose the tree depth by comparing the variance of the observations in a leaf
with the variance of the blackbox model.

*** Random forests

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Dynamic asset allocation using machine learning: seeing the forest for the trees}\\
  C. Mueller-Glissmann and A. Ferrario
\end{book}
A \emph{student tree} is a complex model (e.g., a random forest)
distilled into a tree (on real data + noise).

** TODO Change point detection
*** ML, Clustering, Change point detection

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Information theoretic measures\\ for clusterings comparison:
    variants, properties, normalization\\ and correction for chance}\\
  N.X. Vinh et al.
\end{book}
Prefer the normalized information distance
\[ \text{NID}(U,V) = 1 - \dfrac{
    I(U;V)
  }{
    \max \bigl\{ H(U), H(V) \bigr\}
  }
\]
but, if there are few observations or many clusters, adjust it for chance,
\[ \dfrac{ \text{index} - \E[\text{index}] }{ \max[\text{index}] - \E[\text{index}] }. \]

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Standardized mutual information\\ for clustering comparisons:\\
    one step further in adjustment for chance}\\
  S. Romano et al.
\end{book}
Mutual information and Rand index are often adjusted as
\[ \dfrac{ M - \E[M] }{ \max M - \E[M] }, \]
but this gets larger as the number of clusters increases.
Instead, standardize them,
\[ \dfrac{ M - \E[M] }{ \sqrt{ \var M } }. \]
The expectation is taken over random partitions with the same cardinalities.
For mutual information (MI),
we can compute $\E[\text{MI}]$ and $\var[\text{MI}]$ explicitly.
The standardized mutual information and standardized variation of information coincide:
$\text{SMI} = \text{SVI}$.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Precision and recall for time series}\\
  N. Tatbul et al.
\end{book}
Generalization of precision and recall for range-based anomaly detection,
accounting for:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Existnce (of an overlap between ground truth and forecast);
\item Size of the detected anomaly;
\item Position;
\item Cardinality, \ie, fragmentation of the detected range.
\end{compactitem}

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{BCubed revisited: elements like me}\\
  R. van Heusden et al.
\end{book}
Given two clusterings of a set $D$,
$t$ (truth) and $p$ (prediction), as maps $G \to \mathscr P(D)$,
define precision, recall, and F$_1$ for each element $e\in D$ as
\begin{align*}
  P(e) &= \dfrac{ \va{ p(e) \inter t(e) } }{ \va{ p(e) } } \\
  R(e) &= \dfrac{ \va{ p(e) \inter t(e) } }{ \va{ t(e) } } \\
  F_1(e) &= \dfrac{ 2 P(e) R(e) }{ P(e) + R(e) }
\end{align*}
and average them over all elements $e \in D$.

This is ``flawed'', because $e\in p(e)$, $e \in t(e)$ by construction.
To repair it, remove $\{e\}$:
\begin{align*}
  P(e) &= \dfrac{ \va{ p(e) \inter t(e) } \setminus \{e\} }{ \va{ p(e) } } \\
  R(e) &= \dfrac{ \va{ p(e) \inter t(e) } \setminus \{e\} }{ \va{ t(e) } }.
\end{align*}

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{An evaluation\\ of change point detection algorithms}\\
  G.J.J. van den Burg and C.K.I Williams (2022)
\end{book}
Benchmark (37 time series, annotated by humans):
at-most-one-change, binary segmentation, and \textsc{pelt}
(from R's \texttt{changepoint} package, after grid search)
work best for Jaccard similarity (intersection over union, IoU, as for image segmentation) and F1@$\delta$.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Resampled mutual information\\ for clustering and community detection}\\
  C. Lim
\end{book}
The resampled mutual information between two clusterings $f$ and $g$ is
\[ \text{ResMI}(f,g) = \dfrac{
    I\bigl(
    \mathbf 1_{ f(Z_1) = f(Z_2) } ;
    \mathbf 1_{ g(Z_1) = g(Z_2) }
    \bigr)
  }{
    \tfrac12 \bigl[
    H\bigl( \mathbf 1_{ f(Z_1) = f(Z_2) } ) +
    H\bigl( \mathbf 1_{ g(Z_1) = g(Z_2) } )
    \bigr]
  }
\]
where $Z_1$, $Z_2$ are drawn without replacement.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Improved mutual information measure\\ for clustering, classification\\ and community detection}\\
  M.E.J. Newman et al. (2020)
\end{book}
The reduced mutual information between labelings $r$ and $s$ is \[ \text{RMI}(r,s) = I(r;s) - \tfrac12 \log (a,b) \]
where $(a,b)$ is the number of nonnegative integer matrices with row sums $a = \{a_r\}$ and column sums $b = \{b_s\}$.



*** Change point detection

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Random forests for change point detection}\\
  M. Londschien et al. (2023)
\end{book}
To find a breakpoint,
compare the performance of classification models predicting, from $x_t$,
if $t \in [t_0,\tau]$
or $t \in [\tau,t_1]$:
the best performance is obtained if $\tau$ is a breakpoint.
This can be generalized to several (an unknown number of) breakpoints,
and efficiently approximated.

Alternatives include:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item E-divisive (ECP), which finds breakpoints minimizing the energy distance between segments;
  the \emph{energy distance} between two random variables can be computed from their characteristic functions:
  \begin{align*}
    \mathscr E_a (X,Y) &= \int_{ \RR^d } \va{ \phi_X(t) - \phi_Y(t) } w(t) dt \\
    w(t) &\propto \va{ t }^{ -d-\alpha } \\
    \alpha&= 2.
  \end{align*}
\end{compactitem}

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{A kernel multiple change-point algorithm\\ via model selection}\\
  S. Arlot et al. (2012)
\end{book}
Kernel change point methods (KCP) minimize (with dynamic programming)
the kernel least squares criterion
\begin{align*}
  R(\tau) = {}& \dfrac1n \sum_{ i = 1 } ^n k(x_i,x_i ) \\
& - \dfrac1n \sum_\ell \dfrac1{ \tau_\ell - \tau_{\ell-1} }
\sum_{i,j \in \llbracket \tau_{\ell-1} +1 , \tau_\ell \rrbracket } k(x_i,x_j)
\end{align*}
with a penalty for the number $D$ of segments
\[ \dfrac1n \left[ c_1 \log \binom{n-1}{D-1} + c_2 D \right]. \]

The loss can also be written
\[ R(\tau) = \dfrac1n \n{ \Phi(\mathbf x ) - \Pi_\tau \Phi(\mathbf x ) \mathstrut } ^2 \]
where
\begin{align*}
  & k(x,y) = \bigl\langle \Phi(x), \Phi(y) \bigr\rangle \\
  & \Phi: \left\{
  \begin{array}{rcl} \mathscr X & \longrightarrow & \mathscr H \\ x & \longmapsto & k(x,\bigcdot) \end{array}
  \right. \qquad \text{RKHS} \\
  & \mathbf x = (x_1,\dots,x_n) \\
  & \Phi(\mathbf x) = \bigl( \Phi(x_1), \dots, \Phi(x_n) \bigr)
\end{align*}
$F_\tau$ is the subset of $\mathscr H^n$ of locally constant sequences with locally constant pattern (coarser than) $\tau$;
$\Pi_\tau$ is the orthogonal projection in $F_\tau$.

For the $d$-dimensional simplex
(e.g., if $x_i$ is a histogram -- histogram features are often used for image or audio data),
use the $\chi^2$ kernel
\[ k(x,y) = \exp - \dfrac1{h \cdot d} \sum_{i=1}^d \dfrac{ (x_i-x_j)^2 }{ x_i + x_j } \]
(for some bandwidth $h>0$).

** TODO TO SORT
*** Risk-seeking RL, Symbolic regression

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Deep symbolic regression:\\ recovering mathematical expressions from data\\ via risk-seeking policy gradients}\\
  B.K. Petersen et al.
\end{book}
Symbolic regression usually uses genetic programming.
Instead, use RL to train an RNN model,
to generate a symbolic expression tree in pre-order traversal (depth-first, left-to-right).
The RNN is not only fed the previous token,
but also the parent and sibling nodes.
The reward is the normalized root mean square error.
The constant token is replaced with the constant maximizing the reward (using BFGS).
The \textsc{reinforce} policy gradient
(with baseline, for variance reduction) would maximize the expextec \emph{sum} of rewards --
instead, we want to maximize the maximum reward:
this can be achieved by maximizing the conditional expected reward
\[ \E_{ \tau \sim p_\theta } \bigl[ R(\tau) \,|\, R_\tau \geq R_\epsilon(\theta) \bigr]. \]

*** ML and PDEs, Koopman operator theory

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Machine learning\\ for partial differential equations}\\
  S.L. Brunton and J.N. Kutz (2023)
\end{book}

\textbf{1.}
To learn a PDE from observational data,
PDE-FIND uses
\[ u_t = \Theta( u, u_x, u_{xx}, u u_x, \cdots ) \xi  \]
where $\Theta$ is a library of candidate terms, e.g.,
\[ \begin{array}{ll}
  \text{divergence} & \nabla \cdot u \\
  \text{vorticity} & \nabla x u \\
  \text{shearing} & u_x + v_y \\
  \text{stretching} & u_x - v_y
\end{array} \]
and $\xi$ is a sparse vector.

\textbf{2.}
The \d{Koopman operator}
of a discrete, nonlinear finite-dimensional dynamical system
\[ x_{t+1} = F(x_t) \qquad x_t \in \mathscr X \]
is the linear, infinite-dimensional transformation
\[ K: \left\{
\begin{array}{rcl}
  \RR^{\mathscr X } & \longrightarrow & \RR^{\mathscr X} \\
  g & \longmapsto & Kg = g \circ F.
\end{array}
\right. \]
It satisfies
\[ (Kg) (x_t ) = g \bigl( F(x_t) \bigr) = g( x_{t+1} ). \]

Learn $K$ satisfying those constraints;
diagonalize it,
\[ K \phi_k = \lambda_k \phi_k  \]
(``intrinsinc measurement coordinates'')
and reduce the dimension.
The non-linear system has become linear --
it is easy to solve.

\textbf{3.}
If $u$ is a solution of the linear PDE
$Lu=f$,
then it can be computed from the \d{Green's function}
of the PDE, \ie, the solution $G$ of
$L^\dag G(\bigcdot,\xi) = \delta_\xi$,
where $L^\dag$ is the adjoint of $L$ and $\delta_\xi$ is the Dirac delta,
as follows:
\begin{align*}
  u(x) &= \langle \delta_x , u \rangle \\
  &= \langle L^\dag G(\bigcdot,x) , u \rangle \\
  &= \langle G(\bigcdot,x) , L u \rangle \\
  &= \langle G(\bigcdot,x) , f \rangle \\
  &= \int G(\xi,x) f(\xi) d\xi.
\end{align*}

The PDE can also be solved by looking for an eigen-decomposition of $L$:
\[ L \phi_n = \lambda_n \phi_n. \]
If a solution $u$ can be represented as a sum of eigenfunctions
(Sturm-Liouville theory), then
\[ u = \sum \dfrac{ \langle \phi_n , f \rangle }{ \lambda_n }\phi_n. \]
Neural operators generalize that to nonlinear PDEs.

*** ANN (Approximate nearest neighbour): optimized product quantization

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Optimized product quantization}\\
  T. Ge et al.
\end{book}
Product quantization ($k$-means on $M$ subsets of the coordinates,
for approximate nearest neighbour (ANN) search)
after a learned orthogonal transormation:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Alternatively estimate the cluster centers ($k$-means) and the orthogonal matrix (Procrustes);
\item Initialize with the following heuristic (approximation of the exact solution for Gaussian data):
  \begin{compactitem}[\ensuremath{\bigcdot}]
  \item Compute the PCA and sort the eigenvalues in decreasing order;
  \item Start with $M$ empty buckets;
  \item Sequentially pick the largest eigenvalue
    and assign it to the bucket with the minimum product of eigenvalues (unless it is already full);
  \end{compactitem}
\end{compactitem}
this gives $k^M$ codewords.

*** LLMs with non-text sequences: REcSys

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Recommendation with generative models}\\
  Y. Deldjoo et al. (2024)
\end{book}

Classical RecSys data contains user, item, timestamp, feedback (explicit (rating) or implicit (click)).

Matrix factorization models the feedback as
\[ R_{ui} = \gamma_u \cdot \gamma_i ; \]
one can add offset and bias
\[ R_{ui} = \alpha + \beta_u + \beta_i + \gamma_u \cdot \gamma_i \]
or include the previous item $j$
\[ R_{uij} = \gamma_u \gamma_i + \beta_i^{\text{next}} \beta_j^{\text{previous}}. \]

User-free models only look at the similarity with items in the user's history
\[ R_{ui} = \operatorname*{Mean}_{ j \in u \setminus \{ i \} } \gamma_i \cdot \gamma_j^{\text{history}}. \]

Generative models produce a \emph{probabilistic forecast}:
there is not a single output, but a probability distribution of outputs,
from which we can sample.

A user's history is a sequence of tokens:
we can use LLM-like models to forecast the next token.

RecSys can generate sets (or sequences) of items instead of isolated items:
the items should be complementary or substitutable (ex: outfits).

GANs can improve traditional RecSys training by providing hard negatives (instead of uniformly sampled ones).
But GANs can also be used to model normal users,
and create hard-to-detect fake profiles.

Generative models also provide data augmentation.

Traditional RecSys, when followed by LLM-based reranking,
can be seen as the retrieval part of a RAG system.

The data can also contain text (user profiles, item reviews)
and metadata.

Conversational RecSys provide textual explanations (personalized reviews),
and allow for interactive query refinement --
but also personalized content and advertising.

** No
*** No, ML, Model selection, BIC alternatives

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Extended Bayesian information criteria\\ for model selection with large model spaces}\\
  J. Chen and Z. Chen
\end{book}
The BIC is the Laplace approximation of
\[ \text{likelihood} \times \text{prior} \propto \text{posterior} \]
but the prior gives the same probability to all subsets of predictors --
if there are many of them, this actually penalizes small models.
Instead, give the same (or decreasing) probability to all model sizes.
\[
\text{BIC}_\gamma = - 2 L(\hat\theta) + k \log n + 2 \gamma \log \binom pk,
\quad 0 \leq \gamma \leq 1.
\]
Colinearity, \ie, the fact that the effective number of subsets of predictors
is less than $\binom pk$, has little impact.

*** No, ML: pre-processing

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{The when and how\\ of target variable transformations}\\
  L. Nuyts and J. Davis
\end{book}
ML pipelines pay attention to feature ($X$) transformation, but tend to ignore \emph{target} variable ($y$) transformations:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Normalize, e.g., with (user-specific) average: rating, time, area, number of sunlight hours, CPI, etc.
\item Use a context-specific standardization,
  \[ \dfrac{ y_i - \E[ y | \text{context} ] }{ \text{Sd}[ y | \text{context} ] }
  \text{ or }
  \dfrac{ y_i }{ \E[ y | \text{context} ] }
  \]
\item Logarithm, quantile uniform, quantile normal or Yeo-Johnson transform.
\end{compactitem}

* TODO Model Merging
*** Model merging

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{ZipIt! Merging models\\ from different tasks without training}\\
  G. Stoica et al.
\end{book}
To merge models (same architecture but different training data and/or tasks),
permutation-based methods assume they have exactly the same features,
up to permutation,
and try to recover that permutation, for each layer:
\[ \operatorname*{Maximize}_{ \sigma \in \mathfrak S_n } \sum_i \cor( z_i^1 , z_{\sigma(i)} ^2 ) \]
where $z_i^2$ are the features, for neuron $i$, in layer $\ell$, which contains $n$ neurons.
\[ W_\ell^*** = \tfrac12 W_\ell^1 + \tfrac12 P_\ell W_\ell^2 P_{\ell-1}^\top \]
Instead, concatenate the features,
$z_i^1 \| z_i^2$, and average highly correlated ones:
this accounts for redundancy in each model,
and features unique to each model.

*** Merging LoRAs

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{ZipLoRA: any subject in any style\\ by effectively merging LoRAs}\\
  V. Shah et al
\end{book}
LoRA update matrices are sparse, but aligned LoRA matrices interfere when naively merged,
\[ \Delta W = \lambda \Delta W _{\text{context}} + ( 1 - \lambda ) \Delta W_{\text{style}} . \]
Instead, learn which column of which LoRA to keep,
\[ ( m_1' \otimes \mathbf 1 ) \odot \Delta W_1 + ( m_2' \tens \mathbf 1 ) \odot \Delta W_2. \]

* TODO Data-centric ML
*** data-centric ML with convex optimization; ICML

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{MixMin: finding data mixtures\\ via convex minimization}\\
  A. Thudi et al. (2025)
\end{book}
When training with data from different sources,
choosing the data mix can be formulated as a bilevel optimization problem.
First, train a (cheap) proxy model for each source,
then learn the best convex combination of their outputs,
and use this weighting to remix the data.
This is specific to the cross-entropy or the mean square error loss:
the Bayes optimal model for a mixture
is the mixture of Bayes optimal models for each source.

*** Data-centric ML

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{The curse of recursion: training on\\ generated data makes models forget}\\
  O. Shumailov et al.
\end{book}
Training on generated data causes model collapse:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item At each generation, since the data is finite, there is a nonzero probability that some of the data distribution will be lost;
  the tails will disappear, and the distribution will progressively drift;
\item Since the model is only an approximation
  (it does not perfectly reproduce the target distribution),
  the generated data will follow a slightly different distribution,
  for instance, assigning a nonzero probability outside the support of the target distribution.
\end{compactitem}


* Statistics
*** Statistics, Score function

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Estimation of non-normalized\\ statistical models by score matching}\\
  A. Hyv\"arinen
\end{book}
To fit a non-normalized probability density
\[ p_\theta(\xi) = \dfrac{ q_\theta(\xi) }{ Z(\theta) }, \]
fit its score function
\[ \psi_\theta(\xi) = \nabla_{ \! \xi } \log p_\theta(\xi) = \nabla_{\!\xi} \log q_\theta(\xi)  \]
by minimizing
\[ J(\theta) = \dfrac12 \E_{ \xi \sim \text{data} } \n{
  \psi_\theta(\xi) - \psi(\xi)
}^2 . \]
We do not need to estimate the score function of the data, $\psi$:
expand the square and integrate by parts
\begin{align*}
  \int p(\xi) \psi(\xi) \psi_\theta(xi)^\top d\xi
  &= \sum_i \int p(\xi) \dfrac{ \partial \log p(\xi) }{ \partial \xi_i } \psi_\theta(\xi) _i d\xi \\
  &= \sum_i \int p(\xi) \dfrac1{p(\xi)} \dfrac{ \partial p(\xi) }{ \partial \xi_i } \psi_\theta(\xi)_i d\xi \\
  &= - \sum_i \int p(\xi) \dfrac{ \partial \psi_\theta(\xi)_i }{ \partial \xi_i } d\xi
\end{align*}
and minimize
\[ \tilde J(\theta) = \dfrac1T \sum_t \sum_i
\dfrac{ \partial \psi_\theta(x_y) _i }{ \partial \xi_i }
+ \dfrac12 \psi_\theta(x_t)_i^2.
\]

*** Statistics/ML: Intrinsic dimension

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{The intrinsic dimension of images\\ and its impact in learning}\\
  P. Pope et al. (2021)
\end{book}
The intrinsic dimension of a cloud of points
can be computed by looking at the Euclidean distance to the $k$-th nearest neighbour
$T_k(x)$.
\begin{align*}
  \hat m_k(x) &= \left[ \operatorname*{Mean}_{j=1}^{k-1} \log \dfrac{ T_k(x) }{ T_j(x) } \right]^{-1} \\
  \bar m_k(x) &= \left[ \operatorname*{Mean}_{i=1}^n \hat m_k(x) ^{-1} \right]^{-1}
\end{align*}
ImageNet only has dimension 40.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Minimax rates for estimating\\ the dimension of a manifold}\\
  J. Kim et al.
\end{book}
% Not read. Original paper on the intrinsic dimension

*** Statistics, Kolmogorov-Arnold networks (stacked GAMs)

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{KAN: Kolmogorov-Arnold networks}\\
  Z. Liu et al.
\end{book}
Stacked GAMs.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{KAN: Kolmorogov-Arnold networks}\\
  Z. Liu et al.
\end{book}
A KAN is a neural net with GAM layers: the weights are replaced by learnable nonlinearities (splines of order 3 on $G$ intervals).

*** Statistics: Weibul distribution

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Beyond word frequency: bursts, lulls, and scaling in the empirical distribution of words}\\
  E. G. Altmann et al.
\end{book}
Words have different recurrence time distributions;
they can be modeled with a ``stretched exponential'' (aka Weibul)
\[ F_\beta(\tau) = \exp ( - a \tau ^ \beta ) . \]
This can be explained with a renewal process:
the probability of using a word decays as a power law since the last use of that word.

*** Statistics

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Automatic posterior transformation\\ for likelihood-free inference}\\
  D.S. Greenberg et al. (2019)
\end{book}
The Bayesian approach to statistics estimates the posterior $p(\theta|x)$
from the prior $p(\theta)$ and the likelihood $p(x|\theta)$.

\emph{Approximate Bayesian computation} (ABC, aka likelihood-free inference)
only assumes that we know the prior $p(\theta)$ and that we can sample
from $p(x|\theta)$ -- but we do not know the likelihood.

One can model the posterior as $q_{ F(x,\phi)} (\theta) \approx p(\theta|x)$,
where $q_\psi$ is a family of tractable distributions and $F$ is a neural net, trained with generated pairs
$(\theta, x)$,
\[ \operatorname*{Maximize}_\phi \sum_i \log q_{ F(x_i , \phi ) } ( \theta_i ). \]

Since we are interested in the posterior $p(\theta|x_0)$ at a specific point $x_0$,
we may want to restrict the $\theta$ samples to ``informative'' values
(sampling $\theta$ from $q_{ F(x_0,\phi_{n-1}) } (\theta)$
instead of $p(\theta)$ to estimate $\phi_n$) -- this gives a biased posterior,
but it can be fixed.

*** Sparse PCA, Sparse CCA

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Nonnegative CCA\\ for audiovisual source separation}\\
  C. Sigg et al.
\end{book}
One can compute nonnegative or sparse CCA with nonnegative or sparse regressions:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Standardize $X$ and $Y$;
\item Initialize the deflated matrices:
  $X_p = X$, $Y_p = Y$;
\item Initialize $w_X$, $w_Y$ randomly;
  normalize them
  \begin{align*}
    w_X &= w_X / \n{ X_p w_X } \\
    w_Y &= w_Y / \n{ Y_p w_Y };
  \end{align*}
\item
  Update $w_X$ by regressing $Y_p w_Y \sim X_p$; normalize it;
  update $w_Y$ by regressing $X_p w_X \sim Y_p$; normalize it;
  repeat until the correlation $(X_p w_X )' (Y_p w_Y)$ stabilizes;
  use restarts and keep the best solution (largest correlation);
\item Deflate $X_p$ and $Y_p$
  \begin{align*}
    q_X &= X_p ' X w_X         & q_Y &= Y_p' Y w_Y \\
    q_X &= q_X / \n{ q_X }     & q_Y &= q_Y / \n{ q_Y } \\
    X_p &= X_p - X_p q_X q_X ' & Y_p &= Y_p - Y_p q_Y q_Y ';
  \end{align*}
\item Continue for the other components, and return the $w_X$'s and $w_Y$'s.
\end{compactitem}
R implementation in \texttt{nscancor}.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Deflation methods for sparse PCA}\\
  L. Mackey
\end{book}
PCA can be computed by removing eigenvectors one by one, ``deflating'' the variance matrix (Hotelling):
\[ V \leftarrow V - x x' V x x' . \]
For sparse PCA, prefer projection deflation
\begin{align*}
  V &\leftarrow ( I - xx' ) V ( I - xx' ) \\
  Y &\leftarrow Y ( I - xx' )
\end{align*}
or Schur deflation
\begin{align*}
  V &\leftarrow V - \dfrac{ V xx' V}{ x' V x } \\
  Y &\leftarrow \left( I - \dfrac{ Y xx' Y }{ \n{ Y x }^2 } \right) Y
\end{align*}
(they preserve the positive semidefiniteness of $V$).

To do it iteratively, with more than one vector
(if those vectors are not guaranteed to be orthogonal), do not deflate $x$ but
$q = x - Px$,
where $P$ is the projection on the space spanned by the previous vectors.

*** Covariance estimator

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Numerically stable parallel\\ computation of (co)variance}\\
  R. Schubert and M. Gertz
\end{book}
Do not compute the variance of $\var X = \E[ X^2] - \E[X]^2$,
but (Welford)
\begin{align*}
  \mu_{k+1} &= \dfrac{ k }{ k+1 } \mu_k + \dfrac1{k+1} x_{k+1} \\
  S_{k+1} &= S_k + \dfrac{ k }{ k+1} ( x_{k+1} - \mu_k )^2 .
\end{align*}

*** Information theory

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{On information transfer\\ in control dynamical systems}\\
  S. Sinha and U. Vaidya (2018)
\end{book}
The directed information is
\[ I(X\to Y) = H(Y_1,\dots,Y_T) - \sum_{ 1 \leq t \leq T } H( Y_t | Y_{<t} , X_{\leq t} ). \]
The transfer entropy is
\[ T_{X \to Y } = H( Y_t | Y_{<t} ) - H( Y_t | Y_{<t}, X_{<t} ). \]
A dynamical system
\begin{align*}
  x_{t+1} &= f(x_t, y_t ) + \text{noise} \\
  y_{t+1} &= g(x_t, y_t ) + \text{noise}
\end{align*}
defines a conditional probability distribution
$\rho( y_{t+1} | y_t )$.
Similarly, if we freeze $x$,
\begin{align*}
  x_{t+1} &= x_t \\
  y_{t+1} &= g(x_t , y_t ) + \text{noise}
\end{align*}
defines $\rho_{\not x} ( y_{t+1} | y_t )$.
The information transfer is
\[ \left. T_{x \to y} \right|_t^{t+1} =
H\bigl( \rho( y_{t+1} | y_t ) \bigr) -
H\bigl( \rho_{\not x}( y_{t+1} | y_t ) \bigr).
\]

* Diffusion
*** Diffusion

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Diffusion forcing: next token prediction\\ meets full sequence diffusion}\\
  B. Chen et al.
\end{book}
Combine Bayesian updates
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=1.5,yscale=1,inner sep=2pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline=0]
  \node (Z-2) at (0,1) {$z_{t-2}$};
  \node (Z-1) at (1,1) {$z_{t-1}$};
  \node (Z)   at (2,1) {$z_{t}$};
  \node (Z+1) at (3,1) {$z_{t+1}$};
  \node (X-2) at (0,0) {$x_{t-2}$};
  \node (X-1) at (1,0) {$x_{t-1}$};
  \node (X)   at (2,0) {$x_{t}$};
  \node (X+1) at (3,0) {$x_{t+1}$};
  \draw[->] (Z-2) -- (Z-1);
  \draw[->] (Z-1) -- (Z);
  \draw[->] (Z)   -- (Z+1);
  \draw[->] (Z-2) -- (X-2);
  \draw[->] (Z-1) -- (X-1);
  \draw[->] (Z)   -- (X);
  \draw[->] (Z+1) -- (X+1);
  \draw[->] (-.5,1) -- (Z-2);
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
with diffusion
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=1,yscale=1,inner sep=2pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline=0]
  \node (x0) at (0,0) {$x^0$};
  \node (xk) at (2,0) {$x^k$};
  \draw[->] ([yshift=+1.5pt]x0.east) to node[anchor=south,inner sep=0pt]{\footnotesize +noise} ([yshift=+1.5pt]xk.west);
  \draw[->] ([yshift=-1.5pt]xk.west) to node[anchor=north,inner sep=0pt]{\footnotesize denoising} ([yshift=-1.5pt]x0.east);
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
or conditional diffution
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=1,yscale=1,inner sep=2pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline=0]
  \node (Z) at (1,1) {$z$};
  \node (x0) at (0,0) {$x^0$};
  \node (xk) at (2,0) {$x^k$};
  \draw[->] (Z) -- (x0);
  \draw[->] ([yshift=+1.5pt]x0.east) to node[anchor=south,inner sep=0pt]{\footnotesize +noise} ([yshift=+1.5pt]xk.west);
  \draw[->] ([yshift=-1.5pt]xk.west) to node[anchor=north,inner sep=0pt]{\footnotesize denoising} ([yshift=-1.5pt]x0.east);
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
trying to forecast the added noise
$\epsilon_t = x_t^{k_t} - x_t^0$
from $z_{t-1}$, $x_t^{k_t}$ and $k_t$.

One can sample on a grid, time$\times$noise,
with an arbitrary (time-dependent)
noise schedule -- noise can be seen as a form of masking.
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=1.3,yscale=1.5,inner sep=2pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline=0]
  \node (Z1) at (-1,1) {$z_{t-1}$};
  \node (Z) at (1,1) {$z_t$};
  \node (x0) at (0,0) {$x^0_t$};
  \node (xk) at (2,0) {$x^{k_t}_t$};
  \draw[->,green!60!black] (Z) to node[anchor=east,pos=.2,font=\footnotesize]{observation} (x0);
  \draw[->,red!70!black] (Z1) -- (x0);
  \draw[->,blue!80!black] (Z1) -- (Z);
  \draw[->,blue!80!black] (xk) to node[anchor=west]{\footnotesize\color{blue!80!black}Bayesian update} (Z);
  \draw[->,green!60!black] ([yshift=+1.5pt]x0.east) to node[anchor=south,inner sep=0pt]{\footnotesize +noise} ([yshift=+1.5pt]xk.west);
  \draw[->,red!70!black] ([yshift=-1.5pt]xk.west) to node[anchor=north,inner sep=0pt,align=center,text width=2cm,font=\footnotesize\color{red!70!black}]{conditional\\[-.5mm] diffusion} ([yshift=-1.5pt]x0.east);
\end{tikzpicture}
\]

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Classifier-free diffusion guidance}\\
  J. Ho and T. Salimans (2021)
\end{book}
Classifier guidance is a diffusion model whose score is combined
with the gradient of a classifier -- but we cannot use an off-the-shelf classifier:
it has to be trained on noisy data (this is reminiscent of GANs).
Instead, jointly train a conditiona and an unconditional diffusion model,
and combine their scores
\[ (1+w) \epsilon_\theta(z,c) - w \epsilon_\theta(z). \]

*** Diffusion

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Consistency models}\\
  Y. Song et al.
\end{book}
The diffusion SDE
\[ d x_t = \mu( x_t, t ) dt + \sigma(t) d W_t \]
defines a probability flow ODE
\[ \dot x_t = \mu(x_t,t) + \tfrac12 \sigma(t) ^2 \nabla \log p_t(x_t); \]
the score function
$s_\theta(x,t) \approx \nabla \log p_t(x)$
is learned from data.
Diffusion models solve this ODE,
mapping $(x_{t+1},t+1)$ to $x_t$.
Consistency models instead directly map
$(x_t,t)$ to $x_0$ (or, rather, $x_\epsilon$);
they are trained from trajectories,
and try to enforce the constraints
\begin{align*}
  f(x_t,t) &= f(x_s,s) \\
  f(x_\epsilon,\epsilon) &= x_\epsilon.
\end{align*}

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Diffusion models as masked autoencoders}\\
  C. Wei et al.
\end{book}
Diffusion model conditioned on masked input for image inpainting.

*** Diffusion, Normalizing flows

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Designing losses for data-free training of normalizing flows on Blotzman distributions}\\
  L. Felardos et al.
\end{book}
Normalizing flows approximate a target distribution by learning a bijective transformation
from some reference distribution (e.g., Gaussian).
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=1.2,yscale=.5,inner sep=1pt,outer sep=0pt,baseline=8]
  \node (A) at (0,1) {$q_N$\strut};
  \node (B) at (1,1) {$p_G$\strut};
  \node (C) at (0,0) {$q_F$\strut};
  \node (D) at (1,0) {$p_B$\strut};
  \draw[->] (A) -- (B);
  \draw[->] (D) -- (C);
  \node[anchor=east, left  = 2mm of A] {Gaussian\strut};
  \node[anchor=west, right = 2mm of B] {Generated\strut};
  \node[anchor=west, right = 2mm of D] {Target\strut};
\end{tikzpicture}
\]

One can minimize
$\text{KL}( p_G \| p_B )$:
this only requires the unnormalized target density,
but leads to mode collapse.
Minimizing $\text{KL}(q_F \| q_N)$ requires data, which can be sparse.

Instead of data,
one can use samples from the generated distribution,
with importance sampling.
For better convergence, the importance weights $p_B/p_G$ should be close to 1.

As loss function, use
\[ \operatorname*{Var}_{ x \sim \text{generated} } \log \dfrac{ p_B(x) }{ p_G(x) } \]
or, more precisely,
\[ \operatorname*{Mean}_i \Bigl( r( x_{G,i} ^\ddag ) - k^\ddag \Bigr)_+^2 \]
where
\begin{align*}
  r(x) &= \log \dfrac{ p_B(x) }{ p_G(x) } \\
  k &= \operatorname{Mean} r( x_{G,i} ) \\
  \ddag &= \text{stop-gradient}.
\end{align*}

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Action matching:\\ learning stochastic dynamics from samples}\\
  K. Neklyudov et al.
\end{book}
If particles are described by the ODE
$\dot x_t = v_t( x_t )$,
their density satisfies
$\dot q_t = -\nabla \cdot (q_t v_t )$.
Under mild conditions,
$v_t = \nabla s_t$ for some ``action'' $s_t$.
It can be recovered by minimizing
\[ \mathscr L_{\text{AM}} (s) =
\E_{ x \sim q_0 } \bigl[ s_0(x) \bigr]  +
\E_{ x \sim q_1 } \bigl[ s_1(x) \bigr] +
\int_0^1 \E_{ x \sim q_t } \bigl[ \tfrac12 \n{ \nabla s_t(x) }^2 + \dfrac{ \partial s_t }{ \partial t } \bigr] dt
\]
This can be generalized to SDEs (entropic action matching)
\[ d x_t = v_t (x) dt + \sigma_t d W_t \]
and unbalanced action matching
(creation or destruction of probability mass).

*** Schrodinger bridge, ICML

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Trajectory inference\\ with smooth Schr\"odinger bridges}\\
  W. Hong et al. (2025)
\end{book}
% Not read
In the Schr\"odinger bridge problem,
\[ \begin{array}{ll}
  \text{Find} & P \text{ stochastic process} \\
  \text{To minimize} & \text{KL}( P \,\|\, \text{Brownian motion} ) \\
  \text{Such that} & \forall k \in [K] \ \ P_k = \mu_k ,
\end{array} \]
replace Brownian motion with a smooth Gaussian process (Mat\'ern).

* Transformers, Transformers vs RNN
*** Transformers

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{RMT:\\ retentive networks meet vision transformers}\\
  Q. Fan et al.
\end{book}
A retentive block is an attention block with exponential decay
\begin{align*}
  \text{Ret}(X) &= (Q K^\top \odot D ) V \\
  Q &= (X W_Q ) \odot \Theta \\
  K &= (X W_Q ) \odot \bar\Theta \\
  V &= X W_V  \\
  \Theta_n &= e^{ i n \theta } \\
  D_{nm} &= \gamma^{n-m} \mathbf 1_{ n \geq m }
\end{align*}
It can be generalized to 2-dimensional data
(Manhattan self-attention, MaSA).

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Transformers from an optimization perspective}\\
  Y. Yang et al. (2022)
\end{book}
% Not read
\emph{Unfolded optimization} aims to replace the feed-forward layers of a neural network
$ y = f(x) $
with a gradient step from an optimization problem
\[ \operatorname*{Minimize}_y g(y;x), \]
one layer at a time.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Tokenformer: rethinking transformer scaling with tokenized model parameters}\\
  H. Wang et al.
\end{book}
Alternate standard attention
\[ X \longmapsto \text{Att}(  X W_Q, X W_K, X W_V ) \]
with token-parameter attention
\[ X \longmapsto \text{Att}(  X W_Q, W_K, W_V ) .\]

*** Transformers, MoE, PE variants

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Switch transformers:\\ scaling to trillion parameter models\\ with simple and efficient sparsity}\\
  W. Fedus et al.
\end{book}
MoE (mixture of experts) selecing just one expert, with a load-balancing loss to use all experts.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Train short, test long: attention with linear biases enables input length extrapolation}\\
  O. Press et al.
\end{book}
Position encoding (PE) adds a sine (or learned) positional encoding to the first layer.
\emph{RoPE} multiplies keys and queries with a sine encoding, at each layer; the values are left untouched.
\emph{T5 bias} adds a learned, distance-dependent bias to the pre-softmax attention, at each layer (the values are left as is).
\emph{ALiBi}
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=.2,yscale=-.2,inner sep=2pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline=0]
  \foreach \i in {0,...,4}
    \fill[blue!20] (\i,\i+1) -- (\i+1,\i+1) -- (\i+1,\i+2) -- (\i,\i+2) -- cycle;
  \foreach \i in {0,...,3}
    \fill[blue!40] (\i,\i+2) -- (\i+1,\i+2) -- (\i+1,\i+3) -- (\i,\i+3) -- cycle;
  \foreach \i in {0,...,2}
    \fill[blue!60] (\i,\i+3) -- (\i+1,\i+3) -- (\i+1,\i+4) -- (\i,\i+4) -- cycle;
  \foreach \i in {0,...,1}
    \fill[blue!80!white!80!black] (\i,\i+4) -- (\i+1,\i+4) -- (\i+1,\i+5) -- (\i,\i+5) -- cycle;
  \foreach \i in {0}
    \fill[blue!60!black] (\i,\i+5) -- (\i+1,\i+5) -- (\i+1,\i+6) -- (\i,\i+6) -- cycle;
  % Grid
  \foreach \i in {0,...,5}
    \foreach \j in {0,...,5}
      \draw (\i,\j) -- (\i+1,\j) -- (\i+1,\j+1) -- (\i,\j+1) -- cycle;
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
adds a penalty proportional to the distance to the pre-softmax attention.
\[ \text{softmax} \left(
q_i K_{ : i } ^\top - m \begin{bmatrix} i-1 \\ \vdots \\ 2 \\ 1 \\ 0 \end{bmatrix}
\right) \]

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{RoFormer: enhanced transformer\\ with rotary position embedding}\\
  J. Su et al.
\end{book}

*** MAMBA

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Hyena hierarchy:\\ towards larger convolutional language models}\\
  M. Poli et al.
\end{book}
Convolutions can be efficiently computed with the FFT
\begin{align*}
  (h *** u )_t &= \sum _{n=1} ^{L-1} h_{t-n} u_n \\
  (h *** u )&=
\left[\!\!\!
\begin{tikzpicture}[baseline={([yshift=-.8ex]current bounding box.center)},outer sep=0pt]
%\clip (-1.25,-1.15) rectangle (1.25,1.1);
\matrix (m) [
  matrix of math nodes,
  nodes in empty cells,
  row sep={3ex,between origins},column sep={6ex,between origins},
  ampersand replacement=\&% <= to change col separator for align env
]{
  h_0 \& h_{-1} \& \& h_{-L+1} \\
  h_1 \& h_0    \&   \&   h_{-L+2} \\
  \\
  h_{L-1} \& h_{L-2} \& \& h_0 \\
};
\draw[thick,dotted] (m-1-2) -- (m-1-4);
\draw[thick,dotted] (m-2-2) -- (m-2-4);
\draw[thick,dotted] (m-2-1) -- (m-4-1);
\draw[thick,dotted] (m-2-2) -- (m-4-4);
\draw[thick,dotted] (m-4-2) -- (m-4-4);
\end{tikzpicture}
\!\!\!\right]
  \begin{bmatrix} u_1 \\ u_1 \\ \vdots \\ u_{L-1} \end{bmatrix}.
\end{align*}
The filter $h$ can be implicit,
$h_t = \gamma_\theta(t)$,
where $\gamma_\theta$
is described by a neural net (with sine activations
to capture frequency information,
and possibly multiplied by a ``shaping factor'', e.g., exponential decay)
or comes from a state space model (SSM)
\begin{align*}
  x_{t+1} &= A x_t + B u_t \\
  y_t &= C x_t + D u_t
\end{align*}
\ie,
\begin{align*}
  y_t &= \sum_{n=0} ^t ( C A^{t-n} B + D \mathbf 1_{t=n} ) u_n \\
  h_t &= \begin{cases}
    X A^t B + D \mathbf 1_{t=0} &\text{if } t \geq 0 \\
    0 & \text{otherwise}
  \end{cases}
\end{align*}
where $A$, $B$, $C$, $D$ are learned
(the memory extent is determined by the spectral radius of $A$).

Hyena is a subquadratic replacement for self-attention:
it first computes projections of the input
$(v, x^1,\dots,x^N)$
(to mimick transformers, use 3, and call them value, key and query)
and iterates
\begin{align*}
  z_t^1 &= v_t \\
  z_t^{n+1} &= x_t^n ( h^n *** z^n )_t \\
  y_t &= z_t^{N+1}
\end{align*}

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Hungry hungry Hippos: towards language modeling with state space models}\\
  T. Dao et al.
\end{book}
The H3 state space model outputs
\[ Q \odot \text{SSM}_{\text{diag}} ( \text{SSM}_{\text{shift}} (k) \odot V ) \]
where the shift SSL uses
\[ A =
\left[\!\!\!
\begin{tikzpicture}[baseline={([yshift=-.8ex]current bounding box.center)},outer sep=0pt]
\clip (-1,-.9) rectangle (1,.9);
\matrix (m) [
  matrix of math nodes,
  nodes in empty cells,
  row sep={3ex,between origins},column sep={3ex,between origins},
  %ampersand replacement=\&% <= to change col separator for align env
]{
  0 & & & 0 \\
  1 \\
  \\
  0 & & 1 & 0 \\
};
\draw (m-1-1) -- (m-4-4);
\draw (m-2-1) -- (m-4-3);
\end{tikzpicture}
\!\!\!\right]
\]
and the diagonal SSM uses a diagonal $A$.

*** Algorithm, parallel prefix scan

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Were RNNs all we needed?}\\
  L. Feng et al.
\end{book}
GRU and LSTM can be simplified, by removing the influence of the hidden state $h_{t-1}$ on the gates.
\[
\def\a{\color{blue!80!black}}
\def\b{\color{red!70!black}}
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=1,yscale=1,inner sep=1pt,outer sep=0pt,baseline=8]
  \node (A) at (0,2) {\a$h_{t-1}$};
  \node (B) at (3,2) {\a$h_{t}$};
  \node (C) at (2,1) {\a$z_t$};
  \node (D) at (3,1) {\b$r_t$};
  \node (E) at (4,1) {\a$\tilde h_t$};
  \node (F) at (3,0) {\a$x_t$};
  \draw[->,blue!80!black] (A) -- (B);
  \draw[->,red!70!black] (A) -- (C);
  \draw[->,red!70!black] (A) -- (D);
  \draw[->,red!70!black] (A) -- (E);
  \draw[->,blue!80!black] (C) -- (B);
  \draw[->,red!70!black] (D) -- (E);
  \draw[->,blue!80!black] (E) -- (B);
  \draw[->,blue!80!black] (F) -- (C);
  \draw[->,red!70!black] (F) -- (D);
  \draw[->,blue!80!black] (F) -- (E);
  \node at (2,-.5) {\footnotesize{\b GRU} and {\a minGRU}};
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
\[
\def\a{\color{blue!80!black}}
\def\b{\color{red!70!black}}
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,xscale=1,yscale=1,inner sep=1pt,outer sep=0pt,baseline=8]
  \node (A) at (0,3) {\b$h_{t-1}$};
  \node (B) at (2,3) {\b$o_t$};
  \node (C) at (4,3) {\b$h_t$};
  \node (D) at (0,2) {\a$c_{t-1}$};
  \node (E) at (4,2) {\a$c_t$};
  \node (F) at (2,1) {\a$f_t$};
  \node (G) at (3,1) {\a$i_t$};
  \node (H) at (5,1) {\a$\tilde c_t$};
  \node (I) at (4,0) {\a$x_t$};
  \draw[->,red!70!black] (A) -- (B);
  \draw[->,red!70!black] (A) -- (F);
  \draw[->,red!70!black] (A) -- (G);
  \draw[->,red!70!black] (A) -- (H);
  \draw[->,red!70!black] (B) -- (C);
  \draw[->,blue!80!black] (D) -- (E);
  \draw[->,red!70!black] (E) -- (C);
  \draw[->,blue!80!black] (F) -- (E);
  \draw[->,blue!80!black] (G) -- (E);
  \draw[->,blue!80!black] (H) -- (E);
  \draw[->,blue!80!black] (I) -- (F);
  \draw[->,blue!80!black] (I) -- (G);
  \draw[->,blue!80!black] (I) -- (H);
  \node at (2.5,-.5) {\footnotesize{\b LSTM} and {\a minLSTM}};
\end{tikzpicture}
\]

The gradients can be computed with the \emph{parallel prefix scan} algorithm.
\begin{align*}
  h_k &= a_k \odot h_{k-1} + b_k \\
  \mathbf a ^*** &= \text{cumsum}( \log \mathbf a ) \\
  \mathbf c &= \log \operatorname{cumsum} \exp \bigl( \log \mathbf b - \mathbf a^*** \bigr) \\
  \mathbf h &= \mathbf a^*** + \mathbf c
\end{align*}

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Efficient parallelization\\ of a ubiquitous sequential computation}\\
  F.A. Heinsen
\end{book}
The recurrence $x_t = a_t x_{t-1} + b_t $
can be computed with two cummulated sums
(which can be computed in parallel),
\[ \mathbf x = \left(
\prod^{\text{cum}} a_t
\right) \odot \left(
x_0 + \sum^{\text{cum}} \dfrac{ b_t }{ \prod\limits^{\text{cum}} a_t }
\right), \]
\ie
\begin{align*}
  \log x_t &= a_t^*** + \log ( x_0 + b_t^*** ) \\
  a_t^*** &= \sum_t^{\text{cum}} \log a_t \\
  b_t^*** &= \sum_t^{\text{cum}} \exp( \log b_t - a_t^*** )
\end{align*}
For numeric stability, prefer
\[ x_t = \exp \bigl[
  a_t^*** + \text{tail}( \text{LCSE}( \text{cat}( \log x_0 , \log b_t - a_t^*** )))
  \bigr].
\]

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Prefix sums and their applications}\\
  G.E. Blelloch
\end{book}
Cumsum, cummax, cummin can be computed in parallel.

The recurrence
$x_i = ( x_{i-1} \otimes a_i ) \oplus b_i $
can be computed in parallel.
\begin{align*}
  (a,b) \bigcdot (a',b') &= ( a \otimes a' , b \otimes a' \oplus b' ) \\
  \mathbf x &= \text{pr}_2 \bigl( \overset{\text{cum}}{\bigcdot} (\mathbf a, \mathbf b ) \bigr)
\end{align*}
This can be generalized to higher order recurrences
(replace $x_i$ with $(x_i,x_{i-1},\dots,x_{i-k})$);
applications include
$x_i = a_i + b_i/x_{i-1}$, Fibonacci numbers, etc.

*** RNN, No?

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{xLSTM: extended long short-term memory}\\
  M. Beck et al.
\end{book}
In the LSTM, replace
\begin{align*}
  c_t &= \sigma( f_t ) c_{t-1} + \sigma(i_t) z_t \\
  h_t &= o_t \psi( c_t )
\end{align*}
with
\begin{align*}
  c_t &= \exp( f_t ) c_{t-1} + \exp( i_t ) z_t \\
  h_t &= o_t \dfrac{ c_t }{ n_t } \\
  n_t &= \exp( f_t ) n_{t-1} + \exp( i_t ).
\end{align*}
To make it more transformer-like,
replace the scalar $c$ with a matrix,
and use matrix products.
(To avoid numeric overflow, keep track of $m=\log n_t$, and divide everything by $e^m$.)
