* Graphs, GNN

*** Graphs: correlation clustering

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{KwikBucks: correlation clustering with cheap-weak and expensive-strong signals}\\
  S. Silwal et al. (2023)
\end{book}
Given a graph $(V,E)$,
\emph{correlation clustering} finds a clustering $C$ minimizing
\[ \sum_{ (i,j) \not\in E } C_{ij} + \sum_{ (i,j) \in E } (1-C_{ij}). \]
KwikCluster picks a vertex at random,
forms a cluster by adding its neighbours, removes it, and iterates.
Alternatively, find a \emph{maximum independent set}
and assign (in parallel) the other nodes to their first neighbour in that set --
these are 3-approximations.

This can be generalized to budgeted correlation clustering --
we have a limited number of queries to the exact graph,
and an unlimited number to an approximation of the graph.

*** Graph sparsification: union of random spanning trees

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Spectral sparsification of graphs:\\ theory and algorithms}\\
  J. Baston et al. (2009)
\end{book}
The \emph{Laplacian quadratic form} of a weighted graph $(V,w)$ is
\[ Q_G(x) = \sum_{ (u,v) \in E } w_{uv} ( x_u - x_v ) ^ 2. \]
The \emph{cut} of a subset of nodes $S \subset V$ is
\[ \text{Cut}_G(S) = \sum_{\substack{ u \in S \\ v \not\in S }} w_{uv} = Q(\mathbf{1}_S ). \]
Two weighted graphs are \emph{cut-similar},
resp. \emph{spectrally similar} if
\[ \begin{array}{lr@{\,}c@{\,}l}
  \forall S & \sigma^{-1} \cdot \text{Cut}_{\tilde G} (S) \leq & \text{Cut}_G(S) & \leq \sigma \cdot \text{Cut}_{\tilde G} (S) \\
  \forall S & \sigma^{-1} \cdot Q_{\tilde G} (S)          \leq & Q_G(S)          & \leq \sigma \cdot Q_{\tilde G} (S).
\end{array} \]
The \emph{effective resistance} between two nodes $u$, $v$ is
\[ R_{uv} = \biggl( \min_{ x: \ x_u=1,\, x_v=0 } Q_G(x) \biggr)^{\!\!-1} \]
(the equivalent resistance if you replace each edge with a resistor of resistance $1/w$).

The hypercube is a spectral approximation
(a spectral sparsifier) of the complete graph on $2^n$ vertices
(set the weights on the hypercube to $n/2\sqrt{\log n}$).

One can obtain spectral sparsifiers:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item By sampling $q$ edges (with replacement)
  with probabilities $p_e \propto w_e R_e$
  and assigning them weights $w_e / q p_e$;
\item Or as a union of random spanning trees.
\end{compactitem}

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Spectral sparsification of graphs}\\
  D.A. Spielman and S.H. Teng (2010)
\end{book}

\begin{compactitem}[--]

*** Bayesian optimization, ICML

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Earl-NO: reinforcement learning\\ for multi-step lookahead,\\ high-dimensional Bayesian optimization}\\
  M. Cheon et al.
\end{book}
Traditional Bayesian optimization looks for one-step optimal decisions.
Instead, use reinforcement learning (PPO) for multi-step lookahead decisions.
Encode the state (set of points examined so far, with their values)
with attention and DeepSet,
to have a constant-size state space.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Understanding\\ high-dimensional Bayesian optimization}\\
  L. Papenmeier et al. (2025)
\end{book}
% Empty / survey
High-dimensional Bayesian optimization relies on:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Embedding (linear or not) to a low-dimensional space;
\item Assumptions, such as additivity or axis-alignment;
\item Local (CMAES-like) sampling;
\item Large scale length hyperprior (multiply by $\sqrt d$).
\end{compactitem}

*** Optimization, Influence functions, data-centric ML, ICML

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Understanding black-box predictions\\ via influence functions}\\
  P.W. Koh and P. Liang (2017)
\end{book}
The influence of a training point $(x_i,y_i)$ on the loss
\[ L = \sum_j \ell( x_j, y_j; \hat\theta ) \]
is
\[ - \dfrac{ \partial L }{ \partial \hat\theta } H^{-1} \dfrac{ \partial \ell( x_i, y_u; \hat\theta )}{ \partial \hat\theta } . \]
% Derivation provided
The influence of a perturbation on $x_i$ is
\[ - \dfrac{ \partial L}{ \partial \hat\theta } H^{-1} \dfrac{ \partial^2 \ell( x_i,y_i ; \hat\theta ) }{ \partial x_i \partial \hat\theta }. \]
There is no need to explicitly invert $H$:
we can use a few steps of conjugate gradient to estimate
\[ H^{-1} v = \operatorname*{Argmin}_t t' H t - v' t , \]
which only requires Hessian-vector products

Alternative, notice that (LiSSA)
\begin{align*}
  H^{-1} &= \bigl[ I - (I-H) \bigr] ^{-1} \\
  &= \sum_{ i \geq 0 } (I-H)^i \\
  H_j^{-1} &= \sum_{ i = 0 }^j ( I - H ) ^i \underset{j\to\infty}{\longrightarrow} H^{-1} \\
  H_j^{-1} &= I + ( I-H) H_{j-1} ^{-1}
\end{align*}
and substitute $H$ with an unbiased estimate, for instance, the Hessian for a single point
\begin{align*}
  \tilde H_0 v &= v \\
  \tilde H_j v &= v + \bigl[ I - \nabla_\theta^2 \ell( x_j, y_j ; \hat\theta ) \bigr] \tilde H_{j-1} v
\end{align*}
(iterate until $\tilde H_j v$ stabilizes, and average over several runs).

Applications include:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Identifying useful/useless/detrimental training samples;
\item Identifying detrimental samples in case of domain mismatch (if $L$ is computed on the test set);
\item Building adversarial training examples.
\end{compactitem}

% For non-differentiable losses, use smooth approximations.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Outlier gradient analysis:\\ efficiently identifying detrimental\\ training samples for deep learning}\\
  A. Chhabra e al. (2025)
\end{book}
Detrimental samples can be identified by the influence function
\[ I(z_j) = - \sum_z \nabla_{ \hat\theta} \ell( z, \hat\theta ) ^\top H_{\hat\theta}^{-1} \nabla_{\hat\theta} \ell (z_j, \hat\theta) \]
(provided $\ell$ is strictly convex, so that the Hessian is invertible).
Instead (inverting the Hessian is expensive),
look for outliers in gradient space, e.g., with an isolation forest.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Second-order stochastic optimization\\ for machine learning in linear time}\\
  N. Agarwal et al. (2017)
\end{book}
Optimization problems in machine learning are often solved with
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item First order methods, with variance reduction (SVRG, SAGA, SDCA),
  and acceleration (AccSDCA, Catalyst, Katyushka);
\item Quasi-Newton (approximation of Newton's method using only gradients, e.g. L-BFGS).
\end{compactitem}
To solve
\[ \operatorname*{Minimize}_x f(x) = \dfrac1n \sum_i f_i(x)  \]
LiSSA uses Newton's method
\begin{align*}
  x &\leftarrow x - H^{-1} g \\
  g &= \nabla f \\
  H &= \nabla^2 f
\end{align*}
without explicit matrix inversion
\[ H_n = I + (I-H) H_{n-1}  \]
and with a stochastic estimator for the Hessian
\[ H_n = I + ( I - \nabla ^2 f_i ) H_{n-1}. \]
Since the Newton step is a quadratic optimization,
we can replace it with a first-order step to solve it
(with a new, single $\nabla^2 f_i$ each time).

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{On second-order group influence functions\\ for black-box predictions}\\
  S. Basu et al. (2020)
\end{book}
The first-order approximation of the influence function
\[ I(z, z_t ) = - \nabla_\theta \ell( z_t )^\top H^{-1} \nabla _\theta \ell(z) \]
could be applied to a group $U \subset S$ of observations,
but the approximation is too loose.
Prefer a second order approximation: replace
\[
I(z) = - H^{-1} \nabla_\theta \ell(z)
\]
with
\[
  I(z) =
  \underbrace{
    - H^{-1} \sum_{ z \in U } \nabla _\theta \ell(z)
  }_{\theta^{(1)}}
  + (I-H)^{-1} \sum_{ z \in U } \nabla_\theta^2 \ell(z) \theta^{(1)}.
\]

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Discriminative Jackknife:\\ quantifying uncertainty in deep learning\\ via higher-order influence functions}\\
  A.M. Alaa and M. van der Schaar (2020)
\end{book}
Define a confidence inferval for $f(x,\hat\theta)$
with the quantiles of
$\bigl\{
f(x, \hat\theta_{-i} ) \pm \bigl| y_i - f(x_i, \hat\theta_{-i} ) \bigr|
\bigr\}_{i=1}^n$
and approximate $f(x, \hat\theta_{-i})$
with a higher-order expansion of the influence function.
% It can be computes with "von Mises calculus": Taylor expansion for functionals.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Beta Shapley: a unified and noise-reduced data valuation framework for machine learning}\\
  Y. Kwon and J. Zou
\end{book}
\emph{Data Shapley} values compute the contribution of each training sample to a model's performance.
They are computed as the average change in performance when a particular training point is removed
from a set of a goven cardinality, averaged over all cardinalities.
Removing the efficiency axiom
\[ \sum_ { z \in D } \psi(z) = U(D) \]
allows for non-uniform weights in that average, for instance,
beta-distributed weights:
$B(\alpha,1)$ to focus on small cardinalities and remove the noise from the large cardinalities,
or $B(1,\beta)$ to be closer to leave-out-one (LOO).

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{DataInf: efficiently estimating data influence in LoRA-tuned LLMs and diffusion models}\\
  Y. Kwon et al.
\end{book}
If the loss $K = \sum L_i$ is a negative log-likelihood,
\[ \E [ \nabla_\theta^2 \ell ] = \E \bigl[ ( \nabla_\theta \ell ) ( \nabla_\theta \ell )^\top \bigr] \]
the Hessian $H = \nabla_\theta^2 L$ in the influence function
\[ I_k = - H^{-1} \nabla_\theta \ell_k \]
can be replaced with
\[ G = \operatorname*{Mean}_i ( \nabla_\theta \ell_i  ) ( \nabla_\theta \ell_i )^\top \]
which only requires gradients.

For large neural nets:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item $G$ is not invertible: dampen it by adding $\lambda I$;
\item $G$ is too large: make it block-diagonal, with one block per layer.
\end{compactitem}

To compute $G^{-1}$, either use LiSSA,
or swap the average and the inverse:
\begin{align*}
& \bigl( \operatorname*{Mean}_i  ( \nabla_\theta \ell_i  ) ( \nabla_\theta \ell_i )^\top + \lambda I \bigr)^{-1}
  \approx {} \\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad
\operatorname*{Mean}_i \bigl(   ( \nabla_\theta \ell_i  ) ( \nabla_\theta \ell_i )^\top + \lambda I \bigr)^{-1}
\end{align*}
and use the Sherman-Morrison formula to compute the inverse (identity plus rank-one) in closed form.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Nested expectations with kernel quadrature}\\
  Z. Chen et al. (2025)
\end{book}
Kernel quadrature (aka Bayesian quadrature) approximates
\[ \E_{ X \sim \pi } \bigl[ h(X) \bigr] \approx \sum_{ n=1 }^N w_n h( x_n ) \]
where $h \in \mathscr H$, a RKHS with kernel $\kappa$
(e.g., the Sobolev space $X_2^s(\mathcal X)$ of functions on $\mathcal X$ whose (weak) derivatives
of order up to $s$ are in $L^2$).
\begin{align*}
  \mathbf w &= \mu_\pi(\mathbf x ) ( K + N \lambda I )^{-1} \\
  K &= \kappa( \mathbf x, \mathbf x ) \text{ Gram matrix} \\
  \mu_\pi(x) &= \E_ { X \sim \pi } \bigl[ \kappa( X, x ) \bigr ]
  \text{ kernel mean embedding}
\end{align*}
The convergence rate is optimal, but non-iid choices of the $x_i$'s can reduce the rate constant.

It can be used to compute nested expectations
\[ \E_{ \theta \sim q } f\biggl( \E_{ X \sim p_\theta} \bigl[ g(X,\theta) \bigr] \biggr). \]
Alternatives (with no smoothness restrictions on $f$ and~$g$) include
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Nested Monte Carlo: $O(\epsilon^{-4})$;
\item Nested quasi Monte Carlo: $O(\epsilon^{-2.5})$;
\item Multi-level Monte Carlo: $O(\epsilon^{-2})$.
\end{compactitem}

In the Black-Litterman framework
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,yscale=.4,xscale = .8,inner sep=1pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline=0]
  \node[anchor=east] (r) at (0,2) {$r \sim N(\mu, \Sigma)$};
  \node[anchor=east] (p) at (0,0) {$p_r \sim N(v,\Omega)$};
  \node[anchor=west] (o) at (1,1) {$r^*** \sim N(\mu^*, \Sigma^*)$};
  \draw[->] (r.east) -- ([yshift=+5pt]o.west);
  \draw[->] (p.east) -- ([yshift=-5pt]o.west);
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
have a neural net compute $v$ and $\Omega$ from asset-specific data.

*** No, COnvergent cross-mapping

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Convergent Cross Mapping (CCM)}\\
\end{book}
CCM is a nonlinear Granger causality test:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Start with two time series $(x_t)_t$ and $(y_t)_t$;
\item Build their delay embeddings $(X_t)_t$ and $(Y_t)_t$
  (choose the lag $\tau$ and the embedding dimension $E$);
\item Forecast $x_t$ from $Y_t$, and $y_t$ from $X_t$,
  with $k$-NN ($k=E+1$) and weights
  \[ w_i \propto \exp( - d_i / d_1 ) \]
  where $d_i$ is the distance to the $i$th closest neighbour;
\item Compute $\cor( x_t, \hat x_t )$ and $\cor( y_t, \hat y_t )$;
  consider values above 0.3 significant.
\end{compactitem}

*** ML, k-means, no

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Ckmeans.1d.dp: optimal $k$-means clustering\\ in one dimension by dynamic programming}\\
  H. Wang and M. Song
\end{book}
In dimension 1, the $k$-means problem can be solved with dynamic programming,
computing $D[i,m]$, the minimum within-cluster sum of squares
of a clustering of $x_1,\dots,x_i$ into $m$ clusters.
R implementation in \texttt{Ckmeans.1d.dp}.

K-means
\[ \operatorname*{Minimize}_{\substack{
    \mu_1, \dots, \mu_k \in \RR^d \\
    c_1, \dots, c_n \in \llbracket 1 , k \rrbracket
}}
\n{ y_i - \mu_{ c_i } }^2
\]

can be generalized to regression
\[ \operatorname*{Minimize}_{\substack{
    \alpha_1, \dots, \alpha_k \in \RR \\
    \beta_1, \dots, \beta_k \in \RR^d \\
    c_1, \dots, c_n \in \llbracket 1 , k \rrbracket
}}
\bigl[ y_i - ( \alpha_{ c_i } + \beta_{ c_i }^\top X_i ) \bigr] ^2.
\]

*** Bootstrap, No

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Maximum entropy bootstrap for time series: the meboot R package}\\
  H.D. Vinod and J. L\'opez-de-Lacalle
\end{book}
The \emph{maximum entropy bootstrap}, for potentially non-stationary time series,
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Separates the order of the observations from their distribution;
\item Resamples the observations, using a piecewise uniform distribution (more precisely, a piecewise maximum entropy distribution, with a pre-specified mean on each interval, to allow values slightly more extreme than observed);
\item Keep the order (sic).
\end{compactitem}

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Maximum entropy ensembles for time series inference in economics}\\
  H.D. Vinod (2006)
\end{book}

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Replacing cross-validation with interrogation:
  a universal test for underfitting and overfitting}\\
  M. Czasonis et al. (2025)
\end{book}
Using partial dependency plots, decompose the output of a trained model into
a univariate linear, univariate non-linear,
pairwise interactions, and higher interaction components
\[ f(x) = f_{\text{lin}}(x) + f_{\text{nonlin}}(x) + f_{\text{bivar}}(x) + f_{\text{higher}}(x) \]
anc check how much they overlap, by fitting a regression
\[ y = \alpha + \beta_1 f_{\text{lin}}(x) + \beta_2 f_{\text{nonlin}}(x) + \beta_3 f_{\text{bivar}}(x) + \beta_4 f_{\text{higher}}(x). \]
If the model does not overfit the data, the components are almost independent,
and we have $\beta_1=\beta_2 = \beta_3 = \beta_4 = 1$, which can be assessed by an $F$ test.
[Does not seem to work: I have $p>0.05$ whether the model overfits or not.]

*** No?, Low discrepancy

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Constructing optimal $L_\infty$ star discrepancy sets}\\
  F. Cl\'ement et al. (2024)
\end{book}
The \emph{$L^\infty$ star discrepancy} of a finite set $ P \subset [0,1]^d$ is
\[ d_\infty^*(P) = \sup_{ q \in [0,1] } \va{
  \dfrac{ \va{ P \inter [0,q] } }{ \va P } - \lambda\bigl( [0,q] \bigr)
} \]
where $\lambda$ is the Lebesgue measure.
It suffices to consider $q \in \bar\Gamma(P)$.
\begin{align*}
  \bar \Gamma(P) &= \bar\Gamma_1(P) \times \cdots \times \bar\Gamma_d(P) \\
  \bar\Gamma_i(P) &= \Gamma_i(P) \union \{1\} \\
  \Gamma_i(P) &= \{ x_i : x \in P \}
\end{align*}

*** No?, Statistics

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Strong laws for L and U statistics}\\
  J. Aaronson et al. (1996)
\end{book}
\d{U-statistics} are averages of kernels of random samples, \ie, they are of the form
\[ U_n(X_1,\dots,X_n) = \operatorname*{Mean}_{\substack{
    I \subset \llbracket 1, n \rrbracket \\ \va I = r }}
f(X_I) \]
where $f$ is a symmetric function of $r$ variables,
e.g., $f(x_1)=x_1$ for the mean, or $f(x_1,x_2)=\tfrac12(x_1-x_2)^2$
for the variance.

\d{L-statistics} are linear combinations of order statistics,
\ie, they are of the form
\[ L_n(X_1,\dots,X_n) = \sum_{ i=1 }^n
\mu \left( \left[ \dfrac{i-1}{n}, \dfrac in \right] \right) X_{n;i}, \]
e.g.,
\begin{align*}
  & \text{density} && \text{statistic } L(F) = \int_0^1 F^{-1} d\mu \\
  & J(u) && M_1(F) = \E[X] \\
  & J(u) = \alpha u^{\alpha-1} && P_\alpha(F) = \int x dF^\alpha(x) \\
  & J(u) = 4u-2 && g_1(F) = \E \va{ X - X' }.
\end{align*}

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Softmax is not enough\\ (for sharp out-of-distribution)}\\
  P. Veli\v ckovi\'c et al.
\end{book}
``Softmax'' should have been called ``softargmax''.

A function is \emph{sharp} if it only depends on a constant number of inputs, e.g., max.
Softmax cannot approximate shaprness with increasing problem size:
use a temperature-adjusted softmax instead,
with the temperature depending on the entropy of the initial probability distribution.

\textbf{1.}
Deterministic models (Kermack-McKendrick): compartmental models,
basic reproduction number $R_0$.
Reduction of contact as the epidemic progresses:
$\beta S I \to \beta S f(I)$.
The time spent in each compactment is exponential, but this can be generalized.

\textbf{2.} Compartmental models and basic reproduction number.
SIS: two equilibria, disease-free, and endemic.
\begin{align*}
  \dot S &= - \beta S I + \alpha I \\
  \dot I &= \beta S I - \alpha I \\
  R_0 = \beta N / \alpha \\
  N &= S + I = \text{const}
\end{align*}
Viruses tend to be SIR, bacteria SIS.
Adding births and deaths.

\textbf{4.} Epidemic models.

Compartment models do not adequately describe the begining of an epidemic:
use a branching process instead,
to describe the epidemic on a graph.
The random contacts of an individual with others can be described by a random graph.
If $G_0$ is the generating function of the degree distribution,
then
\begin{align*}
  \langle k \rangle &= G'_0(1) \\
  G_1(z) = \dfrac{ G_0'(z) }{ \langle k \rangle } \\
  R_0 &= G_1'(1) = \dfrac{ G''_0(1) }{ G_0'(1) }
\end{align*}

\textbf{5.} Models with heterogeneous mixing

With several suceptible and infectious compartments, e.g., vaccinated and non-vaccinated,
or age groups, the notion of reproduction number becomes more complicated:
$R_{ij}$ is the average number of suceptible of group $i$ invected by an infective member of group $j$.

\begin{align*}
  \dot x &= F(x,y) - V(x,y) &\text{disease}\\
  \dot y &= g(x,y) &\text{non-disease} \\
  F &\text{: secondary infection} \\
  V &\text{: net outflow}
\end{align*}
Linearize around a disease-free equilibrium: $\dot x = (F-V) x$,
$k = F V^{-1}$ (next generation matrix),
$R_0 = \rho( F V^{-1} )$ (largest eigenvalue).

Residual networks
\[ x_{i+1} = x_i + \ell_{i+1} (x_i) \]
can be generalized to Riemannian manifolds
\[ x_{i+1} = \exp_{ x_i } \ell_{i+1} (x_i ) \]
where the vector field $\ell_i$ is defined with a standard neural net
\[ n_i : \RR^D \longrightarrow \RR^D \]
after embedding $\mathscr M$ into a higher-dimensional vector space
$\mathscr M \hookrightarrow \RR^D$,
\[ \ell_i(x) = \text{proj}_{ T_x \mathscr M} n_i(x). \]

The Peter-Weyl theorem states that if $G$ is a compact group and $X$ a homogeneous space,
then $L^2(X,K)$ ($K=\RR$ or $\CC$) is a direct sum
of unitary irreducible representations,
each with multiplicity at most its dimension;
for $X=G$ and $K=\CC$, the multiplicity is the dimension.

  G. Viggiano

In the Taylor expansion
\[ f(x) = \sum_{ j=0 }^k \dfrac{ f^{(j)} (c) }{ j! } (x-c)^j +
\dfrac{ f^{k+1} (\xi ) }{ (k+1)! } (x-c)^{k+1} \]
for $f \in \mathscr C^{k+1}$,

*** TDA

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Differentiable Euler characteristic transforms for shape classification}\\
  E. R\"oell and B. Rieck
\end{book}
The Euler characteristic of a simplicial complex $K$ is
\[ \chi(K) = \sum_{ n \geq 0 } (-1)^k \va{ K^n } \]
where $\va{ K^n}$ is the number of $n$-simplices.
If the 0-simplices $\sigma \in K^0$
have coordinates $x_\sigma \in \RR^n$,
a function $f: \RR^n \to R$
defines a function $f: K \to \RR$ by
\begin{align*}
  \tilde f(\sigma) &= x_\sigma &&\text{for 0-simplices} \\
  \tilde f(\sigma) &= \max_{ \tau \subset \sigma} \tilde f(\tau) &&\text{for higher-dimensional simplices}
\end{align*}
and a filtration
$K_r = \tilde f^{-1} \bigl( (-\infty , r ] \bigr) $.

The \emph{Euler characteristic transform} (ECT)
of $K$ is the function
\[ \left\{
\begin{array}{rcl}
  \SS^{n-1} \times \RR & \longrightarrow & \ZZ \\
  (\xi,h) & \longmapsto & \chi \bigl( \tilde f^{-1}_\xi \bigl( (-\infty,r] \bigr)\bigr)
\end{array}
\right.
\]
where $f_\xi = \langle \bigcdot , \xi \rangle$.

It can be written as a sum of step functions
\[ \text{ECT}(\xi, h ) = \sum_k (-1)^k \sum_{ \sigma_k } \mathbf 1_{
  \bigl[ \tilde f_\xi ( \sigma_k ) , \infty \bigr)
  } (h) . \]
To make it differentiable, replace the step functions with sigmoids.

*** Bootstrap

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{The uncertainty of machine learning predictions in asset pricing}\\
  Y. Liao et al. (2025)
\end{book}
There are many types of bootstraps:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Classical: resampling with replacement;
\item Residual: fit a model and esample the residuals;
\item Wild: idem, after multiplying the residuals by $N(0,1)$
  (for heteroskedastic data);
\item Blocks: blocks of constant sizes;
\item Stationary: blocks of random length, from a geometric (exponential) distribution;
\item Parametric: $X \sim F_{ \hat\theta}$;
\item Smooth: $X \sim \text{kde}$;
\item etc.
\end{compactitem}
To compute prediction confidence intervals for deep learning models on panel data,
use a variant of the wild bootstrap:
\begin{align*}
  \hat y_{it} &= \hat g( x_{i, t-1} ) && \text{forecast} \\
  \epsilon_{it} &= y_{it} - \hat y_{it} && \text{residual} \\
  \eta_t &\sim N(0,1) && \eta_t \text{, not } \eta_{it} \text{ or } \eta_i \\
  y_{it}^*** = \hat y_{it} + \eta_t \epsilon_{it}
\end{align*}
and fine-tune the model (starting from the trained one) for $k$ epochs.

Applications include worst-case (uncertainty-aware) portfolio optimization
\[ \operatorname{Maximize}_w \min_{ \mu \in \text{CI} } w' \mu - \dfrac\lambda2 w' V w. \]

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{The stationary bootstrap}\\
  D.N. Politis and J.P. Romano
\end{book}
Resample blocks of random lengths, from a geometric distribution.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Relevance-based importance: a comprehensive measure of variable importance in prediction}\\
  M. Czasonis et al. (2024)
\end{book}
The adjusted fit, from a relevance grid model,
defines function
\[ \begin{array}{rcl}
  \mathscr P( \text{predictors}) \& \longrightarrow & \RR \\
  \theta & \longmapsto & \text{Fit}_{\theta, t} ^{\text{adj}}
\end{array} \]
for each observation $t$;
the \emph{relevance-based importance}
$\text{RBI}_{t,k}$ is the corresponding Shapley value for predictor $k$.

*** Relevance

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Relevance}\\
  M. Czasonis et al.
\end{book}
The OLS forecast can be written as a ``relevance-weighted'' average of past observations,
\[ \hat y = \bar y + \dfrac1{N-1} \sum_{i=1}^N r_{it} ( y_i - \bar y ) \]
where the relevance between observations is
\begin{align*}
  r_{ij} &= \text{similarity}_{ij} + {}\\
  &\hphantom{={}} \tfrac12( \text{informativeness}_i + \text{informativeness}_j )
\end{align*}
\begin{align*}
  \text{similarity}_{ij} &= -\tfrac12 ( x_i - x_j ) ' V^{-1} ( x_i - x_j ) \\
  \text{informativeness}_i &= ( x_1 - \bar x )' V^{-1} ( x_i - \bar x).
\end{align*}
If you only use the subset $I \subset \llbracket 1, N \rrbracket$
of most relevant observations, use
\begin{align*}
  \hat y &= \bar y + \dfrac{ \lambda^2 }{ \va I - 1 }
  \sum_{i \in I } r_{it} ( y_i - \bar y ) \\
  \lambda^2 &= \left.
  \dfrac1{N+1} \sum_{i-1}^N r_{it}^2
  \middle/
  \dfrac1{\va I + 1 } \sum_{ i \in I} r_{it}^2.
  \right.
\end{align*}

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Relevance-based prediction: a transparent and adaptive alternative to machine learning}\\
  M. Czasonis et al.
\end{book}
Relevance-based prediction
\begin{align*}
  \hat y &= x'_{\text{new}} \hat\beta \\
  &= \bar y + \dfrac1{N-1} \sum r_i ( y_i - \bar y ) \\
  &= \sum w_i y_i
\end{align*}
expresses OLS forecasts as
(data-dependent) linear combinations of observations
\begin{align*}
  r_i &= \text{sim}( x_{\text{new}} , x_i ) + \tfrac12 \bigl[
    \text{info}( x_{\text{new}} ) + \text{info}( x_i )
    \bigr] \\
  \text{sim}( x_1,x_2 ) &= - \tfrac12 ( x_1 - x_2 ) ' V^{-1} ( x_1 - x_2 ) \\
  \text{info}(x) &= ( x - \bar x ) ' V^{-1} ( x - \bar x ).
\end{align*}
We can also recover $R^2$ as
\begin{align*}
  R^2 &= \dfrac1{N-1} \sum \text{info}(x_i) \text{fit}_i \\
  \text{fit}_i &= \cor( w, y )^2
\end{align*}

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{The virtue of transparency: how to maximize the utility of data without overfitting}\\
  M. Czasonis et al. (2024)
\end{book}
Use partial sample regression
(relevance-based prediction on a subset of high-relevance observations)
on a grid, with various relevance thresholds $r^*$ (deciles)
and all possible subsets of variables;
use the adjusted fit as weights to combine the forecasts
(as in BMA, Bayesian model averaging)
\begin{align*}
  \hat y &= \sum w_i y_i \\
  \text{fit} &= \cor(w,y) \\
  \text{asymmetry} &= \tfrac12 \bigl[
    \cor(w^+,y) - \cor(w^-,y)
    \bigr] ^2 \\
  \text{adjusted fit} &=
  \text{\#variables} \times
  ( \text{fit} + \text{asymmetry} )
\end{align*}
where $w^+$ and $w_-$ are the weights
if we use the observations with $_i \geq r^*$
and $r_i < r^*$ (so that $w^+ = w$).

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{A transparent alternative to neural networks with an application to predicting volatility}\\
  M. Czasonis et al. (2024)
\end{book}
Relevance grid numeric example:
predicting volatility from 14 variables (do not use all the subsets of predictors, just 100 random ones).

*** MAB, ICML

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Log-sum-exponential estimator\\ for off-policy evaluation and learning}\\
\end{book}
Log-sum-exp
\[ \text{LSE}_\lambda(\mathbf z) = \dfrac 1\lambda \log \dfrac1n \sum_{i=1}^n e^{ \lambda z_i } \]
is a (biased) mean estimator with low variance and mean square error for fat-tail distributions;
it is the KL-regularized average:
\[ \min_{ p \in \Delta }  \sum p_i z_i - \dfrac1\lambda \text{KL}\bigl( p \,\|\, \tfrac1n\bigr). \]
Use it to estimate the value of a multi-arm bandit (MAB) policy (off-policy evaluation, OPE).

the forecaster can sample from the arms $n$ times
and then output a recommended arm;
it is evaluated on the simple reward.
Applications include cosmetic tests
(contrary to medical tests, there is no cost for the ``wrong'' treatment in the test phase)
or the maximization of a function observed with noirs

\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Fits a LinUCB model for each user;
\item Clusters the coefficients of those models ($k$-means);
\item Selects the action using the average model in each cluster.
\end{compactitem}

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Nonparametric bandits with covariates}\\
  P. Rigolet and A. Zeevi (2018)
\end{book}
For the (non-contextual) 2-arm bandit, UCB is asymptotically optimal and its regret is $O( \log n )$.
For a contextual 2-arm bandit, bin the context vectors (using a grid) and use UCB on each cell;
the regret is polynomial -- there exist bandits for which this cannot be improved.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Nonparametric stochastic contextual bandits}\\
  M.Y. Guan and H. Jiang (2018)
\end{book}
(Under reasonable assumptions), $k$-NN-UCB has sublinear regret.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{A practical method for solving contextual bandit problems using decision trees}\\
  A.N. Elmachtoub et al.
\end{book}
For each possible action, fit a decision tree on a bootstrap sample,
and use it as a Thompson sample:
predict the reward, and pick the action with the highest reward.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{The epoch-greedy algorithm\\ for contextual multi-armed bandits}\\
  J. Langford and T. Zhang
\end{book}
Iterate:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item One exploration step (random arm);
\item $\lceil \epsilon_n \rceil$ exploitation steps, where $\epsilon_n$ is an average regret bound for each exploration step,
  for the policy optimal (from a finite set $\mathscr H$ of candidate policies)
  on the exploration data only.
\end{compactitem}

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Gambling in a rigged casino:\\ the adversarial multi-armed bandit problem}\\
  P Auer et al. (1998)
\end{book}
\emph{Exp3} solves the fixed-horizon multi-arm bandit (MAB) problem with $k$ arms as
\begin{align*}
  p_i(t) &\propto \exp \eta \sum_{ \tau < t } \hat r_{i \tau } \\
  \hat r_{it} &= \begin{cases}
    \dfrac{ r_{it} }{ \hat p_{it} } & \text{if arm $i$ was chosen at time $t$} \\
    0 & \text{otherwise}
  \end{cases} \\
  \hat p_{it} &= (1- \epsilon) p_{it} + \dfrac{ \epsilon }{ k }
\end{align*}

\emph{Exp4} uses the same idea to choose between experts (each providing a probability distribution on arms).

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Approximate planning in large POMDPs\\ via reusable trajectories}\\
  M. Kearns et al.
\end{book}
Given a POMDP you can sample from,
with a finite action space, build \emph{trajectory trees}:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Nodes: state, observation pairs
\item Children for all actions, with the edge labeled by the corresponding reward;
\item Depth $H$ (there is an exponential number of nodes in each tree).
\end{compactitem}
Use those trees to evaluate policies:
a deterministic (resp. stochastic) policy defines
a (distribution on) path(s).

*** MAB, Doubly robust

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Doubly robust policy evaluation\\ and optimization}\\
  M. Dud\'ik et al. (2014)
\end{book}
The \emph{direct method} (DM, aka controlling for confounders, backdoor adjustment)
estimates the value of a (MAB) policy
from an estimate $\hat r(s,a)$ of the reward conditioned on the state (
state = context = exogenous variables) $s$ and action $a$
\[ V = \operatorname*{Mean} _t \sum_a \pi( a, s_t ) \hat r( s_t , a ) ; \]
it has low variance but potentially high bias.

\emph{Importance sampling} (IS) uses the (known) data collection policy $\pi_0$
\[ V = \operatorname*{Mean} _t \dfrac{ \pi( a_t, s_t ) }{ \pi_0( a_t, s_t ) }  r_t \]
(there is no bias, but the variance is high if $\pi$ and $\pi_0$ are very different).

The \emph{inverse propensity} score (IPS, or inverse propensity weighting, IPW)
method uses some estimator $\hat \pi_0$ of the collection policy,
\[ V = \operatorname*{Mean} _t \dfrac{ \pi( a_t, s_t ) }{ \hat \pi_0( a_t, s_t ) }  r_t. \]

The \emph{doubly robust} (DR) estimator uses $\hat r$ (DM) as a baseline,
and corrects it with the propensity score
\[ V = \operatorname*{Mean} _t \sum_a \pi(a, s_t) \hat r(s_t, a) +
\dfrac{ \pi( a_t, s_t ) }{ \hat \pi_0( a_t, s_t ) }  ( r_t - \hat r_t(s_t, a_t)  ).
\]

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Open bandit dataset and pipeline: towards realistic and reproducible off-policy valuation}\\
  Y. Saito et al. (2021)
\end{book}
Besides DM, IPW, DR, \emph{off-policy evaluation} (OPE) of contextual MAB policies include
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Self-normalized IPW
  \[ V = \dfrac{
    \operatorname*{Mean}_t w(a_t,s_r) r_t
  }{
    \operatorname*{Mean}_t w(a_t,s_r)
  }, \qquad
  w(a,s) = \dfrac{ \pi(a,s) }{ \hat\pi_0(a,s) } \]
\item Switch estimator: DR when $w \leq \tau$, DM when the importance weights are too large;
\item DR with optimistic shrinkage
  \begin{align*}
    V &= \operatorname*{Mean}_t \hat r(s_t,\pi) + \hat w( a_t, s_t, \lambda) \bigl[ r_t - \hat r(a_t, s_t ) \bigr] \\
    \!\!\hat w(a, s, \lambda) &= \dfrac{ \lambda }{ w(a,s) ^ + \lambda } w(a,s)
  \end{align*}
\item Cross-fitting.
\end{compactitem}

*** MAB

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{A contextual bandit approach to personalized news article recommendation}\\
  L. Li et al. (2010)
\end{book}
The UCB algorithm, for multi-arm bandits (MAB), chooses the arm $i$ maximizing
\[ \text{UCB}_i = \hat\mu_i + \sqrt{ \dfrac{ 2 \log t }{ n_i } } . \]
For contextual bandits, use
\begin{align*}
  \text{LinUCB}_i &= x_i ' \hat\theta_i + \alpha \sqrt{ x_i ' A_i ^{-1} x_i } \\
  A_i &= X_i ' X_i + I \\
  \hat\theta_i &= ( X_i ' X_i + I )^{-1} X_i ' b_i \quad \text{(ridge)} \\
  x_i &\text{: feature vector} \\
  X_i &\text{: design matrix} \\
  b_i &\text{: response vector}
\end{align*}
This can be generalized to hybrid models
(some parameters are shared among the arms).

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Thompson sampling\\ for contextual bandits with linear payoffs}\\
  S. Agrawal and N. Goyal (2013)
\end{book}
\emph{Thompson sampling} can be generalized to contextual MABs:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Compute the posterior distribution of $\theta_i$, for each $i$:
  $N(\hat\theta_i, \alpha^2 A^{-1} )$;
\item Sample from them;
\item Choose the arm $i$ maximizing $x_i ' \theta_i$.
\end{compactitem}
\emph{LinTS} is similar to LinUCB:
the former picks the arm maximizing a random sample,
the latter an arm maximizing an upper bound.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Value-difference based exploration: adaptive control between epsilon-greedy and softmax}\\
  M. Tokic and G. Palm
\end{book}
% Not read
The \emph{softmax} MAB algorithm is a modification of $\epsilon$-greedy
paying attention to the difference in rewards:
it picks the arm $i$ with probability
$p_i \propto \exp( \hat\mu_i / \tau )$.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Algorithms for the multi-armed bandit problem}\\
  V. Kuleshov and D. Precup (2009)
\end{book}
The \emph{pursuit} MAB algorithm is
\[ p(t+1) = p(t) + \beta \bigl[ e_i - p(t) \bigr] \]
where $e_i$ is the basis vector for
\[ i = \argmax_i \hat\mu_i(t) . \]
The \emph{Boltzman} (softmax) algorithm uses
\[ p_i(t+1) \propto \exp \hat\mu_i(t) / \tau. \]
The \emph{reinforcement comparison} method uses ``preferences'' $\pi$
instead of average rewards $\hat\mu$:
\[ p_i(t+1) \propto \exp \pi_i(t) \]
where $\pi_i(t+1) = \pi_i(t) + \beta \bigl[ r(t) - \bar r(t) \bigr] $
if arm $i$ is chosen at time $t$ and gives reward $r(t)$.

There are many variants of \emph{UCB}:
\begin{align*}
  j(t) &= \argmax_i \hat\mu_i + \sqrt{ \dfrac{ 2 \log t }{ n_i } } \\
  j(t) &= \argmax_i \hat\mu_i + \sqrt{
    \dfrac{ \log t }{ n_i } \min \biggl\{ \dfrac14 , V_i \biggr\}
  } \\
  V_i(t) &= \hat\sigma_i(t) + \sqrt{
    \dfrac{ 2 \log t }{ n_i(t) }
    }
\end{align*}

* Unsorted

*** Low rank approximations

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Fastfood -- approximating kernel expansions\\ in loglinear time}\\
  Q. Le et al. (2013)
\end{book}
The \emph{fastfood transform}
\[ V = \dfrac1{ \sigma \sqrt d } D H_d G \Pi H_d B \]
where
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item $\Pi \in \{0,1\}^{d \times d}$ is a permutation matrix;
\item $H_2 = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 1 \\ 1 & -1 \end{bmatrix}$,
  $H_{2\ell} = \begin{bmatrix} H_\ell & H_\ell \\ H_\ell & - H_\ell \end{bmatrix}$;
\item $B$ is diagonal with $\pm1$ entries;
\item $G$ is diagonal, Gaussian;
\item $D$ is diagonal;
\end{compactitem}
approximates Gaussian matrices
$Z \sim N( 0_{n\times d}, \sigma^2 ).$

*** Optimal transport, ICML

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Tree-sliced Wasserstein distance:\\ a geometric perspective}\\
  V.H. Tran et al. (2025)
\end{book}
Instead of projecting on one line, one can project on several
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,scale=.3,inner sep=1pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline=0]
  \begin{scope}
  \draw[thick] (0,4) -- (7,4);
  \draw[thick] (0,.5) -- (7,5);
  \draw[thick] (1,0) -- (1,6);
  \draw[thick] (3,0) -- (2,6);
  \draw[thick] (6,0) -- (3,6);
  \end{scope}
  \node[anchor=east] at (0,4) {\scriptsize 1};
  \node[anchor=east] at (0,.5) {\scriptsize 2};
  \node[anchor=north] at (1,0) {\scriptsize 3};
  \node[anchor=north] at (3,0) {\scriptsize 4};
  \node[anchor=north] at (6,0) {\scriptsize 5};
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
after choosing which intersections can be used to move from one line to another,
so that there is only one path between any two points
(this amounts to choosing a spanning tree whose nodes are the lines);
is is a tree metric space.
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,scale=.3,inner sep=1pt,outer sep=1pt,baseline=0]
  \begin{scope}[inner sep=0, outer sep=0,shift={(0,2.5)},scale=2.5]
    \coordinate (A) at (90:1) {};
    \coordinate (B) at (162:1) {};
    \coordinate (C) at (234:1) {};
    \coordinate (D) at (306:1) {};
    \coordinate (E) at (18:1) {};
    \draw (A) -- (B) -- (C) -- (D) -- (E) -- cycle;
    \draw[line width = 2pt, line join=round, line cap=round, blue!70!black!80] (A) -- (C) -- (E) -- (B);
    \draw[line width = 2pt, line join=round, line cap=round, blue!70!black!80] (D) -- (E);
    \node at (90:1.3) {\scriptsize 1};
    \node at (162:1.3) {\scriptsize 2};
    \node at (234:1.3) {\scriptsize 3};
    \node at (306:1.3) {\scriptsize 4};
    \node at (18:1.3) {\scriptsize 5};
  \end{scope}
  \begin{scope}[shift={(10,0)},every path/.style={thick},inner sep=1.5pt]
  \draw[name path=L1] (0,4) -- (7,4);
  \draw[name path=L2] (0,.5) -- (7,5);
  \draw[name path=L3] (1,0) -- (1,6);
  \draw[name path=L4] (3,0) -- (2,6);
  \draw[name path=L5] (6,0) -- (3,6);
  \path [name intersections={of=L1 and L3,by=P13}];
  \path [name intersections={of=L1 and L4,by=P14}];
  \path [name intersections={of=L1 and L5,by=P15}];
  \path [name intersections={of=L1 and L2,by=P12}];
  \path [name intersections={of=L2 and L3,by=P23}];
  \path [name intersections={of=L2 and L4,by=P24}];
  \path [name intersections={of=L2 and L5,by=P25}];
  \node[circle,fill] at (P13) {};
  \node[circle,fill] at (P14) {};
  \node[circle,fill] at (P15) {};
  \node[circle,fill] at (P24) {};
  \node[circle,fill=white] at (P23) {};
  \node[circle,fill=white] at (P25) {};
  \node[circle,fill=white] at (P12) {};
  \draw[name path=L1] (0,4) -- (7,4);
  %\draw[name path=L2] (0,1) -- (7,5);
  \draw[name path=L3] (1,0) -- (1,6);
  \draw[name path=L4] (3,0) -- (2,6);
  \draw[name path=L5] (6,0) -- (3,6);
  \node[anchor=east] at (0,4) {\scriptsize 1};
  \node[anchor=east] at (0,.5) {\scriptsize 2};
  \node[anchor=north] at (1,0) {\scriptsize 3};
  \node[anchor=north] at (3,0) {\scriptsize 4};
  \node[anchor=north] at (6,0) {\scriptsize 5};
  \end{scope}
\end{tikzpicture}
\]

The Radon transform of a measure $\mu$ on $\RR^d$
\[ R f_\mu(t,\theta) = \int_{\RR^d} f_\mu(x) \delta( t - \langle x, \theta \rangle ) dx \]
and the sliced Wasserstein distance
\[ \text{SW}_p(\mu,\nu) =
\int_{ \mathbf{S}^{d-1} }W_p^p\bigl(
R f_\mu(\cdot,\theta) , R f_\nu( \cdot , \theta )
\bigr) \,d \sigma(\theta) ^{ 1/p} \]
generalize to those \emph{systems of lines}.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{UltraTWD: optimizing ultrametric trees\\ for tree-Wasserstein distance}\\
  F. Yu et al. (2025)
\end{book}
The tree Wasserstein distance (TWD) replaces the Euclidean distance with the distance on a tree whose nodes are the data points --
if the tree structure and the edge weights are well chosen,
it is an aproximation of the Euclidean distance, and the Wasserstein distance has a closed form solution.

There are various ways of building that tree (quad tree, cluster tree) and of computing edge weights (lasso);
one can also average several random trees.

UltraTWD optimizes both the tree structure and the edge weights
by looking for the closest ultrametric distance (rather than just a tree distance).
\[ \operatorname*{Minimize}_{ \tilde D \text{ ultrametric} } \n{ \tilde D - D }_\infty
\quad \text{ or } \quad
\operatorname*{Minimize}_{ \tilde D \text{ ultrametric} } \n{ \tilde D - D }_F
\]

An ultrametric distance is the distance between the leaves of a tree when all leaves are at the same distance from the root.
\[
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth,yscale=.4,xscale=.2,inner sep=1pt,outer sep=0pt,baseline=0]
  \begin{scope}
    \draw[thick] (0,0) -- (2.5,2.5) --(5,0);
    \draw[thick] (2,0) -- (1,1);
    \draw[thick] (3,0) -- (4,1);
    \node[circle,fill] at (0,0) {};
    \node[circle,fill] at (2,0) {};
    \node[circle,fill] at (3,0) {};
    \node[circle,fill] at (5,0) {};
    \node at (2.5,-1) {\footnotesize ultrametric};
  \end{scope}
  \begin{scope}[shift={(12,0)}]
    \draw[thick] (0,0) -- (2.5,2.5) --(5,0);
    \draw[thick] (1.5,.5) -- (1,1);
    \draw[thick] (3.5,.5) -- (4,1);
    \node[circle,fill] at (0,0) {};
    \node[circle,fill] at (1.5,.5) {};
    \node[circle,fill] at (3.5,.5) {};
    \node[circle,fill] at (5,0) {};
    \node at (2.5,-1) {\footnotesize not ultrametric};
  \end{scope}
\end{tikzpicture}
\]
\begin{align*}
  &\text{distance} && d_{ij} \leq d_{ik} + d_{kj} \\
  &\text{tree metric} && d_{ij} + d_{k\ell} \leq \max\{ d_{ik} + d_{j\ell} , d_{i\ell} + d_{jk} \} \\
  &\text{ultrametric} && d_{ij} \leq \max\{ d_{ik} , d_{jk} \}
\end{align*}

For the $\ell^\infty$ norm, the optimal ultrametric can be computed from the minimum spanning tree:
\begin{align*}
  D^*** &= D_{\text{MST}(D)} + \dfrac12 \n{  D_{\text{MST}(D)} - D }_\infty \mathbf 1 \\
  T^*** &= \text{MST}(D^*).
\end{align*}
For the Frobenius norm, the ultrametric nearness problem is NP-hard,
but can be solved with iterated projections or gradient descent (alternate between inner node height updates and tree structure updates).

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Tree-sliced variants of Wasserstein distances}\\
  T. Le et al. (2019)
\end{book}
Tree sliced Wasserstein distances replace the Euclidean distance (cost) with the distance measured on a tree:
quad tree, hierarchical clustering, etc., on a random subset of the data points -- link the remaining points to the closest leaf.

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Lightspeed geometric dataset distance\\ via sliced optimal transport}\\
  K. Nguyen et al. (2025)
\end{book}
We want to compare datasets
\[ \mathcal D_1 = \{ (x_i,y_i) \} _{i=1}^n \qquad \mathcal D_2 = \{ (x_j',y_j') \} _{j=1}^m \]
with optimal transport.
We often have a distance on the features
\[ d : \mathcal X \times \mathcal X \longrightarrow \RR_+ \]
(e.g., Euclidean)
but rarely on the labels -- we do not know that
$d(\text{dog},\text{cat}) < d(\text{dog},\text{plane})$.
We can use a disance between conditional distributions
\[ d(y,y') = \mathrm D \bigl(
p(X|Y=y) , p(X'|Y'=y') \bigr), \]
e.g., the Wasserstein-2 distance, after approximating the conditional distributions with Gaussians.
The \emph{optimal transport dataset distance} (OTDD)
between $\mathcal D_1$ and $\mathcal D_2$ is then the Wasserstein distance for the ground metric
$d^p = d_{\mathcal X}^p + d_{\mathcal Y}^p$.

It is computationally intensive, but we can replace the distance between labels with a sliced distance:
\begin{compactitem}[--]
\item Start with a (conditional) distribution on $\mathcal X$;
\item Project $\mathcal X$ onto $\RR$ (random orthgonal projection,
  or something leveraging prior knowledge of the feature space) to get a distribution on $\RR$;
\item Map that distribution to a scalar, the $\lambda$th scaled moment,
  \[ \int_\RR \dfrac{ x^\lambda }{ \lambda! } f_\mu(x) dx . \]
\end{compactitem}

*** Domain adaptation, ICML

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{A general representation-based approach\\ to multi-source domain adaptation}\\
  I. Ng et al.
\end{book}
Given an SEM
\[ Z_i \sim \text{Pa}(Z_i) ; \quad Y \sim \text{Pa}(Y) \]
where the $Z_i$ are not observed, but $X=g(Z)$ is,
and given enough multidomain data,
it is possible to recover
$Z_{\text{pa}}$ (parents of $Y$),
$Z_{\text{ch}}$ (children) and
$Z_{\text{sp}}$ (spouses)
(they form the Markov blanket of $Y$ -- the minimal predictive set of $Y$)
up to invertible transformations.

*** ViT

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Patch n' Pack: NaBiT, a vision transformer for any aspect ratio and resolution}\\
  M. Dehghani et al.
\end{book}
Instead of resizing images before giving them to a ViT,
pack patches from several images, and have the network output a sequence of labels, one for each input image.

*** ReLU

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Any deep ReLU network is shallow}\\
  M.J. Villani and N. Schoots
\end{book}
A ReLU network is a locally linear function on a partition into polytopes.
Any such function can be represented by a ReLU network,
and 3 hidden layers are enough.
This transformation can be automated,
and the resulting network may be easier to interpret (Shapley, etc.)

*** Symbolic regression, deep RL

\nextbook
\begin{book}
  \emph{Deep symbolic regression for physics\\ guided by unit constraints:
  towards\\ the automated discovery of physical laws}\\
  W. Tenach et al. (2023)
\end{book}
Physical-unit-aware symbolic regression,
with deep reinforcement learning,
for low-noise data
(0.1\% to 10\%):
dimensional analysis drastically reduces the search space.
